# Issues

**The register of everything open in this repo.** Defects, gaps, and questions
awaiting a ruling. One id per item, stable forever.

**This file is the source of truth for what is open.** Nothing is open unless it
is here. Other documents no longer carry their own open-item lists — they link
here. Long analysis may stay where it was argued (`packages/*/PROJECT_STATE.md`,
`IDEAS/*.md`); the **Detail** column points at it. What must
never happen again is an item existing in exactly one document that nobody reads.

**What belongs here:** shipped behaviour that is wrong, a gap that blocks
something, a question that needs a decision. **What does not:** features and
roadmap (`IDEAS/`), settled rulings (`DECISIONS.md`), traps that are correct
behaviour you must know about (`CLAUDE.md` § Live hazards), history
(`packages/*/CHANGES.md`).

**Closed items age out to [ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md).**
§ Closed below keeps the recent ones; the archive keeps the rest, verbatim.
Together they hold every id ever issued, and an id is never reused — so a
`FJS-###` named in a comment, a handoff, or a package file resolves in exactly
one of the two files. Search both before concluding an id is unknown.

## Conventions

| | |
| --- | --- |
| **Id** | `FJS-###` for a defect or gap, `FJS-D##` for a decision. **Never reused, never renumbered.** Closing an item moves it to § Closed with its id. |
| **Sev** | `S1` blocker — data loss, a security hole, or cannot ship · `S2` high — silently wrong, costs a day to find · `S3` medium · `S4` low or cosmetic |
| **Status** | `open` — verified open on the date shown · `stale?` — inherited from an older audit and **not re-verified**; probe before acting · `contested` — the recorded diagnosis is doubted, see the note |
| **Verified** | The date somebody last ran it. A `stale?` row's date is when it was *written*, not confirmed. |

**A row has exactly the columns its table's header names**, and `fli
register:check` fails on anything else (`row-shape`). Both ways of getting it
wrong are silent: a row with FEWER columns is one pasted from the other table,
and the reader infers a shape from the cell count, so it is given a status
nobody wrote and no other rule can see it; a row with MORE has the excess
**dropped when the file is rendered** — in § Closed that is the *How* column, so
the row displays with no citations while they sit in the file. Links belong
inside the last declared column, joined with ` · `, and a `|` that is part of a
sentence is escaped `\|`.

`stale?` is load-bearing. The eight rows inherited from the 2026-07-31 audit
(now `IDEAS/coherence-review.md`) were re-probed on 2026-08-10 and **five of the eight were wrong**:
two were already fixed (conduit's secrets, `admin:generate`), one was never
reproducible (conduit's version skew), and two were right about a defect and
wrong about what it was — sierra's prefetch is not an auth *bypass* but a
cached 401, and caravan's row named three bugs of which two were fixed.
**Treat a `stale?` row as a lead, not a fact** (`VERIFYING.md`).

**The register also goes stale in the closing direction, which nothing here was
watching for.** `FJS-043` was fixed in the tree with a comment describing the
old defect in the past tense, and stayed `open` for eight days because closing
is a second action nobody owed. A row's `Verified` date is evidence about when
somebody last *looked*, not about whether the defect survives — 22 rows carried
`open` at 2026-08-02 when this audit ran, which is the same fiction `stale?`
exists to name, wearing the other label.

## S1 — blockers

| Id | Pkg | Title | Status | Verified | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| <a id="fjs-544"></a>FJS-544 | cli · ci | **The `deploy` CI phase fails after reporting that it wrote the Dockerfile, and the failure is not the documented one.** `scripts/ci.mjs`'s `deploy` phase prints `✓ make:deploy wrote a Dockerfile` and then `fli deploy:local` dies in `docker build`. Two DIFFERENT failures are involved and only the first is known. **(a) The documented one**: without `FJS_CI_WORKDIR`, `unable to prepare context: path … not found` — a shell whose `/tmp` is private to it, which `CLAUDE.md` names and which the phase itself now explains in its own output. **(b) The one underneath it**: with `FJS_CI_WORKDIR` set to a directory the daemon shares, the context loads and the build fails instead with `#1 transferring dockerfile: 2B done` → `ERROR: failed to solve: failed to read dockerfile: open Dockerfile: no such file or directory`, for both `--source npm` and `--source local`. So the workdir variable does not clear the phase; it moves it to a second failure that nothing has ever seen, because (a) masked it. **What IS confirmed, separately, and is a defect on its own terms**: `commands/deploy/local.md` builds with `docker build -t <tag> -f ${dockerfile} ${context.paths.root}` where `dockerfile` is the RELATIVE string `deploy/Dockerfile` and the context is ABSOLUTE, and `context.exec` is `execSync` with no `cwd`, so the command inherits the process's. Docker resolves `-f` against the caller's cwd, so the build only works when cwd happens to be the app root. Measured: from the app root it builds, from anywhere else it is `resolve : lstat deploy: no such file or directory`. The existence check three screens above uses `resolve(context.paths.root, dockerfile)` — the ABSOLUTE path — so the check passes and the build fails, which is exactly the shape of *the Dockerfile was written and docker cannot read it*. **Not claimed**: that this is (b). The reproduced signature (`lstat deploy`) is not CI's (`open Dockerfile`, after a 2-byte transfer), so the cwd defect is a real latent bug found while investigating rather than a diagnosis of the phase. Fix the `-f` to be absolute regardless — a build that depends on cwd is one nobody can invoke from a script — then re-run the phase with `FJS_CI_WORKDIR` set and see what is left. **Neither is caused by the capability work**; the phase was already red before it, and `FJS_CI_REQUIRE_DOCKER=1` is what the workflow sets to stop this skipping quietly **Measured 2026-08-29, which answers the row's own instruction to re-run with `FJS_CI_WORKDIR` set and see what is left.** (a) is confirmed and is cleared by the variable: with `FJS_CI_WORKDIR=$HOME/fjs-ci-work` both `--source npm` and `--source local` **pass**, so `deploy:local` is not broken and the phase's first two thirds were only ever blocked by a private `/tmp`. **(b) reproduced, and it is (a) wearing a different face.** A two-line hand-written Dockerfile in a private-`/tmp` directory gives exactly the recorded signature — `#1 transferring dockerfile: 2B done` then `failed to read dockerfile: open Dockerfile: no such file or directory`. The daemon cannot read the context, gets an EMPTY one (2 bytes), and blames the Dockerfile rather than the path; where the directory is merely unreachable it says `path not found` instead. One root cause, two sentences, which is why the second looked like a defect nobody had seen. With the variable set neither occurs. No `open Dockerfile: no such file or directory`, no 2-byte transfer — the build definition loads at 2.54kB and the image exports. What is actually underneath is a THIRD failure that neither half of this row describes, in the journal cycle rather than in `deploy:local`, and it is `FJS-595`. The cwd defect recorded above is still a real latent bug and still worth fixing on its own terms; it is not what the phase was dying of. CI goes 4 failures to 1 with the variable set | open | 2026-08-29 | [commands/deploy/local.md](packages/cli/commands/deploy/local.md) · [core/vendor.js](packages/cli/core/vendor.js) · [scripts/ci.mjs](scripts/ci.mjs) |

## S2 — high

| Id | Pkg | Title | Status | Verified | Detail |

| <a id="fjs-713"></a>FJS-713 | junction · litestone | **`$readAs` and the compiled row policy disagree about who owns a row whenever the id column is an `Int`, so broadcast grading refuses the owner their own socket while HTTP answers the row.** A `SessionContext` carries `userId` as a STRING, and the same predicate is then evaluated by two engines with different equality rules: through a query `customerId == auth().id` is **TRUE**, because the comparison reaches SQLite and the column's affinity coerces the parameter; inside `$readAs` it is **FALSE**, because that path compares in JS and `5 === '5'` is false. So on any model keyed by an integer — which is most of them — the owner reads their own row over HTTP with a 200 and is then graded out of the broadcast for it, and the live screen that made the change never sees the change. It is `FJS-D175`'s `toDataPrincipal` hazard one column type over, and it is the reason the grading path's own test deliberately uses a `String` id column and says so in a comment (`tests/realtime-grading.test.ts`) — so the suite is green and the shape it is green on is the rarer one. **Found while fixing `FJS-672`** and left where it belongs: the disagreement is at the Data boundary, not in the fan-out. Two fix shapes and they are not equivalent — `toDataPrincipal` coerces `id` to the declared id type of the `User` model, which fixes every consumer of the principal at once and needs the schema; or `$readAs` compares with SQLite's affinity rules, which `compareValues` in junction's `core/sort.ts` already implements and which would have to move to litestone or be re-derived there. The first is narrower and the second closes the class | open | 2026-09-02 | [channels.ts](packages/junction/src/transport/channels.ts) · [litestone client.js § `$readAs`](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [core/litestone.ts § `toDataPrincipal`](packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts) · [sort.ts § `compareValues`](packages/junction/src/core/sort.ts) · [FJS-D175](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d175) · [FJS-672](#fjs-672) · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) |
| <a id="fjs-681"></a>FJS-681 | junction | **The webhooks plugin lets any signed-in caller register a `*` subscriber to any URL and then delivers ungraded rows to it.** There is no capability or level on registration, no URL validation, and no read gate on the payload, so the plugin is an SSRF instrument and an exfiltration channel at once. **Measured**: a shopper subscribed to `*` and received `{"password":"hunter2","internalNote":"do not leak"}` from a `@guarded` model — the delivery is built from the post-hook row, which is the row as the writer saw it rather than as the subscriber may read it. The destination is unrestricted: `169.254.169.254` (cloud instance metadata), `localhost:8503/api/jobs/1/retry` (the devtools job runner) and `file://` were all accepted, and redirects are followed, so an allowlist on the registered URL alone would not hold either. Dead subscribers are never disabled, so a URL that stops answering is retried indefinitely. This is `FJS-631`'s class one layer over — a row reaching an audience nobody graded. Fix: put registration behind a capability or gate level; add `validateWebhookUrl()` requiring https and a DNS-resolved public address, re-checked at send time; `redirect: 'manual'`; auto-disable after N failures; and send either ids-only thin events or a payload passed through the model's read gate for a stated audience. Finding `batteries-2` | open | 2026-09-02 | [plugins/webhooks](packages/junction/src/plugins/webhooks/index.ts) · [FJS-631](#fjs-631) · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) |
| <a id="fjs-682"></a>FJS-682 | junction | **A nested transactional service call breaks all three post-write guarantees: `afterCommit` runs early, the announcement fires inside the open transaction, and the row announces twice.** The effect queue and the announcement are properties of the CALL, while the rows belong to the outermost transaction, so a call nested inside another one settles on the wrong clock. **Measured**: on the rollback path, `AFTER ROLLBACK rows: audits=0 busEvents=1 afterCommit RAN` — the effect that `CLAUDE.md` § API promises runs only on the success path after the commit ran for a row that no longer exists, which is an email sent about a cancelled order. On the success path, `busEvents for ONE inner create: 2`, because `callService` announces and the litestone tap announces too: the tap's by-name suppression compares against the service span and misses under an outer span. So a subscriber sees the same write twice, and a screen applying both is doing the work twice. Fix: make the effect queue and the announcement properties of the OUTERMOST transaction — a nested call pushes its `afterCommit` functions and its announcements onto the parent's queues, and one flush owns them; either stop announcing from `callService` inside an open transaction, or tag the tap's flushed events with the originating service so the suppression can see them. Finding `adapter-1` | open | 2026-09-02 | [core/litestone.ts](packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts) · [service.ts](packages/junction/src/core/service.ts) · [hooks-builtin.ts](packages/junction/src/core/hooks-builtin.ts) · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) |
| <a id="fjs-684"></a>FJS-684 | conduit | **The circuit breaker opens on a target that is working, because every non-2xx answer is `server_error` and every `server_error` is a fault.** The kind is derived from the status class and nothing else, so a 404, a validation failure against the declared response shape, an HTML error page and a truncated body all arrive under one word — and that word feeds three consumers at once: the retry decision, the `retryable` flag a caravan job acts on, and the breaker's failure count. **Measured**: five 404s in a row opened the circuit (`circuit_open`) on a healthy target, after which correct requests were refused locally. The README documents the current behaviour, so this is a taxonomy that was chosen rather than one that drifted. `FJS-648`, `FJS-649` and `FJS-650` each carved one case out of the same word; these are the next of the same carving, and the Stripe connector's `translate()` already wants the split — it re-parses `raw` to work out that a 402 was a decline rather than an outage. Fix: make the kind say who is at fault — `client_error` for 4xx carrying `raw`, `invalid_response` for validation, HTML and bad JSON, `server_error` for 5xx alone — and count only `connection_failed`, `timeout` and `server_error` as faults. Finding `conduit-1` | open | 2026-09-02 | [resilience.ts](packages/conduit/src/resilience.ts) · [transports/http.ts](packages/conduit/src/transports/http.ts) · [example stripe connector](example/api/src/providers/stripe/index.ts) · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) |
| <a id="fjs-685"></a>FJS-685 | conduit | **There is no default concurrency cap, so a burst queues inside Bun's connection pool, times out, and opens the breaker on a target that answered everything.** Nothing bounds in-flight sends per target, and the per-attempt timer starts when the send is issued rather than when the socket is dispatched, so time spent waiting for a connection is charged to the target as a timeout. **Measured**: 5000 concurrent `GET /slow?ms=500` took 10 080 ms and returned `{ok:4864, timeout:136}` while the server saw 256 connections and the process went from 23 to 533 file descriptors — then `breakers: open (failures:136)`, on a target that had served every request it received. With `max_concurrent: 50` the same burst answered `{ok:50, overloaded:450}` instantly with no queue, which is the honest answer. Keep-alive is not the problem and is working: 1000 sequential sends cost 0.32 ms each on one connection. Fix: a default `max_concurrent` of roughly 64 per target with a bounded `queue_ms`, start the per-attempt timer at socket dispatch, and classify a timeout spent queued as `overloaded` rather than as a fault. Finding `conduit-4` | open | 2026-09-02 | [transports/http.ts](packages/conduit/src/transports/http.ts) · [resilience.ts](packages/conduit/src/resilience.ts) · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) |
| <a id="fjs-687"></a>FJS-687 | junction · litestone | **`$` survives the call it belongs to and follows it into timers, where `$.db` is the settled transaction client and still accepts writes.** The async-local store propagates into every timer and microtask created inside a call, and nothing marks the call ended, so an ambient object whose whole safety argument is that it refuses outside a call does not refuse. **Measured**: a `setTimeout(30ms)` scheduled inside a transactional create found `$` answering `{method:'create'}` after the call had resolved, and `txClient.post.create(…)` from inside that timer **succeeded**, writing row id 2 through a committed transaction's client, with zero warnings. `CLAUDE.md` § Bridge index states the opposite — that `$` throws by name outside a call, and that a loud failure is the whole of what makes an ambient dependency acceptable. The shape reaches real code easily: a `setTimeout` for a retry, a floating promise, anything deferred from a hook. Fix in three places, because each is a different boundary: mark the context `ended` when `_callService` resolves and make `held()` throw naming the call; null `ctx.locals.db` in the transaction hook's `finally`; and have litestone refuse writes on a settled transaction proxy. | <a id="fjs-688"></a>FJS-688 | junction | **With `transactional` off, which is the default, an after-hook throw leaves the row committed, answers 500, and announces nothing.** The fan-out is guarded on `!pipelineError`, so a throw anywhere after the method suppresses the announcement, and the litestone tap is suppressed too because the write happened inside the service span. **Measured**: `rowsInDb: 1, busEvents: []` — the write is real and permanent, the caller is told it failed, and every open tab still shows the old value with nothing to correct it. The caller's natural response to a 500 is to retry, which writes a second row. This is `FJS-089`'s neighbour and the cost is the divergence rather than the error: three parties end up with three different beliefs about one write. Fix: announce on METHOD success rather than on pipeline success, and answer the error with `data: { committed: true }` so a client re-reads instead of retrying. Finding `core-3` | open | 2026-09-02 | [service.ts](packages/junction/src/core/service.ts) · [channels.ts](packages/junction/src/transport/channels.ts) · [FJS-089](#fjs-089) · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) |
| <a id="fjs-693"></a>FJS-693 | junction | **Shutdown has no deadline, keeps accepting work, and a plugin whose `shutdown()` never resolves makes the process exit 0 with every remaining step skipped.** `FJS-460`'s drain holds and these sit beside it. **Measured**: a new request arriving during the drain answered 200 and `/health` stayed 200 throughout, so a load balancer keeps sending traffic to a process that is leaving; a request longer than `drainTimeout` is cut with no 503 and no `Connection: close`, so the client cannot tell a shutdown from a failure; and a plugin `shutdown()` that never settles ends with the **process exiting 0 in 54 ms** — every timer is unref'd, the loop empties, node exits successfully — with the caravan pool, the outbox relay and the litestone close all skipped and *Shutdown complete* never printed. A zero exit code is what an orchestrator reads as a clean stop, so the skipped steps are invisible. There is also no `unhandledRejection` policy: a rejected promise in a timer kills the process without `stop()` running at all. Fix: set `draining = true` so readiness answers 503 with `Connection: close`; `Promise.race` each plugin's `shutdown()` against a per-plugin timeout; an overall deadline then `exit(1)`; install `unhandledRejection` and `uncaughtException` handlers that log structurally, call `stop()` and exit 1; and await an `app:shutdown` event. | <a id="fjs-694"></a>FJS-694 | junction · litestone | **A model with a composite `@@id` is unusable through the derived service, because the cursor tiebreaker appends a column called `id` that does not exist.** `orderTotal` appends the literal `id` to make an ordering total, which is right for every model keyed by one column and wrong for a tuple. **Measured**: `GET /memberships` answers `400 orderBy.id: Unknown orderBy field 'id'`, so the list is unreachable; and get-by-id is inexpressible, because `idField` is one name and the key is two. `FJS-608` fixed create for these models, so a row can be made through the service and then never read back through it — which is the worst place for the gap to sit, since the schema parses, migrates and snapshots cleanly and the failure arrives at the first request. Fix: `orderTotal` appends the model's PRIMARY KEY tuple rather than the literal `id`; `idField` defaults to the schema's declared key and accepts a tuple; and `fli check` refuses a composite-keyed model behind a derived service until it does. | <a id="fjs-698"></a>FJS-698 | caravan | **`stats()` and `list()` are unindexed scans wired to `/metrics` and `/health`, so the two endpoints an operator leans on during an incident make the incident worse.** `stats()` is registered through `registerMetricsSource`, whose sources must be synchronous, so the scan runs on the scrape. **Measured** against 1M terminal rows plus 1k pending: `stats()` costs **680 ms** per scrape and per health probe; `list()` page one costs 571 ms with `SCAN jobs then TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY` and no index on `created_at`; the cleanup sweep costs 166 ms with nothing to delete and **3 075 ms** on its first pass past retention; and the file stays 130 MB after deleting 1M rows, because nothing vacuums. Fix: a covering index or a few-second cached aggregate for `stats()`; indexes on `(created_at DESC)`, `(status, created_at)` and `(queue, created_at)`; a batched `DELETE … LIMIT` spread across ticks; and `auto_vacuum = INCREMENTAL`. | <a id="fjs-701"></a>FJS-701 | junction | **There is no event id, cursor or resume, so a reconnect loses every write in the gap and reports nothing missing.** Resubscription works and the missed window simply vanishes; `resource.stale`, which exists to count exactly this, stays at zero because nothing knows a gap occurred. **Measured**: rows 3505–3507 written during a 1 s reconnect were absent from the store afterwards, `stale: 0`, and the next write (3508) arrived normally — so the store is live, consistent-looking and wrong, which is the worst combination for a screen somebody is reading. A reconnect is not an edge case: it is every deploy, every laptop lid, every mobile handover, and `x-fjs-build` already treats the reconnect as the moment a client finds out about a deploy. This is `D7`'s second face — the second half of a protocol never written, with each half green on its own. Fix: a per-connection monotonic sequence on event frames; the client sends its last sequence on upgrade; the server answers `resync: true` when it cannot serve the gap, and every live resource re-`load()`s and bumps `stale`. | <a id="fjs-703"></a>FJS-703 | junction | **Presence is unconditional, cannot be turned off, and is O(N²), so a reconnect storm is not survivable.** `channel()` wraps join and leave for every channel there is, and `presenceJoin` sends the full member list to the joiner plus a join frame to every existing member. **Measured** at two channels per connection: N=200 produced 40 600 frames and 14.3 MB; N=500 produced **251 500 frames, 89.5 MB out and 172 MB of heap**. The anonymous control is the diagnosis — 500 anonymous connections produced 1000 frames, because presence skips anonymous callers — so the cost is paid exactly by the signed-in users an application has. A post-deploy reconnect of 5000 users is the ordinary event this makes fatal, and it compounds with `FJS-701`, since the reconnect is already lossy. Fix: `channels({ presence: false })` as the default with per-channel opt-in, and send a diff rather than the full member list. Finding `realtime-6` | open | 2026-09-02 | [channels.ts](packages/junction/src/transport/channels.ts) · [send-queue.ts](packages/junction/src/transport/send-queue.ts) · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) |
| <a id="fjs-704"></a>FJS-704 | junction | **`subscribe` meta is unvalidated, unbounded and rebroadcast, so any connection is a 200× amplifier into any channel it belongs to.** Whatever a client sends as meta is stored and fanned out as `presence:update` to every member, with no size cap, no declared shape and no rate limit. **Measured**: one inbound 200 KB frame produced **39.8 MB of egress to 199 members in 114 ms**. The amplification factor is the channel's membership, so it grows with the application's success, and the cost is borne by the server and by every other member's connection. It needs no privilege beyond being in the channel, which for a public channel is nobody's privilege at all. Fix: cap the meta in bytes, require an app-declared shape, and rate-limit presence updates per connection. | <a id="fjs-705"></a>FJS-705 | junction | **The WebSocket surface has no inbound rate limit, no in-flight cap, no connection cap and no configured `maxPayloadLength`, and the HTTP rate limiter does not cover it.** Every bound the HTTP transport has stops at the upgrade. **Measured** from one anonymous socket: a 15 MB frame moved a victim's latency from 9.2 ms to 49.8 ms; 20 000 `find` frames were answered in 1.1 s and took the victim's latency to **1 093 ms**, with the offending socket still open and unthrottled; and 3000 anonymous sockets were accepted in 2.2 s. Bun's 16 MB default is the only payload bound, and a 20 MB frame kills the socket with a 1006 and no message, so the caller cannot tell a limit from a crash. Every service call is reachable this way, so the cheapest denial of service against an application is the transport it prefers. | <a id="fjs-595"></a>FJS-595 | cli · ci | **`fli deploy --resume` after a crash takes the lock over and then starts the deploy from step 1 instead of continuing from the step the lock records.** Newly reachable: this is what is underneath `FJS-544`, whose (a) had masked the whole journal cycle for as long as it existed, so `deployJournalCycle` — the only thing in the repo that runs `fli deploy` at all — had never got this far in this environment. Reproduced with `FJS_CI_WORKDIR=$HOME/fjs-ci-work node scripts/ci.mjs`: the cycle crashes a deploy inside `05-backup`, the lock correctly records `step=05-backup`, the rerun prints `--resume took the lock over — it held: run=365e9c51 · actor=j · target=dev · step=05-backup`, and then runs `[1/12] 01-preflight` onward and performs a **full `docker build`** (`#1 transferring dockerfile: 2.54kB`, 17 build stages, a fresh image export) rather than continuing at the recorded step. **Diagnosed 2026-08-29.** The journal names it: the crashed run is Release `cc563ab953c8`, the resume minted `5129301385b2`, with `02-pull` reporting *Already up to date* between them. A Release id is `sha(format, app, digest, bindings, schema, pivot)`, so with the source unchanged only `digest` can have moved — and `digest` is the **local docker image id**, which is not a content address. Measured directly: one Dockerfile and one unchanged file, built twice, gives `1f021e1eccf8` cached and `a9c17ea37ed9` with `--no-cache`. So any rebuild that is not a FULL cache hit mints a new Release from identical bytes, the transition id changes with it, and there is nothing for `--resume` to find. This is also why *an unchanged redeploy mints the same Release* passes: deploys 1 and 2 are a total cache hit (both `e44aef42ba90`). That assertion is therefore green by luck and would fail on a cold cache — a latent second defect on the same fact. **The step list is a red herring**: `resumeDecision` skips a succeeded step and reruns a `running` one, so walking all 12 is correct; the run never got that far because the journal had no matching transition. **Two fixes are possible and they are different subsystems.** `FJS-D156` states a resume is safe without a probe because *a succeeded step replays into a no-op*, which would make restarting from 1 the design and the cycle's assertion the thing that is wrong — but a full image rebuild is not a no-op, and the whole reason the lock was taught to record a step was that four minutes in `04-build-api` and four minutes in `06-swap` mean different things. So either the resume should skip to the recorded step, or the step in the lock is diagnostic only and the assertion should say so. Whoever takes it should decide that first — the code change is small either way and the wrong one makes the register agree with a bug. The same run also failed its pre-deploy backup, which is **[FJS-574](#fjs-574)** and already open — not restated here | open | 2026-08-29 | [scripts/scaffold-build.mjs](scripts/scaffold-build.mjs) · [core/lock.js](packages/cli/core/lock.js) · [FJS-544](#fjs-544) · [FJS-D156](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d156) |

| <a id="fjs-706"></a>FJS-706 | junction | **The service pipeline's construction-time surfaces return an object where they should return a verdict, so ten authoring mistakes compile green.** Umbrella for the medium and low core findings; each is `D1`'s shape — fail-closed on the caller, fail-open on the author. **`core-4`**: a custom method named after an option key (`cache`, `channel`) is dispatched AND consumed as the option, so caching turns on with no bust while `describe().cache` reports false — a row inserted between two GETs was invisible to the second. **`core-5`**: a throwing `error` hook is swallowed with no log; an `around` hook that never calls `next()` answers 204; `before: { creat: … }` compiles a dead pipeline with no warning, where `CLAUDE.md` claims it warns. **`core-6`**: `junction.config.js` has no completeness tripwire — `{ prot: 4444, apiPrefx: '/api' }`, `plugin:` and `middlewares:` are all merged or stashed with `warnings: []`, which is `FJS-431`'s neighbour. **`core-7`**: a plugin `boot()` throw leaves earlier plugins' resources alive with `shutdown` never called and the process hanging; `start()` twice fails with `Cannot add middleware after the router is built`; a `ready()` throw is only logged, while already serving. **`core-8`**: measured, 20 concurrent creates with a 50 ms after-hook cost 60 ms with `transactional: false` and **1023 ms** with it on, fully serialised on SQLite's write lock — wants a bounded `transactional: { timeout }`. **`core-9`**: a custom method announces whatever it returns, per element — an array is a list envelope AND one event per element, a primitive announces `posts:prim 42`, and a `find` returning `{data, errors:'oops'}` passes `errors` through unvalidated. **`core-10`**: `isServiceResult` is structural, so a row with columns `kind`/`object`/`data` is mistaken for an envelope — wants a Symbol brand. **`core-11`**: no per-call timeout, no `AbortSignal` into a call, no trace parent (a nested call is a sibling), and `app.service('x')` is `Promise<unknown>` on the server while `ServiceTypes` types the browser. **`core-12`**: `registerErrorMapper` and `setServiceCache` are process-global across apps, `app.claim()` is bypassable by plain assignment, and `ready()` runs after `listen`. **`core-13`**: the loader takes the first alphabetical service on a duplicate name and continues, swallows a factory throw into a 404, silently keeps only the first of two `create*Service` exports in one file, and lets a service named `health` shadow the mounted route while answering `/health/1`. Promote any of these to its own id when it is worked | open | 2026-09-02 | [service.ts](packages/junction/src/core/service.ts) · [app.ts](packages/junction/src/core/app.ts) · [loader.ts](packages/junction/src/core/loader.ts) · [config](packages/junction/src/config/index.ts) · [envelope.ts](packages/junction/src/core/envelope.ts) · [FJS-431](#fjs-431) · [audit § 3.1](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) |
| <a id="fjs-707"></a>FJS-707 | junction | **The HTTP transport bounds one end of each limit and inherits the rest of its behaviour from Bun without deciding it.** Umbrella for the remaining transport findings, `D2` and `D4`. **`transport-4`**: `trustProxy: true` takes the LEFTMOST `X-Forwarded-For`, which the client controls, while the shipped nginx template uses `$proxy_add_x_forwarded_for`, which appends — so behind the documented proxy the rate limiter and the DDoS guard key on a spoofable value; 5 requests with 5 forged headers counted as 5 distinct clients. Wants a hop count or CIDR list resolving rightmost-untrusted, with bare `true` meaning one trusted hop. **`transport-6`**: the static server blocks `..` and NUL but follows symlinks out of the root — `assets/css/link.css → ../../secret.txt` served 200 and the contents — wants a `realpath` containment check. **`transport-7`**: no HEAD (404 on every GET resource), no default OPTIONS, and an unknown verb 404s where it should be 405 with `Allow`. **`transport-9`**: no request, handler or body-read timeout, no `idleTimeout` passed to `Bun.serve` (Bun's 10 s default, unexposed — Bun prints the fix in its own log), and no concurrent-connection cap; a slowloris was closed at 11.5 s by Bun while a slow handler ran unbounded. **`transport-10`**: a chunked body with no `Content-Length` bypasses the pre-read size check — 50 MB was read whole and THEN answered 413, with Bun's 128 MB default as the real ceiling — wants `maxRequestBodySize` passed through from `config.http.maxBodySize`. **`transport-11`**: idempotency does not fingerprint the body, so the same key with a different body silently replays the first answer where Stripe answers 422. | <a id="fjs-708"></a>FJS-708 | junction · litestone | **The junction–litestone seam has two units of work, and the caches, the outbox and the filter checker each pay for it.** Umbrella for the remaining adapter findings, `D3`'s shape — litestone caches per client while junction makes a client per request. **`adapter-5`**: every adapter cache is a `WeakMap` keyed on the client and junction hands it a fresh `$setAuth()` proxy per request, so the JSON Schema is regenerated, the validator recompiled, the gate string re-parsed and the column set rebuilt on EVERY write — measured at 1.52 ms/request against 0.14 ms/op direct on a 46-model schema, `generateJsonSchema` alone 0.71 ms, 100 POSTs missing in each of four caches. Wants keying on the root client's `$schema` identity, or the root stashed on `ctx.locals`. **`adapter-6`**: the outbox relay has no attempt cap and no dead letter, so a poison row is retried every 5 s forever with `attempts` climbing; 5 SQL statements per pass per database while idle; and under `strategy database` a full scan of EVERY tenant database every pass, so 300 tenants means 300 opens through a pool of `maxOpen` 100, thrashing `FJS-D172`'s pool. Delivery order is not preserved across a failure. Crash replay is correct. **`adapter-7`**: `autoValidate` and `validateInput` strip unknown keys silently with no strict mode reachable, so `{title, titel:'typo'}` answers 201; and `update` (PUT) is documented as a replace and implemented as a merge. **`adapter-8`**: relation filters and `$populate` are not validated — `$checkWhere` is one level deep — so `customer[is][nope]=1` answers **500** `no such column: t.nope`; it is quoted and not injectable, but a caller-supplied name entered a SQL pattern, which is Invariant 8's class, and a modelless service gets no filter validation at all. **`adapter-9`**: the `$after` cursor is plain base64url JSON with no shape check and no signature — garbage answers 500, wrong arity answers a silent empty list. **`adapter-10`**: a custom method has no API-level gate, so one anonymous caller gets 401 on CRUD and 403 on a custom method over the same model, which sierra renders differently. **`adapter-11`**: `$tapQuery` does not see the statements `include` and `$populate` run, so telemetry reports `SQL=1` for a 100-row populate; includes are batched rather than N+1. **`adapter-12`**: no read-replica or read-only client path, no HTTP upsert (`PUT /orders/99` answers 404), no strict validation. | <a id="fjs-629"></a>FJS-629 | example · junction | **There is no way to remove a person, and the reason is a real gap rather than an omission: a custom method cannot ask the schema for its own authority.** `example`'s `users` service declines DELETE deliberately — auth keeps `Credential` and `Session` at `@@gate("8")` with `userId` as a bare String and no relation behind it, so removing a `User` through the ORM leaves live sessions authenticating against a row that is gone. The fix is a naming rule that can tell the two apart — a distinct prefix for the constraint form is the obvious one and is not free, because the prefix IS the ownership test in `migrate.js`, so anything new has to be recognised there or litestone stops dropping its own index. | <a id="fjs-598"></a>FJS-598 | repo · litestone | **The tool built to find holes in the checks is run by hand or not at all — `bun run ci` has no phase for it.** `grep mutate scripts/ci.mjs` answers nothing. `litestone mutate` mutates the SCHEMA and runs the checks derived from the ORIGINAL against a database built from the mutant, so a surviving mutant is a statement about the SUITE rather than about the schema — and every survivor it has reported so far named a check that did not exist yet and now does (`verifyGateLadder`, `verifyConstraints`, `verifyFieldProtection` and `verifyRowPolicies` are each in the default set because a mutant survived without one). | <a id="fjs-401"></a>FJS-401 | junction · litestone | **A declared input gets a `type`'s validators and not its transforms, and wiring them naively would make the two boundaries disagree about what is VALID.** `methods: [{ method, input: 'PayOrder' }]` validates a custom method's payload against a `type T { … }` in the seed — required keys, types, `@length`, `@gte`, the author's `x-messages`, all of it. What it does not get is `@trim`/`@lower`/`@upper`/`@slug`: those are enforced in litestone's `applyTransforms` at the Data boundary and are emitted into JSON Schema **nowhere**, so nothing downstream can see them, and a custom method's payload never becomes a model write and therefore never meets them. The reason this is a filed question rather than a small patch is ORDER: litestone transforms BEFORE validating (`validate.js:155`), junction's `FieldDef.transform` runs AFTER (`schema.ts:233`), so `@trim @length(3,12)` on `'  ab  '` fails at the Data boundary and passes through a service — the two would not merely produce different output, they would disagree about what is legal, which is the shape Invariant 4 exists to prevent. Doing it properly is three moves and each has a decision in it: litestone emits the transform names (a new keyword in a document with a committed snapshot and three readers); junction gains a PRE-validation slot rather than reusing `transform`, so nothing existing moves; and the implementations want one owner, which by the shape of `/inflect` and `/directives` is a `@frontierjs/toolbelt` subpath both packages read — `@slug` in particular is not a one-liner anyone should write twice. | <a id="fjs-660"></a>FJS-660 | conduit | **`result.meta.duration_ms` reports the LAST ATTEMPT and `stats()` reports the whole call, so the number on the public result under-reports every retried request.** **Measured** 2026-09-02 against a server failing twice then succeeding: `meta.duration_ms: 1` on a call that really took **1,715 ms** across three attempts — the transport builds `meta` per attempt, and the conduit layer measures the whole call correctly but only into its own counters. So an app logging latency off the result is right until something retries and then silently wrong in exactly the case worth logging, while `/metrics` disagrees with it by three orders of magnitude. Not a new finding: `packages/conduit/docs/AUDIT.md` carries it under *Still open* and has since 2026-08-01, which is the one-document-nobody-reads shape this register exists to close — it is filed here so it is visible from the same place as everything else in the package. Fix is to stamp the total at the conduit layer where it is already computed, and to decide whether the per-attempt number is worth keeping beside it under its own name | open | 2026-09-02 | [transports/base.ts:31](packages/conduit/src/transports/base.ts#L31) `ok()` · [conduit.ts](packages/conduit/src/conduit.ts) where the real number is measured · [docs/AUDIT.md](packages/conduit/docs/AUDIT.md) |
| <a id="fjs-610"></a>FJS-610 | example | **A soft decline is never re-presented, so every invoice is charged exactly once and the distinction the code makes between soft and hard buys nothing.** `declineKind` separates *the money may be there tomorrow* from *this card will never work*, and the whole value of the first is that the clock keeps running and the charge is tried again. Nothing tries it again: `renew-subscription` dispatches `collect-invoice` once, and `dun-subscriptions` grades STATES — lapse at the grace, cancel at the deadline, recover when the ledger is clean — and dispatches nothing. So a soft-declined invoice sits `issued` until the deadline cancels the subscription, having been presented one time. **Caravan's retry ladder does not cover it and cannot**: a transport failure throws and retries, but a decline is a domain ANSWER returned as a value, which exits the ladder successfully. The fix is a second dispatch on the dunning pass, under `unique` for [`FJS-609`](#fjs-609)'s reason, with the cadence a decision rather than a default — a shop that re-presents daily for 21 days is a shop the card networks notice. Not a defect in anything built: every piece is correct on its own, and this is the half the design implies and nobody wrote. `verify:collect` asserts the soft decline leaves the subscription alone, which is true and is not the same as the money being asked for again | open | 2026-08-30 | [dun-subscriptions.job.ts](example/api/src/jobs/dun-subscriptions.job.ts) · [billing.ts `declineKind`](example/api/src/domain/billing/billing.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-607"></a>FJS-607 | ui · mesa | **A `slot="actions"` child wrapped in `{#if}` turns the generated field list OFF, and the form renders with no controls in it and nothing said.** `<Form>` decides whether to generate from `auto ?? !$slots.default`, and `slot=` is an attribute on an ELEMENT — so `{#if cond}<Button slot="actions" …/>{/if}` puts the BLOCK in the default slot, `$slots.default` is truthy, and the caller is read as having written the form themselves. The failure is total and silent: every field disappears, the form still submits, and the page looks like a component that did not load. Measured 2026-08-30 writing `example`'s `PlanVersion.mesa`, where the reprice screen rendered a Save button over nothing and the drive failed on a missing `[name="price"]` rather than on anything about money. It is arguably correct semantics — a block is content — which is what makes it worth a rule rather than a repair: **the cheap half is a warning**, since `<Form>` already knows it generated nothing AND rendered no children of its own, and that pair is not a legitimate form. The expensive half is asking whether a block whose every branch is slotted should count as default content, which is a Mesa question about `$slots` and not a `<Form>` one. Related in shape to the comment case already noted in root `CLAUDE.md` § UI (a slot whose only child is an HTML comment counted as children). The workaround is to render both buttons unconditionally, which is what every other resource file here already does **The cheap half shipped 2026-08-30 and the row is now the expensive half alone.** `<Form>` can see the pair it was always able to see — it generated nothing AND nothing a person can type into is inside it, over a resource that had fields to offer — and warns naming them and the cause. It is asked of the DOM in `$.onMount`, because children arrive as opaque content and counting them says nothing about whether any is a control; the gate is the resource's own offer, so a form built entirely by hand and a form over a resource with nothing writable both stay silent. **What is still open is the Mesa question**: should a block whose every branch is slotted count as default content? Until that is answered the failure still happens, it just says so — the workaround is unchanged, render the slotted children unconditionally. `packages/ui`: `test/browser/specs/form-generate.spec.mjs` PINS the behaviour rather than asserting it is right, so settling the Mesa half turns two rows red on purpose, with the warning asserted beside them | open | 2026-08-30 | [Form.mesa](packages/ui/components/forms/Form.mesa) · [PlanVersion.mesa](example/web/src/resources/PlanVersion.mesa) |
| <a id="fjs-606"></a>FJS-606 | litestone | **The array-type refusal names three types the parser refuses, so following its advice is a second error.** A `Float[]` is refused with *array [] is only supported for Text, Integer, File, an enum name, or a model name for many-to-many* — but `Text` and `Integer` are the OLD names, cut rather than aliased, and writing either gets *Type 'Text' was renamed to 'String'*. The allow-set in `parser.js` is correct (`String`, `Int`, `File`); only the message it formats is stale, so nothing behavioural is wrong and no test can see it — a refusal test asserts that the schema was refused. **It is a defect against the reader rather than the schema**, and the reader it costs most is one that cannot ask a second question: an agent takes the sentence literally, writes `Text[]`, is refused again, and has been walked one step further from a schema that parses. | <a id="fjs-373"></a>FJS-373 | cli · css | **Invariant 13 is the only styling rule the framework states and nothing checks it.** *Style with a tone and a treatment, never a colour* is enforced inside `packages/css` (a literal `font-size` outside `tokens.css` fails its suite) and nowhere in an app. Measured on `KOBAMI/my.maid.tech`, 56k lines written to this framework's ideas: it HAS the semantic layer — `theme-primary`, `theme-danger`, `.btn.outlined`, `.btn.danger`, arrived at independently — and uses **578 raw colour utilities against 156 semantic ones**, about 4:1 (`text-gray-500` 74, `bg-gray-100` 54, `text-red-600` 13). The vocabulary was not rejected; it was out-competed by whatever was quicker under deadline, which is what a rule with no check always loses to. `fli check` is the place — it already owns everything derived from the seed while Biome owns generic JavaScript (`FJS-D33`), and it reads `.mesa`, which Biome does not. Open question the rule has to answer before it can exist: an app may legitimately run UnoCSS alongside `@frontierjs/css` (`packages/css/README.md` § Using it with UnoCSS), so the check counts and reports a ratio rather than refusing a class — a warn naming the worst files, in the shape of `package-root-md`, which warns and names rather than failing because the rule cannot tell a stray from the next necessary thing | open | 2026-08-21 | [core/checks.js](packages/cli/core/checks.js) · [vocabulary.js](packages/css/vocabulary.js) · [IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md](IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md) § 2.3 |

## Needs a decision

These are questions, not defects. A ruling moves to `DECISIONS.md` and closes the
row here.

| Id | Area | Question | Detail |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| <a id="fjs-d121"></a>FJS-D121 | litestone · sierra | **Does a per-caller ORDER belong in the schema — the third value-set axis?** Axis 3 of `IDEAS/value-sets.md`, held out of `FJS-D120` deliberately and WANTED, not parked. The case is the one this whole line of work started from: assigning a task, a person picks the same one to three people over and over, and an alphabetical list makes them search every time. Float the ones they actually use. Mozilla calls the general shape *frecency*; a sliding window gives decay for free. **It is not membership and must not be fused with strength.** A *suggested* binding and a *suggested* order are different sentences, and a system with one word for both cannot say *anyone may be assigned, but show me the three I actually use*. Nothing about order changes what is legal. **What makes it a question rather than a build**: it needs somewhere to KEEP the recency, and that is per-caller state rather than a fact about the data — so unlike membership and strength it is not obviously a schema declaration at all. Three shapes were considered: a `Recent` model (its own table, `Int @id` — see `packages/litestone/docs/modelling.md`), a JSON field on `User`, and localStorage. The argument FOR declaring it here anyway is the one that put the control table in one module: a hand-written picker and a generated one must not order differently. The argument AGAINST is that it is per-caller and non-authoritative, which is the definition of a UI-realm concern. A ruling has to say where the counter lives, whether the schema names it at all, and whether `resource.options()` applies it or a control does. | [value-sets.md](IDEAS/value-sets.md) § open questions · [FJS-D120](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d120) |
| <a id="fjs-d122"></a>FJS-D122 | litestone · sierra | **How does a DEPENDENT set compose — one whose members depend on another field's value?** The second axis held out of `FJS-D120`, and WANTED. Pick a country, then the state list is that country's. Pick a category, then the subcategory list narrows. Salesforce calls it a controlling field, Frappe a Dynamic Link. **The prior art is a warning rather than a model**: dependent picklists are the one feature Salesforce could not compose with global value sets, which is the exact shape `FJS-D120` just adopted — so taking their mechanism wholesale imports the incompatibility. Two halves and they may want different answers. The DATA half is a `where` that reads a sibling column, which the `@@allow` expression language can nearly say already (`@@scope` is a predicate over the row, and `@derived` reads this row's own columns) — the open part is whether the sibling being read is the value ON the record or the value currently in the FORM, which are different rows on a create. The UI half is a picker that reloads when its controlling field changes and clears a value the new list no longer contains, which touches `resource.options()`'s cache key and `$context.form`. A ruling has to say whether the dependency is declared on the set or on the binding, and what happens to a stored value that a later change to the controlling field makes illegal — refuse the write, clear the field, or leave it and report. | [value-sets.md](IDEAS/value-sets.md) § open questions · [FJS-D120](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d120) |
| <a id="fjs-d119"></a>FJS-D119 | repo | **Should every package run under Bun, or is the node/bun split load-bearing?** Today it is mixed and the mix is not documented as a choice: `ui` runs five node phases, `jetty` ten, `scripts/ci.mjs` is plain node, while `litestone` is `bun test`, `css` is `bun test/run.js`, `toolbelt` is its own harness and `sierra` is vitest. **Measured 2026-08-22: almost none of it is a constraint.** `ui/test/browser/run.mjs`, `jetty/test/phase0.test.js` and `css/test/run.js` use no `Bun.*` and no `bun:` import between them — `css`'s harness even carries `#!/usr/bin/env bun` while `CLAUDE.md`'s runner table lists it as *node driving headless Chrome*, so the two disagree about a harness that would run under either. The one genuine binding is **junction, which is Bun-only and cannot be otherwise** — `Bun.serve` is the transport, `Bun.file` is logging and static, `bun:sqlite` is cache and database. So the question is not *can we*, it is *what does the split buy*. The argument FOR keeping node: `bun run ci` is documented as plain node precisely so CI has no bun-specific behaviour in the harness itself and runs identically on a laptop — a claim worth keeping or worth retiring deliberately, not by drift. The argument AGAINST: two runtimes is two sets of traps, and `CLAUDE.md` already carries a hazard about one of them (`bun test` vs `bun run test` picking up ~35 runner artefacts in mesa) that only exists because both are in play. **What a ruling has to cover**: the runner table in `CLAUDE.md`, `scripts/ci.mjs`'s own entry point, and whether *plain node for CI* is a property being defended or an accident. Raised because a session used node exactly where the package scripts do and it was not obvious from the outside that this was by design. | — |
| <a id="fjs-d118"></a>FJS-D118 | sierra | **Does a page state where *back* goes, and if so is it an attribute?** The sibling of [FJS-D115](#fjs-d115) and deliberately not ruled with it. `KOBAMI/my.maid.tech` writes `data-return` in the same shape as `data-confirm` — an attribute, one delegated listener — to stash the path a create or edit page should return to, so a form reached from three places goes back to the right one. The mechanism looks identical and the OWNER is not: a confirmation is a UI affordance and belongs to the kit, while *where does back go* is navigation, which is `@frontierjs/sierra`'s router — the only thing that knows the history stack, the route table and whether the previous entry is even this app. Putting it on the kit's listener would make `@frontierjs/ui` reach for a router it does not depend on. What a ruling has to settle: whether the state lives in `history.state` (survives a reload, dies on a fresh tab), in the URL as a `?return=` (shareable, and a caller-supplied path is an open redirect the moment it is followed without checking), or in a store (cheapest, gone on reload); and whether an attribute is the right surface at all when the router could answer it from the route table without anybody writing anything | [IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md](IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md) § 3.1 · [FJS-D115](#fjs-d115) · [sierra router](packages/sierra/src/router/index.js) |
| <a id="fjs-d117"></a>FJS-D117 | sierra · cli | **Does a co-located route part carry its folder in its NAME, and does `fli check` grade it?** Sierra already rules the roles — `index.mesa` routes, `_module.mesa` is the layout, `_prefix` and PascalCase are co-located components — and says nothing about what those components are CALLED. maid.tech adds the discipline on top: `_Plural.mesa` is the reusable collection panel imported cross-route, `_plural.Thing.mesa` is the folder-scoped part. What it buys is that an import list is readable without paths and a flat search is unambiguous, instead of twelve files named `List.mesa`. **The same app measures how it fails**, which is the substance of the question: a fork keeps the old prefix, so `routes/contacts/` holds four byte-identical copies of `routes/clients/` files still named `_clients.*`, and `routes/messages/` is a fork of `routes/notes/` still named `_notes.*` — at which point the prefix, the one thing that made the convention worth having, is lying. So the convention is worth little stated and a lot checked: *a `_prefix.Part.mesa` whose prefix does not match its folder* is a cheap rule in `core/checks.js`, which already owns everything derived from the file tree, and it catches the exact failure that was measured. Blocked behind [FJS-D114](#fjs-d114) only for the cross-route half — what a `_Plural.mesa` legitimately is depends on whether a Resource is the model's whole client-side surface (§4.6, *a route file may not be imported by a non-route file*) | [IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md](IDEAS/app-conventions-audit.md) § 3.4, 4.2 · [sierra README § File roles](packages/sierra/README.md#L186) · [core/checks.js](packages/cli/core/checks.js) |
| <a id="fjs-d38"></a>FJS-D38 | mesa · cli | **Does a TUI reuse `.mesa`, or is it a separate authoring model?** `FJS-D37` ruled that if a TUI is built its engine is bought, and deferred the build past alpha; this is the one part it left open, because the two answers are different projects. Measured 2026-08-17: `runtime.js`'s reactive core (lines 1–760) holds **2** DOM references and the remaining 4,200 lines hold **99**, and the compiler emits a template as an HTML *string* parsed by `htmlToFragment()` then walked by `refer(root, path)` — the dom-expressions strategy, unportable for the same reason it is fast. So **reuse costs a second compiler backend emitting a cell tree, not a renderer**, which is more than it looked like before anyone counted; every Mesa target that exists produces markup, SSR and `email-kit`'s `target: 'email'` included, so nothing in the tree says a non-markup target is cheap. Against that, **separate** means Ink or a wrapper over it — available now, and it makes the framework's answer to *how do I build a terminal app* be *use React*, which is a second component model in a repo whose thesis is one mental model. Nothing is blocked on this today; what it protects is that the tone table and the two output owners `FJS-D37` §1–§2 order do not foreclose either answer | [IDEAS/terminal-surface.md](IDEAS/terminal-surface.md) §5, §9 · [runtime.js](packages/mesa/src/runtime.js) · [compiler.js:1211](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js#L1211) · `DECISIONS.md` `FJS-D37` |
| <a id="fjs-d25"></a>FJS-D25 | css | **Ship `Lead` and `Kicker` as terms?** Both are concepts the package already names in `tokens.css` and then provides no way to write. `--text-lg` is commented "**lead paragraph**" and `--text-2xs` is "**the uppercase overline**" — the rungs are tuned for these two jobs and neither has a class, so every consumer hand-rolls one. **Lead**: the standfirst under a page title — larger than body, muted, held to a measure. The guide's `.sg-lead` is 18px + `--ink-soft` + 60ch, and two of those three are now Prose's, so the term may be as small as the size. **Kicker**: the short uppercase label ABOVE a title ("Structure", "Learn") — the thing publishing calls a kicker, distinct from a Badge (a status, inline) and from `.divider-label` (a break between things, not a label on one). Evidence it needs a name: the guide drifted **two** of them with no shared owner — `.sg-eyebrow` at 12px/500/accent and `.sg-wiz-eyebrow` at 11px/600/`--ink-mute` — and `.navlist-label` is a third instance of the same shape already shipped but scoped to nav. Three implementations, one idea, no name is the condition that produces exactly that drift. Open questions: does Kicker carry a tone (the guide's is accent-coloured, the wizard's is muted); is Lead just `.text-xl` + Prose's ink, in which case it is a utility composition and not a term; and does a Kicker + title + Lead group want naming as page-header anatomy, or is it a Section with three children (leaning the latter — naming it adds a term that composes entirely from terms). Not to be confused with `.sg-h1`, which is a display treatment (44px/500/serif vs the h1 rung's 36px/700) and is legitimately the guide's own | [packages/css/guide/guide.js](packages/css/guide/guide.js) § pageHeader() · [packages/css/src/foundation/tokens.css](packages/css/src/foundation/tokens.css) § type scale · [packages/css/vocabulary.js](packages/css/vocabulary.js) |
| <a id="fjs-d24"></a>FJS-D24 | css | **Is there a term for a horizontal group that does not wrap — and does Bar mean "a strip" or "a section"?** Two halves of one gap, found putting the guide's topbar onto shipped terms. **First half:** `.cluster` is the horizontal group, and wrapping is its *definition* (`layout.css` § "cluster — horizontal group that wraps"), not a default. Inside anything with a fixed height that is wrong — a Topbar has `--topbar-height`, so a wrapped second line is drawn OUTSIDE the bar rather than growing it. Measured at 640px: the guide's version badge landed at y=46 in a 56px bar starting at y=8, on top of the page below. Nothing in the vocabulary says *row, together, one line*: Split forces `space-between`, Toolbar promises arrow-key movement the app must implement, and Bar is the right geometry with the wrong scale. Candidate is a `.cluster.nowrap` modifier — one line, `NOT_A_TERM` as a modifier, and it generalises to every fixed-height container. **Second half:** the guide now writes `bar start` for the two groups inside its Topbar, which works and reads oddly — Bar is Region-tier and means *a horizontal strip*, so two of them inside a Topbar says there are three strips where there is one. The missing concept may be a Bar-shaped thing at a smaller scale (a *group* within a strip) rather than a Cluster modifier; if that is the answer it subsumes the first half. Deliberately unruled — the guide's use is marked in place | [packages/css/guide/guide.js](packages/css/guide/guide.js) § topbar() · [packages/css/src/foundation/layout.css](packages/css/src/foundation/layout.css) · [packages/css/src/patterns/bars.css](packages/css/src/patterns/bars.css) |

## Closed

Kept so a stale document cannot reopen them. Newest first.

**Older closures live in [ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md).** This
section holds the recent ones and is trimmed past ~40 rows; the archive holds the
rest, verbatim. An id is never reused, so a `FJS-###` named anywhere in the repo
resolves in one file or the other.

| <a id="fjs-702"></a>FJS-702 | junction — **A token that was present and failed to verify connected as ANONYMOUS, so a revoked session kept its socket and the client's own `4001` branch was dead code** | 2026-09-02 | `_wsOpen` swallowed the throw, so a revoked, expired or forged session held a live socket for its whole life and the plugin's doc comment promised an `auth_failed` message nothing ever sent. It closes **4001 before `open` runs**, so nothing joins a channel and no `connected` frame goes out. **No token at all is still anonymous** — a caller who claimed nothing is a different answer from one whose claim was rejected, and folding the two would have refused every guest basket. Part of the realtime batch: 1781 pass, 34 new, typecheck clean. | <a id="fjs-700"></a>FJS-700 | junction — **Broadcast grading resolved the model from the SERVICE NAME, so a service whose name maps to no model broadcast to nobody, silently** | 2026-09-02 | The accessor is the declared `model:` first and the name only as a fallback, at **both** call sites — `orders2` over `Order`, a modelless service and any Invariant 19 irregular now reach their subscribers. Introduced by `FJS-672`'s own fix, which is why it closes with it. **The tripwire is the half that keeps the class shut**: a channel that grades to nobody warns once per service, because a correct refusal and a misresolved accessor look identical from the send side — the silent failure is the one that would have survived another audit. Finding `realtime-3` · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) · [channels.ts](packages/junction/src/transport/channels.ts) · [FJS-672](#fjs-672) |
| <a id="fjs-699"></a>FJS-699 | caravan — **`dispatch({ unique })` answered a raw `UNIQUE constraint failed` under the one shape the option exists for** | 2026-09-02 | `findByUniqueKey` then insert is check-then-act across processes, and the catch beside it recovered only a primary-key collision, so three processes racing one key each surfaced SQLite's own sentence — naming a physical table — out of an HTTP request. **The partial index is what decides the race, so the loser asks it who won**; a key freed by the winner going terminal in between is one retried insert. Measured after: **900 dispatches of one key from three processes gave 0 throws, 1 row, and all three processes the same id.** Finding `caravan-8` · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) · [db.ts](packages/caravan/src/db.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-697"></a>FJS-697 | caravan — **A timer could take the process down, an idle queue took a write lock three times a second, and `stop()` closed the database under a running handler** | 2026-09-02 | `sweepOwners` threw `database is locked` out of a `setInterval` — an `uncaughtException`, and the heartbeat it missed on the way out is exactly what makes another instance reclaim this one's running rows. Every timer callback catches its own throw now. `_claim` asks a **read-only question before `BEGIN IMMEDIATE`**, because an empty poll of every queue was opening a write transaction with nothing to do: measured at **0 write transactions across ~90 idle polls, against 3/s before**. And `stop()` marks the workers closed BEFORE closing the database — past `drainTimeout` the handler's completion write used to reject with `Database has closed` as an unhandled rejection on the ordinary SIGTERM path — while the hourly cleanup sweep is held and cleared where the heartbeat is, since a `const` local to `start()` went on firing against that closed handle and a restart added a second one. Findings `caravan-6` and `caravan-10` · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) · [db.ts](packages/caravan/src/db.ts) · [worker.ts](packages/caravan/src/worker.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-696"></a>FJS-696 | caravan — **Throughput was exactly `concurrency / pollInterval` — two jobs a second at the defaults — because a worker never re-polled when a job finished** | 2026-09-02 | A queue with capacity free and work queued slept the interval out. `_execute` polls from its own `finally` now and **the interval becomes the backstop for work that ARRIVES** rather than the clock the queue runs on. Measured: 100 empty jobs at a 1 s poll and concurrency 2 took **50 s and now take under one**. | <a id="fjs-695"></a>FJS-695 | caravan — **`PRAGMA synchronous` was never set, so every queued row cost an fsync on the caller's own thread** | 2026-09-02 | Same code path, one pragma: FULL inserted 2000 jobs in **5913 ms** and NORMAL in **136 ms** — 43× dispatch, 24× claim — and a `dispatch()` inside an HTTP handler stops blocking that process's event loop on the disk. NORMAL is the default now, with `synchronous: 'FULL'` for a deployment that would rather pay it. **What it trades is stated where it is set**: WAL plus NORMAL loses committed rows on POWER LOSS and not on a process crash, and a lost claim is a running row whose owner stops heartbeating, which the lease sweep already recovers by design. Finding `caravan-4` · [audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/afba85f4-efe9-4959-92ca-a1b010dd2a16) · [db.ts](packages/caravan/src/db.ts) |

Beside it: `nosniff` on every response and `attachment` for anything outside a small image allow-list that EXCLUDES svg (the type comes off a caller-supplied filename, so an upload named `x.svg` was served `image/svg+xml` inline — stored XSS); a **416 carrying `bytes */N`** for an unsatisfiable range, where `bytes=50-10` used to answer 206 with `content-length: -39`; and an optional `maxBytes` on `save`, refused before the write. **That it is a SECOND owner of file storage stays open as a ruling rather than a refactor** — litestone ships the real `FileStorage` — and the file says so, pointing here. | <a id="fjs-691"></a>FJS-691 | junction — **The devtools console bound every interface, and the only thing between it and a café network was `NODE_ENV === 'production'`** | 2026-09-02 | `Bun.serve({ port })` with no `hostname`, guarded by one exact string that is unset in dev — and unset is the common case. **Three refusals that fail apart**: `hostname` defaults to `127.0.0.1`; binding anywhere else REQUIRES `auth` regardless of `NODE_ENV`, refusing to bind and naming why; and every POST and the WS upgrade check `Sec-Fetch-Site` then `Origin`, because a `text/plain` POST needs no preflight and a page on any origin could otherwise run a job by name. **A request carrying neither header is not a browser and is left alone**, which is what keeps `curl` and the drives working. | <a id="fjs-665"></a>FJS-665 | litestone — **The audit trail lost rows, mis-indexed them and killed writers whenever a second process touched it — and a second process is the shape the docs recommend** | 2026-09-02 | `F8` of the [foundation audit](https://claude.ai/code/artifact/f629d9fc-1ae7-4aaf-b7fe-20a863c41211), the one finding of its eight that had no id. `driver logger` routes through the jsonl driver and its auto-model carries three `@@index` attributes, so every trail in every app is on the indexed path; both apps here declare it. **Three defects, all measured on the shipped code.** `statSync(f).size` then `appendFileSync` is two syscalls, so a second process appending between them made the recorded offset name the OTHER writer's line — **1,999 of 8,000, one in four**, with no artificial delay, after which an indexed read answers the wrong record with no error. Three layers already agreed — the write merges, sierra grades a form treating `update` as a patch, and a sierra resource never issues `update` at all. | <a id="fjs-664"></a>FJS-664 | mesa — **A render the caller ABANDONS strands its temp module, in this package's own root, where it fails CI for everyone until somebody deletes it by hand** | 2026-09-02 | `renderComponent` cleans up in a `finally`, which covers every path the process gets to run. Two it does not. Sierra's prerender races a render against a timeout with `Promise.race` ([prerender.js](packages/sierra/src/build/prerender.js) `bounded`), so a component that hangs at import leaves a promise that never settles and a `finally` that never runs — the build reports the route skipped, finishes, and exits with the file still there. And a process that dies mid-render takes the pending cleanup with it. **The default tmpDir is mesa's own package root**, because a temp module carries `import '@frontierjs/mesa/runtime.js'` and has to be written where that resolves — so the leak lands on a tracked package, and `bun run ci`'s `hygiene` phase reports it as an ignored source file. Two sat there three days, red for everyone, produced by a build that had reported nothing. Fixed with a **live set plus a synchronous exit sweep**: `process.on('exit')` covers a normal end, an explicit `process.exit()` and the exit Node takes after an uncaught throw, and SIGINT/SIGTERM sweep then **re-raise** — removing our own listener first, and only when nothing else is listening, since merely HAVING a listener suppresses Node's default and an app with its own shutdown handler would otherwise run it twice. Measured: Ctrl-C still exits 130, an app handler still runs exactly once and its own exit code still wins. Nothing covers SIGKILL, so a second half **sweeps leftovers a killed process left** — once per directory per process, age-gated at an hour, which spares a concurrent render's file (nothing here holds a temp module for an hour and sierra bounds a render at seconds). That is what removed the two. Both halves are pinned in [render-component.test.js](packages/mesa/test/render-component.test.js) and both fail with the fix reverted; the abandonment case runs in a CHILD PROCESS, because what it asserts is an `exit` handler and no process can observe its own. Proven end to end by `example`'s `build:site` — 18 pages prerendered, no strays anywhere in the tree |

| <a id="fjs-661"></a>FJS-661 | litestone — **A field `@allow('write', …)` was bypassed entirely by the atomic operators, so a caller who could not write a column could still `increment` it** | 2026-09-02 | Found while reading the write path for [FJS-658](#fjs-658), and **measured before it was fixed**: on a model whose `views Int @allow('write', auth().role == 'admin')` correctly refuses `{ views: 999 }` from an ordinary caller, `{ views: { increment: 5 } }` from the same caller moved it 10 → 15, and `{ tags: { push: 'hacked' } }` appended to a column with the identical grant. [`FJS-D129`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d129) says a field write predicate is the WHEN of `SET col = CASE WHEN … THEN ? ELSE col END` — evaluated against the STORED row, which is what stops a caller writing a column on somebody else's row by stating `ownerId: me` in the same payload. `setFragment` is the one owner of that wrapper and **an operator never reached it**: `extractWriteOps` emitted a finished assignment (`"views" = "views" + ?`) which `update` and `updateMany` spliced into the SET clause whole. So the grid held for a value and not for an operator, on both paths. **The shape is why no test could see it** — `write-operators.test.ts` builds its client with `asSystem()`, which drops every field policy by design, and `field-predicate.test.ts` writes values. Each file is right about its own subject and the crossing is in neither. Fixed by making an op carry `{ col, expr, params }` instead of finished SQL and adding `setFragmentExpr`, which is the same predicate with an expression in the THEN instead of a parameter; `setFragment` is now one line of it, so there is one wrapper and not two. **Every refusal is paired with the same call by an admin** and with an unpoliced column in the same payload, because a fix that stopped applying operators altogether would pass every assertion made from the refused side ([FJS-351](#fjs-351)). 5 new tests, all five red against the previous code; litestone 3930 pass · [client.js `setFragmentExpr`](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [write-operators.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/write-operators.test.ts) · [FJS-D129](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d129) |
| <a id="fjs-658"></a>FJS-658 | litestone · junction — **A write key naming a real `Json` column with a path — `{ "settings.commute": {…} }` — was dropped in silence at every layer, so the request was a 200 that changed nothing** | 2026-09-02 | **Measured**: a column holding `{"commute":{"source":"x"},"theme":"dark"}` came back byte-identical with no throw, no warning and no `errors` array — typed or untyped alike, so a `Json @type(Settings)` column whose type declares `commute` was stripped the same way. Three strips, each right about the case it was written for: sierra leaves a key with no field rule alone (a `@transient` is legitimate), junction's `createSchema` builds its output from the declared fields so an undeclared key is never copied, and litestone's `writeData` drops it as mass-assignment protection. **Fixed at two of the three and sierra needed no change** — it already passes the key through. litestone refuses by name, because it is the only layer that knows the column's TYPE and can therefore say which mistake this is: a document column takes the whole document (*read the row, change "commute", write "settings" back*), a scalar one has no inside at all (*"name" is String and has no "first" inside it*). junction CARRIES a key whose head names a declared field rather than refusing it itself, so one boundary owns the sentence — two would drift, and the value never becomes a column because the Data boundary throws on the key before any write. **The strip it is not is the whole of the risk**, so half of both test files is the control: an unknown key, and a dotted key whose head is NOT a field, are both still dropped in silence — a fix that carried every unknown key would have traded a silent no-op for mass assignment. | <a id="fjs-631"></a>FJS-631 | junction · litestone · example — **A broadcast was delivered to every connection in the channel with neither the gate nor the row policies applied, so an anonymous socket received rows the same caller was refused over HTTP** | 2026-09-02 | Ruled [`FJS-D175`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d175). Reproduced before fixing and after: a socket at `ws://localhost:8110/ws` with **no token** received a whole `Order` row — reference, status, subtotal, tax, total, `customerId`, `trackingCode` — one publish after an admin `PATCH`, while the same caller got **401** on `GET /api/orders`. **Prior art picked the design and then changed the implementation.** Rails, Phoenix and Laravel authorize at JOIN with the audience in the channel name; Supabase and Feathers grade at PUBLISH. Join-time was rejected because the publisher must then decide per row which channel it goes to, re-deriving in app code what `@@allow` states declaratively. Supabase supplies the ceiling — 100 subscribers is 100 checks, switch to plain broadcast past ~3,000 — and **Phoenix names the term that actually multiplies**: the ENCODING, not the verdict ("encoded N times instead of a single shared encoding"). Measured here exactly so — `JSON.stringify` 288 ns against 684 ns for a scoped context — **so the unit is Hasura's COHORT, keyed on the principal's object identity**: 100 connections cost **14.9 µs** where the model needs no grading, **49.8 µs** as one cohort and **445.6 µs** as 100 distinct principals, a 9× collapse. **The rule stays at the Data boundary** — `$readAs(accessor, row, principal)`, litestone's fifth `$`-sibling, taking its subject as an argument for `$capabilitiesFor`'s reason: gate, then row policy, then the recipient's own field policies. Junction owns the fan-out and the cohorts and nothing else. Shaping in JS is safe because every protection STRIPS for a non-system reader and a recipient is never system, so no decrypt branch runs; what it gives up is stated (a `@from`/`@computed` is the writer's, since the row is not re-read). `$readGrading` skips a model that can only say yes — a catalogue, the busiest channel an app has. **The defect this nearly shipped with is the one the drive caught**: a `SessionContext` puts the id at `userId` and `auth()` reads `.id`, so the first working version refused the anonymous socket correctly AND delivered the row the recipient may not read while withholding the one they own — `userId == auth().id` is false against `undefined` and TRUE for a guest order whose `userId` is null. `toDataPrincipal` is the owner. | <a id="fjs-656"></a>FJS-656 | conduit — **A caller could join or displace a target's credential by spelling the header differently, and the test that guarded it used the one spelling that worked** | 2026-09-02 | The call site read *"auth headers last: auth always wins — reversing this lets any path where user data reaches `req.headers` substitute or strip the target's credential"*, and an object spread cannot deliver it: keys are case-SENSITIVE, header names are not, so `authorization` from a caller and `Authorization` from `buildAuthHeaders` were two keys and `fetch` joined them. **Measured across every auth type**: `authorization: "Bearer FORGED, Bearer REAL-SECRET"`, `x-api-key: "FORGED, REAL-SECRET"`, `x-fjs-signature: "deadbeef, sha256=…"` with the forged value FIRST, and `content-type: "application/json, text/plain"`, which re-opened [FJS-556](#fjs-556)'s hole by the other door. A strict far side refuses the request (a caller-triggered denial of auth); a lenient one reads first-value-wins, which is the substitution the comment says is prevented. **Not a coverage hole** — `http.ts` was at 100% lines and the three assertions guarding this all used `'Authorization'`. Fixed with `mergeHeaders` in `base.ts`, one owner, lowercasing every key so a later source really does replace an earlier one; the new test is a row per spelling · [base.ts](packages/conduit/src/transports/base.ts) · [http.ts](packages/conduit/src/transports/http.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-652"></a>FJS-652 | conduit — **A target could not declare a constant header, so a header the counterparty requires on every call was spread by hand at every call site** | 2026-09-02 | `TargetDescriptor` grew `encoding` because *how this one takes a body* is a fact about who is on the other end; a pinned API version and a `User-Agent` are the same kind of fact and had nowhere to live. `stripe.ts` worked around it with a `VERSION_HEADER` constant spread into each `send()` — tidy, and one omission from running against whatever version the account defaults to.
