| <a id="fjs-651"></a>FJS-651 | conduit — **A binary body was silently JSON-encoded into an object of byte indices and sent as `application/json`** | 2026-09-02 | `encodeBody` returned a `string` and knew `json` and `form`; a `Uint8Array` fell through to `JSON.stringify`. **Measured** with a four-byte PNG header: the wire carried `{"0":137,"1":80,"2":78,"3":71}` under `content-type: application/json`, with nothing said. Not exotic — an upload endpoint taking raw bytes with a `Content-Type` and a `Content-Length` is Basecamp's `POST /attachments.json` and most object storage, and `IDEAS/conduit-connectors.md` predicted `conduit-s3` would expose it. | <a id="fjs-650"></a>FJS-650 | conduit — **A 429 was classified as a server error, so `Retry-After` was ignored and being rate limited opened the circuit breaker** | 2026-09-02 | Two defects behind one mapping. The transport turned 429 into `server_error` with the header stuffed into `error.raw` as an unparsed string, while the backoff was a fixed `[500, 1500, 1500]` ladder: **measured** against a server answering `Retry-After: 7`, conduit retried after **396 ms and 1385 ms**. And `server_error` is in `resilience.ts`'s `TARGET_FAULTS`, so five rate-limited answers opened the breaker and every send after failed `circuit_open` — load shed by the one status that means *slow down* rather than *I am broken*. Fixed with `rate_limited` as its own `ConduitErrorKind`, carrying a parsed `retry_after_ms` (seconds AND the HTTP-date spelling, both in the wild), honoured by the retry loop in place of its own backoff and capped by the deadline, and deliberately absent from `TARGET_FAULTS` with a comment saying why. | <a id="fjs-649"></a>FJS-649 | conduit — **A 304 Not Modified was reported as a server error, so a conditional request failed in the only way it can succeed** | 2026-09-02 | `if (!res.ok)` caught everything outside 200–299. **Measured**, sending `If-None-Match` at a server answering 304: `{"kind":"server_error","retryable":false,"message":"HTTP 304"}`. | <a id="fjs-648"></a>FJS-648 | conduit — **A response's headers were read and discarded, so a paginated or cacheable API could not be spoken to at all** | 2026-09-02 | `ResponseMeta` was `{protocol, target, status, duration_ms}` and no transport ever put a header on it. **Measured** against a server answering `ETag`, `Link: <…>; rel="next"` and `X-Total-Count: 42`: `meta: {"protocol":"http","target":"bc","status":200,"duration_ms":8}` — all three gone. | <a id="fjs-643"></a>FJS-643 | litestone — **A plain `Int` past 2^53 corrupted on READ, of a value the database was holding correctly** | 2026-09-02 | `safeIntegers` appeared nowhere in `src/`, so `bun:sqlite` answered a JS `number` on every path: a raw-SQL write of `9007199254740993` stores exactly (`CAST AS TEXT` confirms) and the ORM read back `…992`. Both self-consistent, which is why nothing could see it. Closed by `Int @big` — ruling [`FJS-D174`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d174) — and **the fork the row named was settled by measurement, not argument**. Global `safeIntegers` costs **+68 %** on every read for every app (1884 → 3171 µs / 5,000 rows) and `JSON.stringify` **throws** on a `BigInt`, which is every response, every WS frame and every audit snapshot — so the value crosses as a STRING of digits, the answer node-postgres gives `int8` and what mysql2's `bigNumberStrings` is for. **The column keeps `INTEGER` storage and that is what makes it worth doing**: a text parameter takes the column's affinity, so `ORDER BY` puts `100` before `9007…` where TEXT storage puts it after, a range filter compares numerically, EXPLAIN answers `SEARCH … USING COVERING INDEX (v=?)`, and AUTOINCREMENT continues past 2^53. **Measuring also killed my own first cut**: it emitted `CHECK (typeof(col) = 'integer')` by analogy with `@scale`, and litestone emits `STRICT` on every table, which already refuses all three ways a wide value stops being exact — past int64 (a loose table demotes it to REAL `9.22e+18`), a non-numeric string, a fraction. The distinction is the bound: `@scale`'s is NARROWER than the column's, `@big`'s IS the column's. **And the obvious split was an enumeration** — asking for BigInts at the statement while narrowing in the row read left `count()` answering `0n`, because a statement also serves counts and aggregates that reach a caller through neither `read` nor `readAll`; the statement narrows what it returns. `litestone import` now maps a foreign `BIGINT` onto `Int @big`, moving `bigint` from `changed` to `noted` — keys and foreign keys keep the structural exemption, or a Rails import turns every id in the app into a string. litestone 3894 pass (31 new) · sierra 1141 · [client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [ddl.js](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) · [big-int.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/big-int.test.ts) · [exact-numbers.md](packages/litestone/docs/exact-numbers.md) · [FJS-583](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md) · [FJS-D142](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d142) |
| <a id="fjs-642"></a>FJS-642 | litestone — **The live layer was in-process callbacks, so a second process announced nothing to the first — and the package's own docs recommended running one** | 2026-09-02 | Ruled and built: [`FJS-D173`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d173). Not a gap in a mechanism — SQLite has no central server, so `sqlite3_update_hook`, the pre-update hook and the sessions API are in-process by construction, which is why Rails 8 ships Solid Cable (a messages table polled at 100 ms) as its Redis-free default. `database main { announce crossProcess }` records each announced write and every other process on the machine hands it to its own `$tapEvents` subscribers, on the SAME seam — so junction needed no change and cannot tell a foreign event from a local one. **Declared, because it costs +14 µs on a 25 µs single-row insert and nothing on a bulk one**; the row carries the ID and never the row, or the plaintext of every `@encrypted` column would sit in a table beside the ciphertext. Measured, not assumed: `fs.watch` woke on 5/5 foreign commits at a median **0.7 ms** against 50 ms for a 100 ms poll, `data_version` is 2.5 µs prepared, moves not at all for a connection's own commits, is a MARKER rather than a counter (50 foreign transactions move it by 1) and is FROZEN inside a read transaction. **The flake is what found the real bug**: draining advanced the cursor to `MAX(id)` to skip its own rows, which also skipped a foreign row committed between the two queries — 6 failures in 8, and exactly the silent staleness the layer exists to remove, so the cursor now only moves over a row it actually looked at. Two defects fell out of the boot race and are separate rows: [`FJS-654`](#fjs-654), [`FJS-655`](#fjs-655). | <a id="fjs-654"></a>FJS-654 | litestone — **Two replicas booting against one file both applied the same migration, and the loser reported a failure that was not one** | 2026-09-02 | Measured at 5 of 10 simultaneous boots: one replica answers `migrated`, the other `failed — SQLite refused the rebuild: duplicate column name`. The database is correct either way, because the winner did the work; what was wrong is that a second replica booting is the NORMAL shape and it reported an error. `autoMigrate` used a deferred `BEGIN`, which takes no write lock until its first write, so both processes read, both decide the same migration is needed, and the loser applies statements the winner already applied. `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` serialises them and a guard is what makes the loser NOTICE — a lock alone only makes it wait its turn to do the wrong thing — and the hash is now stamped and re-read INSIDE that transaction, or there is a window where the winner has applied and not yet said so. | <a id="fjs-655"></a>FJS-655 | litestone — **`createClient` threw `SQLITE_BUSY_RECOVERY` when two processes opened one file at the same moment, before a line of the app had run** | 2026-09-02 | `PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL` takes a brief exclusive lock and runs WAL recovery, and it ran **six lines before** `applyBusyTimeout` — so the loser of that race had no timeout to wait on and the exception came out of `openSqliteConnections`. Measured at 1 in 10 simultaneous boots, and only visible because [`FJS-654`](#fjs-654) was fixed first and stopped masking it. The busy timeout is applied FIRST now, before any pragma that can contend, on the read connection for the same reason: a reader does not queue behind a writer in WAL, but it does during a checkpoint and on the recovery a crashed writer leaves behind — which is exactly when the timeout has to already be set · [client.js `openSqliteConnections`](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) |

| <a id="fjs-640"></a>FJS-640 | litestone — **The tenant pool closed a client another request was still holding, and the close it paid 8 ms for was not even freeing the connection** | 2026-09-02 | `LRUPool.set` at capacity called `$close()` on the evicted entry — no lease, no refcount, no pin — while `get()` hands a client to a request that holds it across every await it makes. **Measuring it first turned one defect into three, and killed the fix the row proposed.** *(a)* A closed client is **not dead, it is mixed, and deterministically so**: `count()` off a cached statement answered `1` while `findMany()` and `create()` threw, because bun's `close()` is `sqlite3_close_v2` — it defers destruction until the last prepared statement is finalised — and `wrapDb` holds up to 500 and finalised none. So a request failed only if it took a branch it had not taken before, which is why this read as random. *(b)* Therefore **the eager close freed nothing**: measured, a close with one live statement freed **0 fds** and finalising that statement freed 3. The pool was paying a synchronous `wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` — **7.97 ms against 0.008 ms for a pool hit, 991×, on the request path** — for a release that did not happen, and `maxOpen` has never bounded file descriptors in any process that ran one query per tenant. `openCount` reports the map size and is not a resource number. *(c)* At **5 fds per client** (db + wal + shm, read and write), the default `maxOpen: 100` is ~500 fds — already over macOS's 256 default — so the cap was not defending an OS limit either. **Closed on four rulings** ([`FJS-D172`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d172)). Eviction never closes a client that is lent; `maxOpen` is a target for how many to keep warm. A **lease** makes the common case deterministic — junction's `withTenantDb` pins for the length of a request and releases in a `finally`, so an eviction whose every lease has ended closes immediately instead of waiting for a collection that fd pressure does not trigger; a client from a bare `get()` was never leased, so it is dropped and bun's finaliser closes it when the last holder lets go. A **fan-out inserts COLD** into a ring sized to its concurrency, so an admin dashboard no longer evicts the tenants being served. And `$close()` is **definitive**: it finalises the statement cache — which is what makes it release anything — and every path afterwards throws `ClientClosedError` naming the file. **Prior art moved one decision and confirmed the rest.** `sqlite3_close_v2` is this exact design one layer down, and POSIX `unlink` is why deferring a tenant `delete` is safe; Postgres's seqscan ring buffer and MySQL's midpoint insertion are the scan resistance. Where it disagreed was GC: HikariCP, Go's `database/sql` and pgbouncer all evict idle entries only and retire a busy one **when it is returned**, and the `FinalizationRegistry` proposal itself says not to rely on cleanup callbacks — which is what put the lease in junction rather than trusting collection. **The new tests caught two bugs in the fix**: the cold ring defaulted to the fan-out concurrency (8) with no cap, so on any pool smaller than that it could never fill, never recycled, and every cold insert evicted a hot entry — scan resistance silently off for exactly the small pools that need it; and the recycle threshold was `>` where the victim is chosen before the insert, letting the ring grow a slot per insert. Same churn, leased against unleased: **140 fds outstanding against 240, and 60 uncollected clients against 110**. The overflow warning fires only when every slot was pinned — the one condition an operator can act on and the only one that cannot be GC lag. litestone 3829 pass (7 new), junction 1692 pass, typecheck clean · [tenant.js](packages/litestone/src/tenant.js) · [client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [litestone.ts](packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts) · [tenancy.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/tenancy.test.ts) · [FJS-D172](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d172) |

| <a id="fjs-638"></a>FJS-638 | litestone — **Single-row writes and every read bypassed the transaction machinery: dirty reads, ghost writes, torn nested writes, a fake rollback, and rolled-back rows broadcast to every open tab** | 2026-09-01 | Five symptoms, one cause: [`FJS-237`](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md) built the FIFO lock and the AsyncLocalStorage ownership and only `$transaction`, `createMany` and `upsertMany` ever acquired them. **All four measured before and after.** *(a)* The read router picked its connection off a GLOBAL `txState.depth`, so while any transaction was open anywhere every read in the process went to the write connection and saw uncommitted rows — it asks `ownsTx()` now, the same question `wrapExclusive` asks to spot a genuine nesting, so the two cannot disagree about who is inside. *(b)* Every write verb now runs its database region through the lock — `create` and `update` through `exclusive` (their nested writes are themselves table calls, so the body must be async), `remove`, `delete`, `restore` and the three bulk verbs through the cheaper sync-body `wrapExclusive`. A bare create arriving during a stranger's transaction used to be enrolled in it, return the row as a success, and be erased by that stranger's rollback. *(c)* A nested `create` was a parent insert plus sequential child inserts with no transaction, so a child violating a `@unique` left the parent committed — and a `@sequence` model kept the counter bump its failed insert had already made. *(d)* The post-update policy check's "rollback" was a compensating UPDATE writing the before-snapshot back: visible to concurrent readers in the window, permanent if the process died between the two, and clobbering anything that landed in between. It is a real rollback now — the throw is the rollback — and the compensating write is gone. *(e)* Announcements are held on the transaction and flushed on commit ([`FJS-D170`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d170)), with a savepoint-accurate mark. **One contract moved and it is ruled rather than absorbed**: serialising writes makes the in-process transition race impossible, so the loser of two concurrent moves now gets the `TransitionViolationError` the sequential case always gave — the old retryable answer was an artefact of both callers evaluating before either committed, and *somebody moved this under me* belongs to a declared precondition, which is `@version` ([`FJS-D171`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d171)). **Cost, measured A/B on one machine**: ~60 → ~74 µs/op on the fastest possible write (empty model, no policies, `select: false` — the largest the ratio gets). litestone 3815 pass, junction 1692, sierra `test:safety` 5/5, caravan 188, auth 244 · [client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [policy.js](packages/litestone/src/core/policy.js) · [FJS-237](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md) · [FJS-611](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md) |
| <a id="fjs-637"></a>FJS-637 | litestone — **A table rebuild committed foreign-key orphans, because step 11 of SQLite's own 12-step procedure was the one that was missing** | 2026-09-01 | `runInTransaction` wraps every migration in `PRAGMA foreign_keys = OFF` and never ran `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` before `COMMIT`. Measured: a plain `postId Int` holding the value 999 with no matching row, given a `@relation` and migrated, answered `state: migrated` and left the violation in place **permanently** — SQLite enforces a foreign key when the CHILD row is written, so a violation created underneath a row surfaces at the next unrelated update of it, months later, as a refusal about a row somebody was only renaming. The same hole covers the sharper case: a rebuild copies only the columns the two tables share, so a parent row not carried over orphans children in a table the migration never names. Closed by checking inside the transaction, before the commit, over the whole database rather than the migration's own tables — and a failure rolls the migration back and names the table, rowid and parent of the first five violations. The row-count guard that was there is untouched and does not cover this: it counts rows, not references · [migrations.js](packages/litestone/src/core/migrations.js) · [migrate.js](packages/litestone/src/core/migrate.js) |
| <a id="fjs-630"></a>FJS-630 | example — **`verify:users` drove `/admin/` pages that are in nobody's tree, so it could not pass on a clean checkout.** The drive's last section opened `/admin/` and waited for five `a[href^="/admin/"]`, over the panel `fli admin:generate` writes — output that was run once and never committed, so `example/web/src/routes/admin/` was untracked, un-gitignored and absent. | 2026-09-01 | **Neither of the two ways out this row named.** It proposed committing the generated pages (a fixture that goes stale the next time the generator changes) or putting `fli admin:generate` in the preamble (which would make it the only thing here that runs a generator before asserting on its output). What happened instead is that the generated panel was deleted — 95 files — and the drive was pointed at the app's OWN roster screen, `web/src/routes/users/`, which is hand-written, tracked, and the thing a reader is meant to copy. The generator keeps its own proof, which is running it against `example` and `fli check`ing the output; a drive is the wrong place to hold it, because a green run then says the generator worked and not that the app works. Measured: `verify:users` is **29/29**, and the drives table's *nothing — starts and stops both servers itself* is true again. · [verify-users.mjs](example/web/test/verify-users.mjs) · [PROJECT_STATE.md](example/PROJECT_STATE.md) · [CLAUDE.md § drives](CLAUDE.md) |
| <a id="fjs-627"></a>FJS-627 | litestone — **A cross-row invariant — *the lines sum to `subtotal`* — has no declarable spelling, and `@seals` turned out not to supply the missing half** | 2026-09-01 | **Ruled not built** ([`FJS-D168`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d168)), measured against the whole demand — three invariants, all in `example`; `basecamp` declares none. The candidate shape (`@@check` over an aggregate of a `@sealed` relation, evaluated in the sealing transition) reaches **one of the three**: `JournalEntry` has no status column at all and `Payslip`'s state is on its parent `PayRun`, so neither has a seal to hang a check on — and both are the ones that would gain most, since each is written only through `asSystem()`, the writer a JS assertion misses and a table constraint catches. **The deeper finding is that it is not a *moment* problem but a LAST-WRITE one.** A declared check reads the child table and can only fire once rows exist, where all three hand-written ones read the caller's own array and refuse before the first INSERT; run against the real write order it fires on statement one — `payslip.create({ net: 45000 })` with zero lines under it. `postJournal` survives only because it happens to batch its lines into one `createMany`; a legal loop of `journalLine.create` refuses on line 1 of 4, and a constraint that turns on whether the caller batched is not a constraint. The invoice escapes only because `issue` says *don't look yet*, and journal and payslip can have no equivalent: the child carries the parent's id, so the last statement is always a child insert and only the caller knows which one is last — which is the thing a declaration was meant to remove. `@from` is the neighbouring spelling and is deliberately wrong here, because it re-derives and a document stores. Reopen on a fourth demand with a declarable moment, or a second writer of one of these three. | <a id="fjs-626"></a>FJS-626 | litestone · repo — **`db/access.snapshot.md`, the artefact whose whole job is *who may do what*, was silent about the identity model** | 2026-09-01 | `litestone access --schema schema.lite` reads that FILE, and `example` assembled its seed in memory — `appSchema + authSchemaFragments('main')` — so `User` was a real model with a real `@@gate`, two row policies and three field policies, and **the snapshot did not mention it**: `grep -c User` answered 0 against a 32-model file. Same for `db/jsonschema.snapshot.md` and `db/release.snapshot.md`, which made it sharper than a missing line — `release:check` graded a deploy it could not read, so raising a gate on the identity table was a CONTRACT no comparison could see. **Closed by taking the second of the two shapes this row proposed**: `db/schema.lite` now declares `model User` on disk and imports the rest by PACKAGE SPECIFIER — `import "@frontierjs/auth/schema.lite"`, `import "@frontierjs/junction/outbox.lite"`, `import "./user.lite"` — so there is one schema, every tool reads it, and the in-memory assembly is gone rather than worked around. The snapshot now carries `User`'s gate, all three `@@allow` rules (`read`, `create`, `update`) and all three field policies. It also removes the class rather than the instance: an app has no reason left to append a fragment at boot, so nothing can drift this way again. [FJS-625](#fjs-625) stays closed on its own terms — `core/app-schema.js` is still what `fli check` and `fli admin:generate` read, and still correct for an app that has not moved |
| <a id="fjs-624"></a>FJS-624 | example — **The app had no user management at all, so the gate ladder was demonstrated by every screen and administered by none** | 2026-08-31 | Twenty-six drives, 904 assertions, five surfaces — and no `users` service, no `User` resource, no roster screen. The only thing in the whole app that changed who anybody was were two hardcoded palette entries, *Sign in as sam* and *Sign in as alex*. `db/user.lite` had declared `isStaff Boolean @allow('write', auth().isAdmin)` since tenancy landed and **nothing in any surface had ever written it**: the column that separates a shopper from a member of staff was set by the seed and by no person, ever. Closed with `users.service.ts` (`methods: ['find', 'get', 'patch']` — create and delete are declined by the SERVICE and not by the ladder, which permits both: a `User` row with no `Credential` is an address nobody can sign in as, and a delete leaves `Session` rows pointing at nothing, since auth keeps its machinery at `@@gate("8")` with no relation into the app's schema), `resources/User.mesa`, `routes/users/`, and one line in `db/user.lite` that is the substance — a read policy admitting your own row OR a member of staff. auth ships `User` with a read gate of USER(4) and NO read policy, which is right for an app whose only people are its staff; this shop registers shoppers on a public storefront who grade USER(4) exactly as staff do, **so the shipped default handed every shopper the entire roster**. Not a defect in auth — it is the case a gate cannot express, and it is now the app's own answer. Adding somebody came later and is the sharper half: authority is `@@allow('create', auth().isAdmin)` rather than a level check in the service, because `@@gate` is single-valued and an `extend model` may not restate it — and create is the one operation where a policy THROWS rather than filtering, so a shopper is refused by name where a read would have been an empty list. Finishing it found two defects that were each silent. **`onPasswordResetRequested` had never been wired** and the app's own comment said so, so `requestPasswordReset` minted a token and mailed nobody — invisible by construction, because the route answers 200 either way on purpose so as not to reveal whether an address is registered, making *reset works* and *reset mints a row nobody will ever see* the same response. And **`ctx.result` inside a hook is the ENVELOPE** (`{ kind, object, data }`), so reading `.email` off it was `undefined` with no error anywhere: the hook ran, the account was made, and the invitation never went out. `resultData()` is exported for exactly this. Proven by `verify:users`, 29 assertions. Removing somebody is [FJS-629](#fjs-629) |
| <a id="fjs-625"></a>FJS-625 | cli — **Every tool that reads `db/schema.lite` off disk reads a different schema from the one the app runs, and both readers were wrong about the models that matter most** | 2026-08-31 | An app's seed is `schema.lite` PLUS fragments a package ships and the app appends in memory (`authSchemaFragments()`, `outboxSchemaFragment()`) PLUS `extend model` in its own files. A file scan sees the first only, so the models it cannot see are exactly `User`, `Session` and `Credential`. Two readers, failing in opposite directions. **`fli check`'s `service-model` failed CLOSED**: a correct `users.service.ts` stating `model: 'User'` was reported as naming a model that does not exist — a rule firing on a correct app, which is a rule people baseline. **`fli admin:generate` failed OPEN and had since it was written**: it generated a complete admin panel with no Users screen in it, silently, which is the one screen an admin panel is most about. Both read `core/app-schema.js` now, one owner, for [FJS-372](#fjs-372)'s reason. The cost is stated rather than hidden: a service naming a package model the app never assembled resolves instead of being reported, which is accepted because the alternative is a false error on the documented in-memory install. **Three more defects fell out of RUNNING the generator rather than reading it**, which is the whole of what `FJS-372` says is missing. (1) A service name is a FILENAME and was being derived — `servicePlural('ShippingMethod')` is `shippingMethods` and the app serves `shipping-methods`, so five generated screens called a URL the app does not answer, each a 404 with the page rendering normally around it. (2) A model with no service was generated anyway and then warned about, which is the wrong half; and filtering it out of the TARGETS alone was not enough, because the layout and dashboard are built from the full list on purpose, so the nav went on linking two sections never generated. (3) The export name took the service string, and `export const shipping-methods = createResource(…)` does not parse — a defect this change introduced and the first end-to-end run caught in ninety seconds. **Also settled: `@@gate(8+)` models are skipped by name** — `asSystem()` itself grades 8, so a page over `Credential` would ask forever and would be an editor for password hashes if it did not; the gate is what tells machinery from domain and it is in the schema. Leaves [FJS-626](#fjs-626) for the half that cannot take this fix |
| <a id="fjs-623"></a>FJS-623 | cli — **A test waited for a file to EXIST when what it needed was the pid inside it, and left the empty file behind for the next run** | 2026-08-31 | `children.test.js`'s process-group test spawns a shell that writes its pid with `echo $$ > f` and then waits for it. `echo` **creates the file before it writes to it**, so `existsSync` is not the signal the test wants: an empty read parses as `0`, `expect(grandchild).toBeGreaterThan(0)` fails, and the sentence is about a pid rather than about a shell that had not got there yet. Under load the window is wide enough to hit — it fired 2/2 on the full suite and passed 3/3 in isolation, which is the signature that reads as *someone else's change broke it*. **The second half is what made it persist**: the path is keyed on the RUNNER's pid and only the success path removes it, so a run that failed here left the file, and `/tmp` held nine of them including a **0-byte one** — the failure state itself, sitting on disk waiting for a run whose pid recycled onto the same number to read it instantly. Fixed by waiting for a parseable pid rather than for the path, and clearing it before the spawn as well as after. Found because adding an unrelated test file moved the suite's timing enough to expose it, and the control ruled the obvious explanation out: the same reorder using only existing files stayed green, so it is the window and not the position · [children.test.js](packages/cli/tests/children.test.js) |
| <a id="fjs-622"></a>FJS-622 | cli — **The generated nginx vhost declared no `access_log`, so every app on a machine wrote into one undifferentiated file** | 2026-08-31 | A server block with no `access_log` falls back to nginx's machine-wide default, which is a working config, a green `nginx -t` and a site that serves. The only symptom is that the file cannot be read back: nothing in a line says which app took the request, so on a box with two apps — the normal case for a fleet machine — neither one's traffic is attributable, and an incident is investigated against a file that mixes them. It also made every form of traffic analysis impossible rather than merely unbuilt, which is how it was found: designing one (`IDEAS/traffic-analysis.md`) against CapRover's GoAccess integration, which needs exactly the per-app log this was not writing. Fixed with a path carrying `${appId}`, and **the directory is load-bearing** — `/var/log/nginx/*.log` is the glob the distro's own `logrotate.d/nginx` already rotates, so naming the files inside it and ending them `.log` bounds them by a rule that is already on the machine; writing them anywhere else would have created [FJS-616](#fjs-616)'s defect one layer up, an unbounded log nobody is watching, while appearing to fix something. `combined` is stated rather than left to default because it is the format an analyser reads unasked, and a custom `log_format` cannot be declared here at all — it is http-level only and this step writes a server block. Four tests read the generated config as text, since it is built inside a markdown step's JS block and there is nothing to import; the two that matter assert the app id appears in every log path and that both paths sit under the rotated glob · [05-nginx.md](packages/cli/commands/deploy/_steps-setup/05-nginx.md) · [nginx-config.test.js](packages/cli/tests/nginx-config.test.js) · [traffic-analysis.md](IDEAS/traffic-analysis.md) |
| <a id="fjs-619"></a>FJS-619 | litestone — **a field `@allow('read')` that reads only the caller was evaluated once per field per ROW through the expression interpreter** | 2026-08-31 | The 2026-07-18 audit's **M7**, the worst per-row cost on its list, and the one place a read policy is answered in JS rather than in SQL — `FJS-D129` compiles a field read predicate into the WHERE so a caller cannot FILTER by a column they may not read, but which columns come back on a returned row is still a per-row strip. `@allow('read', auth().isAdmin)` has ONE answer for a whole result set and was asked once per field per row. **The classifier is an ALLOW-LIST and that is the whole of its safety**: `referencesRow` names the node kinds that read only the caller — `literal`, `auth`, `and`, `or`, `not`, `compare`, `ternary`, `list` — and anything else, including a kind the language grows later, is assumed to read the row and evaluated per row. A deny-list would silently stop stripping a column the day a kind was added. **`now()` is deliberately refused** though it reads no row: hoisting a clock-dependent predicate across a page answers one instant for rows read at another. Two caches with different lifetimes — row-freeness is a property of the AST and is global, the ANSWER depends on the caller and hangs on the context, keyed by `ctx.auth` so a context that ever reassigned it invalidates rather than going stale. Measured on 5,000 rows × 4 protected columns, three interleaved runs: floor **0.61–0.67 µs/row**, hoisted **0.77–0.84**, per-row **1.11–1.21** — about **30% off the read** and ~70% of the gap to a model with no field policy, added to `bench/audit-bench.mjs` as case 12. 12 tests, and almost all of them are about the hoist NOT applying: a wrong one is silent in the worst way, since every row would take the FIRST row's answer and a list looks right whenever its rows happen to agree — so every case reads a page whose rows DISAGREE, including a mixed expression with one caller-only branch beside a row-reading one, and two `@allow` on one field of which only one reads the row. Found surveying which litestone features are expensive · [policy.js `referencesRow`](packages/litestone/src/core/policy.js) · [client.js `hoistedFieldRead`](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [field-read-hoist.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/field-read-hoist.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-618"></a>FJS-618 | litestone — **A jsonl model that gains an indexed field silently stops being written, for ever, after one warning** | 2026-08-31 | A logger/jsonl database keeps a companion `<file>.index.db` with one column per indexed field, and the shape check on open compared the TYPES of columns present in both tables. A column that is MISSING is the other way the shape can be wrong, and it is the one an UPGRADE produces: `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` does nothing to the existing table, every insert then fails `has no column named …`, and `@@log(audit)` is fire-and-forget so the failure is swallowed — one `console.warn`, then every audit write dropped for the life of the deployment. **Found by causing it**: [FJS-617](#fjs-617)'s work added `@@index([correlationId])` to the logger auto-model, which is exactly that upgrade for every app that already has a trail, and the reproduction is a hand-built index table one column short. The index is a CACHE — every column is re-derivable from the .jsonl — so the fix is the one already there for a type change: drop it and let it refill. Three tests, and the one that has to stay is the negative control: an index whose shape ALREADY agrees must not be rebuilt, because a rebuild throws away every offset and doing it on each open makes an append-only log rescan itself for ever · [jsonl.js](packages/litestone/src/drivers/jsonl.js) · [test/jsonl-index-drift.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/jsonl-index-drift.test.ts) · [IDEAS/logbook.md](IDEAS/logbook.md) |

Closed three ways: basecamp's path is an `env()` bound to `/data/audit/` in a new `.env.example` (its `envCheck: true` now refuses a deploy that has not bound it), and `fli check` grew **`log-db-unbound`** — a warning, with two findings that differ in how sure they are, since a literal path is certain from the tree and an undeclared `env()` is strong evidence about a server this cannot see · [checks.js](packages/cli/core/checks.js) · [db-preflight.js](packages/cli/core/db-preflight.js) · [basecamp/db/schema.lite](packages/basecamp/db/schema.lite) |
| <a id="fjs-616"></a>FJS-616 | cli · outpost — **Every container this framework starts had an uncapped log, in all four places one is started** | 2026-08-31 | Docker's default `json-file` driver sets no size limit, so a container that logs on each request writes until the disk does — and the symptom is not a lost log line, it is a machine whose database stops writing before anybody looks at `/var/lib/docker`. None of the four sites set one: the deploy swap, the rollback, `deploy:local`, and the Outpost, which starts every app on a fleet machine and would take every other app on it down with the disk. Four copies of one argv and no owner, so the fix is `core/docker-logging.js` and the three CLI sites now call it; outpost restates the three constants with a note, because a dependency edge from a machine agent to the CLI is worse than a copy. **The driver is deliberately not forced**: `--log-opt max-size` is `json-file`'s spelling and journald refuses it outright, so emitting it unasked breaks exactly the machine that was configured correctly — `logs: false` says *the daemon already answers this*, and naming a driver hands the decision over · [docker-logging.js](packages/cli/core/docker-logging.js) · [deploy/_module.md](packages/cli/commands/deploy/_module.md) · [outpost/src/docker.js](packages/outpost/src/docker.js) |
| <a id="fjs-615"></a>FJS-615 | litestone — **`nested.length` on an object is `undefined`, so three guards meant to detect a nested write all read *there is none* — and a `create` with `select: false` wrote the parent and dropped every child in silence** | 2026-08-31 | `extractNestedWrites` returns `{ scalar, nested }` where `nested` is an OBJECT, and three separate guards asked it for `.length`. That is not a typo three times: `.length` on an object is silently falsy rather than an error, which is exactly what lets one wrong spelling be written three times and never fail. **Three consequences and only one was loud.** `create({ select: false, data: { …, lines: { create: [...] } } })` took the skip-RETURNING branch, so there was no parent id to attach children to — the parent was written, every child dropped, `null` returned, nothing said. `update` had the same guard and the same hole, but only where the payload ALSO carries a scalar column: a nested-only payload sets no columns, misses that branch entirely and worked by accident, which is why the create case went unnoticed. And the `upsert` fast path's two `break fastPath` guards never fired, so a legitimate nested write was handed to the column validator and refused as `lines: must be an array` — a confusing refusal rather than data loss. Fixed at the shape rather than the three call sites: `extractNestedWrites` now returns a `hasNested` BOOLEAN and `nested.length` appears nowhere. `select: false` keeps its contract — RETURNING is still used when children need the parent's id, because that is the method's need and not a change to what the caller asked for, and `null` is still what comes back; the ANNOUNCEMENT keeps the row, since `null` there means *the RETURNING was skipped*, which is now a different fact. Eight tests in `test/nested-write-guards.test.ts`, five of them measurements, three of them controls — the guards exist to keep a real saving on the ordinary call, so a fix that simply stopped taking the fast path would pass every other assertion and be worth nothing. Found reading `create`'s fast path while pricing `FJS-D162`'s frozen-aggregate option, which rests on nested create being sound · [client.js `extractNestedWrites`](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [nested-write-guards.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/nested-write-guards.test.ts) · [relations.md](packages/litestone/docs/relations.md) |
| <a id="fjs-611"></a>FJS-611 | litestone — **`transition(id, name)` and an update carrying the column reached the boundary as one question, so a move onto the state a row already held was a silent success — and skipped the gate, the capability and `@system` with it** | 2026-08-31 | **The row's diagnosis was wrong and what was there is worse.** `transition()` is NOT read-then-write: the UPDATE has always carried `AND status = <from>`, so four concurrent movers give one winner and three `TransitionConflictError`s, measured. What was missing is the difference between two callers of one code path — an update carrying the value the row ALREADY holds is legitimate, because a form round-trips the whole row, while the same state under a NAMED move means the move did not happen here. `transition()` desugars into `update()`, so all that arrived was a column and a value, and the early return on `currentValue === newValue` answered the first question to both. **Half of it is an access defect**: that return fired before the gate, the capability and `@system`, so a `@gate(5)` move and a `@system` move were each makeable by any caller who could update the model as long as the row was already at the target — both measured succeeding. **`$transaction` was the documented mitigation and made it worse**, because serialising the callers means each re-reads AFTER the winner committed, which is exactly the state the early return called a no-op: four transactions, four successes. `transition()` now passes `_move`, the one thing it knows and `update()` cannot derive; a named move resolves by NAME, and the row already at `to` is a conflict, anywhere else a violation, a legal `from` the compare-and-swap as before. **`retryable` separates two opposite races under one class** — losing to a DIFFERENT move stays `true` (re-read and decide), the move already having been made is `false`, since re-applying can never succeed and `isStaleWrite()` would loop a worker against a settled row. The error carries `expected`, `actual` and `move` and DECLARES them as `data`, because instance properties do not cross junction's error boundary and sierra's `toConflict()` reads exactly those. **A named move is graded on its CALLER before its row**, the order every other layer here reads in — telling somebody who could never make the move that the row is in the wrong state is a refusal confirming state — and that applies to a named move alone, since an ordinary update matching no `(from, to)` pair has identified no move: **naming the move is what makes its gate askable**. The three grading rules moved into one `gradeMove` shared by both paths. Ten tests in `test/transition-race.test.ts`, five of them measurements of the code as it stood, with the already-passing four-concurrent case kept as the control so a green suite cannot claim credit for the CAS. | <a id="fjs-613"></a>FJS-613 | litestone — **A transition declared `@system` was byte-identical to one anybody may ask for in all three committed artefacts, so the widest narrowing a state machine can make produced no diff at all** | 2026-08-31 | **It was three files, not one.** `access.snapshot.md` dropped `@system` at DERIVE — `deriveAccess` carried the `@gate(n)` alone — `release.snapshot.md` inherited that hole because its surface is built from the same derivation, and `jsonschema.snapshot.md` had the fact in the document (`x-transitions` has always emitted `system`) and dropped it at RENDER. So a move going from *any caller at the update level, subject to the row policies* to *no caller, ever, the application included* was invisible in every file whose whole job is to make that a review artefact. **The fix is two columns and not one**, because the two facts COMPOSE: `@gate` says who may ASK and `@system` says the APPLICATION makes it, so `@system @gate(5)` is a move the engine decides on behalf of a caller senior enough to ask for it — which basecamp declares four times on `Server`, and which folding one into the other would lose exactly where it is hardest to see. The access snapshot grows a **Made by** column (`caller` / `application`) beside the level, the release surface carries `@system` on its transition line, and the jsonschema snapshot renders it beside the gate. **The deploy grade follows the same reading and is on its own axis**: gaining it is a CONTRACT and `narrows`, losing it an EXPAND and `widens`, reported separately from a gate change on the same move so that raising a gate while making a move `@system` does not read as an ordinary level bump. Regenerating the six committed snapshots surfaced **twelve `@system` moves** that had been invisible — seven in `example` (`Subscription`'s four, `Invoice.settle`, `PayRun.calculate` and `pay`) and five in `basecamp` (`Server`'s five, four of them the composing case). Also fixed in the same area: `db.<model>.transitions()` declared no `system` and no `refusedBy`, and `transition()` no `{ system: true }`, though the runtime has answered all three since the feature shipped. Six tests in `test/release.test.ts` — one per artefact, both directions, the composing case and the two-findings case — plus `@system` in `litestone.test.ts`'s shared access fixture so the derive and the render carry it. | <a id="fjs-603"></a>FJS-603 | litestone — **`@@unique` took no predicate, so *one OPEN row per parent* — the constraint effective dating is built on — could not be declared, and the near miss was refused with the opposite answer** | 2026-08-31 | **`@@unique([cols], where: <expr>)`**, and it does not reopen `FJS-204`: that ruling refused a predicate the FRAMEWORK derives onto every unique index, and this is one the AUTHOR declares over a domain column. There is one answer to *is this column unique* per declaration, which is what Invariant 4 asks; the derivation would have produced two answers to ONE declaration. **One word, two node kinds** — a predicate cannot ride a table constraint in any dialect, so the predicate form parses to `partialUnique` and emits a standalone `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX … WHERE`, which is what makes four downstream readers correct with no edit: the table emitter cannot pick one up, a one-to-one relation cannot be satisfied by a constraint holding over only some rows, `advise`'s foreign-key coverage does not count it, and the migrator's `tableUniques` already filtered to `origin` `u`/`pk` so it lands in the index diff by construction. Django makes the same split under the same word. **The line the feature turns on is a removal**: `createIndexes` ANDs `@@softDelete`'s `"deletedAt" IS NULL` into a declared `@@index(where:)` and must NOT into this — on a unique index the predicate IS the constraint, so ANDing it frees the `@unique` slot a soft-deleted row holds and `SoftDeletedUniqueError` can never fire, which is `FJS-204`'s derivation arriving through the back door. Asserted against the emitted TEXT, because nothing else can see it. **The record's grammar argument was half wrong and measuring it is what found the rest**: enforcement on INSERT never consults the planner, so the zero-parameter rule is not a correctness rule here — but SQLite refuses a BOUND PARAMETER in a partial index predicate whichever kind it is (`parameters prohibited in partial index WHERE clauses`, at migration time), and this compiler binds every value. The literals are inlined instead, safe because they are the schema's own; `now()` is refused for a sharper reason than on an index, since SQLite ACCEPTS a clock there and a constraint whose coverage moves under a row that never moved is a duplicate. **The one correctness hole was `release.js`**, which keyed a unique on its sorted column list alone: a narrowed or widened predicate graded as no change at all. It carries the predicate now — gained is EXPAND, lost is CONTRACT, and a move between two non-empty ones is UNKNOWN, because implication between two SQL expressions is not a text comparison. **The importer carries it** where the reading can express it and drops it WHOLE where it cannot; `predicateToLite` grew the value form under a flag asked for on the unique path alone, since carrying one onto an `@@index` would write a `.lite` this parser refuses (`FJS-594`), and a tuple with a nullable member is dropped for the same reason. **The refusal that sent people wrong now names both answers**, with the column list CHANGED in the suggestion. `example` declares all three: `PlanVersion`, `PayWindow` and `PayRate`, and `verify:employment`'s `gap.twoOpenWindowsAreAcceptedByTheSchema` — written to pin this gap — went red on the first run and is now eight `constraint.*` assertions of the opposite, including the two a service could never make: `asSystem()` is refused, and so is a raw INSERT · [IDEAS/partial-unique.md](IDEAS/partial-unique.md) · [parser.js](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js) · [ddl.js `createIndexes`](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) · [release.js](packages/litestone/src/release.js) · [index-predicates.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/index-predicates.test.ts) · [verify-employment.mjs](example/web/test/verify-employment.mjs) · [schema.lite](example/db/schema.lite) |
| <a id="fjs-612"></a>FJS-612 | mesa · ui — **`{...$attributes}` on a component was applied once at mount and never updated, so every dynamic attribute a caller forwarded to a kit component was frozen at its first value** | 2026-08-30 | **Two halves, and each was load-bearing on its own.** `restProps` built a plain object ONCE, at component init, out of the props the caller passed at mount — so `$attributes` was a copy and everything reactive around it (the parent's push effect, the child's `spreadAttributes` effect) was working correctly on a dead value. It returns a **Proxy over a signal** now, so a read inside an effect subscribes; shallow equality on the signal, because the object is rebuilt on every push and reference equality would never hold — without it every forwarding component wakes on a push that changed nothing it holds. **The second half is that the undeclared props had nowhere to arrive**: `pushProps` walks the child's prop registry, and an attribute the child never declared has no signal in it, so it was dropped — the rest object could not have moved even if it had been live. The child registers a sink under a `REST_PROPS` symbol and the push writes it **wholesale**, because a merge can never remove a key and a spread that stops carrying one would leave it on the element for ever. `componentApi` skips non-string registry keys, which keeps the sink out of `bind:this`. **The compiler change follows from writing it wholesale** — the sync effect pushes the WHOLE props object rather than the reactive half, since a declared prop written twice is a no-op against its own signal while an undeclared STATIC attribute was otherwise lost the first time anything else on the element changed. **The answer to the row's open question is mesa, not `@frontierjs/ui`**: no kit component changed. `test/browser/runtime/forward-attributes` is the assertion and every row in it is a PAIR — the forwarded attribute beside the same expression on a plain element — so a fix that froze both would look like a fix that froze neither; it also covers the two things the wholesale rebuild buys and a merge cannot, a static attribute surviving a push and a key LEAVING a spread. The negative control is the one that was already written: `verify:payroll`'s `gap.aForwardedAttributeIsFrozenAtItsFirstValue` was the single failure on the first run after the fix, and is now three `forward.*` assertions of the opposite. 1351 vitest + 107 runtime-browser + 47 vite-browser in mesa, 875 across all 70 components in `@frontierjs/ui`, 1141 + 25 in sierra · [runtime.js `restProps`](packages/mesa/src/runtime.js) · [compiler.js](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js) · [forward-attributes.spec.mjs](packages/mesa/test/browser/runtime/specs/forward-attributes.spec.mjs) · [verify-payroll.mjs](example/web/test/verify-payroll.mjs) |
| <a id="fjs-608"></a>FJS-608 | litestone · junction — **A primary key the CALLER supplies was absent from the create schema, so a create carrying it was answered `orgId is required` — about the field it had just sent** | 2026-08-30 | **The symptom filed with this row was wrong and the measured one is worse.** `generateJsonSchema(…, { mode: 'create' })` excluded every `@id` as *server-assigned*, so a key the server does not generate was not merely un-required but ABSENT — and junction STRIPS what the create schema does not declare rather than refusing it. So the key was removed from the payload in silence and the refusal came from the Data boundary one layer down. Measured against the pre-fix emitter through a real service: `POST /memberships {orgId, userId, role}` → **400 `Validation failed — orgId: orgId is required, userId: userId is required`**, naming the two fields the request carried. Not a rejection for presence; a rejection for the absence of something present. **Two readers asked one question and neither owned it.** `jsonschema.js` excluded every `@id`; `client.js`'s required pre-flight skipped `attrs.some(id) && f.type.name === 'Int'` — the TYPE and not the key — so an `Int` member of a composite `@@id` was treated as a rowid alias, and a create omitting it reached SQLite and came back as a raw `NOT NULL constraint failed` naming a physical table, the error shape every required field exists to avoid. `isServerAssignedId(field, model)` in `core/ids.js` is the one owner now, and the rule is three lines: a declared `@default` is the server's, a LONE `Int @id` is SQLite's rowid alias, and everything else is the caller's — **a composite key is never a rowid alias whatever the column types**, which is the half the type test could not see. **It is not about composite keys**, which is only how it was found: a single `code String @id` — a slug, a stock keeping unit, an external system's identifier — behaved identically. Every `@id` was treated as `Int @id @default(autoincrement())`, the one case where excluding it is right. **Update mode is untouched and deliberately so**: there the key is in the `where`, not the payload, and it was already emitted as an ordinary property. **The blast radius in this repo is two lines**, on the one model here with a caller-supplied key — `basecamp`'s `OutpostNonce`, whose committed `jsonschema.snapshot.md` had been spelling the defect out in its own words: *On create: required — nothing · **not accepted** — `nonce`*. 12 tests in litestone (a grid of 7 key shapes, each RUN rather than read off the helper, plus the property that the schema's `required` and the client's refusal agree for every one) and 5 in junction, which is the layer litestone's own suite cannot reach — there the schema is a document, here it is compiled into a validator standing between an HTTP body and a row. The junction file's negative control is that omitting the key is still a 400 naming it, so the file cannot pass against a schema that stopped validating · [ids.js](packages/litestone/src/core/ids.js) · [jsonschema.js](packages/litestone/src/jsonschema.js) · [client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [caller-supplied-id.test.ts](packages/junction/tests/caller-supplied-id.test.ts) · [jsonschema.md](packages/litestone/docs/jsonschema.md) |
| <a id="fjs-609"></a>FJS-609 | example — **A job dispatched under an id built from a row id can never run again for that row, and the row id is reused** | 2026-08-30 | `renew-subscription` presented its invoice with `dispatch(collectInvoice, …, { id: occurrenceKey('collect', invoiceId) })`. A dispatch id is the jobs table's PRIMARY KEY, so a taken one is a no-op **for all time** — once `collect:56` exists, the invoice with id 56 can never be presented again. *Presented again* is an ordinary thing to want: a soft decline leaves the invoice `issued` and owed, and the only reason nothing had broken is that nothing re-presents one yet. What the line means is *never two presentations of one invoice in flight at once*, which is `unique` — a lock that frees itself at a terminal state. **And the key cannot be built from a row id under either option**: SQLite reuses a rowid once the row is gone, so `collect:56` names two different invoices months apart. Caravan's own docstring says exactly this about `unique`, and it is worse for `id`, which never lets go. Found by `verify:collect`'s end-to-end chain, which fails only after a run whose cleanup deleted invoices — three green runs, then two assertions fail with the renewal visibly having worked. Fixed by moving to `unique`; the reuse half is a hazard every `dispatch` in this repo has to be read against · [renew-subscription.job.ts](example/api/src/jobs/renew-subscription.job.ts) · [types.ts `DispatchOptions`](packages/caravan/src/types.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-153"></a>FJS-153 | basecamp — **The UI covers 35 of the mock's 41 screens** | 2026-08-30 | **41 of 41 now, and the mock is no longer a reference for anything.** § Phase 14 built the six that were left, and the split it had to make first is the whole of what was decided: two of them are reads this app can already answer and four are a statement about a third party that is not wired. **The two real ones share one service** — `infra`, over no model, because both are a projection assembled from several tables and storing either would be a second answer that goes stale. `graph` walks servers, apps, networks, domains and the three join tables into nodes and edges (a browser cannot: `apps.find` answers the environment and the domains, and WHERE an app runs is only on `apps.get`, one request per app); `onboarding` is the six checks as six counts, with no `done` column anywhere — three of them through `asSystem()`, since `Secret` and `Invitation` are `@@gate("5")` and `WorkspaceMember` reads only the caller's own rows, so a developer asking *does this workspace have an SSH key* got a refusal rather than an answer. **The other four render the skeleton and the reason beside it**, and two of those read the reason off the portal's own ping rather than hardcoding it — `/git-activity/` and `/observability/` print `unconfigured` because the adapter is a stub, and will print something else the day it is not. `/dns/` and `/cloud-spend/` are named for the job rather than for the vendor the mock drew: each has a REAL half out of this app's own rows (hostnames with their certificate status; the fleet a bill would be computed from) and a skeleton where the vendor's numbers would go, because there is no Cloudflare and no DigitalOcean in `api/src/providers/index.ts` and inventing an account in the UI is the thing this whole exercise was against. What remains is adapters, which is not a screen problem: four conduit targets and a widened `IGit`/`IObservability`. **The two adapters that had no boundary now have one** — `IEdge` and `ICloudSpend` are declared with stubs, `IGit` grew the three counts `/git-activity/` renders, and the portal reports ten providers in two kinds (`hosted` separates an appliance an operator installs from an account with a token, because *unconfigured* means different work for each). That is what let all four adapter screens stop hardcoding their own status and ask instead. **Proven rather than declared** — `verify:screens` grew 26 checks to 66, and its fixture creates a domain and a network through the app's own API first, because the seed makes neither and two of the graph's four node kinds would otherwise have been asserted against an empty list. It found the last thing in the way: three resources here have no model (`portal`, `hub`, `infra`) and Sierra warned on every boot for each, so the console check could not pass — `createResource(name, { model: null })` is the declaration that turns it off, which keeps the warning meaning *this model name did not resolve* · [SCREENS.md](packages/basecamp/docs/SCREENS.md) · [infra.service.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/services/infra/infra.service.ts) · [verify-screens.mjs](packages/basecamp/web/test/verify-screens.mjs) · [resource.js](packages/sierra/src/junction/resource.js) |
| <a id="fjs-561"></a>FJS-561 | litestone — **A composite primary key was expressible all along — what was missing was the SPELLING, and with it the key's column ORDER** | 2026-08-30 | The row said *not expressible* and *a table whose key IS its pair takes a surrogate id plus `@@unique`*. Both were wrong, measured: two `@id` fields parse clean, emit `PRIMARY KEY ("orgId", "userId")`, and carry `create` · `findUnique` over both columns · `update` · `remove` · `restore` · `include` · `findManyCursor`, with a duplicate refused as `UniqueConflictError: orgId "o1" + userId "o1" is already taken — those values must be unique together`. Litestone's own implicit m2m join table has been exactly that since it was written — a real `PRIMARY KEY (colA, colB)`, no surrogate — which is the second thing the row got backwards. **So the question was never whether to support composite keys but whether to give the model-level word a grammar**, and the answer is yes for a reason the row did not have: `@@id` is the only place the key's column ORDER can be written down. A primary key builds an implicit index and an implicit index is prefix-matched, so `(orgId, userId)` answers `WHERE orgId = ?` and the swap does not; with `@id` on the fields the key order is the FIELD DECLARATION order, which is a different fact about the model. **That is not theoretical and it is the defect this closes**: `litestone introspect` read `PRIMARY KEY ("userId","orgId")` off a real table, emitted `@id` per column, and wrote a schema building the key the other way round — silently, and now visibly, since [FJS-596](#fjs-596) made a reordered key migrate. **Shipped as pure sugar**: the parser marks each named field `@id` and leaves the attribute on the model, read by exactly one caller (`tableConstraints`) for the order — so no reader downstream learned a new shape. Five refusals, each a key that would not identify a row: a field-level `@id` beside it, a second `@@id`, a nullable member (SQLite permits one on a rowid table and there is no `nullsDistinct` reading of a primary key), a relation, and an array or virtual column. **The dead guard the row named is now live** — `TRAIT_FORBIDDEN_MODEL_ATTRS` has held `'id'` since traits were written and could never fire, because the parser refused the word first; `docs/traits.md` and `PROJECT_STATE.md` become accurate rather than being deleted. **The conversion cost the row priced is paid**: `litestone import` carries the key instead of inventing a surrogate, in all three readers that can see one, and the corpus regenerated through the shipped readers turns the row's own measurement — 7 occurrences, Cal.com 4, Trigger.dev 2, Documenso 1 — into 7 `@@id` declarations and **0 `composite-primary-key` gaps**. The kind stays in the tier table for the one case that genuinely cannot be carried: a source key with a NULLABLE member, where the surrogate is the honest reading and is still graded. 18 tests in `composite-id.test.ts`, the sharpest being a FIXED POINT rather than a string — introspect the table, build from the output, introspect again, same text — plus the negative control that the schema it wrote migrates nothing against the database it came from · [parser.js](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js) · [ddl.js](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) · [introspect.js](packages/litestone/src/tools/introspect.js) · [composite-id.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/composite-id.test.ts) · [schema.md](packages/litestone/docs/schema.md) |
| <a id="fjs-596"></a>FJS-596 | litestone — **Uniqueness a table declares itself was in no diff at all — so ADDING a `@@unique` migrated nothing, and the constraint the schema declared was never in the database** | 2026-08-30 | Filed as the reorder alone and the reorder was the small half. `@unique` on a column and `@@unique([a, b])` are both emitted INSIDE `CREATE TABLE`; SQLite builds an implicit index for each, and an implicit index has NULL `sql` in `sqlite_master`, which is exactly what the index read filters on. So none of it was compared: add one, remove one, reorder one, or reorder a composite primary key — **`tableDiffs` was empty in every case**, measured, before anything was written. The first two are correctness rather than performance: `UniqueConflictError` and `SoftDeletedUniqueError` are litestone's words for a constraint the DATABASE holds, so a constraint that never reached the database is one that never fires and the duplicate lands; the mirror leaves the live table refusing writes the schema allows, in SQLite's own words about a table nobody named. **Read off `PRAGMA index_list` rather than parsed out of the CREATE text** — there is a pragma here and it answers the column ORDER directly, the same reason `generated` comes from `table_xinfo` and a CHECK does not. `origin` does the separating and each value earns its place: `u` is a UNIQUE constraint in either spelling, so `@unique` and `@@unique([thatColumn])` compare EQUAL and moving between them migrates nothing; `pk` is a composite primary key, whose column order was invisible for the same reason; `c` is an explicit `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX`, filtered out, or the index diff and this one would each report the other's as missing — and an index the APP made is `foreign` to `diffIndexes`, which deliberately leaves it alone, so reading it here would rebuild the table to remove a constraint litestone never declared. **The decision the row left open was settled the way [FJS-592](#fjs-592) was — by measuring it.** Diffed each real database in this repo against its own schema with the change in: `example/db/shops/flagship.db` and `packages/basecamp/db/basecamp.db`, both **zero churn**, and no table added to any diff. Not luck — the emitter writes declaration order, so for any database litestone created the live order already IS the pristine order. The cost where it does fire is a full rebuild, because no `ALTER` reaches a table constraint, and a rebuild that ADDS uniqueness fails on the copy if the rows already violate it — inside the transaction, so it rolls back. Eight tests in `index-predicates.test.ts`, three of them negative controls: the same order migrates nothing, the spelling swap migrates nothing, and an explicit `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX` is not read here at all · [migrate.js](packages/litestone/src/core/migrate.js) · [index-predicates.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/index-predicates.test.ts) · [migrations.md](packages/litestone/docs/migrations.md) |
| <a id="fjs-599"></a>FJS-599 | mesa — **A `$:` watch whose dependency list is PARENTHESISED emits an identifier nothing declares, and the module parses — so the failure is a `ReferenceError` inside an effect at runtime** | 2026-08-30 | Two separable defects with one cause: a `$:` dep list is a comma expression, so a parenthesised group inside one is another `SequenceExpression` rather than a leaf. It reached the collector as a single node whose `memberPath` is null and whose `collectRefs` is the ROOT alone, so `$: (a.x, a.y), () => f()` silently became a watch on the whole object — wrong on its own, since a property change fires no whole-object watch. **`flattenSeq`/`watchDeps` in the analyser is the fix** and the parenthesised form now compiles BYTE-IDENTICALLY to its unparenthesised twin, which is the whole claim and is how the test asserts it; the same flattening runs for the bare multi-path form and for a `$: { }` group entry. The loud half was in the emitter and is not about parentheses at all: a dep whose root is a proxy but which carries no dot resolves to `$$watch_<root>`, the whole-object signal, and only the bare `$: a` form ever declared one — so `$: a.x` beside `$: a, () => f()` emitted the same undeclared reference. A `$: { }` group's deps were collected for declaration NOWHERE, so every dotted one referenced a signal nothing declares and every group with no other watch on its root emitted `deps: []`, an entry subscribed to nothing. `watchedPaths` now takes the group deps and the bare deps whose root is ALREADY a proxy root — already, because adding a root is the deep-watch opt-in only `$: a` may trigger. **`requireWatchSig` is the backstop**: the set of `$$watch_*` this component declares, checked at both emit sites, so an undeclared one is a compile error rather than a module that parses. `test/watch-paren-deps.test.js` — 13 cases, 11 of which fail against the pre-fix compiler, including two MOUNTED components, since the `ReferenceError` is raised when the effect runs and no assertion over the emitted string can see it. The undeclared-signal cases are asserted as a PROPERTY — every `$$watch_*` read is one declared — so a new way to reach one fails here too |
| <a id="fjs-605"></a>FJS-605 | litestone — **`@default(now())` on a new column threw a raw SQLite syntax error at application boot, naming no column and no table.** SQLite allows an expression default in `CREATE TABLE` and refuses one in `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN`, where it wants a constant — and `now()` emits `DEFAULT (strftime('%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%fZ', 'now'))`. The diff classified it as a *simple add*, so the ALTER was generated and `runInTransaction` threw `near "(": syntax error` out of `autoMigrate`, at the line an app calls on first open. The rebuild path was already correct — it omits added columns from the copy, so the new table's own DEFAULT fills every existing row — and what was missing was only the classification. Fixed by testing for a LITERAL rather than for a leading paren: what is compared is SQLite's reading of the pristine database, which does not always keep the parens the emitter wrote, so the narrow test passed and threw at the ALTER anyway. | <a id="fjs-604"></a>FJS-604 | litestone — **A migration that BLOCKED a column reported `migrated`, and the reason was a comment inside a SQL string nobody reads.** `autoMigrate` checked its blocked list on the REBUILD path only. | <a id="fjs-579"></a>FJS-579 | litestone — **A client threw on an unknown PROPERTY and took an unknown OPTION in silence, so the same typo was loud in one place and a capability that quietly did not apply in the other** | 2026-08-30 | `createClient` destructured a fixed list with no rest capture, so a key it did not name was dropped the way JavaScript drops any undeclared key. `createClient({ autoMigrate: true })` is the shape that made it visible — never an option, does nothing, and **passed by five of this package's own test files** (`scale`, `arc`, `corpus`, `retention`, `one-to-one`). None of them could see it: a client emits DDL for a table it does not find, so a suite that opens a fresh database passes identically with the option, without it, and with `autoMigrateee: true` — all three measured. **Refused by name now, from a rest capture**, so what counts as unknown is whatever the destructure did not bind and the check cannot go stale. | <a id="fjs-565"></a>FJS-565 | litestone — **A `@guarded` or `@system` column with a generated `@default()` refused the value litestone itself injected, so the one pairing the two attributes exist for made the model uncreatable** | 2026-08-29 | The write refusals graded the payload `writeData` was handed, and by then the create path has stamped its own columns into it — `@default(uuid()/ulid()/cuid()/nanoid())`, `@createdBy`, `@version`, `@sequence`, `@default(auth().x)`, `@default(field)`. So the key was present, the caller was not system, and the write was refused by name for a value the caller never sent. **What it cost was a whole combination**: *a token the server mints that nobody may read and nobody may write* is exactly what `@guarded` plus a generated default describes, and `@secret` expands to `@encrypted @guarded(all)`, so `@secret` + `@default(uuid())` was uncreatable too. `advise.js`'s own `required-guarded-uncreatable` rule has offered *a `@default` generates it at the Data boundary* as its first way out since it was written, and that advice did not work. **Graded against what the CALLER sent now.** Each stamp records the columns it INJECTED into a `stamped` set — the keys the payload did not carry — and both refusals skip those; absence is the test rather than a null value, so naming a guarded column and setting it to `null` is still naming it. Six write paths pass one and `createMany`/`upsertMany` mint one **per row**, because rows are not required to be uniform and a shared set would let row 0's stamp excuse row 1's caller. **The old trade-off was real and is answered rather than reversed** — grading the caller's payload separately meant every write path had to remember to, and a forgotten one is a hole; the remembering is the stamp's job now, so there is one place to add to, and a path that passes no set is refused rather than let through. | <a id="fjs-589"></a>FJS-589 | cli — **A deploy step that REFUSES exits 0, so seven of the pipeline's nine refusal sites report success — including all six of `fli deploy:revert`'s named refusals** | 2026-08-29 | **Two outcomes, and `abort` is now the fail-closed one.** `context.config.abort` is a REFUSAL and the runtime exits non-zero on it even when nothing threw; `context.config.stop` is the deliberate early exit that SUCCEEDED, which is what `--plan` sets. Both skip every later step and they differ only in the verdict, so the next refusal somebody writes is loud without being told to be. **It had to be asked on both return paths**: a command with steps runs the orchestrator then the loop, and one WITHOUT steps returns `config.run` directly — `deploy:logs`, `:status`, `:run` and `:unlock` are that shape, and every one of their refusals exited 0. `assertNotRefused` is called from both. The throw is `quiet`, so `bin/fli.js` prints one line naming the step and nothing else: the refusal has already printed its reason and the ways out, and a generic message on top of a specific one — with an invitation to re-run with `--debug` for a stack that says nothing — buries what was said. **Three sites were the deliberate kind** (`fli deploy --plan`, `fli deploy:revert --plan`, and doctor's trailing abort, which turned out to protect nothing: doctor declares no `steps:` and is not `index.md`, so no step folder is ever discovered for it).
