| <a id="fjs-533"></a>FJS-533 | example · basecamp — **Sixteen screens keep a row that nothing can update, and the half that could not be fixed the way the rule said is now a declaration.** The framework half was closed by `resource.record(id)` ([FJS-518](#fjs-518), [FJS-D138](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d138)) and `detail-read-dead` found sixteen readers of `service.get(id)`, which answers a plain object nothing reaches: not a WS push, not a job, not another tab. Each looks correct the whole time, because a screen re-reads after its OWN actions and never after anyone else's. **Adopting it split the sixteen, and the split is what the fix is built on.** A store node holds ONE shape and `Store.upsert` REPLACES it, so a plain watch is only correct where the detail row IS the row — converting `apps/[id]` took the drive from 302/302 to two failures, `app.domains` empty at the first push, and `dashboards` (`withWidgets`), `blueprints` (`WITH_PARAMS`), `flags` (`flagWithOverrides`), `recipes` (the row plus its runs), `deployments` and `jobs` are all that shape. **Ruled [FJS-D161](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d161): a composed read is a TRIGGER, not a value** — `record(id, { composed: true })` keeps its own value, never writes the composed row into the shared node, and re-runs the read whenever that node moves, coalesced per burst; `changed`, the announcement that names no row, re-runs it in BOTH modes, which is the one write that used to leave a watched row stale for good. Thirteen converted (eleven basecamp, two example), and five hand-rolled reload-on-push subscriptions went with them, each of which carried its own copy of *was that a read?*. **The two left are the exception the rule now names**: `portal.get()` builds an entry from an adapter ping over no model, so there is no node and no announcement will ever arrive — baselined with the reason. `record()` also honours the resource's own `detailQuery`, which it had silently ignored | 2026-08-29 | [index.ts](packages/junction/src/client/index.ts) · [resource.js](packages/sierra/src/junction/resource.js) · [checks.js](packages/cli/core/checks.js) |
| <a id="fjs-597"></a>FJS-597 | litestone — **`litestone mutate` could not be pointed at the app most worth pointing it at, and on the way to fixing that it turned out to report 100% having graded nothing** | 2026-08-29 | Three defects in one command, each hiding the next. **It read the schema as TEXT** — `readFileSync` into `schemaMutants` — so nothing followed an `import`, and a schema importing a fragment parsed as a file full of `extend model` naming models never declared. `basecamp` (45 models, all gated) died at `mutationScore: the original schema does not parse`. `FJS-264`'s class, fourth instance, and the only one that fails loudly. Fixed with `inlineImportsFromDisk` rather than `parseFile`, because the mutation catalogue is line-oriented and wants TEXT — the same reason `createTestEnv` keys its template cache on one string — and an import that cannot be read is now NAMED, since its models are otherwise silently outside the run. **That unblocked the second**: with the fragment in, every mutant came back *refused by the loader* and the run printed **100% killed, 14/14**, having graded nothing — `createTestEnv` cannot build a schema declaring `@secret` without a key, so the refusal was about the schema and not about the mutation. Counting a build refusal as a kill is CORRECT (a schema the framework will not load cannot ship) and is correct only while the ORIGINAL builds, which nothing checked. `mutationScore` builds the original first now and refuses with the reason attached — the same control it already kept one level down, where an `error` row from a check is not a kill, and the same one `verifyRowPolicies` keeps about rows on one side of a predicate. **And the third was why it could not build**: this command alone read `cfg.encryptionKey`, which `loadConfig` does not populate; every other command in the file reads `getEncKey()`. Measured: `basecamp` goes from 300 mutants all killed by the parser and the run refused, to **328 mutants, none killed by the parser**, with `guarded-drop` 1 → 6 — the `@secret` and `@guarded` columns of the credential tables had been outside anything mutate could grade. · [cli.js `cmdMutate`](packages/litestone/src/tools/cli.js) · [mutate.js](packages/litestone/src/mutate.js) · [litestone.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/litestone.test.ts) · [cli-smoke.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/cli-smoke.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-594"></a>FJS-594 | litestone — **`litestone introspect` wrote a `.lite` litestone could not read, and every test of it was a substring match** | 2026-08-29 | The adoption door — point it at a database you already have, or run `fli db:pull` — and its output did not parse. Reproduced through the shipped command on a database litestone itself built from a valid schema: three defects in ten lines. `references: [id] onDelete: CASCADE` carries no comma, so the file stops at `Expected COLON, got ')'`; `CASCADE` is SQLite's word where `ON_DELETE_ACTIONS` wants Prisma's, and `ddl.js` had only ever translated that one way; and `@@softDelete` was emitted beside `@@index([deletedAt])`, which [FJS-480](#fjs-480) refuses BY NAME — that ruling added the rule and nobody asked who was PRODUCING the pair. **Why it survived**: `generateLiteSchema` had six tests and every one was `expect(schema).toContain(...)`; not one fed the result back to `parse()`, and the FK case asserted a relation with no `onDelete` on it. Closed with the property instead of the three fixes — `test/introspect-roundtrip.test.ts` asserts that reading a database built from the output is a FIXED POINT, over the seven corpus schemas and the 188-model `openmrp` fixture. It found four more the same day, each one invisible to any substring assertion: a **SQL expression default emitted as a string literal**, so a `@default(uuid())` column got 200 characters of SQL as its value and `ddl.js` doubled the quotes inside it on every pass; **`@@index(where:)` re-emitting the clause `createIndexes` ANDs on for `@@softDelete`**, so the predicate nested one level deeper each time until it stopped being readable; an **enum member that is not an identifier emitted bare** (`Half-yearly`), which is [FJS-593](#fjs-593)'s spelling never reaching the second producer; a **relation field named for the table it points at**, which collides with a real COLUMN of that name and DELETES it, since the parser keeps one field per name; and an **enum name colliding with a MODEL name**, which makes the parser resolve that model's own relation fields as enums and turn them into TEXT columns. **And the documented door could not reach any of it**: `fli db:pull` runs `litestone introspect --schema <schema>` and passes no path, so the command read `cfg.db` — which `loadConfig` answers as `./development.db` when nothing said otherwise — and named a file the schema never mentions, for every app that declares a `database` block. `FJS-449`'s class: the declaration is the answer. It resolves from the declaration now, and a schema declaring several is asked WHICH by name rather than served the first, because the output carries no `@@db`. Also `--report` and `--strict`, and the three tiers now grade both converters — `src/import/tiers.js` is the one table, and `test/import.test.ts`'s totality guard reads `introspect.js` alongside the four readers, both directions. · [introspect.js](packages/litestone/src/tools/introspect.js) · [tiers.js](packages/litestone/src/import/tiers.js) · [introspect-roundtrip.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/introspect-roundtrip.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-593"></a>FJS-593 | litestone — **An enum member had to be a legal identifier, so a closed set written for a person to read could not be expressed at all** | 2026-08-29 | `enum S { Draft }` parsed and `enum S { "On Hold" }` did not. Measured over seven published schemas, **283 Frappe Select fields declare a set `.lite` could not carry**, and almost every one is blocked by a space and nothing else: `On Hold`, `To Receive and Bill`, `Grand Total`, `Per Week`. Each landed as a bare `String` with the constraint gone. **A member may be a quoted string now, and the stored value IS the string** — no second name, nothing translates. That is Postgres's own answer. Prisma's `@map` on the member is the other one and buys a separate code-name at the price of a bidirectional layer on every read and write, which nothing in the corpus asks for; `@label` already covers display, so the code-name would be a third thing. A quoted member that IS a legal identifier normalises to the bare spelling, so `"Draft"` and `Draft` collide rather than becoming two members — the reading [`FJS-564`](#fjs-564) gave the redundant array default. The empty string is refused. **Four paths name a member and all four take it**: a `@default("To Receive and Bill")`, a `@@transitions` state, the JSON Schema `enum` array, and the client's own write validator, which answers *must be one of: Draft, To Receive and Bill, Completed*. Typegen was already a string union. The enum CHECK now **escapes an apostrophe** — without it a member like `Don't ship` closes the SQL literal and the whole CREATE TABLE stops parsing, and the test executes the DDL rather than matching its text. **An UNNAMED move onto a quoted member is refused**, the same reasoning the boolean rule already makes one step along: `-> refunded` reads as `refund` and a sentence does not name an action. **The Frappe reader is the payoff**: an option list only has to be a SET now, so `select-not-an-enum` fell **283 → 60** and ERPNext's `lost` fell 633 → 410. The 60 left are single-option `naming_series` — `PUR-ORD-.YYYY.-`, a format string wearing a Select, and one option is not a set. Widening it made one name collision reachable (`Supplier Scorecard Period` is a doctype AND `SupplierScorecard.period`), reported by name and suffixed rather than dropped. |
| <a id="fjs-591"></a>FJS-591 | litestone — **An index column could not carry a direction, so a composite index whose columns disagree was inexpressible — and the migrator could not have seen one anyway** | 2026-08-29 | `@@index([a, b])` took a bare field list. trigger.dev writes `@@index([organizationId, type, createdAt(sort: Desc)])` twenty-one times; every one imported as an ascending index, which serves a different set of queries. **Adopted Prisma's spelling, and it is ZenStack's too**: `stdlib.zmodel` in both v2 and v3 declares `@@index(_ fields: FieldReference[], …, sort: SortOrder?, …) @@@prisma` — byte-identical, and `@@@prisma` is their own marker for *inherited unchanged* — so one spelling covers all three and an import carries it through. `Asc`/`Desc`, and lowercase is accepted because it is the client's own `orderBy` word for the same thing. **`fields` stays a plain array of names** and the directions travel beside it as `sorts`, aligned by position, because the index NAME is derived from the field list and three other readers walk it. **Three places in the migrator were blind to it and all three had to move.** `indexKey` did not carry the direction, so a declared `sort: Desc` compared equal to the ascending index already there; the added-index emit rebuilt its column list from names alone, so the drop would have been followed by a CREATE of the index it had just removed; and `parseIndexColumns` de-quoted `"createdAt" DESC` into `createdAt" DESC`, which `isIndexExpression` then reported as an expression — the [`FJS-584`](#fjs-584) shape one modifier along. A direction change now drops and recreates, in that order, which matters because the name is unchanged and `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS` over a live index is a silent no-op. The Prisma reader carries `sort:` through; `type:`/`ops:`/`length:` are a Postgres access method, an opclass and a MySQL prefix length, still reported as lost because SQLite has none of them. **The SQL and Rails readers do not carry it yet** — a `DESC` in a Postgres dump is still dropped in silence. |
| <a id="fjs-588"></a>FJS-588 | litestone — **A `bigint` was graded a non-issue on a claim measurement contradicts, and two of the four readers never reported it at all** | 2026-08-29 | `litestone import` graded `bigint` `noted`, reasoning *"SQLite INTEGER is 64-bit: the range holds, the name does not"*. [`FJS-583`](#fjs-583) measured that the range does **not** hold — the value crosses a JS `number` at both ends — so a `bigint` becomes an `Int` that cannot round-trip past 2^53, silently, and that is `changed`. trigger.dev writes `TaskEvent.startTime BigInt` as nanoseconds since epoch (≈1.7e18); imported, it loses roughly microsecond granularity with nothing said. **Worse, only the Prisma reader reported it**: `sql.js` and `rails.js` mapped `bigint\|int8\|bigserial` → `Int` in their type tables with no gap, so the three largest sources of it were silent and `--strict` would have failed a Prisma import and passed a Postgres one over the same column. **Fixed by regrading and by giving the other two readers the report**, with the exemption that makes it readable rather than true-and-ignored: a generated key counts from 1 and cannot reach 2^53, and a foreign key holds one of those values. Where the source declares relations the exemption is purely structural — a Prisma `BigInt` no relation owns is supplied, which is how `GithubAppInstallation.appInstallationId` stays reported where a rule keyed on the `Id` suffix would skip the one case it most needs. A Postgres dump commonly declares no foreign keys at all, so `namesATable` is the one named fallback: an unconstrained `*_id` whose prefix IS a table in the same dump. Measured, that removes 185 of discourse's 221 conventional keys and touches none of lago's. Corpus after: **235 reported** across five schemas — discourse 124 of its 458 bigint columns, lago 64 of 67, because lago declares its keys as `uuid` and its values as `bigint`, and 61 of those are `*_amount_cents`. |
| <a id="fjs-583"></a>FJS-583 | litestone — **A scaled column promised an exact round trip and stopped delivering one at 2^53, silently — the exact failure `@scale` exists to prevent** | 2026-08-29 | `docs/exact-numbers.md` said nine places leaves "nine figures in front of the point", reasoning from SQLite's 64-bit `INTEGER`. But the value crosses a JS `number` at both ends and litestone sets `safeIntegers` nowhere, so `bun:sqlite` returns a `number` on every path — a column read and an aggregate alike. Measured: `12345678900000001` reads back `…000`, `9007199254740993` reads back `…992`, `sum()` rounds the same way. At nine places the true range is **seven** figures, not nine, and two distinct minor-unit values collide into one row value with nothing raised. That is `prisma#20635` — the bug [`FJS-D142`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d142) cites as the reason to prefer `Int @scale` over a `Decimal` scalar — reproduced one layer up, and the same silence Rails has on SQLite. **Fixed both ends.** The boundary refuses a value past `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER` by name, naming the bound in minor units and working it out at the column's own places (`must be at most 9,007,199,254,740,991 minor units … at 9 decimal place(s) that is 9,007,199`) — a separate sentence from the fraction refusal, because not knowing the unit and exceeding the range are different mistakes. And `@scale`/`@money` now emit `CHECK ("c" BETWEEN -9007199254740991 AND 9007199254740991)`, because four writers never reach the boundary: a migration, a seed, a raw statement, and `asSystem()`, which drops the gate, the row policies and `@@softDelete` and cannot drop a rule that is in the table ([`FJS-519`](#fjs-519)). `release:check` does not grade it — the constraint is field-level and `release.js` compares model-level `@@check`/`@@arc`, so `release.snapshot.md` did not move; deliberate, because the only writes it refuses are ones that were already being stored wrong. **A plain `Int` is deliberately not bounded** — it makes no exactness promise, and bounding every integer column in every app to buy back one is the wrong trade; the hazard is stated in the doc instead, since a snowflake id kept in an `Int` has the same ceiling and nothing reports it. |
| <a id="fjs-575"></a>FJS-575 | litestone — **`@scale` caps at 9 places and usage-based billing writes 15 — but the cap was never the binding constraint, and raising it would have made things worse** | 2026-08-29 | The row asked for a bigger cap. Measuring the ceiling answered a different question. **The binding limit is 2^53, not int64**: the value arrives and leaves as a JS `number` and `bun:sqlite` returns one on every path, so nine places leaves *seven* figures in front of the point rather than the nine the docs claimed, and past it the rounded double was stored silently — filed and fixed as [`FJS-583`](#fjs-583). A bigger cap would have moved that hole further into the dark. **What the corpus actually needs splits in two**, and Lago's own `Fee` writes both: `amountCents Int` beside `preciseAmountCents numeric(40,15)`, `unitAmountCents Int` beside `preciseUnitAmount numeric(30,15)` — and `preciseCouponsAmountCents` at scale 5, which `@scale` took cleanly. A **rate** is small-magnitude and many-placed, and `@scale(9)` holds every rate in the 25 columns found across lago, discourse and triggerdev. A **precise accumulated total** is big-magnitude *and* many-placed, fits no 64-bit integer at any useful scale, and is an un-rounded intermediate rather than a stored quantity — so where it lives is rounding and allocation, which is [`FJS-D154`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d154), since ruled. Prior art agrees on the split: Postgres has `NUMERIC` and we do not; **Stripe writes two fields** — an integer `unit_amount` beside `unit_amount_decimal`, *"a decimal string with at most 12 decimal places"* — and Prisma's `Decimal` on SQLite is broken by its own maintainers' account (`prisma#20635`), which is why `Int @scale` exists at all. Said in [exact-numbers.md](packages/litestone/docs/exact-numbers.md) § *What this does NOT do*. **What remains is a feature, not a defect**: reaching Stripe's twelve places means BigInt at the boundary — `JSON.stringify(1n)` throws, so the wire needs a string, and junction's `type: 'integer'`, the form controls and `example`'s `pricing.ts` all assume `number`. That is a separate ruling and Candidate C does not need it. |
| <a id="fjs-582"></a>FJS-582 | sierra — **`@money` and `@scale` reached the JSON Schema and stopped there, so the contributed control the docs show could not be written off the declaration** | 2026-08-29 | `buildFieldRules` carries a fixed list of keys onto a rule and neither `x-money` nor `x-scale` was on it — so nothing downstream could tell a SCALED integer from an ordinary one, and a generated form gave a price column a spinner stepping by 1. Both docstrings advertised `registerControl('money', …)` and had to resolve it on a column name ending in `Cents`, a convention no schema in this repo uses. **Fixed 2026-08-29** by carrying both, for the reason `x-time` is carried: the keyword decides a CONTROL and nothing else on the rule can answer. The doc examples now read `rule['x-money']`. What the control IS stays an app's decision — the currency's symbol, whether the box is in major units, what a blank means. | <a id="fjs-581"></a>FJS-581 | example — **The extension's dock printed a pound sign on a dollar amount, at a hundredth of it, and its drive was green throughout** | 2026-08-29 | `extension/src/dock/App.mesa` formatted an order total as ``£${(pennies / 100).toFixed(2)}`` against a column that held DOLLARS, so a $87.00 order read `£0.87` in the toolbar — the wrong currency and the wrong magnitude, in the one surface a shop owner glances at rather than reads. `verify:extension` asserts the order arrives, moves and leaves the queue and never reads the figure, which is why 13/13 said nothing. **Fixed 2026-08-29** by the adoption that made the assumption true — `Order.total` is `@money(USD)` and therefore is cents now — and by routing it through `formatMoney(fromMinor(…))`, so the symbol and the number of decimals are both the currency's. | <a id="fjs-570"></a>FJS-570 | sierra · junction — **A relation picker asked for a service named after the MODEL, and a service is named after its FILE — so every foreign key onto a multi-word model offered an empty list** | 2026-08-29 | Ruled `FJS-D159` and fixed. A kebab FILENAME was a fourth spelling of a name Invariant 2 says three resolvers must agree on, and it broke **two** resolutions rather than the one reported: `deriveModelName('product-variants')` singularises to `product-variant`, which is not the accessor, which is why all six multi-word services in `example` hand-write `model:`. `deriveName` folds `-`/`_` into camel, so the service is `productVariants`; the FILE keeps its name and its spelling is registered as an ALIAS, so no URL, `app.service()` call or WS frame moves. Every lookup already went through `ServiceRegistry.get`, so one resolution covers HTTP, the socket, `app.service` and OpenAPI — and both transports now build the context from `service.name`, so a call arriving under the old spelling announces and resolves its model under the one name the service has. **Proved across the two packages rather than asserted**: `example`'s app booted and asked for real — `ProductVariant → productVariants FOUND`, and the same for `ProductImage`, `OrderLine`, `ShippingMethod`, `TaxRate` and `PaymentEvent`, all six of which were MISSING before. **The second half is that a miss is no longer silent**: `resource.options()` answers `error` where it could not ask, so *there are none* and *I could not reach the service* stop being the same empty list — which matters for a DECLARED name, where the ruling deliberately does not guess (basecamp's `hub-config`, auth's `api-keys`). The kit does not render that error yet — [FJS-587](#fjs-587). The alias is committed in every `surface.snapshot.md` as **also answers to**. And a return is now loud rather than silent — the prerender's clock fails the build naming the route and the phase. The second shape this row carried — an island with element CHILDREN — is in the same answer: it builds, and a marker still is not a slot, so what children mean there is a question for whoever wants them rather than a defect. · [fixtures/layout-island](packages/sierra/tests/fixtures/layout-island/README.md) · [build-imports.test.js](packages/sierra/tests/build-imports.test.js) · [prerender.js `bounded`](packages/sierra/src/build/prerender.js) |

| <a id="fjs-551"></a>FJS-551 | sierra — **A module whose top-level `await` throws reports the real error ONCE; every import after that is a TDZ naming a random binding, and the static build only ever showed the second kind** | 2026-08-29 | **The runtime half cannot be fixed and is not the defect.** A failed TLA module re-imports as a partially-initialised namespace rather than re-throwing — measured again under bun: `the real one`, then `Cannot access 'other' before initialization.`, then the same forever. **What was Sierra's is that it threw the one truthful error away**, at two doors that each read `catch { return null }`. `packages/sierra/src/build/app-import.js` is the one owner now: it records the first failure that is not itself a TDZ, refuses to import a module that already failed (re-importing is what MANUFACTURES the lie), and **touches every binding of a namespace it just imported** — a half-built module does not throw on import, it throws when somebody reads a binding, so without that the TDZ lands in whichever caller reached for `.db` or `.load` and outside any explanation. `explainModuleInitFailure` then prints the recorded cause rather than advice to go and reproduce it. **And the two answers are separated**: `resolveBuildDb` still warns and continues for a db module that is ABSENT or wrong-shaped — that argument was always right — and FAILS the build for one that threw; `importCompanion` answers `null` only for a companion that is not there, and throws naming the route for one that would not load, which closes the same fail-open shape `FJS-439` closed for a render. Verified end to end against a real `vite build`: a db module whose TLA throws now fails with `static safety: 'db-broken.js' threw while it was loading` and the module's own sentence — the schema parse error, naming its line — where the build used to print four TDZs and exit. | <a id="fjs-564"></a>FJS-564 | litestone — **An array column is expressible and its default is not — including the empty array** | 2026-08-29 | **The ruling the row asked for was already made and already shipped — in the DDL, unsaid.** `columnDefaultExpr` gives every array column `NOT NULL DEFAULT '[]'` because an empty array is the null state of a list, so an omitted one already reads back `[]` (measured) and a write of `null` is refused. `@default([])` is therefore a restatement, not a new behaviour — and it parses now, because a language that refuses the redundant spelling of its own behaviour fails a port on a line that means what the tree already does. **The case with no other spelling at all is the non-empty one**: `@default(["a", "b"])`, `@default([1, 2])`, and `@default([Active, Pending])` with enum members written bare, exactly as a scalar enum default is. `parseDefaultArray` refuses a call (`[now()]` is one timestamp frozen at migrate time) and a nested array by name. **Two spellings arrive and one AST leaves**: the JSON-string form is normalised into the literal at parse, so `defaultExpr`, the JSON Schema and `release:check` read one kind — and it is now graded by the same rules, which it never was (`String[] @default("[1,2]")` was valid JSON and put numbers in a text column; refused). **The empty default is also stated at the second boundary**: every array column emits `default: []` into its JSON Schema whether it declares one or not, so a form, a factory and a generated type seed what the column can actually hold instead of `undefined`. `Json` takes the literal too, since it can hold a list; a column that holds one value refuses it by name. basecamp's five array columns moved to the literal. Green: litestone 3348, junction 1600, sierra 1126 · [parser.js `parseDefaultArray`, `elementErrors`](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js) · [ddl.js `defaultExpr`](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) · [jsonschema.js](packages/litestone/src/jsonschema.js) · [schema.md](packages/litestone/docs/schema.md) |
| <a id="fjs-590"></a>FJS-590 | litestone — **The two conversion paths disagree about a partial index, and one of them is a few hours out of date.** `litestone introspect` emits `@@index([kind], where: archivedAt == null)` since [FJS-586](#fjs-586); `litestone import` (`src/import/sql.js`) still returns `@@index([kind])` and records the gap as *emitted without the predicate*. Same question, two owners, two answers — Invariant 4's shape — and the importer's is worse only because it was written before `@@index(where:)` existed. It reaches **97 of the 132** in-scope plain partial indexes in the reference corpus (90 null tests + 7 booleans), every one of which could now be emitted whole. **The two already agree on the harder half**, which is why this is S4 and not S3: both refuse a partial UNIQUE for the same stated reason — *emitting it unconditionally would be a stronger constraint than the source declares* — and both emit `nullsDistinct: true` for a composite unique over a nullable column. So what is missing is the newly-possible half, not the safety half. The fix is the predicate translator, which needs one owner rather than a second copy: `predicateToLite` currently lives in `tools/introspect.js` and would move beside `parseIndexColumns`/`indexPredicate` in `core/migrate.js`, with the shipped importer reading it. Its own `gaps` reason string and the corpus README's counts move with it. Not fixed on sight because `src/import/` was under active edit in another session at the time (**do not** patch it without checking) **Fixed 2026-08-29.** `predicateToLite` moved beside `parseIndexColumns`/`indexPredicate` in `core/migrate.js` — one owner, three converters — and the `sql` and `rails` readers emit `@@index([…], where: …)` where the predicate is one `.lite` can hold. It also learned Postgres's boolean spelling: the readers consume a dump where SQLite writes `= 1` and Postgres writes `= true`, and only the first was understood. **Measured on the corpus: 94 of the 251 partial indexes now survive the conversion whole** (37%), where the number was 0 — 45 discourse, 23 lago, 26 mastodon, 89 `where:` clauses in the committed fixtures. The other 119 are unique and correctly dropped, and 38 hold a predicate `.lite` cannot express. **Building it broke the corpus and that is the finding worth keeping**: the readers deduped indexes on the EMITTED STRING, which worked only while a predicate was stripped — two indexes over one column list both rendered `@@index([a, b])` and collapsed. Emitting the predicate made them different strings, so both survived, and litestone names an index for its COLUMNS, so the regenerated fixtures no longer parsed. Keyed on the column list now, in both readers, with the gap reason corrected. It reached the committed fixtures because another session regenerated the corpus while this change was half-applied. 44 cases in `test/index-predicates.test.ts`. Green: litestone 3406, typecheck clean | 2026-08-29 | [import/sql.js](packages/litestone/src/import/sql.js) · [tools/introspect.js `predicateToLite`](packages/litestone/src/tools/introspect.js) · [corpus README](packages/litestone/test/fixtures/corpus/README.md) |
| <a id="fjs-586"></a>FJS-586 | litestone — **`litestone introspect` turns a PARTIAL unique index into a total one, so the generated schema declares a stronger constraint than the database it was read from.** `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX u ON note (email) WHERE deletedAt IS NULL` — uniqueness among LIVE rows, the commonest partial index there is — and `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX u ON note (email)` introspect to the **identical** `email String? @unique`. The predicate is discarded with nothing said. Measured 2026-08-29 by running the command against both. **The strengthening is real and it bites on the round trip**: the source admits two soft-deleted rows sharing an email and admits reusing a deleted row's address, and the generated schema admits neither — so `introspect` → `db push` refuses writes the original database accepted, and [FJS-204](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md)'s ruling (a soft-deleted row KEEPS its unique slot) makes that permanent rather than transient. **This repo already made the call the other way and introspect did not get the memo**: the corpus converters DROP a partial unique rather than emit it whole, in the README's own words — *emitting it would be a stronger constraint than the source declares*. Two answers to one question, and the user-facing one is the wrong one. **The plain-index half is now fixable rather than merely lossy**: a partial composite emits as `@@index([kind, email])` with its predicate gone, and since `@@index([col], where: …)` exists it can be emitted whole wherever the predicate is one litestone accepts (a null test or a boolean), and dropped with a comment where it is not. A solo non-unique index is still dropped entirely, which is pre-existing and noted in [FJS-480](#fjs-480). Found while checking whether [FJS-584](#fjs-584) had any live path — it does not, and this does **Fixed 2026-08-29, and the asymmetry is the fix.** Dropping a predicate from a UNIQUE index STRENGTHENS the constraint, so a partial unique is now handed over as a `// FIXME` naming the predicate rather than emitted — the same call the corpus converters already made. Dropping it from a PLAIN index only WIDENS the index, same rows answered, so that stays safe and is what happens where the predicate is one `.lite` cannot hold (with a `// NOTE` saying so). Where it CAN be held it is emitted whole — `@@index([kind], where: archivedAt == null)`, `where: live == true` — which is [FJS-578](#fjs-578) paying for itself, and the soft-delete clause stays implicit because declaring it is refused. **The headline assertion is that the generated file PARSES**, and asking that found three more: a `///` note at the end of a model body attaches to no declaration and stops the parse; litestone names an index for its COLUMNS so two partial indexes over one list cannot both be declared (first wins, the rest are handed over); and a composite unique over a nullable column needs `nullsDistinct: true` to be legal at all ([FJS-D130](DECISIONS.md)) — SQL's own word for what the source database is already doing. 44 cases in `test/index-predicates.test.ts`, every guard negative-controlled. Green: litestone 3382, typecheck clean | 2026-08-29 | [transform/framework.js `introspectSQL`](packages/litestone/src/transform/framework.js) · [cli.js `introspect`](packages/litestone/src/tools/cli.js) · [corpus README](packages/litestone/test/fixtures/corpus/README.md) |
| <a id="fjs-584"></a>FJS-584 | litestone — **`introspect` parses the columns of an index over an EXPRESSION as garbage** — `CREATE INDEX i ON t (lower(a))` is read as one column named `lower(a`, because the parse takes the first parenthesised group and an expression opens a second one. Measured 2026-08-29; a partial expression index (`(lower(a), b) WHERE d IS NULL`) reads its predicate correctly and its columns wrongly, so the two halves of one record disagree. **Harmless today and that is why it is S4**: `.lite` has no syntax for an expression index, so litestone emits none, nothing is ever on the pristine side, and a hand-made one is filtered into `foreign` by `ownedIndex` and left alone. **It stops being harmless the moment the construct is declarable** — the reference corpus holds **47 index-over-an-expression instances** (discourse, lago), the third-largest thing `.lite` cannot say — because then a pristine expression index and a live one compare on a mangled name and every migration rebuilds it. Named inside [FJS-576](#fjs-576) while that was being fixed; filed on its own because a gap recorded only in a closed row is a gap nothing can find. The fix is a real parse of the column list rather than a regex over the first bracket, which is also what a declared expression index would need **Fixed 2026-08-29 as a byproduct of [FJS-586](#fjs-586)**, which is the only reason it was worth doing: the two copies of the broken regex became one owner — `parseIndexColumns` in `migrate.js`, bracket-counting rather than first-`)`-wins — because a converter that must read a predicate has to read the column list correctly too. `isIndexExpression` is the other half, so `introspect` can hand over an index it has no spelling for instead of emitting a column called `lower(a`. It also steps over a quoted table name holding a bracket of its own. Not a SQL parser and says so: a string literal holding a bracket inside an expression would still fool the depth count, which is a shape nothing here can emit. Still inert on its own — no path reached it — which is why it was filed and not built | 2026-08-29 | [migrate.js `introspect`](packages/litestone/src/core/migrate.js) · [corpus README](packages/litestone/test/fixtures/corpus/README.md) |
| <a id="fjs-578"></a>FJS-578 | litestone — **Two compilers turn a predicate into SQL and they disagree about a boolean, so the same meaning reaches SQLite two ways.** The policy compiler (`@@scope`, `@@allow`, the soft-delete injection) inlines `true`/`false`/`null` as `1`/`0`/`NULL`; the query builder a caller's own `where` goes through inlines only the null test and BINDS a boolean. Measured 2026-08-29: `@@scope(live, live == true)` compiles to `"live" = 1` while `where: { live: true }` compiles to `"live" = ?`, for one model, one column and one question. **Ordinarily invisible** — both answer the same rows — which is why it survived: it costs nothing until something has to compare the two strings. **Where it bites is a partial index.** SQLite proves a query implies a partial index at PREPARE time, so an index built from the policy compiler's `"live" = 1` is matched by a `$scope` query and never by a plain filter. `@@index([kind], where: live == true)` is therefore refused rather than shipped as an index reachable one way and not the other, which narrows the declared surface to the null tests — and the corpus wants the boolean shape (`filtered = false`, `superapp = true`, `rolled_up = false`, `pinned_globally`). **The fix is to inline a boolean in the query builder too**, which has exactly two values and both are safe literals, bounding the plan-cache cost at two strings per predicate. It is four `push()` sites in `query.js` on the hot path of every read, which is why it is filed rather than folded into the index work. Relaxing it is one clause of the reachability reduction in `parser.js` and nothing else moves **Fixed 2026-08-29.** `operandSql(v, push)` in `query.js` is the one decision — a literal where that is safe, a bound `?` otherwise — applied at the six sites a boolean could reach: the scalar shorthand, an array shorthand, `equals`, `not`, `in` and `notIn`. A boolean is 0 or 1 and nothing else, so there is no escaping and no injection surface, and the plan cache grows by at most two strings per predicate. `push` still coerces one for every caller that keeps binding. **Measured through a real client**: `where: { live: true }` emits `"live" = 1`, the declared index is `WHERE "live" = 1`, and EXPLAIN over the bytes the client actually sent answers `SEARCH note USING COVERING INDEX idx_note_kind` — the test EXPLAINs the captured `sql` and `params` rather than a hand-written lookalike, which is what makes it fail when the inlining is removed. `@@index([col], where: <bool column> == true)` is accepted now, taking the shipped corpus surface from 90 of 132 to **97 (73%)**. 30 cases in `test/index-predicates.test.ts`, negative-controlled — 2 red with the inlining neutered. Green: litestone 3361, typecheck clean | 2026-08-29 | [query.js](packages/litestone/src/core/query.js) · [policy.js `compileStatic`](packages/litestone/src/core/policy.js) · [partial-indexes.md](IDEAS/partial-indexes.md) |
| <a id="fjs-577"></a>FJS-577 | litestone — **A table rebuild recreated a `@@softDelete` model's indexes as FULL ones, and the fallback meant to prevent it was unreachable for every caller.** `generateIndexDDL(model, softDelete = false, …)` returns `createIndexes(model, softDelete ?? isSoftDelete(model), …)`, and `false ?? x` is `false` — so the `??` was dead code and the function never emitted a partial index for anybody, including the callers that passed nothing. The migrator's rebuild branch called it as `generateIndexDDL(model, false, { pluralize })`, so **any schema change that rebuilds the table** — a dropped column, a changed type, a STORED generated column — silently dropped the `WHERE "deletedAt" IS NULL` clause from every index on the model. **This is [FJS-443](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md)'s exact shape in the branch its fix did not reach**: that one replaced the NEW-TABLE call with `generateModelDDL` and left the rebuild call, whose own neighbouring comment names the failure. Measured 2026-08-29: index partial before the rebuild, full after, rows preserved. **It compounds with [FJS-576](#fjs-576)** — 577 degrades the index, 576 is why nothing notices, then or ever — so a database drifted one rebuild at a time with `db push` and `migrate apply` diverging further on each. Bounded to speed and drift for the same reason as its sibling: an index predicate never changes which rows match. **Fixed 2026-08-29** by making the parameter default absent rather than `false`, so *unstated* means *ask the model*, which is the only thing a caller who states nothing can have meant; the rebuild call states nothing. 11 cases in `test/index-predicates.test.ts`, negative-controlled — 2 red with the default restored, 3 and 4 red with each half of 576 neutered. Green: litestone 3310 | 2026-08-29 | [ddl.js `generateIndexDDL`](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) · [migrate.js](packages/litestone/src/core/migrate.js) · [index-predicates.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/index-predicates.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-576"></a>FJS-576 | litestone — **The migrator is blind to index predicates, so a partial index and a full one over the same columns diff as identical — and litestone already emits partial indexes.** `introspect` reads `sqlite_master` and keeps `{name, cols, unique}` per index; the `WHERE` tail is dropped, so `diffSchemas` answers `hasChanges: false` for two databases that genuinely differ. Measured 2026-08-29 on byte-identical databases with one index rebuilt without its predicate: `hasChanges: false`, `tableDiffs: []`, while `sqlite_master` holds `CREATE INDEX "idx_note_status" ON "note" ("status") WHERE "deletedAt" IS NULL` on one side and the same statement without the clause on the other. **It reaches a real path**: `@@softDelete` gives every `@@index` on the model a `WHERE "deletedAt" IS NULL` clause ([FJS-480](#fjs-480) is downstream of the same emit), so an existing model that GAINS `@@softDelete` keeps its full index while the schema says partial — measured end to end through `generateMigrationSQL`, rows preserved, index unchanged. `db push` and `migrate apply` therefore build different databases, which is [FJS-443](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md)'s class arrived at from another direction. **Bounded, and that is why it is S3**: an index predicate never changes which rows a query returns, only which index the planner may use, so the whole cost is a bigger index, a slower read and schema drift — never a wrong answer. **It stops being bounded the moment a predicate is author-written**, because then an edited `where:` silently does not apply, which is why it blocks `IDEAS/partial-indexes.md` step 1. **The repair is the technique already load-bearing beside it**: capture the `WHERE` tail and compare it as text, exactly as `parseChecks` does for `CHECK` — both sides being this emitter's own output is what makes a string comparison exact there. Sibling gap found in the same function: the column parse takes the first parenthesised group, which the corpus's 47 expression indexes break for an unrelated reason. **Fixed 2026-08-29**: `indexPredicate()` reads the tail — the first `)` followed by WHERE closes the column list — `indexKey` carries it, and the CREATE emit reproduces it, so a changed predicate is a drop and an add. An unchanged schema still migrates nothing, which is the case that has to stay covered. Sibling: [FJS-577](#fjs-577). 11 cases in `test/index-predicates.test.ts`, negative-controlled. Green: litestone 3310 | 2026-08-29 | [migrate.js `introspect`](packages/litestone/src/core/migrate.js) · [ddl.js `createIndexes`](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) · [partial-indexes.md](IDEAS/partial-indexes.md) |
| <a id="fjs-573"></a>FJS-573 | cli — **A crashed deploy stranded its lock, and the pid recorded in it was the pid of a shell that had already exited — so nothing could tell a stale lock from a live one, and the resume the journal was offering could only be reached by deleting a file by hand** | 2026-08-29 | Ruled rather than patched ([FJS-D156](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d156)): the journal owns *what state did the last run leave*, the lock owns *is another run working here*, and the lock only ever looked like a second answer to the first because it could not expire. A better pid was not available and never could be — `fli` runs on the operator's machine and reaches the target one command at a time, so **there is no process on the target to point at**. So the lock records what is true: the run, who started it, when, and **which step it is inside** — the fact that makes the duration beside it mean something, since four minutes in `04-build-api` is a build and four minutes in `06-swap` is a run that died. The step comes from a `beforeStep` announcement in the step runner, which is the only place it can: the build is the longest thing a deploy does and runs BEFORE the journal opens (`04c-journal`), and a timer cannot serve it because `execSync` blocks the loop for the whole of a step. Neither register judges liveness — that is a fact about a process this machine cannot see — so the refusal reports and names both ways out: **`fli deploy --resume`** continues what the journal holds and takes the lock over, **`fli deploy:unlock`** drops it and starts fresh. A resume was always safe without a probe (a succeeded step replays into a no-op, a step is claimed compare-and-set, and different bytes are a different Release and therefore a new transition); the lock was only what made it unreachable. A TTL was considered and refused — the heartbeat can only tick per step, so the TTL would have to exceed the longest build. One format, one parser, four scripts, three readers (`core/lock.js`) where each of `deploy`, `deploy:status` and `deploy:doctor` used to `cat` the file and read it its own way — `deploy:status` split `<pid>:<iso>:<target>` on `:` and had been reading the hour as the timestamp for its whole life. The old format still parses and says it is legacy. `deployJournalCycle` used to `rm` the lock itself to reach the resume it was testing and now asserts the refusal names the step and offers `--resume`; 21 executed tests cover the scripts in a real shell, including that `set -C` genuinely refuses a second writer where the `[ -f ]` guard was two operations with a window between them |
| <a id="fjs-569"></a>FJS-569 | litestone · junction — **`busy_timeout` was set on four SQLite connections and absent from four others — and the number was a literal an app could not change** | 2026-08-29 | **`busy_timeout` is set on four SQLite connections and missing from four others, so which databases wait under contention is an accident of which file opened them.** Measured 2026-08-29 with two connections, one holding the write lock: with `busy_timeout = 5000` the second waits 5007ms; **with no pragmas at all it fails in 1ms**. Cross-PROCESS the working case is correct — a second process waited 1444ms for a 1.5s hold and then committed — so nothing is wrong with the mechanism, only with where it is applied. **Set**: litestone's main WRITE connection ([client.js:8561](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js)), caravan's jobs db, junction's `storage/database`. **Absent**: the `jsonl`/`logger` INDEX database ([jsonl.js:239](packages/litestone/src/drivers/jsonl.js) — `new Database(indexPath)` with no pragmas of any kind, not even WAL), the tenant REGISTRY and each tenant's own handle (three sites in [tenant.js](packages/litestone/src/tenant.js), all of which set WAL and stop there), junction's sqlite cache, and litestone's own read-only connection. **The jsonl one is not theoretical**: a second `example` API started beside a running one crashed on its first audit write — `SQLITE_BUSY` out of `insertIndexRecord`, thrown in about a millisecond — and a `logger` database is schema-global by design, so it is the ONE file every tenant and every process writes. That makes it the most contended database an app has and the only one with no wait at all. **The floor half SHIPPED 2026-08-29**: `src/core/pragmas.js` is the one owner, every `new Database(...)` in litestone calls it (main write and read, the tenant registry and each tenant handle, four CLI handles, the jsonl index — where WAL was tried and taken back out: the index is deleted by anything that rewrites the `.jsonl`, so WAL would silently make every such place owe two more unlinks, and one that did not recovered the byte offsets the rewrite had invalidated — measured, as a sweep reporting success having removed nothing), and junction's sqlite cache gained it too. Four tests, behavioural rather than a `PRAGMA` read — each takes the lock from a real second PROCESS, because holding it in the test process reproduces the deadlock below and would pass for the wrong reason — and negative-controlled against the pragma commented out. **What measurement added to the story is that the timeout is narrower than it looks**: `bun:sqlite` is synchronous, so a connection waiting on the lock blocks the thread, which in one process is the event loop — five seconds of a server answering nobody, and it can DEADLOCK, since the waiter blocks the loop, the holder's continuation never runs to commit, and the wait can only expire (measured: 5000ms then failure for an 800ms hold in one process, 1444ms then commit for the same hold across two). It is a cross-process device; what makes the in-process case fine is `$transaction`'s FIFO lock. **What is LEFT is the second half — 5000 is a literal and configurable nowhere** — no `createClient` option, nothing in `database { }`, nothing documented. **The second half SHIPPED 2026-08-29 and this closes**: `createClient({ busyTimeout })`, precedence **option → env (`LITESTONE_BUSY_TIMEOUT`) → 5000**, the same shape `resolveTenancy` uses, plus `createCaravan({ busyTimeout })` and `createSqliteCache({ busyTimeout })` for the two connections litestone does not own. Per DATABASE as `{ default, <db> }`, because the database this issue was filed about wants the opposite answer to main: an audit index write is fire-and-forget and its failure swallowed, so `{ audit: 250 }` is an app saying *drop the row rather than stall the loop*. A malformed value and a key naming a database the schema does not declare are refused BY NAME at `createClient` rather than at the connection that would have used it — a dropped key is a database silently keeping the default, which is the class of silence this whole issue is. **There is deliberately no `database { }` spelling** ([`FJS-D155`](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d155)): how long to wait for another process is a fact about THIS process, and the same schema is opened by an API answering a person and a queue draining a batch. Writing the end-to-end test for the per-database form found a second defect and fixed it — `fireLog`'s `try`/`catch` wraps a call to an `async` `create`, so it caught nothing and a failed audit write was an UNHANDLED REJECTION rather than the swallowed row it is documented to be; invisible while the index had a five-second wait, every time under `{ audit: 0 }`. Caught on the promise now, and the first loss per model warns once. `docs/concurrency.md` is the documented half the issue also asked for, including the measured worker-thread answer for a query that is genuinely long: a worker holding the lock 600ms let the main loop keep ticking and the main thread's write waited 639ms and committed, where the same shape on one thread expires its whole timeout because the holder's release never gets a turn. Touched: [jsonl.js](packages/litestone/src/drivers/jsonl.js) · [tenant.js](packages/litestone/src/tenant.js) · [client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) |
| <a id="fjs-571"></a>FJS-571 | toolbelt — **A compound name did not survive the round trip its own rules produce — `UserStatus` → `user_statuses` → `user_statuse`** | 2026-08-29 | Both whole-word lookups in `singularize` — the irregular table and the `-ses` bare-s list — key on the entire string, so no compound ever reached either: `user_aliases` fell past the list to `user_aliase`, `user_statuses` to `user_statuse`, and `sales_people` came back unchanged. **The stakes are the ones the source file already names**: junction derives a model name from a service name with this, and a service resolving to no model has no `@@gate` and no validation, so the miss fails OPEN — and Invariant 2 holds only while three resolvers agree. Fixed by routing a compound through its HEAD, and only through the suffix rules: `pluralize` is deliberately left whole-word, because reaching inside a compound there turns `audit_index` into `audit_indices` and **renames a table in every schema that already has one** — a choice a test already pinned and which stands. That asymmetry is the point: the table a model gets comes from the regular rules, so reading it back has to use the same rules on the head or the trip cannot close. Found while writing a Rails `schema.rb` reader for the corpus, where `account_aliases` has to name a model \| open→closed same day · [inflect.js](packages/toolbelt/src/inflect/inflect.js) · [inflect.spec.js](packages/toolbelt/test/specs/inflect.spec.js) |
| <a id="fjs-563"></a>FJS-563 | litestone — **The non-owning side of a one-to-one reported `unknown type` for a model it had registered** | 2026-08-29 | A singular back-reference is now paired the way the plural one already was. `parser.js` marks it `backRefOne` so it passes the type check, then pairs it against the unlabelled `@relation` pointing back; `client.js`'s `buildRelationMap` looks for a singular back-reference as well as an array one, so the entry lands under the field's own name instead of the model's — `include: { profile: true }` had been `Unknown relation "profile"` even once the parse passed. It is registered as `hasMany` carrying `toOne`, not as a fourth relation kind: 29 sites branch on kind and only the attach needed to change, so the row is handed over instead of a list of one, and an absent side is `null` rather than `[]`. **Three refusals replace the wrong message** — a foreign key that is not unique (a to-many written as a to-one, which would answer one of many rows arbitrarily), no unlabelled back-reference at all (pointing at the labelled one where there is one), and two candidates (naming both) — and `unknown type` now means what it says. Uniqueness counts a field `@unique`, an exactly-matching model `@@unique`, and **a primary key**, which is how calcom writes it (`eventTypeId Int @id` is the FK and the PK). Proved the way it was found: the converter's workaround is off by default now, and all three corpus schemas — 232 models — parse, build and re-boot with zero drift without it. `test/one-to-one.test.ts` pins all nine branches, because two of the three fixtures are git-ignored for licence reasons |
| <a id="fjs-497"></a>FJS-497 | example — **A checkout link carried an order id and nothing else, so naming a sequential integer opened a payment for somebody else's order — and answered its total** | 2026-08-29 | A hosted checkout is reached by a shopper with NO session, so `payments.start` had nobody to grade and looked the order up as the shop for whatever id arrived. The money could never be redirected (`FJS-494`) and the row could never be read — **what leaked was the difference between the answers**: the reply carried the order's TOTAL and the refusal named its STATUS, so counting from 1 was an existence, amount and status oracle over the whole ledger. Closed by giving the link a credential of its own: the id says WHICH order, the code says the caller may pay it. **Two doors and no third** — a `code`, or nothing, in which case the caller's OWN client answers and `Order`'s two `@@allow` rules decide, so staff and the shopper it belongs to need no code to do what they could already do. A gate refusal is caught and converted to the same 404 a missing order gets, because a policy FILTERS and a gate THROWS and both mean one thing here: an identical reply for a real order, an imaginary one and somebody else's is the whole fix, since the oracle was never the intent but the difference. **DERIVED rather than stored, which reverses the obvious copy of `Cart.handoffCode`** — `<orderId>.<hmac>` in `api/src/core/checkout-code.ts`, timing-safe, one owner. A handoff is consumed and needs a column to hold its lifecycle; this must keep working while the order is payable (a declined card is retried), which `Order.status` already says. No column means nothing to strip from a read, nothing to probe through a filter, and no row that could exist without one. **The stored version was built first and reverted**, which is how [FJS-565](#fjs-565) was found: `@guarded` plus a generated `@default()` makes a model uncreatable. Price stated in the module: no per-order revocation, and rotating the secret invalidates every outstanding link. `carts.checkout` answers a code to the shopper who just bought, `orders.paymentCode` to staff who may already read the row — read-shaped, so `$.dispatch = false`, or `callService` would broadcast a payment credential to every socket joined to `orders`. Four new assertions in `verify:pay`, including that a real order and an imaginary one refuse IDENTICALLY; 21/22 green, the one red being [FJS-567](#fjs-567), which is unrelated and older |
| <a id="fjs-568"></a>FJS-568 | cli — **`fli dev` refused to start on a port it was never going to bind** | 2026-08-29 | The preflight asked `appPorts()`, which answers every surface DIRECTORY that exists (Invariant 3); what `fli dev` then runs is the app's own `dev` script. The two are the same set only in a scaffolded app, where `fli new` composes every surface into one `dev` — and in no app in this repo: `example` has five surfaces and a `dev` of `bun run api & bun run web & wait`, so a storefront left on 8610 refused the command by naming a port `bun run dev` would never have taken. Unactionable in the worst way, because it is *correct* that something is on 8610 and stopping it changes nothing. **Fixed as a second function, not a changed one** — both questions are real: `runnables.js` wants the catalogue, the preflight wants what this command binds. `devPorts()` narrows `appPorts()` to the surfaces the `dev` script runs, walked transitively through its `bun run` targets, anchored on `run` rather than on a bare token (`cd web && vite` holds a `web` that is a directory, and an app whose web script is `web` would match it), and matching **either** spelling of a surface's script since an app may declare both and run the other. A `dev` that runs no other script is deliberately NOT narrowed: that is a one-surface app whose `dev` is the surface command, and narrowing to nothing would skip the only check worth making. | <a id="fjs-560"></a>FJS-560 | litestone — **Filed in error the same day — "there is no `Decimal`" was a probe of the wrong spelling, not a gap** | 2026-08-29 | The capability ships and is ruled: `Int @scale(n)` and `@money(currency)`, `FJS-D142`, 2026-08-25, `docs/exact-numbers.md`. `.lite` deliberately has no `Decimal` TOKEN — the answer is the whole number of minor units in an `Int`, which the closed row had itself named as the cheap option while missing that it was already the ruled one. Nothing failed, which is why it survived: the only symptom was that the documented port did not answer. **Fixed at 8502**, because the scheme is the owner of a global tooling port — that block is refused to every app precisely so a tool keeps one number a person types from memory, and 5001 is outside it, unreserved, and common enough to collide with something nobody started. | <a id="fjs-559"></a>FJS-559 | cli · example — **The generated Resource carried a default form with no submit button, and the generated create and edit pages ignored it and wrote a second form each — so the convention was documented, generated, and unusable** | 2026-08-28 | Two halves of one gap. `core/resource-template.js` grew the markup half (`FJS-D112`, Invariant 18) and its own header told a create page it could be `<Model />` and nothing else — but the wrapper was `<Form ...><slot /></Form>` with no button anywhere, so that page rendered five controls and no way to send them. A page reaching for `<Model><Button slot="actions">` did not fix it either: that names a slot on the WRAPPER, which forwarded none, so the button was swallowed in silence. Measured in a browser, both shapes, before anything was changed. Meanwhile `core/crud-templates.js` — written first — kept emitting its own `<Form {resource}>` on the create page AND on the edit page, which is the form written twice in the two files most likely to drift, in the module that exists because those two commands had already drifted. **The example app had the same shape by hand**: `products/create.mesa` and `orders/create.mesa` each held a `<Form>`, and the customers drawer a third. Fixed on both sides — the wrapper takes `record`/`method`/`submitLabel`/`cancelHref`/`oncancel`/`submitId` and forwards an `actions` snippet, which `<Form>` takes over its own slot; the pages render `<Model />` and state only what a screen decides. `Product.mesa`, `Order.mesa` and `Customer.mesa` carry their default form, and the drawer — cancels by closing rather than navigating — reaches the same fields through `oncancel`. The one is `oauthProviderNames(): string[]`, and it answers the app's own CONFIGURATION, which is identical for every shop. Wrapped, it hands `GET /auth/oauth` a Promise; the route writes it straight into `ctx.json`, and `JSON.stringify` renders a Promise as **`{}`** — so the answer is a 200 carrying `{"providers":{}}`, a sign-in page with no buttons, and no error anywhere. `client.auth.providers()` exists precisely so a screen does not carry a second copy of the list, which is what made this reach a person rather than a test. Fixed with an explicit pass-through set, whose entry bar is stated: synchronous AND shop-independent, both, or the pass-through is a different bug. | <a id="fjs-545"></a>FJS-545 | example · ui — **A styled value naming a token nothing defines drops the whole declaration, and thirteen of the storefront's fifteen did — so the public site shipped with no gap, no border and no radius while every drive stayed green** | 2026-08-26 | The storefront, the SPA and `@frontierjs/ui`'s own `FileField` were written against a Tailwind-shaped ladder — `--space-4`, `--radius`, `--border`, `--hairline`, `--link`, `--danger-fg` — where `@frontierjs/css` ships `--space-2xl`, `--card-radius`, `--rule`, `--color-primary`, `--color-danger`. An undefined custom property is **invalid at computed-value time**, so the browser drops the *declaration*, not the value: `gap: var(--space-4)` is no gap, `border-bottom: 1px solid var(--border)` is no border. | <a id="fjs-519"></a>FJS-519 | litestone · basecamp · cli — **`asSystem()` was all-or-nothing, so a caller needing to bypass one rule got every rule — and under declared row tenancy that meant reading every tenant** | 2026-08-26 | Three parts, all shipped. **(1) litestone**: `asSystem()` means *no permission rules*, not *no scope* — `rulesFor()` in `policy.js` is the one answer to *which rules apply to this caller*, read by the SQL builder, the JS evaluator and the `check()` delegation, and under a system context it keeps exactly the tenancy-generated denies, and only while a tenant is in scope. | <a id="fjs-449"></a>FJS-449 | litestone — **A relative `database { path }` resolved against the process CWD, so a command run from the wrong directory minted a second, empty database and every tool then reported on that one** | 2026-08-26 | Closed in two passes; this is the second. The first shipped `resolveFrom: 'schema'` — anchor to the app root, opt-in because isolation-by-CWD is a real contract — and left the embedded half open: an app assembling its schema in memory has no file to anchor to, which is the shape with the sharpest consequence measured (a `vite build` from a surface root prerendering twelve product pages as **zero products, exit 0** — a published static site with nothing in it). **Two ways to say it now, and the first already worked**: `path:` beside `schema:`, for an app that READ `db/schema.lite` and appended to it — the string is parsed, the path is the anchor, and nothing anywhere said so, which is most of why this stayed open; and `resolveFrom: '<dir>'` (a directory or a `file:` URL) for a schema with no file behind it at all, which THROWS on an anchor that is not a directory, because a statement that quietly reverts to the CWD is the failure being closed. | <a id="fjs-458"></a>FJS-458 | junction — **Service autoload defaulted to `services/` beside the ENTRY, which is not where the layout the framework documents and scaffolds puts them, and the miss was silent** | 2026-08-26 | PROBED now, not derived: `./services` then `./src/services` beside the entry, first that exists, with `resolveServicesDir` the one owner of the list — so the flat layout, the canonical one, and an entry that has moved to `api/src/app.ts` all resolve with the app saying nothing. Nothing in it knows an app has an `api/` directory, and a cwd-relative candidate is deliberately absent: that is how a command run from the wrong place picks up somebody else's. **A DECLARED directory is not probed around** — an absent one is reported by name, loudly and everywhere, because the case it is most likely to be is `FJS-449`'s: a relative path resolved against the wrong cwd, landing on nothing and looking exactly like an app with no services. **The miss is on the boot banner** — `autoload=api/src/services`, or `autoload="none — probed api/services, api/src/services"` — as a field rather than a warning, because an app that registers its services by hand is doing nothing wrong and a framework that shouts at a correct app teaches everyone to stop reading. **`build:app`'s bundling guard was a fourth copy of the old default** and is the second defect this closes: it checked `<entry>/services`, so a canonical-layout app downgraded its ERROR to a warning and shipped a bundle that boots clean and 404s every route. | <a id="fjs-512"></a>FJS-512 | mesa — **A `{#if}` whose test read a `$:` derived rendered nothing, while an attribute reading the same derived stayed current** | 2026-08-26 | Neither the derived nor tracking: the blocks re-ran, and their content was REMOVED by a sibling in the same flush. A block took whatever node followed it in the template as its anchor, and the whitespace between two blocks is ONE DOM text node — so `{#if a}…{/if}` and `{#if b}…{/if}` on consecutive lines shared one anchor and each inserted its content before it. The second block's DOM then sat inside the first's `[marker, anchor)` range, so tearing the first branch down took it, and the block that had just built it had no reason to run again. Fixed in the compiler by the rule a COMPONENT invocation has followed since `FJS-110` — the block pushes a comment of its OWN — for `{#if}`, `{#each}`, `{#key}`, `{#await}`, `{@render}` and `{@html}`. **Which page shows it is an ordering accident, which is why it resisted isolation for a day**: a component-root template has its whitespace collapsed, so the same three `{#if}`s in a `<div>` passed at the root and failed inside an outer branch; and a kit `<Button>` in the branch decided it where a plain `<button>` did not, because the component's prop-push effect inverts the subscriber order and the new branch is built before the old one is removed. `test/block-anchor.test.js` is the pin — seven emission shapes and two DOM cases, three of which fail against the compiler as it was. Closes `FJS-468`, the same defect measured from the other end |

| <a id="fjs-536"></a>FJS-536 | litestone — **`$softDelete` answered on the root client alone, so the one flavour an application holds threw the unknown-property error at a question about the schema — and three proxy traps disagreed about every capability** | 2026-08-26 | *A capability that depends only on the schema belongs on every flavour of client* is a stated rule and this one was on one of four: junction scopes `ctx.locals.db` with `$setAuth`, so the client an app actually holds threw `"$softDelete" is not a table in this schema` rather than answering. Found by [FJS-523](#fjs-523) reaching for it. Now on all four through one owner, and it answers a **copy** — the live map is what every read filters against, so handing it out let a caller turn soft delete off for the whole client by assigning to a property they had asked to READ. **The second half is bigger than the first**: `ownKeys` listed the capabilities, `has` denied them and `getOwnPropertyDescriptor` described none of them, so `'$checkWhere' in db` was **false on every flavour for every capability** — and `in` is the documented way to feature-detect one, precisely because a Litestone client throws on an unknown property. Each proxy's capability list is hoisted so `ownKeys` and `has` read one array rather than two, which is what let them disagree. **Not the first of its kind, which is the argument for the rule**: junction's `autoFilter` carries a `try`/`catch` around reading `$checkWhere` and a comment saying why — when that one was missing from the scoped proxies it took down every list read in both apps with a message about a table nobody had named.
