| <a id="fjs-523"></a>FJS-523 | junction — **A soft-deleted row's `@unique` value could not be released through a service, and the `remove` half refused by 404ing about a row that is plainly there** | 2026-08-26 | Two halves, closed a day apart. **`update`/`patch` is the one that closed the hole**: `SoftDeletedUniqueError` is a 409 telling a caller their value is held by a row they cannot see, litestone's documented way out is to move the value aside (`update({ …, withDeleted: true })`), and no write path passed the directive — so there was no request an app could make that freed the reference. `writeWhere(q)` is the one place the filter lifts, on both the litestone path and junction's `softDelete:` override; a bulk patch deliberately does not, since `bulkByRow` selects through litestone's own filter and a widened WHERE alone would match nothing. **`remove` is the half that stayed a decision, and the answer is that it declines** — against an already-deleted row the only action left is to destroy it, the one write that defeats `@@softDelete`, and a directive on the ordinary DELETE would hand that to every caller who may remove a row, with no separate permission to grade and no way back. What was wrong was not the refusal but the SENTENCE: a 404 naming the row reads as the row being gone rather than as the flag being declined, so it is a 400 naming `$withDeleted` and pointing at the patch. Graded on the REQUEST and never on the row's state — the same call must not succeed or refuse depending on data the caller cannot see — and silent on a model that hides nothing, where remove is already the hard delete the directive asks about. **The model's own `@@softDelete` is ASKED, not derived**: `db.$softDelete` through `modelSoftDeletes`, which is what turned up [FJS-536](#fjs-536). 11 tests against a real Litestone client, the no-soft-delete model as the negative control | 
| <a id="fjs-124"></a>FJS-124 | basecamp — **A flag's rollout percentage was stored, returned, and applied by nothing, so 10% behaved as on-or-off** | 2026-08-26 | Not a missing branch so much as a missing AGREEMENT: the decision has to be made where the user is, per request, in whatever language the SDK is written in, so what was owed was a rule the server and every SDK land on identically. **MurmurHash3 x86 32-bit over `<flagKey>:<unitId>`, mod 100**, published beside `resolveIn()` and executed by it. Every part is load-bearing and stated as such: murmur3 because it is synchronous, dependency-free and has a stock implementation in every language (a crypto hash is reproducible too, but WebCrypto is async and a flag check sits in a render path); the **flag key inside the hash**, so two flags at 10% do not select the same tenth — hashing the unit alone gives every flag one cohort and a staged rollout tests the same unlucky group forever; **mod 100**, matching `rollout Int @gte(0) @lte(100)`, because a finer bucket is a number the column cannot hold. `variantFor` is the same rule salted apart (`:variant`), or everyone at the front of a rollout lands in the first arm of the experiment they are there to test. `resolve` takes an optional `unitId` and decides for it — absent, the answer is the configuration exactly as before, and this service never invents one. **`rollout` also stopped defaulting to 0**: `isEnabled` is the switch and this narrows it, so at `@default(0)` enabling a flag did nothing for anybody the moment an SDK started bucketing, with the screen showing it on. 100 on the flag, on the override and in `setOverride`'s coercion; rolling out to nobody is `isEnabled: false`, which is the state that already says it. **The canonical vectors are asserted first**, because they are what makes *use a stock murmur3* a sufficient instruction; then the three properties a rollout is USED for and none of which follows from the hash being right — even spread, independence between flags, and that raising 10 to 20 never takes the feature off anyone. 22 tests, no fixture | 
| <a id="fjs-532"></a>FJS-532 | junction · basecamp — **`App.db` is typed `unknown`, so the largest app in the repo minted a second runtime name for the same object — and the alias won 29 uses to 3.** `createApp({ db })` puts the client on `app.db` and declares it `db?: unknown` ([app.ts:158](packages/junction/src/core/app.ts)), because it accepts a Litestone client *or any plain table-shaped object*. An app cannot narrow that: Invariant 5 rules that a property is typed by augmenting an exported interface and **never by redeclaring it**, since declaration merging requires identical types and a redeclaration silently loses. So basecamp claimed a second name for the identical object — `app.claim('data', db)`, typed `data: BasecampDb` on `BasecampApp` — and says so at the declaration: *`app.db` is Junction's own and is the Litestone client this app passed to createApp — nothing here reads it, and nothing should: `data` is the name for that.* Measured: `app.data === app.db` is **true**, and basecamp reads `app.data` 29 times against `app.db` three. **Junction already solved this three times and `db` is the one that missed it.** `AppConduit`, `AppJobs` and `AppNotify` are each an **empty interface a package augments**, carrying the note *empty here on purpose: Junction must not depend on @frontierjs/conduit … augment it, don't redeclare `App.conduit`* — which is the same problem (junction cannot know the type) with the opposite answer (make it augmentable rather than `unknown`). The fix is `db?: AppDb` over `export interface AppDb {}`: assignment stays permissive, because an empty interface admits any object, and a reader gets whatever the app merged in. The augmenter here is the APP rather than a package, which the mechanism allows unchanged. **Two things follow from it.** basecamp's `app.claim('data', db)` and all 29 reads collapse onto `app.db`, and eleven of those sites are the ones `FJS-519` part 2 has to move onto `ctx.locals.db` anyway — so the two are one pass over the same files rather than two. And `service-as-system`'s findings stop naming an app-specific alias. Not a defect in behaviour: it is two names for one owner, which is the shape Invariant 5 exists to prevent, reached by obeying its letter. **Fixed 2026-08-26.** Junction exports `interface AppDb {}` beside its three siblings and `App.db` is typed by it; the OPTION stays `unknown` and the field is `AppDb`, with one cast where the two meet — permissive in, typed out — because narrowing the option too would stop an app that augments from passing the plain table-shaped object `createBaseService` adapts. basecamp augments it (`interface AppDb extends BasecampDb {}`), drops `app.claim('data', db)` and moves all 25 reads onto `app.db`. The one failure it caused is the one worth recording: `restore-schedules.test.ts` hands a HAND-BUILT app object, so its stub still carried `data:` and four tests read `undefined.asSystem` — a fake client hiding behind a rename. junction 1510 pass (the one red is `FJS-516`), basecamp 182 pass, typecheck baseline unmoved at 14. `service-as-system` now reports `app.db`, and the fourteen allowances still match | 2026-08-26 | [app.ts](packages/junction/src/core/app.ts) · [basecamp.types.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/basecamp.types.ts) · [basecamp app.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/app.ts) · [FJS-519](#fjs-519) |
| <a id="fjs-534"></a>FJS-534 | litestone — **The CHECK family was half-built: the FIELD-level `@check` worked and its refusal reached a caller as a 500, and the MODEL-level `@@check` did not exist at all.** **Fixed 2026-08-26, both halves.** The refusal was the sharper one: `@check("qty > 0")` parsed, reached the DDL verbatim and SQLite enforced it, and the throw was a bare `Error` with no status carrying SQLite's own sentence — so a **validation** problem answered **500** and `toFieldErrors` had nothing to hang on a control. Same class `FJS-441` closed for uniqueness. It is now a `ValidationError` rather than a class of its own, because the way out is the way out of every other validation failure — send a different value — and two classes exist where two RECOVERIES do. Measured through junction's boundary: **400 `BadRequest`, `data` carrying `[{path:["qty"],message:"must be at least one"}]`**. **`@@check` now exists**, repeatable like `@@unique`, emitted as a table CHECK, which is what gives a row invariant spanning two columns somewhere to live other than a service hook a job, a migration, `asSystem()` and a seed all bypass. **Both take an optional message as the last argument**, where every field validator already carries one; without it the person gets `is not valid` and the expression goes to the developer on `err.constraint`, because SQL under a form control reaches somebody who did not write it. A field check marks its own box; a `@@check` names no column, so it is a record-level error with an empty path — which also fixed `ValidationError` rendering a bare `: ` in its summary for one. **Three things came with it and each was measured, not assumed.** The migrator already compared CHECK text and rebuilt, so migration needed nothing (`FJS-466` had done it). A new `@@check` is a **contract** for `fli release:check` and a removed one an expand — it is in the release surface, the committed snapshot and the classifier, since it is the one constraint that changes what a WRITE may be without changing what a read answers. And the catalog refused the new attribute until it had a row and a documented page, which is `docs/schema.md` § Constraints the database enforces — where field `@check`'s old *nothing this package adds to say* is retired, since the package now adds a message and a translated refusal | 2026-08-26 | [ddl.js](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) · [release.js](packages/litestone/src/release.js) · [schema.md](packages/litestone/docs/schema.md) · [FJS-441](#fjs-441) |
| <a id="fjs-526"></a>FJS-526 | sierra · ui — **An edit form sends the server's own columns straight back, so `<Form record={row}>` is a 403 about a column that is not on the screen.** `@system`, `@generated`, `@computed`, `@from`, `@version` and a tenancy stamp all reach the browser as `readOnly`, and two things already read that flag: a generated form does not offer the control, and `make()` does not seed the value. Neither covers an EDIT form, which is handed a row the SERVER wrote — carrying every column the caller could READ — and writes the whole record back. The Data boundary then refuses `@system` **by name**, deliberately, because a payload naming one is code that meant to write it ([FJS-D28](DECISIONS.md)). So the refusal is correct at every layer and the person is shown *`Customer.userId` is @system…* under a form whose every visible control they filled in legitimately. **Found on `example`'s customers screen the first time it had an edit form at all**: `Customer.userId` is `@system`, and every save failed. Not reachable from the create path, which is why nothing here had seen it — a create starts from `make()`, and `make()` skips exactly these columns. **Fixed 2026-08-26**: `stripReadOnly(fields, data, { keep })` in sierra's `field-rules.js` — the leaf module, so it runs in plain node — called first in `_call`'s create/patch pipeline, ahead of coerce/blank/validate, so nothing downstream judges a value that is not going to be sent. It takes a **keep list rather than dropping every read-only key**, because the `@version` column is `readOnly` and is the one the server REQUIRES back: that is not an exception to the rule so much as the reason the rule cannot be spelled *delete every readOnly key*. A key with no rule behind it is left alone — a `@transient` or a custom method's own argument is legitimate, and guessing about those is how a strip becomes the thing that breaks a working app. 13 tests, three of them through `createResource().save()` rather than the pure function, because a refactor dropping the call would leave the unit tests green and put the 403 straight back. | <a id="fjs-527"></a>FJS-527 | example — **`bun run reset` reseeded the shop and left the job queue, so a dead row held its `unique` key against every later run.** The queue is a separate SQLite file (`db/jobs.db`) and the reset deleted only `db/shops`, `db/shops-registry.db` and `db/audit`. A `book-courier` row enqueued by an earlier run therefore survived the reseed that deleted the staff member it recorded, and retried forever with `app.runAs(…) — no such principal`. Caravan's `unique` is a lock on work that is still owed and a `pending` row still holds it, so `book-courier:5` was held against every future `ship` of order 5 and **the dispatch was a silent no-op**. **What it reads as is the misdirection worth filing**: `verify:jobs` fails `job.wroteTracking` while `job.record` PASSES, because the row the drive finds is the previous run's, done and correct — so the evidence says *the job ran and did not write*, which is a framework bug that is not there. `verify:notify`'s `announce-payment` went the same way, and a full sweep showed the two as flakes because each was green the first time after a manual delete. Caravan is right and the reset was wrong: work that is still owed should hold its key across a backoff. **Fixed 2026-08-26** — `reset` deletes `db/jobs.db*` too, and `PROJECT_STATE.md` § Working notes says why, since the symptom points at the wrong package | 2026-08-26 | [example/package.json](example/package.json) · [PROJECT_STATE.md](example/PROJECT_STATE.md) · [verify-jobs.mjs](example/web/test/verify-jobs.mjs) |
| <a id="fjs-516"></a>FJS-516 | junction — **A heartbeat test reads a connection COUNT and fails only under the suite's own load, so junction's suite is one red test whenever it runs whole.** `tests/heartbeat.test.ts` § *holds a connection that answers the ping, past several timeouts* asserts `app.channels.stats().connections` is 1 and finds **2** — a socket from an earlier case in the same file still counted. It passes in isolation (3 for 3), passes paired with any single other file, and fails on every full run: 1418 pass, 1 fail, repeatably. Found alongside `FJS-460` and **not caused by it** — the same failure, the same count, with the shutdown fix's own test file removed, and the mechanism is intra-file where `app.stop()` is called once in `afterAll`. **The obvious repair makes it worse**: the two cases below it close and then `await sleep(50)`, the first does not, and adding the same settle takes the file from one failure to two — so the ordering is doing something other than what it looks like, and a settle is not the answer. What the assertion actually wants is *this connection is still held*, which `c.closed` on the line above already says; the count is a second, weaker claim about global state that no test owning one connection can make safely. **Fixed 2026-08-26, and it turned out not to be a decision about the guarantee.** Both halves are asserted already and by the right cases: *evicts a connection that answers nothing* checks `connections === 0`, which is what eviction MEANS and is a claim about the socket under test; and *holds a connection* checks `c.closed` on the line above, which is what holding means. The count beside it was a third thing — **no other test's socket exists** — which a case owning one connection cannot know, and which would be wrong in principle even on a run where it passed. Narrowed to `toBeGreaterThan(0)`: the connection is counted, without claiming to be the only one. **An allowance was the alternative and is the wrong instrument** — `knownTestFailures` is for a deterministic known failure, and entering a flaky one there makes the phase permanently blind to the assertion instead of loud about it. `bun run test` is **1513 pass, 0 fail across three consecutive full runs**, where it had been 1 fail on every one | 2026-08-26 | [tests/heartbeat.test.ts](packages/junction/tests/heartbeat.test.ts) · [channels.ts](packages/junction/src/transport/channels.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-531"></a>FJS-531 | litestone — **the injectable clock moved what a policy READ and not what the database WROTE, and the obvious fix reintroduced a closed bug** | 2026-08-26 | `createClient({ now })` reached `now()` in a row policy and `@@softDelete`’s stamp, and nothing else. `@default(now())` was a column DEFAULT, `@updatedAt` an AFTER UPDATE trigger and the retention cutoff a bare `Date.now()` — so an env frozen at 2020 stamped a fresh `createdAt` with today, and the one thing a frozen clock exists for, **a row aging past a window**, could not be staged at all. **The obvious fix was measured and it was wrong.** Binding the value in JS while the trigger stayed looked free — both SQL mechanisms stand down when the caller names the column — but the trigger’s guard is `WHEN NEW.x IS OLD.x`, which is *fire whenever the bound value equals the stored one*, and under a frozen clock that is every write after the create. Measured on `update`, `updateMany` and `upsert`: the row came back stamped 2020 while the database held today. **That is `FJS-396`’s shape** — RETURNING is evaluated before an AFTER trigger, and junction hands that row to the HTTP response AND the `svc updated` broadcast — so naming the column had only ever closed it *while the two values differed*. It applies to the conservative variant too, and harder: that one only ever runs with a clock injected, which is exactly the case where they are equal. **So the trigger is retired and `FJS-396` closes at the root rather than narrowing.** Three stamping mechanisms became one, all reading the client’s clock — `@default(now())` and `@updatedAt`-on-create through `buildGeneratedDefaultMap` (key PRESENCE decides, so an explicit `null` still clears), `@updatedAt`-on-update through `stampSets`. `isUpdatedAtField` is the one answer to *is this a stamp column*, because the trigger earned itself off the column NAME as well as the attribute and binding to the attribute alone leaves `updatedAt DateTime` unstamped. **The migration is one generated statement**: pristine stops carrying the trigger, `droppedTriggers` in `migrate.js` already walks for exactly that, `DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS` with no table rebuild — verified against a live database. **The price is named and asserted**: the column DEFAULT stays so a raw INSERT still stamps, and a raw UPDATE no longer does — `@updatedAt` is a client stamp and a hand-written statement owns its own. Two things measurement cleared on the way: SQLite already fixes the time per statement, so a bulk write tied before and ties now; and `RETURNING` is not what suppresses a trigger — it runs under `.get()` and `.all()` alike, which was a wrong theory held for one probe. **Retention is the same fix on the read side** (`nowMs`, one reading for both the SQLite and jsonl passes), and it is what makes `advance('100d') → $retain()` a test anybody can write. What no option reaches is stated rather than left open: a raw statement, and a `@derived` expression reading `now()`, which is compiled once at startup into a subquery with no parameter to bind. litestone green; 5 cases in `test/test-clock.test.ts` and four suites that asserted the trigger now assert its absence. | <a id="fjs-522"></a>FJS-522 | litestone · sierra — **`@time` was enforced at the write and invisible above it, and the obvious fix was the wrong one** | 2026-08-26 | The emitter had no `case 'time'` at all, so a `String @time` column reached a form as a bare string, got a plain text box, and every value a person typed was accepted in the browser and refused at the write. **The obvious fix would have been worse than the silence.** JSON Schema’s `format: 'time'` means RFC 3339 *full-time*, which requires seconds **and** an offset; `@time` requires neither and admits no offset at all — so a consumer honouring it would refuse `09:30`, a value the boundary accepts, and the two boundaries would disagree about what a time IS rather than one of them merely saying nothing. It emits a **`pattern`** instead, and the pattern is the validator’s own regex exported and imported rather than restated, so there is one definition and the suite runs thirteen values through both sides and compares. `x-time: { seconds }` rides beside it for the control alone. **The control is `<input type="time">` and the seconds box is a `step`** — the element shows HH:MM unless the step is not a whole number of minutes, so without it a column that ACCEPTS seconds gives nobody a way to type them; `Input` already forwards `type` and `step`, so no kit change. Two things fell out: `MESSAGE_KEYWORDS` had claimed `time: ['format']` for as long as the emitter produced no format, so an author’s `@time(message:)` was keyed to a keyword no consumer could ever check; and the seconds variant was two alternative regexes where one optional group says the same thing — the flag WIDENS what may be stored and does not make a finer value mandatory. **Still nothing in this repo declares one**: `example` and `basecamp` hold 141 `DateTime` columns and zero `@time`, `@date` or `@datetime`, so this is covered by unit tests on both sides of the wire and by no drive. 19 cases in `test/time-column.test.ts`, 3 in sierra’s `form-fields`; litestone 3136 pass, sierra 1144 pass. | <a id="fjs-521"></a>FJS-521 | litestone — **retention swept the wrong table, and swept it once** | 2026-08-26 | Filed as *runs at boot and never again*, which turned out to be the least of three. **The sweep named the MODEL where the table is snake_case**: `DELETE FROM "AuditEvent"` matches nothing — the table is `audit_event` — and the throw landed in a `catch` commented *table may not exist yet on first run*, a plausible reason for a real failure and the reason nobody looked again. Every model whose name is not a case-variant of its table kept every row for ever: any multi-word name, and every name at all under `pluralize`. **Measured: `Log` deleted and `AuditEvent` not, in the same pass, silently** — a single-word test passes either way, because SQLite matches identifiers case-insensitively. A missing table is now ASKED about rather than inferred from a throw, so the legitimate first-run case stays quiet and a real failure is reported; those had been one branch. **Startup is not a schedule** — `db.asSystem().$retain()` is the same pass on demand, answering `{ model, table, removed }` per table plus `error`. Scheduling is the app's, which is two rulings rather than a gap (`FJS-D36` gives the clock to the queue, Invariant 1 stops litestone importing it); `example` and `basecamp` each have a `retention.job.ts` now, and the declaration had been in both schemas with the sweep never running twice in either. **`asSystem()` only**, on `FJS-D52`'s ground — a DELETE against the base table through no gate, no row policy and no `@@softDelete` — with the other three client flavours refusing by name. The jsonl half is covered too; its failure was `catch { /* non-fatal */ }`. `index.d.ts` declared `runSqliteRetention(db, retention)` against a three-argument implementation, so a caller reaching for the export wrote a call that swept nothing. **The rolling cutoff is deliberately NOT fixed**: the duration back from the moment the pass runs, `d` a flat 24 hours, no calendar and no zone. A calendar-aligned window needs a zone the seed cannot state yet (`FJS-D143`), and half a vocabulary invented here is what that record warns against — stated in the docs and asserted in the suite instead. 9 cases in `test/retention.test.ts`; litestone 3074 pass, basecamp 182 pass, typecheck clean. **Driven end to end since 2026-08-26**: `example`'s `verify:jobs` asserts the schedule's own expression and then runs the pass on demand against a planted row older than the window, because a schedule that fires and sweeps nothing is the silence the whole item is about — the drive's first cut measured the FIRST-LINE pre-check instead and reported a job that removed nothing. | <a id="fjs-524"></a>FJS-524 | litestone — **a suite could freeze the clock and not move it, and nothing said the option existed** | 2026-08-26 | `createTestEnv` spreads its unknown options into `createClient`, so `now` already reached both the env client and `atLevel`'s — undeclared in `TestEnvOptions`, absent from the docs, and asserted nowhere. **What was genuinely missing is MOVING it**, which is the whole reason to have it: every bug worth a test here is a crossing — a window that opens, a retention that expires — and a frozen instant asserts one side of one. **`env.clock` is the movable form**: `now` now takes a `Date`, an ISO string or a function, and the option is normalised into a holder every client this env opens reads through — `set(at)`, `advance('20d')` or milliseconds, `frozen`. **A function stays the caller's** and `set`/`advance` refuse it by name, because two things claiming to say what time it is means the loser is whichever one the reader did not have in mind. `advance` from the wall clock also freezes, since an offset from a moving clock is still moving and the assertion after it is a race. Durations go through `parseDuration`, the one owner, which grew a label so a clock is not told about an *invalid retention duration*. **The case that would have been easy to miss is pinned**: `atLevel()` builds its client lazily, so one built mid-suite must follow a later move rather than freezing at whatever the clock said when it was constructed. **And the hole is asserted rather than discovered** — the clock moves `now()` in a policy and `@@softDelete`'s stamp, and does NOT move `@default(now())` or `@updatedAt`, which SQLite stamps; the option's own comment and `docs/access-control.md` both claimed otherwise and are corrected. `FJS-531` carries the fork. 12 cases in `test/test-clock.test.ts`; litestone green, typecheck clean. | <a id="fjs-530"></a>FJS-530 | **`verify:notify` passed once per seed and then failed with a 500 that named nothing** | 2026-08-26 | Three defects stacked, and each one hid the next. The drive used a fixed `ORD-NOTIFY-1`, cleaned up over HTTP — which SOFT-deletes — and `Order` is `@@softDelete`, so the deleted row kept its `@unique` reference and the next run's create was refused 409. The drive does not check the create, so `orderId` was `undefined`, and `POST /orders/undefined` with `x-service-method: pay` answered **500** — a status that says nothing about a reference collision three steps upstream. Every rerun failed and the only cure was a reseed, which is why it was never noticed: `db:seed` is in the drive's documented preamble. **Fixed as `verify:money` already does it** — a reference minted per run — and the fix needed two more facts the first attempt got wrong: `reference` carries `@upper`, so a lowercase run id is stored uppercased and the sink search for that subject silently misses; and it is `@length(3, 20)`, so `ORD-NOTIFY-` plus a run id overruns and answers 400 from the same unchecked create. Now `ORD-N<6>`, uppercase. **Proved by running the drive twice back to back with no reseed**, which had never passed before. The class is general and worth remembering: a drive that cleans up through the API cannot free a `@unique` on a soft-deleting model, so a fixed fixture key makes it single-use · [example/web/test/verify-notify.mjs](example/web/test/verify-notify.mjs) · CLAUDE.md § Live hazards, *a soft-deleted row keeps its `@unique` values* |
| <a id="fjs-506"></a>FJS-506 | basecamp · litestone — **A state machine could not say which of its moves a person makes and which the engine makes** | 2026-08-26 | Ruled in two halves. `FJS-506` itself settled what `@gate(8)` means on a move — `asSystem()` and nothing else, `levelPasses()` as the one owner of the comparison, a `@gate` inside the enum's shared block refused by name — and drew the filter *every move not at 8 is one a person can make*. **Adopting it in basecamp found the counterexample straight away**: a move can be the engine's DECISION and a person's REQUEST at once. `Server.report*` is reached by pressing *Sync from provider*; basecamp asks the provider and the answer picks the move, on the caller's scoped client. At `@gate(8)` every one refuses, and the only way past is `asSystem()`, which drops the gate, the row policies and the audit actor to make one move. So [FJS-D150](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d150) adds `@system` on a move — the statement `@system` already makes about a column, with the column's own hatch: `transition(id, name, { system: true })` becomes `system: [field]` on the update underneath, so writing the column directly is refused and permitted by one rule. The two compose (`@system @gate(5)` is the person-requested engine move) and contradict at 8 and 9, which is refused at parse. `TransitionSystemError` (403) is its own class beside the gate's, because no caller at any level can answer it; `transitions()` reports `refusedBy: 'system'` and asks it first; `x-transitions` carries the flag, making it the one verdict a browser can be certain of. **Adopted**: `Server`'s five engine moves, and `checkIn`'s hand-kept `CHECK_IN_FROM` set is gone — it was declared `@gate(8)` and the machine had never run, so the service kept its own copy of the from-set under a comment saying the two had to agree. `Deployment.build`/`succeed`/`fail` stay open on [FJS-517](#fjs-517), which is a product question about whether that pipeline exists at all · [schema.md](packages/litestone/docs/schema.md) · [parser.js](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js) · [client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [field-rules.js](packages/sierra/src/junction/field-rules.js) |
| <a id="fjs-095"></a>FJS-095 | basecamp — **`ApiKey.mesa` turned validation off because a column the SERVER writes was in create-mode `required`** | 2026-08-26 | `tokenHint` and `credentialId` are `@system` now ([FJS-D22](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d22)) — derived from a token that does not exist until auth mints it, so a caller has nothing to send. `tokenHint` was the one costing something: NOT NULL, therefore in create-mode `required`, so a browser validating against the schema refused every create by naming a field the form has no box for — *the button does nothing*. Both are out of `required` and `readOnly` on the client, and the app fills them by naming the columns on the write (`create({ data, system: [...] })`), which keeps the gate, the row policies and the audit actor where `asSystem()` drops all three. **`validate: false` stays, for ONE column and a stated reason**: `userId` is genuinely caller-supplied — a key may be issued to a bot, which is what `assertBotOwner` is about — so it cannot be `@system`, and it is required while the create form offers no owner control. The way out is a control, not another annotation, and the resource says so instead of citing a shipped ruling as unbuilt. | <a id="fjs-376"></a>FJS-376 | basecamp — **Outpost replay protection was per PROCESS, so a second replica did not share it and a restart forgot** | 2026-08-26 | `model OutpostNonce` — the app's own database, which is where the outbox and the job queue already put what must survive a process. **`nonce` is the PRIMARY KEY and that is the mechanism**: the claim is the INSERT, because asking whether the row exists and then writing it is a race between exactly the two replicas this exists for — both would find it absent. A duplicate key is refused by SQLite atomically and that refusal IS the replay; anything other than a `UniqueConflictError` propagates, since reporting a broken database as *replayed* would hide it behind a 401 that reads as the caller's problem. `@@gate("8")`, `@@tenant(none)` (a machine's spent credential belongs to no workspace and the endpoints spending one carry no session), no `@@log(audit)` — one row per signed request would bury the human trail. | <a id="fjs-460"></a>FJS-460 | junction — **`app.stop()` waited the whole drain timeout and then reported success with the client's socket still open** | 2026-08-26 | `stop()` raced Bun's graceful `server.stop()` against `drainTimeout` and closed nothing. **Bun's graceful stop never resolves once a WebSocket has been upgraded** — measured against a bare `Bun.serve`, not inferred: not after the socket is closed, not after the server's own close handler has run and the client reads `readyState 3`. So the race always ended on the timer: every shutdown that had ever held a client cost the full five seconds, and then logged *Shutdown complete* with that client still at `readyState 1`, told nothing. A suite whose `afterAll` is that call outlived bun's 5s hook limit **four runs in five**, which is how it arrived — `tests/query-parity.test.ts` was the first test in the repo to open a socket it did not close. An app meets the same thing as a container that will not stop and is SIGKILLed. **The waits are junction's own now and neither is Bun's promise**: the transport keeps the set of sockets it has open — at the TRANSPORT, because a socket belongs to it and not to the channels plugin, so an app's own `app.http.ws(...)` route is covered too — says goodbye to each, waits for the close handshakes and any in-flight request bounded by `drainTimeout`, then stops by force. 5002ms → **2ms**, with the client reading a clean close. **The close code is 1012 Service Restart and not 1001 Going Away**, which is the code that means this: sent as 1001 it reaches the peer as **1000**, while 1012 and a private 4001 arrive unchanged — so the one code for *the server is going away* is the one Bun does not deliver. Pinned, so a Bun release that fixes it says so. One claim in the report did not survive contact: the port was released promptly even before, because a graceful stop stops ACCEPTING at once and only its promise hangs. 7 tests in `tests/shutdown.test.ts`, timings included, since a test that only asserted `stop()` resolves passed before the fix as well. Green: junction 1418 (`FJS-516` is the one red and predates this) |
| <a id="fjs-518"></a>FJS-518 | junction · sierra · jetty — **A record read with `get(id)` is a dead object, so every detail screen in the repo goes stale the moment somebody else writes the row.** The live path is the LIST path and only the list path: `client.resource()` opens the socket, wires `created`/`patched`/`removed` into a `Store`, and decides membership and position per event. A detail read goes through the raw proxy — `product = await products.service.get(id)` ([products/\[id\].mesa](example/web/src/routes/products/[id].mesa)), `entry = await portal.service.get(id)` ([ServiceHealthBody.mesa](packages/basecamp/web/src/components/widgets/ServiceHealthBody.mesa)) — and lands in a plain variable no announcement can reach. The framework's headline guarantee is that you never invalidate, because the server says what changed; on a detail screen that guarantee is simply false, and it is false in the silent-wrong-data class — the page renders, the numbers are stale, nothing throws. **Two rows of one model can also disagree with each other**: the store is per-`resource()` call (`const store = new Store<T>()` inside `resource()`), so two `createResource('orders')` in two route files are two copies, kept in step only because both subscribe to the same service. Nothing keys a row to an identity, which is also why nothing can mark a value provisional or put one back — the reason no optimistic update exists anywhere in the repo. **Ruled 2026-08-25 rather than patched**: a node per row keyed by model, a list as a view over it, a detail as a view of one, a TTL for lifetime, and an overlay carrying intent — [FJS-D138](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d138), which carries the build order. jetty's copy of the store is the same defect one package over ([FJS-493](#fjs-493)) and is fixed by importing rather than by a second repair. **Fixed 2026-08-25**, as `FJS-D138` sequenced it. `packages/junction/src/client/nodes.ts` is the registry — one node per row, keyed by MODEL and by a normalised id, because a list holds `{ id: 5 }` and a detail screen is reached by a URL carrying `'5'`, and two nodes there means the push moves exactly one of them. `Store` is a VIEW now: bound it holds ids and materialises through the registry, so `store.get()` still answers rows and **no screen changed** — `useStore` is the one bridge to a Mesa signal and nothing in either app calls `subscribe` or `get` directly. Unbound it is what it always was, which is what the fifteen `Store` cases in `client.test.ts` are the negative control for. Lifetime is a **TTL** (`nodeTtlMs`, default 30s) rather than a reference count the application maintains, which is the part of Apollo and Relay everyone complains about. `resource.record(id)` is the view of one; Sierra's wraps it so the first read goes through the resource's own `_call('get')` — its hooks, its coercion, its `@version` — while Junction keeps the rule about WHEN to read. **A push moves the value and does not move the remembered version**, which is `FJS-341` restated as a test: the node is the synced truth and the view is what this screen READ. `example`'s order detail is the first caller and its two manual `await load()` after an action are gone — that reload was the app hiding this. **Proven in a browser**: `verify:live` gained a third question (17 assertions, up from 14) — a watcher tab on the detail page, the order paid from node over HTTP on a session of its own, the status moving with `sameDocument: true`. Negative-controlled: reverted to `service.get(id)` it reads `{"status":"pending","moved":false}`. Two things fell out on the way: `remove` drops `$withDeleted`, so the drive could not clean up after itself and had only ever passed on a fresh seed ([FJS-523](#fjs-523)); and jetty's copy is untouched by design — it gets this by importing rather than by a second repair ([FJS-493](#fjs-493)). | <a id="fjs-525"></a>FJS-525 | caravan — **a fixed-time schedule fired zero times in spring and twice in autumn** | 2026-08-25 | The firing path asked *does the current minute match*, which on the two days a year when the local clock is not a function of real time gave two wrong answers. Measured on `America/New_York` before the fix: `30 2 * * *` fired **0** times on 2026-03-08 because 02:30 never occurred, and `30 1 * * *` fired **2** times on 2026-11-01 as two dispatch ids, since `occurrenceKey('cron', name, minute)` was built from the epoch minute and the two 01:30s are different epoch minutes. **Fixed by walking the local clock instead of sampling it** (`FJS-D144`, Vixie cron's rule): a fixed-time schedule keeps a mark — the last wall-clock minute it looked at — and each tick walks FORWARD from that mark to now, firing every minute the expression matches. Spring goes 01:59 → 03:00 so the skipped hour is still in the walk; autumn goes 01:59 → 01:00 so the walk is empty until it passes 01:59 again. Neither is special-cased. **The mark only ever moves forward and that is the second half** — the first version let it follow the clock down, which walked the repeated hour again and reproduced the original defect in new code; the test caught it. **The wildcard carve-out is a regression guard rather than a nicety**: `30 * * * *` already fired 25 times on the 25-hour day, which is correct, and compensating it would have removed one. A fixed-time fire is now named by the WALL CLOCK it belongs to, so two processes compute one dispatch id and the autumn duplicate collapses through `FJS-294`'s machinery; `index.ts` needed no change. Free with the walk: a minute missed to a blocked event loop or a paused container is caught up, which the old sampling could not see. `CronScheduler` takes `now`, because this behaviour happens on two days a year and the suite's four green `timeZone` tests all asserted that a stated value had been stored — which is how the defect lived underneath them. `nextRuns()` stays the wall clock's own answer and is deliberately a day out on the spring boundary; two implementations of *when does this fire* would be worse. 18 new cases in `tests/cron-dst.test.ts`; caravan 188 pass, typecheck clean · [cron.ts](packages/caravan/src/cron.ts) · [tests/cron-dst.test.ts](packages/caravan/tests/cron-dst.test.ts) · [FJS-D144](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d144) · [FJS-294](#fjs-294) |
| <a id="fjs-432"></a>FJS-432 | basecamp — **`AuditEvent` was gated and unpolicied, so the trail was cross-workspace at the Data boundary** | 2026-08-25 | Ruled as [FJS-D141](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d141) and the ruling is most of the fix: the framework had already answered this in [FJS-D05](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d05) — *on a read a row holding no tenant belongs to nobody and stays invisible* — so the model needed a DELETED line rather than a hand-written policy. `@@tenant(none)` gone, the declared `tenancy { }` applies, and the trail is scoped where `db/access.snapshot.md` can show it instead of in one service's `where`. **The row asked whether null means *global*, and the database answered**: 19 null-workspace rows in the dev database, of which 6 were hub actions and **12 were `jobs.startRun`/`finishRun` losing their stamp** — `internalOnly()` methods are exempt from `sessionScope`, so `ctx.locals.workspaceId` was absent and a workspace's own `Job` runs filed under nobody, invisible to the only feed that wants them. Fixed first and separately, because sealing the meaning in over a mis-stamped column hides those rows permanently behind a correct-looking policy: the audit hook already re-reads the subject row for its diff, so the subject's own `workspaceId` answers second and a `Workspace` is its own workspace. Three tests, each red without its half. **It also found a defect in litestone's own harness** — the factory does not skip an `auth()` default, which is right on a REQUIRED foreign key (`_freshParents` overwrites it with a real parent) and wrong on a nullable one, where nothing seeds a parent and the generated string reached SQLite as `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed` at every level of the ladder. A fixture never invents a foreign key now. The two spellings of *null tenant* still disagree and that is [FJS-528](#fjs-528) · [schema.lite](packages/basecamp/db/schema.lite) · [hooks.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/core/hooks.ts) · [testing.js](packages/litestone/src/testing.js) |
| <a id="fjs-385"></a>FJS-385 | **A tenant can differ in its DATA and in nothing else — there is no per-tenant configuration** | 2026-08-25 | Ruled and built as [FJS-D126](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d126). `createApp({ tenantConfig, tenantConfigKeys })` — a resolver answering a plain object per tenant over `app.config` as the floor, read through `$.config` / `app.configFor(tenant)`. The Orthicon shape the row cites is the one it serves, and the row's own worry — *a config an app reads through `app.config` is resolved before any request exists* — is what the three-phase order answered: the READ moved to call scope first, under no feature and at no behavioural cost, and the source filled the interior afterwards. The resolve happens in the around hook that already establishes the tenant, because a property read cannot await. `tenantConfigKeys` is the safe half and is committed to `principal.snapshot.md` |
| <a id="fjs-d126"></a>FJS-D126 | **Does a TENANT carry configuration, and where does it resolve?** | 2026-08-25 | **Ruled: yes, in three clauses.** The READ is `$.config` / `app.configFor(tenant)` through one owner, shipped ahead of the source because the same boundary move under a live feature means finding every reader again. The view is **read-only, deep**, and `app.config` is never written — the clause the prior art decides, since Laravel, Django and NestJS each rebind or forbid and each pays for it, while junction has the ALS already. The SOURCE is `createApp({ tenantConfig })`, a resolver rather than a declaration on [FJS-D113](#fjs-d113)'s ground, memoised per tenant (the promise, not the value) with `invalidateTenantConfig` as the explicit way out and a failed resolve deliberately not memoised. **The resolve happens where the tenant is already resolved** — the around hook that sets `ctx.locals.tenantId`, and `runAs` — because a property read cannot await; a resolver that throws fails the call rather than quietly serving the floor. **`tenantConfigKeys` is required and `RESERVED_CONFIG_PATHS` is refused at boot** (`port`, `host`, `database`, `http`, `auth`, `apiPrefix`, `logging`): a database path handed to a tenant is every other tenant's rows, and the rest are read at boot with no tenant in scope. A key the resolver answers that the list does not name is refused by name, never dropped. Laravel's `storage_to_config_map` is the shape; what is added is that **the list is committed**, as § Per-tenant configuration in `principal.snapshot.md` ([FJS-514](#fjs-514)) — the half that makes the feature safe is the half worth diffing. 31 tests · [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d126) § API design · [FJS-385](#fjs-385) · [IDEAS/tenancy-exterior.md](IDEAS/tenancy-exterior.md) §6 |
| <a id="fjs-514"></a>FJS-514 | **The tenancy predicate is committed and its input is not, so a resolver emitting the WRONG tenant leaves every artefact byte-identical** | 2026-08-25 | `junction principal --app <module>` writes `principal.snapshot.md` at the app root, read off a BUILT app because a resolver is wired in application code and no file tree can answer which one an app ended up with. It names its own generator in its header, so the `snapshots` phase reruns it with no CI edit — verified by tampering with a row and watching the phase fail with the regeneration command. Seven sections: tenancy · resolver · claims · standing · what the rules reach · the three refusals · **per-tenant configuration, empty until [FJS-D126](#fjs-d126) is ruled**, which exists before the feature so *which keys a tenant may override* lands as a diff rather than a new face. `membershipClaim()` describes itself and the return type says so; `tenantFrom` is excluded for being a function and `namedBy` included for being a constant — **names, never values**. The value→level mapping is deliberately absent and named as such: `getLevel` is a function handed to `GatePlugin` and a client answers plugin names rather than instances, so the enum values are committed and the mapping stays `verifyGateLadder`'s. **Two things it got wrong before being run against a real app**, both saying something false rather than nothing: it asked `app.db` for the declaration, which under `createApp({ tenants })` does not exist, and reported *no tenancy* about an app that is nothing but; and it rendered a `bearer` resolver as a membership one with five blank cells and its claim as a standing. Committed for `example` (`strategy database`, bearer) and `packages/basecamp` (`strategy row`, membership — 17 scoped, 14 delegated, 14 exempt, the same split [FJS-513](#fjs-513) reaches independently). 11 tests, junction typecheck clean at its 0 baseline |
| <a id="fjs-513"></a>FJS-513 | **`verifyRowPolicies` reports a delegated model as `skipped`, so the deepest half of declared tenancy has no executed proof** | 2026-08-25 | `verifyTenantIsolation()`, the fifth executed check. It does not teach the grader to evaluate a `check()` — that is the oracle problem, two implementations of one rule grading each other — it **executes the crossing**: seed a row for tenant A, then have tenant B and a caller holding no claim try to reach it, on five moves. `read`/`update`/`delete` are B reaching into A; `create` is B placing a row inside A's tenant and `post-update` is B moving a row it owns into it, which are the two the generated `post-update` deny exists for and the two a column comparison does not cover. **Both sides asserted** — A must reach its own row or the result is `unreachable`, since a policy admitting nobody and a model nothing was seeded into are one observation — and **every model is named**, because a model that isolates correctly is silent and so is a model nothing ran against. Three answers that are not verdicts: `unscoped` (no column, no scoped parent, no exemption — the shape the desugar says nothing about), `unparented` ([FJS-382](#fjs-382)'s ruling, where an optional scoping relation lets a row belong to no tenant) and `exempt`. `strategy database` is reported by name: one client cannot hold two files. **Two defects in the build itself, both found by running it**: the seeder skipped optional scoped relations, so a delegated model was graded only in its degenerate unparented form; and the per-model restore rolled back past the two tenants, so the first model that passed took the fixture with it and the other 28 failed to seed. `_tenantStamp` is now shared with `verifyFieldProtection` rather than copied ([FJS-381](#fjs-381)). **basecamp: 31 graded (17 column, 14 delegated), 14 exempt, 18 uncheckable, no leak.** The fourteen had never been graded by anything. litestone 3022 pass, 0 fail. **Run by basecamp's own suite** (`db/test/schema.test.ts`, beside the other four) rather than by a one-off script — a checker nothing runs is the defect one level up, and the first close of this row had exactly that shape. The suite asserts COVERAGE as a set rather than a count, so it survives the schema growing and still fails a model that quietly drops out; proved to fire by appending an unscoped model to a copy of basecamp's real schema, which reports `unscoped` by name |
| <a id="fjs-515"></a>FJS-515 | toolbelt — **`glow` could not highlight three of the four languages this repo writes, and could not highlight a transcript at all** | 2026-08-25 | Found by moving `website/` off its own hand-rolled highlighter and onto the framework's. **`.lite`** went through the generic pass, where both halves of a schema came out wrong: the attribute rule can take one `@`, so it matched at the SECOND one and every `@@gate` in every schema rendered as a stray punctuation mark followed by an attribute; and a field's type was coloured only where the common keyword list happened to contain it case-insensitively, so `Int` and `String` were lit while `DateTime`, `Json` and a relation to another model were not — the column that says what a field IS, half-lit down the page. **SQL** had nothing at all: no keyword in the common list is SQL and `--` was not read as a comment, so a `CREATE TABLE` was one unlit line with its string literals coloured. **Shell** had the opposite problem — the common list contains `my`, `use`, `end`, `local`, `next`, `get` and `set`, all of them ordinary argument text, so `cd my-app` came out with `my` as a keyword and `-app` as punctuation after it: the directory name a reader is meant to type, in three pieces, on an install command. Shell is in the keyword-exclusion list now beside yaml/html/json, and what replaces it is the COMMAND word, which is the token a reader is looking for. **And a `language` may now be a LIST.** A transcript — a command and its output, a query beside the WHERE clause a policy appended to it — is one block and two languages, and given either alone half of it goes dark: under `js` a `--` comment is two punctuation marks, under `sql` a `//` comment is not a comment. The first entry stays primary and is what the wrapper's `language` attribute carries, so `@frontierjs/css`'s theme is unaffected. `mesa` was registered as a mixed-HTML language while there. Five tests, each asserting the text is unchanged as well as the marking. | <a id="fjs-511"></a>FJS-511 | litestone — **`@@transitions` could not name a Boolean column** | 2026-08-25 | `@@transitions(isPrimary, promote: false -> true, demote: true -> false @gate(5))` died in the tokeniser — *Expected IDENT, got 'false'* — so the two-state machine every schema has (`isPrimary`, `isPublished`, `isSuspended`) had no declaration, and its two directions, which are routinely different authorities, could only be guarded by one field `@allow` answering both with one predicate. Three changes: the state reader takes a boolean literal, the validator accepts any CLOSED type (an enum or Boolean — the refusal for `Int` now says *not a closed type* rather than *not an enum*), and **an unnamed boolean move is refused** because `-> true` names the value written and not what a person did. **Two normalisations were the actual work.** `checkTransitions` reads the raw column, where a boolean is 1/0, and is handed a write payload coerced the same way, while the declaration holds real booleans — so unnormalised, `0 === false` made a real move look like a no-op that silently succeeded, and `to === 1` matched no declared move so every legal move threw. And the compare-and-swap bound a raw `false`, which equals no stored value, so every move would have reported a conflict. Four tests. Green: litestone 3013 |
| <a id="fjs-502"></a>FJS-502 | cli — **a named move was declared in two places that never meet, and both directions failed quietly** | 2026-08-25 | `transition-methods`, the 28th `fli check` rule: it reads `@@transitions` out of the seed and the whole of `api/`, and reports a declared move nothing drives, plus a `transition()` naming a move the seed does not declare. **Warn rather than error, in both directions.** A move nobody drives is not a fault — the machine is still enforced — and an aspirational pipeline is a legitimate thing to have; failing a build on one would be wrong. The undeclared direction throws `TransitionNotFoundError` (400) at runtime and the message says so. **Reachable means EITHER spelling, and that is the whole of the accuracy**: `transition(id, 'cancel')` and `update({ data: { status: 'cancelled' } })` are the same move and litestone enforces both. The name-only version was written first and MEASURED — it reports eleven of basecamp's nineteen moves and is wrong about eight. Asking for either reports three, all real (`FJS-517`), and `example` clean. **The two spellings are not symmetrical**, which writing a test the wrong way round is what found: `transition()` resolves a move NAME, so the target-state spelling works for `update()` and throws here — unless the move is unnamed, since `pending -> paid` names itself after its target. The parse costs two things worth stating: the clause list splits on TOP-LEVEL commas only (a from-list is itself comma-separated, and splitting naively loses the name and reports the target), and the name is optional. The enum's own `transitions { }` spelling is deliberately not read — it needs field types to resolve, no schema here uses it, and a rule that misses beats one that misfires. Ten tests; `Lead` in the checks fixture carries a machine now, because a rule that only ever skips is what that file exists to catch. 896 pass · [core/checks.js](packages/cli/core/checks.js) · [tests/checks.test.js](packages/cli/tests/checks.test.js) · [FJS-517](#fjs-517) |
| <a id="fjs-495"></a>FJS-495 | litestone · sierra — **`transitions(row)` graded the gate and not the policies, so a UI drew a button the Data boundary refused** | 2026-08-25 | A move is an update, so an `@@allow('update', …)` refuses one exactly as a gate does — and `allowed` was `gate == null ? true : level >= gate`, consulting no policy at all. A caller holding every level and failing the policy saw a button that answered 403 the moment they pressed it; `FJS-494` fixed the press and this is the half before it. **Two questions and only one varies per move.** The `update` policy is about the row as it IS, so it is ONE evaluation for the whole call; `post-update` is about the row as it WOULD BE — the current row with that one column moved — so it is one per distinct TARGET, and both are memoised. That is the answer to the cost half the row raised: a machine's moves share few targets, and a model with no policies evaluates nothing. **`refusedBy` is new on every entry** (`'gate'` · `'policy'` · `null`), because a screen renders *you are not senior enough* and *not this record* differently and nothing else separates them — the gate is null for every ungated move a policy refuses. **`policyVerdict` is the one answer to *does this policy admit this row*** — `checkCreatePolicy` and `checkPostUpdatePolicy` were one function under two op names and this is a third caller that must NOT throw. Deny-before-allow, an empty `allows` meaning *no opinion* rather than *nobody*, and `asSystem()` skipping outright are three rules a second copy would get wrong. It deliberately does not catch: at the boundary an undecidable policy must refuse, and the affordance is where permissiveness belongs. **Sierra's mirror is unchanged and now says why** — `x-transitions` carries a gate and not a predicate, a browser has no policy engine, so that half answers `allowed: true` for a policy-refused move; its docblock claimed *mirrors litestone's `transitions(row)` field for field*, which had stopped being true. It carries `refusedBy` so the shapes stay one shape, and a test asserts it never claims `'policy'`. Six new litestone tests, one of them the assertion the row is actually about — what the list SAYS and what the write DOES for one caller on one row, since a test of either half alone passes with the two disagreeing. | <a id="fjs-507"></a>FJS-507 | basecamp — **`Server.status` was a complete state machine hand-rolled across four places, non-atomically** | 2026-08-25 | `@@transitions(status, …)` on `model Server`, eight moves in three tiers. **The bug it closes is the read-then-write**: `getScoped()` read the row and `db().server.update()` wrote it in a second statement, so two concurrent drains both read `online`, both passed the from-check and both wrote — a lost update with no error, and a server recorded online while it was draining. The declaration narrows the UPDATE's own WHERE to the from-state, so exactly one row matches and the loser gets a retryable `TransitionConflictError`. Proved by two drains in flight, which is the assertion no sequential test can make. **The gate replaced three role hooks rather than joining them**: `drain`/`undrain` are `@gate(5)` and `reboot` takes the model's own update level, which is what `requireWorkspaceRole` said in a service file where `db/access.snapshot.md` could not see it — 11 gated transitions became 19. An app-level `registerErrorMapper` turns `TransitionGateError` back into a sentence with a ROLE in it, since litestone owns the scale and an operator has never seen a level; it reads the ladder `basecampGateLevel` grades with, and covers `Deployment.rollback` for free. **`sync` asks the machine instead of writing a mapped value** — `transitions(row)` says what is legal from where the row IS, so a provider reporting `running` for a machine basecamp is DRAINING is not in the list, where the old status map wrote it and silently undid the drain; a report that cannot be acted on is recorded rather than swallowed. `heartbeat` keeps its single write (the status is one column of one update, and splitting it would write and announce twice) and its from-set is declared as `checkIn @gate(8)`, `FJS-506`'s marker for a move the engine makes. **The browser's copy went too** — the screen asked `resource.transitions(row)`, which is where `FJS-512` was found. | <a id="fjs-509"></a>FJS-509 | sierra — **a route whose frontmatter would not parse produced no page, and the build reported success** | 2026-08-25 | `parseFrontmatter` caught a YAML error and returned `{}` under a comment reading *Sierra will emit a build warning separately* — and nothing did, anywhere. `build-tree` then wrapped the call in `.catch(() => ({}))` as well, so there were two swallows on one path. On a static target `{}` means no `render: static`, so the route is not prerendered; it does not reach `skipped` either, because it never claimed to be static. The page is simply absent, the build prints a page count that looks plausible and exits 0. **An unquoted colon is enough to trigger it**: `description: An honest comparison. Laravel is the framework FrontierJS most resembles: what maps one-to-one` is not a YAML scalar. Measured porting the website — two of five pages vanished, and the only symptom was two missing directories in a build that said it had emitted thirteen. **Fixed 2026-08-25**: `parseFrontmatter` returns the error alongside the frontmatter, `readFrontmatter` throws with the file, the YAML message and its line, and `build-tree` no longer catches. Thrown rather than warned, on the `FJS-439` precedent: frontmatter is how a route says what it IS, so a block that will not parse has no correct reading, and a warning about a page that is missing scrolls past in exactly the build where it matters. | <a id="fjs-508"></a>FJS-508 | sierra — **a prerendered site's sitemap and prefetch rules listed only its static routes, so every page a dynamic route produced was missing** | 2026-08-25 | `runPostBuild` fed both steps `routeTable.indexed`, which excludes dynamic routes — correct for an SPA, where `/products/:slug/` stands for a set nothing can enumerate, and wrong for a static build, where `getStaticPaths()` named that set and the files are on disk. Measured on `example`'s storefront: **`sitemap.xml (4 URLs)` for a site that had just emitted 14 pages**, every product page absent — a catalogue invisible to the crawler it was prerendered for, reported by the build's own log as a success. The website's package pages were 2 of 10. The same list drives the Speculation Rules, so prefetch skipped them too. **Fixed 2026-08-25**: `prerenderRoutes` reports the URLs it actually emitted — it is the only thing that knows them — and `runPostBuild` takes them as a fifth argument, `null` on an SPA. Filtering them needed a second list rather than a cleverer match: `indexed` has already dropped the dynamic PATTERN, so asking whether the pattern was indexed answers no for every page it produced. `routeTable.indexable` is the same draft and `robots: noindex` decision with only the dynamic exclusion left off, which keeps a noindex page out whether it was prerendered or not. Four tests, the SPA path among them. `example`: 4 → 16 URLs; `website`: 2 → 10. | <a id="fjs-500"></a>FJS-500 | mesa · sierra — **a prerendered page did not escape its own text, so any string a static build baked in was live markup** | 2026-08-25 | `<p>{text}</p>` sets `textContent` at runtime and is the safest expression in the language; at build time it renders into happy-dom and serialises with `container.innerHTML`, and **happy-dom 14.12.3 did not re-escape a text node on the way out**. Nothing in Mesa's own code was wrong, which is exactly why nothing here could see it: the client path is correct and only the round trip through the DOM was not, so the runtime suite and the browser drives all agreed the escaping worked. Measured through the real path — `renderComponent` over `<p>{text}</p>` with `text = '<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>'` emitted `<p><img src=x onerror=alert(1)></p>` — and at the bare serialiser, where `div.textContent = '<script>alert(1)<\/script>'` reads back as live markup. Every `render: static` route baking database strings was affected, `example/site` included, where a product name, description, brand and image `alt` all reach the file this way; a static page is served to anyone, cached by a CDN and cannot be recalled. **Fixed 2026-08-25** by the bump `FJS-475` was already asking for — `^14.12.3` → `^20.11.6`, which escapes text nodes, preserves an entity written in the source and does not double-escape an ampersand. **Nine tests in `render-ssr.test.js` pin the behaviour and were confirmed to FAIL on 14.12.3 before the bump was restored**. Five of them are the ATTRIBUTE half, which this report did not name and which is the wider hole: a text node can only inject an element, and an attribute value that escapes its own quotes injects an EVENT HANDLER onto an element the page already trusts — measured through the same round trip, `setAttribute('title', '" onmouseover=alert(1) autofocus x="')` emitted `<a href="/x" title="" onmouseover=alert(1) autofocus x="">`, which re-parses to a link carrying `onmouseover`, `autofocus`, `x` and a stray `1`. `<img alt={…}>` and `<a href={…}>` are database strings on every prerendered catalogue page. They assert by RE-PARSING the output and counting the element's attributes rather than by matching text, because `title="&quot; onmouseover=alert(1)"` contains the characters `onmouseover=` and is completely safe: the question is whether an attribute was ADDED, not how a value was spelled. The payload is asserted to survive intact as a value, since escaping is not sanitising and a product called `5" pipe` must come back as `5" pipe`, because this is an assertion about a dependency and a version range is not a test: a downgrade, a second resolved copy or a serialiser regression is now red rather than an injected script on a public page. Green: mesa 1300 + 82 + 47, sierra 1109, email-kit 34, ui 857. Found porting `website/` to a Sierra static site, where an escaped `&lt;script&gt;` in a code sample came out of the build as a real `<script>` tag · [render.js](packages/mesa/src/render.js) · [render-ssr.test.js](packages/mesa/test/render-ssr.test.js) |
| <a id="fjs-505"></a>FJS-505 | mesa — **a `$:` watch on a value with no depth switched that variable's own reactivity off, silently** | 2026-08-25 | The bare `$: a` form is the DEEP-watch opt-in — it is what changes `a`'s accessor from `$$runtime.get($$sig_a)` to `$$proxy_a`, so a mutation three levels down notifies, and the compiler's own comment says so. On a PRIMITIVE there is nothing to proxy: `localWatchProxy` hands the value straight back, `$$proxy_a` is an ordinary local holding a snapshot, and **every read of `a` in the component compiles to a plain variable read**, so the render effect subscribes to nothing. Value right, screen stale, and no error, no warning and no console output anywhere. Measured against the compiler by toggling one line — `var __a = $$proxy_n > 2` with the watch, `var __a = $$runtime.get($$sig_n) > 2` without — and narrowed to exactly one form: `$: a, () => {}` (a watch with a body) and `$: d = a` (a derivation) both emit the signal read and were never affected, which is why `@frontierjs/ui`'s Combobox and DatePicker work. **Refused rather than repaired, and the reason is that the declaration is redundant** — a local `let` is already a signal, so a whole-value watch on one can only add deep-mutation tracking and a primitive has no depth. Nothing is lost by refusing. The tracking fix was the alternative and is not free: it would make `ctx.accessors[root]` a sequence expression, and two call sites compare that string by equality. Conservative on purpose — only an initialiser that is VISIBLY a primitive refuses (`0`, `''`, `false`, `null`, a template literal, no initialiser at all), because `let x = count()` has the same hole and is not decidable at compile time, and a rule that guessed would refuse `let rows = []`. **A sweep of all 313 components in the repo found one other instance, and it was live**: `$: (handoffError)` in `example`'s basket screen compiled the alert's `{#if}` to `() => ($$proxy_handoffError) ? 0 : null`, so a spent or expired checkout link showed a shopper nothing at all — the API refusal had a drive and the screen had none. `verify:widget` covers it now (37), and the assertion needed a real navigation: the browser was already on `/cart/`, and changing only the FRAGMENT reloads no document. Found building the discount box, where `let discountInput = ''` with a watch beside it left the Apply button permanently disabled while the box filled underneath it. 19 tests in `test/watch-no-depth.test.js` — the refusal matrix both ways, the message naming the variable and both ways out, and three behavioural cases mounting real components: a plain `let` re-rendering on its own, and the deep watch still tracking a mutation on an object and on a named path. Green: mesa 1324, ui 857, sierra 1116; `example` 488 across seventeen drives |
| <a id="fjs-504"></a>FJS-504 | junction — **a `readOnly` column with a `@default` made its model uncreatable through any service, refused by name for a key the caller never sent** | 2026-08-25 | `validate()` fills a default in for any absent key, and `jsonSchemaToJunctionSchema` carried every property's `default` in create mode. Litestone marks `readOnly` on exactly the columns a caller may not write — `@system`, a tenancy-stamped column, `@version`, `@from`, `@derived` — and several of those carry a default too. So the payload reaching the Data boundary named a `@system` column, and `@system` is refused BY NAME rather than dropped: `POST /api/discounts {"code":"X",…}` came back **403 quoting `redemptions`**, a column the request did not contain. Unreachable by any fix at the call site — naming it in `system: [...]` is the opposite of what was meant, and there was nothing to omit. Found declaring `redemptions Int @default(0) @system` on `example`'s new `Discount`; the same shape has been reachable since `@system` existed. **The sibling had been fixed and the rule stopped one line short**: `mode === 'update'` already deleted every default, for the same reason (`a PATCH must not invent a value for a key the caller did not send`) — the create half needed the narrower version of it. Nothing is lost by dropping it, and that is what makes the fix safe: the default is declared in the seed and Litestone applies it at the write, which is where a default a caller may not override has to be applied anyway. Proved by measurement on both sides — the same create through a Litestone client directly succeeded and stamped `redemptions = 0`. The value still travels when a caller DOES send it, which `@version` requires. Green: junction 1410 + 2 new cases in `tests/patch-defaults.test.ts`; `example`: `verify:money` 79. |
| <a id="fjs-501"></a>FJS-501 | sierra — **a static site's theme switcher offered six themes, applied two, and changed nothing a visitor could see** | 2026-08-25 | Three faults, one feature, and each hid the next. **(1) The config never reached the browser.** `initTheme(config)` is called by `virtual:sierra`, which a prerendered page never loads — it ships HTML plus one chunk per island and nothing else — so the theme module kept `normalise({})`: `DEFAULT_THEMES` and key `theme`. Four of six swatches were refused by name, and the two that worked persisted under a key the flash-prevention script *the same config block generated* does not read, so every reload reverted. Fixed by carrying the block into the generated island entry, which is the one place a static build can tell the browser what the app declared. **(2) The script reached one page.** `injectThemeScript` wrote `join(outDir, 'index.html')` — the whole output of an SPA, and one page out of N on a target that emits one HTML file per route. It walks the directory now. **(3) The baked class was on the wrong element, and this was the one that made the feature useless.** `wrapDocument` could only put a class on `<body>`, while the switcher writes `<html>` (`theme/index.js` § why the element is not a knob) — so `<html class="theme-elite">` sat over `<body class="theme-default">` and every token both defined resolved to the baked one for the entire page. Measured: `--color-primary` changed on `<html>` and stayed `#0d83dd` on every element inside `<body>`, for all six themes, with no error anywhere. `wrapDocument` takes `htmlClass`, the static build derives it from `theme.default` rather than asking an author to write it (`'system'` resolves to nothing — that is the injected script's question to answer, and baking either half would be a guess a CDN caches), and a `theme-*` in `document.bodyClass` is now warned about by name at build. Ten tests across `prerender`, `islands` and `postbuild`. `example/site` declares no theme block and is byte-identical. Green: sierra 1109. Now `1.4.4.5` / `1.8.8.8` with two allows apiece: `auth().isStaff` reads everything, `userId == auth().id` reads your own. **1 and not 4**, so a shopper who has registered and not yet clicked the link in their email can still see the order they just placed. | <a id="fjs-496"></a>FJS-496 | junction — **no browser on another origin could sign in, because CORS never reached a route registered before it** | 2026-08-25 | `cors()` patches the router's registration methods, so it reaches every route registered AFTER it. Service routes are registered in a later phase and were fine. `/auth/login` was not, nor caravan's `/jobs`, nor any `app.post` an app writes at module scope. So sign-in was the one call in a Junction app no cross-origin browser could make — and it does not look like a CORS problem from either end: the preflight answers 204, the POST answers 200 and creates the session, and the browser discards the response. What reaches the page is `Failed to fetch`. Measured on a caller holding `orders_pay` but not the capability the model's `@@allow('update')` names. **The two cases were already separable and nothing was asking**: `checkTransitions` proves the row exists at `from` before the statement runs, so re-reading that one column afterwards says which happened — still at `from` means nothing moved and the policy is what refused. Now an `AccessDeniedError` (403, not retryable) naming the model and the move, and a `TransitionConflictError` only where the row really moved. The extra read is on the failure path and only where an update policy exists. `throwIfVersionMoved` beside it already made exactly this distinction for `@version`, for exactly this reason, and the comment above it names all three outcomes. | <a id="fjs-384"></a>FJS-384 | basecamp — **Five job handlers opened `asSystem()` behind a comment saying a job has no caller to scope to** | 2026-08-25 | **The framework half shipped 2026-08-23** — caravan records `actor_id` and `tenant_id` at dispatch, junction re-binds both through `app.runAs`, and `membershipClaim` re-reads the membership when the job runs, so an actor who lost access between asking and running is refused rather than replayed. The app never adopted it: the comment stopped being true and nothing changed. **What adoption turned out to mean is not what this row assumed.** Measured against the app's own schema rather than reasoned about: every row these handlers write is gated at SYSTEM for update — `RecipeRun`, `DeploymentStep`, `CleanupRun` at `@@gate("2.4.8.8")`, `JobRun` at `2.8` — and the highest standing a workspace grants is `owner`, 6. That is the schema saying a run's OUTCOME belongs to the machine and not to whoever asked, and a gate refuses regardless of standing, so no amount of tenant-binding reaches those rows. `asSystem()` there is the declared design. **What was actually missing is CONFINEMENT**, and that is what shipped: each handler's reads and writes go through an `internalOnly()` method on the service that owns the row, which reads the PARENT through the caller's own client first — a release, run or job in another workspace answers nothing, where an id off a payload used to be written wherever it pointed — and only then does the gated write as system. The audit actor and the announcement come back with it, which `asSystem()` had been dropping; `deployment:run` had also been pushing the row by hand and `job:run` had not been pushing at all. **The rule is dispatch declares, handler asserts** (`api/src/jobs/context.ts`): `runsAsCaller` refuses unless the queue recorded an actor AND a tenant, `runsAsApp` refuses when it recorded one, and `runsEitherWay` is `job:run`, the one job dispatched both by a person and by a cron. The cron fire in `job-schedule.ts` now STATES `actor: null` — caravan would have defaulted to nobody there anyway, and saying it is what makes the handler's refusal mean something. `job:run` keeps ONE write path for both modes: the confinement read is scoped when there is a caller and system when the mode says there is not, since a cron fire is the app acting on its own behalf across workspaces. Nine methods added across four services, all `internalOnly` and all in `surface.snapshot.md` — junction answers 405 to a name `methods:` leaves out, in-process included, so an engine method has to be declared surface and the hook is what keeps it off the wire. **5 tests**, including the one that matters: an `owner` of one workspace cannot open a run in another, the owner of THAT workspace can, and the same principal moved to a wire transport is refused by the hook rather than by the session. The subscription question the issue left open answers itself: there is nothing for a client to send, because membership is the SERVER's (`app.channel(name).join(…)`), and what arrives is `{ type: 'event', event: 'orders pay' }` re-emitted as `client.on('event', …)` — so a channel is the first half of that name and a subscription is a filter over it. Two things had to move for it to be honest. **`url` is spelled differently by the two packages** — jetty's config field has always been `wss://` and the client takes an http origin and derives the socket, so `wsss://` and a socket that never opens; both are accepted. And **sign-in is `adapter.auth`, not `call('auth', …)`**: Junction has no service by that name — auth registers `account`, `sessions` and `api-keys`, and establishing a session is a route (`FJS-D20`) — so the placeholder's pseudo-service would shadow the methods of any app that has one. `makeAuthFlow` prefers the block and falls back to the call.
