| <a id="fjs-481"></a>FJS-481 | jetty · mesa — **A mesa rename moved the shape jetty's build plugin matches by TEXT, so every component with a delegated event stopped hot-updating and ~450 green tests said nothing** | 2026-08-24 | `injectJettyHMR` wraps compiled Mesa so a component registers itself with `globalThis.__jettyMesa` and can be swapped in place. It does that with two regexes over the compiler's OUTPUT — a shape jetty reads and does not own — and the second one's lookahead was `(?=\n\$runtime\.\$\$delegate\|\s*$)`. **`FJS-470` renamed every emitted identifier to `$$`**, so from that day the lookahead matched nothing for any component carrying a delegated event, which is nearly all of them: Step 1 still renamed the function, Step 2 injected no registration, and the module exported a wrapper that mounts correctly and never registers. The symptom is an edit that does not appear — no error, no warning, a green build. Measured against real compiler output: `Step 1 matches: true · Step 2 matches: false`. **Nothing caught it, and the reason is its own finding.** The only cover was `test/hmr-fullflow.mjs` and `test/hmr-integration.mjs`, which no script runs — and could not have run: they import mesa by an **absolute path from another machine** (`/home/claude/repo/reading-list/frontierjs/mesa/compiler.js`), so they fail at module resolution. jetty's ten phase files, ~450 assertions, had never compiled a component. `fli check`'s `test-files-run` could not see them either, which is [FJS-482](#fjs-482). **Fixed 2026-08-24, three ways.** The lookahead matches the delegate call by SHAPE (`\$+runtime`) rather than by the namespace's name. **The plugin now throws when neither step lands**, naming the file and saying the shape has moved — a wrapper that mounts and never registers is worse than an unwrapped module, and this is a build-time answer instead of one at somebody's desk. And `test/phase9.test.js` drives `injectJettyHMR` with **real compiler output**, both branches of the lookahead plus the unrecognised-shape throw; negative-controlled, the old lookahead turns 3 of its 7 red. The two dead harnesses moved to `packages/jetty/docs/dead/` with a README saying why they are kept — the full-flow shape through jsdom is worth repairing one day and `phase9` covers only the wrapper's output. **`FJS-D134`'s class, one layer out**: the ruling is about compiled output meeting compiled output, and this is compiled output meeting a BUILD PLUGIN that matches it with a regex, where nothing at either end declares the dependency · [mesa-plugin.js:259](packages/jetty/src/build/mesa-plugin.js#L259) · [phase9.test.js](packages/jetty/test/phase9.test.js) · [FJS-470](#fjs-470) |
| <a id="fjs-482"></a>FJS-482 | mesa · cli — **A component could declare the compiler's own calling convention — `function __anchor(){}` threw at mount and `const __prev = 9` rendered `[object Object]`** | 2026-08-24 | `__anchor` and `__props` are the component function's parameters, `__block` is threaded into every `track()` call, and `__prev` is the render callback's. None was in `BUILTIN_LOCALS`, so an author could declare any of them at the top level of a script — where the declaration lands in the SAME scope and wins. **Unlike `FJS-471`'s builtins this is a shadow rather than a redeclaration**, so the output is valid JavaScript and the failure moves to runtime or nowhere: `function __anchor(){}` compiles, parses, and throws at mount with `anchor.before is not a function`; `const __prev = 9` compiles and renders **`[object Object]`**, silently, because the callback's parameter shadows the author's constant. `function __block()`, `function __props()` and `class __dev {}` survived the cases probed — `__dev` is `$$runtime.__dev`, a property rather than a scope name, and cannot be shadowed at all. **Fixed by reserving the four**, which refuses the declaration rather than renaming anything: the names are protocol (jetty's build plugin matches `__anchor, __props, __block` as text — [FJS-481](#fjs-481)), so moving them is `FJS-D134`'s decision and not this one. The message is split by kind, because the existing one says *the compiler injects it into the same scope, and two declarations is a SyntaxError* and that is the wrong sentence here — nothing is injected and there is no SyntaxError. A nested scope is deliberately untouched: a parameter of the author's own function shadows nothing of the compiler's. 5 cases in `emission.test.js`. **The open remainder is the naming decision itself** — `FJS-470` closed saying `__anchor`/`__block`/`__props` did not converge on `$$` and that half *stays open below*, and there was no row below: it was tracked nowhere until this one. The population is three kinds, which is what the convergence framing missed — the calling convention (four names, author-reachable, this issue), runtime PROPERTIES (`$$runtime.__dev`, `n.__resolving`, unshadowable), and generated locals inside emitted callbacks. Only the first group can collide, three of its four are protocol, and with them reserved the four-line rule is broken in appearance rather than in consequence · [compiler.js `BUILTIN_LOCALS`](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js) · [FJS-470](#fjs-470) · [FJS-D134](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d134) |
| <a id="fjs-478"></a>FJS-478 | mesa — **`bind:class` on an element compiled to a write on a DOM property that does not exist, so neither direction ever touched the element** | 2026-08-24 | `VISION` § 10.8 documented class passthrough as *two shorthand forms* — `{class}` one-way and `bind:class` two-way, *for components that mutate their own class set and want the parent to observe the change*. The second one has never worked. `bind:class` and its `:class` alias parse into `bind:class={$class}` and then take the **form-value path**, whose generic branch is `const handler = () => set(el[name])` plus `createEffect(() => { el[name] = readFn() ?? '' })`. With `name === 'class'` that is `el['class']`, and **there is no such DOM property** — it is `className`. Measured against a real DOM: after `el.class = 'raised'`, `getAttribute('class')` and `className` both still read `own hash` and only `el.class` reads back `raised`, which is a JS expando nothing renders. So the write reached nothing and the read-back read its own write. **Clean compile, valid JavaScript, a binding that silently does not exist.** **Nothing caught it because nothing used it** — zero `.mesa` files in this repo, across `packages/ui`'s 69 components and every app — and `compiler.test.js` had a case that PINNED it: *bind:class in child auto-wires $class*, asserting `bindInput` was emitted, which is precisely the broken output. The test passed for as long as the feature was broken. **Refused rather than fixed, 2026-08-24.** The refusal names `{class}` and says why. Fixing it would be a different feature: the observe direction needs a `MutationObserver` on `attributes`, which is a mechanism nobody wrote, for a use case with no user. **No comparable framework offers a two-way class binding** — Svelte has `class:name` and the clsx-style `class`, Vue merges class through fallthrough attributes automatically and reserves `v-model` for form input, Angular has `[class.x]` and `[(ngModel)]` — and where a framework DOES observe element state it makes the binding readonly, which is what Svelte's `bind:clientWidth` is. If the observe direction is ever wanted it is a readonly binding with a new name, not a repair of this one. **On a COMPONENT `bind:class` is untouched** and is an ordinary two-way prop through `bindProp`; the refusal is on the element path alone, which is the only route to `bindInput(el, 'class', …)`. `RULE 31b` in VISION, 4 cases in `emission.test.js` covering the accepted surface and the three refused spellings, and the pinning test replaced with the refusal. Green: mesa 1285 + 82 + 47, sierra 1089, ui 857 · [compiler.js:5703](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js#L5703) · [runtime.js](packages/mesa/src/runtime.js) `bindInput` · [VISION.md § 10.8](packages/mesa/docs/VISION.md) |
| <a id="fjs-477"></a>FJS-477 | mesa — **A compiled door member emitted a reference to a local nothing declared — `$.mounted` worked in one spelling out of four and `$.context.use` in none** | 2026-08-24 | `$.mounted`, `$.context` and `$.inspect` are the three door members that are COMPILED rather than read: a pre-parse AST pass rewrites `$.x` to `$x`, and the local that identifier resolves to is emitted only where a sniff found the shape the compiler knows how to wire. The sniff for `$mounted` was a **regex over the script text** demanding `const NAME = $mounted(`, so three other spellings rewrote to `$mounted` and emitted a reference to a binding that was never declared — `$.mounted(fn)` as a bare call, `const m = $.mounted`, and `{$.mounted}` in a template. **Clean compile, valid JavaScript, `ReferenceError` on first render**, which is Invariant 15's failure in its exact shape. `$.context` had the same hole in a wider form: the compiler consumes the SUGAR (`const v = $.context.k` becomes a `$$ctxRead` + `trackDerived`, `$.context.k = v` becomes a `$$ctxProvide`) and passes the runtime object through untouched — so `$.context.use('k')` and `$.context.provide('k', fn)`, which are a real export (`runtime.js`'s `context = { use, provide }`), emitted `$context.use(…)` against an undeclared `$context`. **Every documented imperative use of context was broken and nothing in the repo used it.** A template made it worse rather than the same: the rewrite is script-only, so `{$.context.k}` reached output verbatim and read `.k` off `{ use, provide }` — `undefined` where the door member existed, a `TypeError` where it did not. **Not a regression from `FJS-D132`** — `$mounted(fn)` as a bare call failed identically before the door — but the bare spelling is refused now, so `$.` is the only way in and it invites three spellings that do not work. **Fixed 2026-08-24.** The gate reads the AST the rewrite produced instead of the text: `refuseCompiledDoorMisuse` blesses the shapes the compiler wires and refuses every surviving `$mounted` by name with the working spelling stated, and answers whether a `$context` reference survived the sugar — which is what now requires the local, so both imperative forms work anywhere. `refuseCompiledDoorInTemplate` refuses `$.mounted` and the `$.context.<key>` sugar in a template, naming the script form; `.use`/`.provide` pass. **The first cut of the template rule was a text scan and it refused a REPL example** whose markup contains the sentence *It provides $.context.count* — prose about the framework is what an example page is made of. Both template scans now read `templateExpressions()`, every `{ … }` region by brace depth, which also closed the same latent trap in `refuseBareBuiltins`. **Also removed: `findContextInBody`**, a dead validator in pass 5 carrying a live `ReferenceError` on an out-of-scope `n` — unreachable, so it had never fired. 12 cases in `emission.test.js`, negative-controlled (7 red with the fix neutered). Green: mesa 1277 + 82 + 47, sierra 1088, ui 857 · [compiler.js:2099](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js#L2099) · [compiler.js:2019](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js#L2019) · [FJS-D132](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d132) |
| <a id="fjs-480"></a>FJS-480 | litestone — **`@@index([deletedAt])` beside `@@softDelete` emitted the same index name twice, so the schema parsed and the database could not be created** | 2026-08-24 | `@@softDelete` emits its own partial index as `idx_<table>_deletedAt`, and a declared `@@index([deletedAt])` derives the identical name. The DDL is emitted, the run reaches SQLite, and the boot dies on `index idx_note_deletedAt already exists` — an error naming a physical index, about a schema that passed validation with no warning. Eight lines reproduce it: `model Note { id Int @id  body String  deletedAt DateTime?  @@index([deletedAt])  @@softDelete }`. Declaring an index on the soft-delete column is the obvious thing to do for anyone who has not read that the attribute already does it, and it is what an introspected schema produces — found converting a real MySQL schema, where the source table had exactly that index. Two ways out and they differ: **refuse at parse**, naming the attribute that already provides it, or **dedupe at emit** and say nothing. **Refused at parse, 2026-08-24.** The declaration buys nothing either way — a declared index on a soft-delete table is already given the `WHERE "deletedAt" IS NULL` clause, so it compiled to exactly the index `@@softDelete` was about to write, and what a dedupe would preserve is the ability to write a line with no effect. Single-column only: `@@index([deletedAt, status])` derives a different name and stays an ordinary composite. The refusal names both attributes and the way out. 4 cases in `test/soft-delete-index.test.ts`, negative-controlled (2 red with the check neutered). `litestone introspect` does not produce the pair — checked rather than assumed: it emits model-level `@@index` for composites only and drops solo non-unique indexes altogether, which is a separate gap. So the shape arrives from a person writing the obvious thing, or from a converter reading another database's DDL, which is where this one came from. Green: litestone 2993 · [parser.js](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js) · [ddl.js `createIndexes`](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) · [soft-delete-index.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/soft-delete-index.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-483"></a>FJS-483 | cli · litestone · auth · basecamp — **Nothing detected an app that pastes a package's models instead of importing them, and a paste drifts in silence** | 2026-08-24 | `extend model` (2026-08-24) removed the REASON to paste: an app can import `@frontierjs/auth/schema.lite` and still add the relation back to its own `User`, `@@log(audit)` and `@@tenant(none)`. What it did not do is stop the paste, and the cost of one is measured rather than argued — basecamp carried hand copies of four auth models for its whole life, and one of them had `@guarded(all)` where the package writes `@secret`, so turning OAuth on there would have stored every provider access and refresh token unencrypted. **137 tests were green either side of the divergence**, because nothing anywhere compares a copy to its original. **Filed with the wrong rule in mind, and that was the whole difficulty.** *An app declares `model X` where a dependency also declares `model X`* fires on every correct install: `@frontierjs/auth` ships one file to be IMPORTED and one — `User` — to be APPENDED and grown, so the copy itself decides nothing, and the filed note concluded a package needed a new way to say which was which. It does not. **What is decidable is a column THE PACKAGE DECLARES, declared differently here.** Adding a column is what an app is for; adding `@@tenant(none)` or a policy is the app's business too; changing the package's own column is the class that costs something. No manifest key, no language change, no parser — a line scan over a file in `node_modules`, reached through that package's own `exports` map. **Fixed 2026-08-24** as `fli check`'s twenty-seventh rule, `package-model-drift`. A WARNING and not an error, because a deviation can be right and the reader has to judge: both declarations are printed, and `check-baseline.json` is how an app accepts one it has argued for. **Its first run on basecamp found three things and closed all three, one of them in the package rather than the app.** (1) `User.emailVerified` and (2) `User.role` had lost `@allow('write', auth().isAdmin)` — basecamp gates update at USER(4) with `@@allow('update', id == auth().id)`, *your own row*, so the field policy is the only thing between a caller and their own `emailVerified`, and marking your own address verified is skipping the verification. **Latent rather than live**: basecamp exposes no `users` service and its gate grades on `status` and `WorkspaceMember.role`, neither of which is this — it becomes real the day a profile screen writes through a service over `db.user`. Both restored, and both now appear in `db/access.snapshot.md`, so a future removal is a diff. (3) `User.accountId` was `String?` against auth's `Int?` — and here the PACKAGE was wrong: every id `@frontierjs/auth` declares is a uuid, so an `Int?` could not hold one, which is what made the column unusable for the one app that reached for it. auth ships `String?` now; `SessionContext.accountId` was always a string either way. Seven cases in `tests/checks.test.js`, mutation-checked both directions — never comparing, and comparing the APP's columns instead of the package's (the false-positive shape), each turn it red. Green: cli 848, auth 244, basecamp 143 + 301/301 · [checks.js](packages/cli/core/checks.js) · [checks.test.js](packages/cli/tests/checks.test.js) · [user.lite](packages/auth/db/user.lite) · [schema.lite](packages/basecamp/db/schema.lite) |
| <a id="fjs-470"></a>FJS-470 | mesa — **Mesa carries three internal naming conventions with no principle between them, so `$foo` cannot be promised as an ordinary user name** | 2026-08-24 | `FJS-D132` folds the twelve author-facing builtins onto one `$` object and deliberately leaves the compiler internals where they are, which means the rule it establishes is only two-thirds true. Three prefixes are in use and the split is historical rather than designed: **single `$`** holds the per-instance frame locals (`$runtime` 217 emit sites, `$dom` 82, `$option` 37, `$parentElement` 31, `$class` 29, `$domFirst` 18, `$domLast` 7 — 421 in all), **`$$`** holds roughly 150 uses across `$$sig_*`, `$$proxy_*`, `$$watch_*`, `$$set_*`, `$$snippet_*`, `$$item`, `$$v`, `$$e`, `$$arg0` plus the `$$eachBlock` / `$$virtualEach` / `$$delegate` runtime exports, and **`__`** holds 132 more in `__block`, `__anchor`, `__dev`, `__props`. A shape is discernible in the `$$` tier — it wears two dollars precisely where a name is generated from a user identifier or injected into a scope the author's own code runs in, which is the collision-avoidance job — but nothing enforces it and the single-`$` frame locals are in author scope too. **The fix is to converge on `$$`, not on `__`.** `__runtime` would say the internals are generic JavaScript noise; `$$runtime` says same family, one layer down, and it leaves the four-line rule true end to end: `$` is the door, `$:` / `$_name:` is the label, `$$` is the compiler's, everything else is yours. It also converts `FJS-D132`'s deferred win into a delivered one — `$foo` becomes an ordinary user variable name and `$$foo` becomes the reserved prefix nobody types by accident — and it makes RULE 31a (`$class` is compiler-internal, never declare it) enforceable rather than advisory. **Two things must be checked before a line moves.** Invariant 12 says compiler output is reproducible because scope ids are content-addressed, and CSS dedupe across the two compilers a static build runs depends on it — so whether emitted identifiers feed that hash decides whether every scope id in the repo moves at once. And `__` has to converge in the same pass or the three-tier story is fiction with a nicer name. **Checked and clear**: there is no `$$`-in-replacement-string trap, the only `.replace()` with a `$` in its replacement being a `'$1'` capture reference; and Chrome devtools' `$$` console helper is a distinct identifier that a `$$foo` local cannot shadow, so the single rule that follows is never to emit a bare `$$`. **Blast radius**: 421 sites in compiler emit strings and 15 test files that string-match `$runtime`; zero user `.mesa` files, since none of these names is author-facing. **Attempted 2026-08-24 and reverted — the rename is not a rename, which is the finding.** A blanket textual pass over the 430 emit sites went green on the compiler loading and then failed 84–257 tests in three waves, because this compiler encodes its own emitted identifiers in **four** ways and only one of them is a plain identifier. **Regex literals that match its own output**: `\$parentElement` is an ESCAPED dollar, so appending a second one produced `\$$parentElement`, where the new dollar reads as an end-of-input anchor mid-pattern — and the sibling case `\$\$sig_` is a legitimately doubled name that must NOT move, so the two cannot be told apart by a rule about dollars. **String keys paired with property reads**: `ctx.require('$emit')` is a key and `ctx.inuse.$emit` is a property, so a dot-guard that correctly protects the second silently renames only the first — and the failure is not an error but a binding that stops being emitted at all. **A cross-component protocol name**: `$class` is not a scope-local, it is the prop the `{class}` shorthand travels under, and `runtime.js` reads it as the string `'$class'` in `restProps`' skip set and as `$option.$class`; renaming it breaks class passthrough between two compiled components. **And the runtime's own exports** (`$onMount`, `$context`, `$tick` — seven of them) are the public API a composable helper imports and must keep the bare spelling, so they sit in the same file as names that should move. The tree was returned to green (mesa 1264 + 82 + 47, ui 857, sierra 1088, typecheck clean) and nothing of `FJS-D132` phases 0-5 was disturbed. **What this changes about the fix**: it is not a codemod. Each emitted name wants ONE definition the compiler reads — a constant, not a literal repeated across emit strings, regexes and registry keys — and that refactor is the actual work, with the rename falling out of it for free. Doing it the other way round is what was measured here. **Also cleared: Invariant 12 does not gate it.** `cssHash` is fed the style content and nothing else, deliberately, so renaming an emitted identifier cannot move a scope id. **Probed 2026-08-23, and the answer inverts the assumption**: the `$`-prefixed INTERNALS are clean — `$runtime`, `$dom`, `$class` and `$parentElement` compile and parse under `let`, `const`, `var` and `function` alike — while the injected BUILTINS collide, which is [FJS-471](#fjs-471) and is `FJS-D132`'s to fix. So this item carries no defect behind it and stays S4: it is a naming rule, not a bug. **Closed 2026-08-24, on the second attempt and after the ground moved.** What made it possible was doing the runtime's exports FIRST: `$runtime.$onMount` was two namespaces sharing a spelling, and once the export is `onMount` the local beside it can move without ambiguity. The rename then went through with the four encodings named above handled explicitly — plus a FIFTH the first attempt never reached, which is that `$$` inside a `.replace()` REPLACEMENT STRING collapses to one `$`, so `hoistTemplates` emitted `$tpl0 = $runtime.template(…)` from declarations that read `$$tpl0 = $$runtime.template(…)`; it is a callback now. Two more names turned out to be protocol rather than internal, in the sense `FJS-D134` had just ruled: `$dom` is the key a block factory answers with and `runtime.js` reads 59 times, and the three COMPILED door members (`$context`, `$inspect`, `$mounted`) are AUTHOR-space names after the pre-parse rewrite, so their injected locals have to keep the bare spelling to be reachable at all. Emitted output now carries no single-`$` name; `$runtime`, `$option` and `$dom` are ordinary user variable names, which is the sentence this issue existed to make true. `__anchor`/`__block`/`__props` did NOT converge and that half stays open below. Green: mesa 1265 + 82 + 47, ui 857, sierra 1088, jetty 46, cli 811, typecheck clean · [compiler.js:3076](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js#L3076) · [compiler.js:7503](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js#L7503) · [FJS-D132](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d132) |
| <a id="fjs-410"></a>FJS-410 | basecamp — **A workspace admin could promote themselves to owner, so level 5 granted itself level 6** | 2026-08-24 | Found by `gate-over-own-standing` in litestone's `advise.js`, which can SEE that `WorkspaceMember` is where the tenancy claim is read from and therefore that its `role` column is the one every gate in the app is graded off. **Closed in three tiers, and the last one is the door a person actually walks through.** The Data boundary came first: `@@deny('update', userId == auth().id)`, which covers a job, a hub screen and a `fli tinker` session at level 5 — and the first attempt at it was a FIELD policy, `role @allow('write', userId != auth().id)`, which reads like the rule and is none, because a field write predicate is graded against the PAYLOAD, so `userId` is undefined in a patch of `{ role }` and the column is permitted (`FJS-433`). Then the read half, since a model with no `@@allow` for an operation is OPEN and a scoped client was reading every workspace's membership — four allows, read being `userId == auth().id` so the switcher still answers across workspaces, which is why `@@tenant(none)` is on this model. **Then the service tier, 2026-08-24, and the row had called that half a missing sentence — it was not.** Driven through `@frontierjs/testing` against the real app: an admin naming their own `userId` with `role: 'owner'` got **no throw, a 200, and OWNER(6)**. No schema rule can reach that path — `members()` is `asSystem()`, because membership is what decides access and cannot be read through the caller it is deciding about, and `asSystem()` is the context every policy is bypassed in. So the boundary held every door but the one with a person behind it. Three refusals, because the escalation has three shapes and *not your own row* is only the first: a self-check in `setMemberRole` (your own role, in either direction); `refuseRoleAboveOwn()`, registered on `workspaces.addMember`, `workspaces.setMemberRole` and `invitations.create`, because an admin who cannot promote themselves can invite an address they own AS owner and sign in as it; and an outrank check, because `Cannot demote the last owner` only catches an admin demoting an owner where there happens to be exactly one. Equal is allowed — an admin appointing an admin is what the role is for. The hook is registered per method rather than on `all`: `role` is a word two other models use, and a hook grading every payload carrying the key would refuse a fleet write for holding the wrong kind of role. **6 tests in `api/test/services.test.ts`, mutation-checked** — neuter the hook and two go red, the self-check one, the outrank check one. The members screen narrows both pickers to what the caller may hand out (a slice of the enum, which is declared in ascending authority, rather than a fourth copy of the ladder) and disables the row that is your own; an affordance, with the refusal in the service. Green: basecamp 143 tests, typecheck at baseline, `fli check` clean, surface snapshot regenerated. **Note for the deploy**: `litestone release` grades the field policy `contract` — it takes a write away from a release still serving — so this is the pivot and only forward after it · [workspaces.service.ts § setMemberRole](packages/basecamp/api/src/services/workspaces/workspaces.service.ts) · [hooks.ts § refuseRoleAboveOwn](packages/basecamp/api/src/core/hooks.ts) · [db/schema.lite](packages/basecamp/db/schema.lite) |
| <a id="fjs-476"></a>FJS-476 | auth — **A verification token of one kind is accepted at the lookup of another, and is refused two steps later by a coincidence of string handling** | 2026-08-24 | **The table was redone rather than patched** — no app has shipped on it, so the compatible shape was not worth having. `Verification` was doing three jobs and `purpose` alone would only have labelled that: it now carries a `VerificationPurpose` ENUM (`passwordReset` · `emailVerify` · `oauthLink`), a REQUIRED `identifier` — every purpose proves control of an address, which is the test for whether something belongs in the table at all — and `value` is `@unique`, because a token that could collide is a lookup that could be ambiguous. Both cross-kind lookups filter on `purpose`, so the refusal is the query's and the error names what happened; the `identifier.replace(/^reset:/, '')` parsing is gone, and so is the fifth call site it had (`deleteUser` matched `endsWith(':' + email)` against the prefix). **An OAuth flow is not a verification and is now its own model.** Reusing this one was reuse-by-shape: nobody is proving anything during an authorization, there is no address yet and there may be no account at the end of it, `state` is a CSRF token echoed by a redirect rather than a secret sent to a person, its life is minutes against hours, and it carries a PKCE verifier no other row has a use for. `OauthFlow` — spelled `Oauth` for the reason basecamp's `ThreeCX` is not `3CX`: the accessor lowers the first character and nothing else, so `OAuthFlow` would be reached as `db.oAuthFlow`. Both tables are swept by the one cleanup timer. **A test already existed for the defect and asserted the accident**: `tokens do not cross protocols` expected `UserNotFoundError`, which is what the door being OPEN looks like from outside — the token did cross, and what refused it was an address lookup missing on a prefix two steps later. It asserts `InvalidTokenError` now. auth 241 pass, typecheck clean, and `example`'s `verify:oauth` green against the new schema. |
| <a id="fjs-474"></a>FJS-474 | sierra — **The boot restore short-circuits on `!client.token`, so a `cookieAuth` app renders signed out on every cold load while a valid session sits in the jar** | 2026-08-24 | `initSession` read `client.token`, which is the BEARER answer to a question that has two modes. In cookie mode the session is an httpOnly cookie no script can read, so the token is empty for a signed-in caller and for a stranger alike — the restore returned early, `session.user` stayed null on every cold load, and the navigation guard redirected an authenticated caller to sign-in with a valid session in the jar. **Signing in worked, which is what hid it**: `_adopt` already handles the no-token answer deliberately and `refresh()` runs on the way through, so the app behaved correctly right up until the first reload. The fix names the question: `client.hasCredential` on the browser client — `cookieAuth \|\| !!token` — where false means *there is definitely no credential*, the only safe reason to skip asking, and true means *ask*, which is not a claim that the session is valid. `cookieAuth` joins `apiPrefix`, `authPrefix` and `authServices` as a client option that MUST match the server's; the client cannot read the server's plugin options and these are two programs. **A second half was found while fixing it and is closed here too**: the boot socket branch read `client.token` for the same reason, so a cookie-mode app opened no WebSocket after any reload, for the life of the page, with nothing saying so. It waits on the session restore rather than on `hasCredential`, because that is true for a stranger too and opening a socket for every anonymous visitor to a public page is a cost the Bearer path never pays. **The stub was the reason it survived a suite of 1088**: `tests/session.test.js` builds a fake client, so the contract it models was the contract that was wrong — the repo's own *fake clients hide real bugs* rule, in the one place nobody applies it. The stub now carries the real accessor and a cookie-mode case sits beside the token one. sierra 1089 pass, junction 1403 pass, typecheck clean. Found while scoping `IDEAS/third-party-credentials.md`, where cookie mode is the v1 answer for the OAuth callback. |
| <a id="fjs-465"></a>FJS-465 | mesa — **a local `const` shadowing a derived was rewritten as a read of the derived: valid JavaScript, no warning, wrong value** | 2026-08-24 | `function go() { const cellFor = (x) => x; pick = cellFor(1) }` emitted `$$set_pick($$runtime.get(cellFor)(1))` — the module-scope derived, not the local declaration two lines above it. The cause was a scope the rewriter could not see: `rewriteAssignments` rewrote an assignment's right-hand side by slicing THAT EXPRESSION alone out of the source and handing it to `rewriteExpr`, so the enclosing function body and everything it declared were absent from the text being walked. `rewriteExpr` gets shadowing right when it is given the whole statement — `collectBlockDeclared`, added for [FJS-319](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md) — and it was the RHS-only call that was blind. **Fixed 2026-08-24** by deleting that call: `rewriteAssignments` rewrites assignments and leaves identifiers alone, which is what its name says, and every one of its seven call sites already applies `rewriteExpr` to the whole statement or fragment afterwards. **No decision was needed and the row saying otherwise was wrong on its own facts** — FJS-319 had already settled that a shadow is respected rather than refused, and the claim here that six callers skip `rewriteExpr` came from a grep matching the word in nearby context lines rather than actual calls; every real caller composes the pair. One unit test pinned the old contract by calling `rewriteAssignments` alone; it now asserts the composition every caller performs, plus the compound-operator case, which builds its own signal read and was never affected. mesa 1279 + 82 + 47 green; `ui` 857; `example` `verify` 39/39, `verify:ui` 35/35, `verify:values` 14/14, `verify:catalogue` 20/20, `verify:stock` 41/41 — [compiler.js](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js) `rewriteAssignments` · [test/derived-double-wrap.test.js](packages/mesa/test/derived-double-wrap.test.js) |
| <a id="fjs-490"></a>FJS-490 | junction — **the per-request principal resolver never ran for an anonymous caller under `strategy database`, so a claims-only feature had no claim** | 2026-08-24 | `withLitestoneDb` runs `createApp({ principal })` for a GUEST as well as for a session, and says why in a comment: the resolver is the only thing that can turn something the request carries — a cart token, an invitation — into a claim a row policy can read, and the population that needs it most is exactly the one with no `auth().id`. `withTenantDb` kept the older half of the same seam: `if (principal && ctx.auth?.user)`. Two hooks are one seam wearing two names, and only one of them was fixed. Measured in `example` the hour it adopted `strategy database`: every basket call answered **404 No such basket** to every shopper, because `Cart` is `@@allow(read, token == auth().cartToken)` and nothing put the token on the principal. It runs for a guest now, with `null` where a session would be, exactly as the other one does |
| <a id="fjs-489"></a>FJS-489 | junction — **`announceDataWrites` was not installed under `createApp({ tenants })`, so a write that went through no service reached no open tab** | 2026-08-24 | Stated in the code as a limitation — *not installed for a tenant registry: there the client is per request rather than per app, so there is no single client to tap* — and the consequence is `FJS-010` and `FJS-464` again, one strategy over: a job writing with `asSystem()`, a signed webhook moving a row through a Litestone client, a bulk write, all silent. `example`'s `verify:pay` is what caught it: the payment webhook settled an order and the seller's open tab stayed on `pending`, with nothing logged. The tap goes on each TENANT's client the first time `withTenantDb` opens it, kept to once by a `WeakSet` — a client evicted from the LRU pool and reopened is a new object and is tapped again, which is right. On the tenant client and never on a scoped view of it: a scoped client is a proxy built per call, so a tap there would last one call |
| <a id="fjs-488"></a>FJS-488 | junction — **a raw route ran outside the request scope, so `requestMeta()` was undefined inside `/auth/login` and inside every webhook** | 2026-08-24 | `enterRequest` is documented as having five entry points and one owner, and the HTTP one is the SERVICE dispatch handler. A route registered with `app.get`/`app.post` — which is every route an app or a plugin mounts, `@frontierjs/auth`'s `/auth/*` included — ran outside it, so anything that reads the request without holding a `ctx` was blind exactly there. Found adopting `strategy database` in `example`: a per-shop auth provider resolves the shop from the request, and inside `/auth/login` there was no request to resolve it from — so a sign-in with one shop's credentials succeeded at another, against the wrong database. The shortcuts wrap their handler now; the principal is deliberately left out, because a raw route is below the service pipeline and `ctx.user` is the transport's own answer, which the route already has |
| <a id="fjs-487"></a>FJS-487 | junction · auth — **a session was verified before any tenant was known, so an app whose people live per tenant could authenticate nobody** | 2026-08-24 | The transport resolves `Bearer` through `IAuth.verifySession(token)` before any hook has run, and `withTenantDb` is a hook — so under `tenancy { strategy database }`, where `User`, `Credential` and `Session` are rows in the tenant's own file, a provider handed a bare token has no way to know which tenant's people it is being asked about. `verifySession(token, from?: CredentialOrigin)` now carries the request's `host` and headers — the two things `resolve subdomain` and `resolve header(…)` read. Optional, and every existing provider ignores it: `@frontierjs/auth` binds one client at construction and is correct for a single-database app, which is almost every app. The HTTP path falls back to the URL's host, because a Request built in-process carries no `Host` header at all (it is a forbidden header name), and the WS path hands over the UPGRADE request's headers, which are the only ones a socket ever has |
| <a id="fjs-486"></a>FJS-486 | litestone — **`resolve subdomain` could not be exercised on localhost, so it was the one resolution nobody could develop against** | 2026-08-24 | `tenantFrom` required three labels — written for `acme.example.com` — and `acme.localhost` is two. So the first thing anybody types trying the feature answered `null`, which reads as the registry not knowing the tenant rather than as the host never naming one. `.localhost` is a reserved TLD (RFC 6761) that browsers and resolvers already send to loopback, so two labels count when the last one is it. `localhost` alone is still not a tenant called localhost |
| <a id="fjs-491"></a>FJS-491 | litestone — **two tenants writing one schema-global jsonl database collided on its index and crashed the app** | 2026-08-24 | A `jsonl` or `logger` database is schema-GLOBAL under `tenancy { strategy database }` — the registry says so at startup — so every tenant's client writes the audit trail through its own driver instance over one file. `insertIndexRecord` used a plain `INSERT` where the model has no `@id`, on a table whose primary key is `_offset`, so the first write after a second shop was opened died with `UNIQUE constraint failed: auditLogs_idx._offset` — a crash from inside an audit write, about a table nobody named, in an app that was merely serving its second tenant. `INSERT OR REPLACE` either way now: `_offset` is the line's identity in the file, so re-indexing one is idempotent by construction. `refillIndex` beside it has always used `OR REPLACE`, for the same reason |
| <a id="fjs-485"></a>FJS-485 | mesa — **`++x` and `x++` on a reactive `let` evaluated to `undefined`, and postfix compiled to the prefix form** | 2026-08-24 | Found building the storefront's search box in `example`. `const mine = ++inflight` is the ordinary guard against an out-of-order response — take a ticket, and drop the answer if a newer keystroke has taken another. `mine` was `undefined`, so `mine !== inflight` was true for every response and **every answer the shop gave was discarded**: no error, no warning, an empty list, and a search box that looks like the server is not answering. **Two defects in one emit.** Both rewriters turned `x++` into `$$set_x($$runtime.get($$sig_x) + 1)` — correct as a STATEMENT and wrong twice as an expression. `$$runtime.set` returned nothing, so the whole expression was `undefined`, which is also true of `x += 1` and `--x`. And prefix and postfix compiled to the SAME string, so `const b = n++` would have answered the new value once it answered one at all. `set` answers the value it wrote now, which fixes the prefix and compound forms with one line; postfix emits `$$runtime.postUpdate($$sig_x, $$set_x, +1)`, which holds the old value across the write. **A runtime call rather than an inlined arrow, and the first attempt is why**: the inlined form starts with `(`, this house writes no semicolons, and a statement beginning `(` continues the line above it — `@frontierjs/ui`'s form fixture has `done++` under a `throw new Error('rejected')`, which parsed as a call on the Error and never ran on either path. Caught by one assertion in that package's 857-case browser drive. **Five tests pinned the wrong output and are why it survived** — every one asserted the emitted TEXT, and the two things wrong here are only visible in a VALUE. `test/emission.test.js` now builds a component, clicks it and reads `1,1,12,12` off the page; the text tests are updated beside it. mesa 1295 vitest + 82 + 47 green |
| <a id="fjs-484"></a>FJS-484 | sierra — **a Vite hash containing a hyphen was not recognised as a hash, so hashed assets were served `must-revalidate` instead of `immutable`** | 2026-08-24 | `example`'s site build emitted `island-CatalogList-C_TQPJ-f.js` and `verify:site` went red on a cache assertion that had passed for months — the drive reads the FIRST `.js` in the assets directory, and directory order changes when the files do, so which asset it graded was luck. The pattern was `/-[A-Za-z0-9_]{8,}\.[a-z0-9]+$/`: the hash is base64url and may contain a `-`, and one that does was read as an unhashed name. The cost is quiet — a content-addressed file revalidated on every visit, on the files a site is mostly made of. Anchored on LENGTH now (`/-[A-Za-z0-9_-]{8}\.[a-z0-9]+$/`): a `-` exactly eight allowed characters before the extension. That still refuses `my-file-name.js`, which is the direction that matters — a name wrongly called hashed is cached for a year and the only way back is to rename the file — and a build configured for a longer hash falls out and is revalidated, which is the safe way to be wrong. **It was written twice**, in `site/serve.js` and `widget/serve.js`, and both copies had it. One owner now: `src/serve/hashed-asset.js`, which is the only thing the two servers share — every other cache answer they give is deliberately different. Two cases added to `tests/site-serve.test.js`, one per direction |
| <a id="fjs-471"></a>FJS-471 | mesa — **Nine compiler-injected builtin names emit invalid JavaScript when an author declares them with `const`, `var` or `function`, and the compile reports no error** | 2026-08-23 | The builtins are injected into the component init function as a run of `const` declarations — `const $option = { props: __props }`, `const $slots = $runtime.makeSlots(__block)`, `const $onMount = $runtime.$onMount` and their neighbours. An author who declares the same name at script top level gets a **second** declaration in the same scope, which is a duplicate-binding `SyntaxError`. **Measured** across four declaration forms × thirteen names, compiling and then parsing the output with acorn as `sourceType: 'module'`: `let` is safe on every name, because a reactive `let` is renamed to `$$sig_<name>` before emit; `const`, `var` and `function` each produce invalid output on nine — `$option`, `$slots`, `$onMount`, `$onDestroy`, `$onCleanup`, `$props`, `$attributes`, `$context`, `$emit` — for **27 silently-invalid combinations**. Negative control: `$zzz` and a plain identifier compile and parse in all four forms, and the compiler INTERNALS (`$runtime`, `$dom`, `$class`, `$parentElement`) are clean in all four, so the surface is exactly the injected builtin set and not the `$`-prefixed internals. **The compile reports `errors: none` in all 27**, which is Invariant 15 exactly — a clean compile is not proof of valid JS — so the failure surfaces as a parse error in generated code in the browser, pointing at a line the author did not write. `const $context = …` is the plausible trigger: an author aliasing the context object locally is a natural thing to reach for, and it is one of the nine. **`FJS-D132` retires this class by construction** rather than by patching it — one injected name instead of twelve, and a shadow of `$` is one of the three refusals that ruling already requires — but the fix is worth landing FIRST and on its own, because it is small, it is the only part of that work with a user-visible defect behind it, it builds the refusal machinery the ruling needs later, and it is valuable whether or not the namespace change proceeds. The test must PARSE the emitted output, not merely assert a clean compile; asserting the compile is what let this run. Filed S3 rather than S2 because the trigger requires the author to pick one of nine specific names; the cost once triggered is S2-shaped — silent at compile, loud and misdirected at runtime · **Fixed 2026-08-23.** `BUILTIN_LOCALS` names the thirteen injected names beside `RESERVED_WORDS`, and `analyzeScript` walks its own `ast.body` — not `vars`, which carries neither function nor class declarations, both of which collided — refusing every declaration form by name. It THROWS rather than pushing to `errors`, because the call site downgrades that whole array to warnings and a module that does not parse is not a warning. Scoped to the factory, so a nested `const $props` is still legal; over-refusing there would break code that never collided. Verified 65/65 refusals across five declaration forms including destructuring, with eight controls still compiling and parsing. Seven cases added to `test/emission.test.js`. Green: mesa 1233 vitest + 82 runtime-browser + 47 vite-browser, `@frontierjs/ui` 857 with 69/69 components opened. The surface was found to be 13 names rather than the 9 first measured — `$inspect` collides only when `debug !== false`, and `$ctxProvide`/`$ctxRead` only where `$context` is in use, which is why the reserved set is keyed off what the compiler MAY inject rather than what a given config does inject · [compiler.js](packages/mesa/src/compiler.js) · [emission.test.js](packages/mesa/test/emission.test.js) · [FJS-D132](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d132) · [IDEAS/mesa-dollar-namespace.md](IDEAS/mesa-dollar-namespace.md) |
| <a id="fjs-422"></a>FJS-422 | sierra — **Every MULTI-WORD model was unpublishable on a `render: static` route, and the refusal said the schema does not describe a model the schema declares** | 2026-08-23 | **Renumbered on filing** — a concurrent session issued the same number for an unrelated defect while this was being written. The static-safety gate taps what `load()` read and resolves each name through `modelNameFor`. Litestone's `$tapQuery` reports the TABLE, which is snake_case of the model name, and `registerSchemas` indexed four spellings — the model name, the accessor, `accessor + 's'` and the conventional plural — none of them the table. A one-word model resolved **by accident**, its table being its own accessor, which is why every fixture and every app in this repo passed: `product` → `Product`. `ProductVariant` is stored as `product_variant`, which resolved to `null`, and an unresolved read is `unknown`, and unknown **fails closed**. So `bun run build:public` in `example` refused the catalogue page with *read `product_variant`, which the schema does not describe, so its gate is unknown* — about a model on line 98 of `db/schema.lite` — and told the author to declare `publishes: 0`, which is the line that turns the check off. Failing closed is the right direction and is why this is not a leak; the cost is that the static target was unusable for any app with a two-word model, and that the message sent you to disable the gate. **Fixed 2026-08-23.** `registerSchemas` also indexes the snake_case table and its plural, deriving it the way `modelToTableName` does — restated in sierra rather than imported, because sierra may not depend on litestone (Invariant 1), and noted as not seeing `@@map`, which always wins on the Data side. The existing test asserted "resolves the table name the tap reports" using only one-word fixtures, so it passed throughout; the fixture now carries `ProductVariant` and `InvoiceLine`, and the second new case asserts that resolving the name does not turn the GATE off. Negative control taken before the fix: `modelNameFor('product_variant')` → `null`. 1041 sierra tests green; `example`'s `verify:public` 22/22 with the safety table printing `Product(0) ProductVariant(0)` |
| <a id="fjs-473"></a>FJS-473 | sierra — **the scanner plugin took the BUILD branch on every dev boot, crashed on its own warn path, and could not fail a build with the error it raises** | 2026-08-23 | Three defects in one function, found by `bun run dev:site` refusing to start with `ReferenceError: warn is not defined`. **(1)** `buildStart` read `this.environment?.mode !== 'serve'` to decide whether it was a build. Vite 8 reports `dev` there for a dev server, never `serve`, so the test was true in both and every dev boot ran the production-only `getStaticPaths` check. It reads `command` off `configResolved` now, which is `serve` or `build` and is the question being asked. **(2)** `checkStaticPaths` referenced `warn`, which is a parameter of `runScan` and not of it — so the one branch that mentions a companion it could not import threw a `ReferenceError` instead of warning. Passed through. **(3)** The `getStaticPaths` refusal sat INSIDE the `try` that guards the companion import, and `error` is rollup's `this.error`, which throws — so the refusal was caught by its own guard and reported as *could not import companion*, on a green build. Only the import is guarded now. That third one is `FJS-439`'s shape again: a static route that produced no page, warned about rather than failed. **The reason all three survived is in `tests/static-paths.test.js`, which restates the plugin instead of running it** — it scans the tree, imports the companion and builds the error message by hand, so every assertion passed against a function that was never called. `tests/scanner-plugin.test.js` calls `buildStart` through a context that behaves like rollup's (`this.error` throws, `this.warn` collects) and asserts the three: a dev server boots over a dynamic `render: static` route with no `getStaticPaths`, a build over the same tree fails naming it, and a companion that throws on import warns **with the cause** rather than crashing. The warning carries `err.message` now; without it the message read as the companion being absent when it is usually the companion's own imports throwing. Verified: `example` `dev:site` boots and answers 200, `build:site` prerenders 13 pages, sierra 1088 |
| <a id="fjs-472"></a>FJS-472 | junction — **the webhooks plugin signed `${timestamp}.${body}`, so a captured signature replayed against any other endpoint on the same receiver** | 2026-08-23 | Filed and closed in one pass, because it was found by reading `@frontierjs/toolbelt/signature`'s own header, which names this delivery path as one of the three signers it exists to unify — and the drift was not cosmetic. The old string bound neither the METHOD nor the PATH, so a subscriber trusting one secret across two endpoints could have a signature moved between them, and it carried no NONCE, so a repeat inside the freshness window was indistinguishable from a first delivery. Both are things a receiver cannot add from its side. It signs the canonical string now — `METHOD \n path \n timestamp \n nonce \n sha256(body)` — under its own `X-Webhook` prefix, which is what the prefix parameter is for. **The nonce is per ATTEMPT and that is the substance**: this plugin retries a delivery up to six times, so a nonce reused across attempts would make every legitimate retry look like a replay and dead-letter it against exactly the receivers that implemented replay protection properly. The event's identity is `x-webhook-id`, stable across attempts, and is what a receiver deduplicates on — two mechanisms, two lifetimes, the shape `verify:pay` already had to separate. The tests VERIFY now instead of recomputing, which is the whole point of one definition: five cases including a path swapped under a real signature and a clock an hour out. `tests/webhooks.test.ts`, 29 pass |
| <a id="fjs-461"></a>FJS-461 | frontierjs-vscode — **the language server's `parses clean` assertion counted a DECLARED standing warning as a parse failure, so `bun run ci` was red on a correct schema** | 2026-08-23 | The server was already right: it maps `result.errors` to Error and `result.warnings` to Warning. The TEST asked `diags.length === 0`, which conflates them. basecamp's schema legitimately raises one warning — the tenancy declaration infers a scope through a parent for fourteen models that carry no `workspaceId`, and `@@tenant(via:)` would narrow to ONE relation where seven of them have two, so declaring it drops rules rather than adding them. A warning the author cannot remove without making the schema WORSE is not a broken parse. Errors are graded; warnings are PRINTED as a note naming all fourteen models, because a standing warning that silently stops being reported is the other failure. This was the `tests` phase's only standing failure and was in neither this register nor `knownTestFailures`. `npm test` 43 pass |
| <a id="fjs-441"></a>FJS-441 | litestone · junction — **an ordinary `@unique` conflict reached the browser as a 500 carrying SQLite's own words and the physical table name** | 2026-08-23 | `UniqueConflictError` — **409**, `retryable: false`, naming the model and the field, values redacted the way the audit trail redacts them (a `@unique` column may be `@encrypted`, where the stored bytes are ciphertext and belong in a message even less than a plaintext). The channel is `errors` rather than `data`, matching `ValidationError`, so sierra's `toFieldErrors` puts the sentence under the control that caused it with nothing new to teach it. A composite says `this combination is already taken (team + slot)` per field, because `"red" is already taken` under `team` is FALSE on its own and the constraint is about the tuple. **The update path never translated at all and now does** — same defect, other verb, found by reading rather than by a report: `update`, `updateMany` and the `select: false` variant each reached SQLite's message untouched. `isUniqueConflict` recognises the translated error, because upsert's race fallback and the factory's rebuild-and-retry both ask it AFTER translation and would have stopped recognising their own case. Measured through junction over a real client: `tests/unique-conflict.test.ts`, 5 cases including that no response body contains `UNIQUE constraint failed` or the table name |
| <a id="fjs-389"></a>FJS-389 | sierra — **a route declared `title:` and the SPA never put it in the tab; only the static target did** | 2026-08-23 | `document.title` appeared nowhere in `packages/sierra/src` outside the prerenderer, so every route of an SPA showed whatever `index.html` hardcoded — one string for all of `example`, one for all of basecamp — and a screen reader announced the same document name on every navigation. The router reads the SAME two sources the static target reads, in the same order: `head({ params, data, url })` off the route's companion, then frontmatter. **Two decisions came with it, both stated rather than defaulted.** No template and no site name is appended, because the static half composes neither and two halves of one feature that disagree about where a title comes from is worse than the bug; an app wanting `Page · Acme` writes it in `head()`, which is the one place that can see both. And `title` stays an ordinary frontmatter key rather than joining `PAGE_RESERVED` — every example in the docs renders `{page.title}` in a heading, and claiming the name would empty them. A route declaring none puts the DOCUMENT's own title back, not the previous page's. A `head()` that throws falls back to frontmatter and warns in dev, where the static build refuses to emit the page — the page is already on screen here. `tests/document-title.test.js`, 6 cases |
| <a id="fjs-379"></a>FJS-379 | cli — **`fli list` piped to `head` died with an unhandled EPIPE, and every piped invocation was a coin flip** | 2026-08-23 | One `error` listener on stdout and stderr at the entry point, exiting quietly on EPIPE — not a guard per write, since every read-only listing has the same shape. **Measured both ways in a scratch directory with no workspace**: `fli list 2>err.txt \| head -3` left 980 bytes and two `Unhandled 'error' event` stacks before, and 0 bytes after, on every run. It survived because merging stderr into the same pipe hides it completely — the trace goes into the pipe that just closed — so it is only visible the way a person actually types it. `tests/pipe.test.js` captures stderr to its own file for that reason |
| <a id="fjs-360"></a>FJS-360 | cli · auth · junction · basecamp — **`AUTH_SECRET` was generated, declared and required across four packages with no reader anywhere** | 2026-08-23 | Settled by asking what there is to sign: a session issued by `@frontierjs/auth` is a ROW — `generateSessionToken()` is a random UUID stored on `Session` and verified by lookup — and an API key is hashed with `ENCRYPTION_KEY`. Nothing is signed, so the secret is removed rather than wired; sessions becoming stateless tokens is what would bring it back. Gone from `fli auth:install` (generation, `.env.example`, the env declaration), from `fli new`, from junction's generated `config/default.ts` — which wrote an `auth: { secret }` block `AppConfig` does not declare, into the one file people copy from — and from basecamp, where it was `required: true` with a public placeholder and refused a production boot over a value no code path would have used. **Junction's own doctor was checking for it**, failing apps that did not have one and telling them to add the key junction ignores; it grades `ENCRYPTION_KEY` now, including the trap that litestone parses it as HEX, so 64 characters is 32 bytes and non-hex padding decodes short. The name-based soft warning in `defineEnv` stays and now says why: it is a courtesy to an app holding its OWN signing key, never a sign the framework reads the value — reading it the other way is how this got built |
| <a id="fjs-444"></a>FJS-444 | litestone — **`access --from <ref>` answered *no change to who may do what* for a branch that added nine gated models** | 2026-08-23 | A model with no counterpart at the baseline has nothing to be compared against, so every per-model rule skipped it and it left the access axis entirely. It is a third bucket now — **`new`**, carrying what the model DECLARES rather than what moved: its `@@gate`, which operations have a row policy, how many protected fields, and the unrestricted case (`declares neither @@gate nor @@allow — every caller reaches every row`) leading, because that is the one worth stopping on. Ranked BELOW `narrows`, which is what keeps `--strict` meaning what it meant: a branch whose only findings are new models passes, one that also narrows still reads as narrowing, and nothing that used to fail now passes. The deploy axis is untouched — a new model is still an expand. A REMOVED model gets the same treatment in the other direction, graded `narrows`. Measured on `example`, which now reports its nine tables and their gates where it reported silence. `test/release.test.ts`, 6 cases |
| <a id="fjs-380"></a>FJS-380 | litestone — **`access --from` graded an allow→deny inversion as WIDENS, so the commonest tenancy refactor failed `--strict` on a change that narrows** | 2026-08-23 | An allow removed (widens) beside a deny added (narrows) is ONE change, and the classifier took the worst half. Recognised as an inversion now when the operation sets are EQUAL, and reported `undecidable` — the answer the module already gives for a predicate whose text moved, and for the same reason: `deny(!X)` admits what `allow(X)` did only when the two are complements, which is predicate algebra this deliberately does not attempt. **One finding on the access axis and two on the deploy one** — both halves are real to a deploy, and a reviewer reading the same change twice under two verdicts is how a 46-line report stops being read. A partial overlap is NOT an inversion: some operations inverted and some left bare is a mixed change, and grading the bare half as undecidable would hide a widening inside a refactor. Measured on basecamp's move to `tenancy { strategy row }`: **15 widen · 0 undecidable · 31 narrow, verdict WIDENS** → **0 widen · 15 undecidable · 16 narrow · 1 new, verdict UNKNOWN**. `test/release.test.ts`, 7 cases |
| <a id="fjs-469"></a>FJS-469 | litestone — **a baseline was graded by TODAY's validator, so a rule invented after it turned every older comparison into *no baseline*** | 2026-08-23 | Found the same day it bit: [FJS-437](#fjs-437) made `@@unique` over a nullable column a parse error, and every ref written before that commit stopped being a baseline — `release --from` and `access --from` both answered *no baseline*, and `--strict`, which fails on no baseline **by design**, would have failed every branch until the base ref moved past the fix. Every rule this parser learns is retroactive, so this was latent behind every tightened rule the project has ever added and would have fired again on the next one. The schema at that ref shipped; refusing to compare against it because it breaks a rule invented afterwards grades the past by today's law. A **validation** failure demotes to a named note now (`the schema at that ref breaks 1 rule this version enforces and shipped before they existed — compared anyway (…)`) and the comparison runs on the schema the parser built; a **syntax** failure still refuses, which is the honest line — validation rejects a schema the parser understood, and there is nothing to compare when it did not. `test/cli-smoke.test.ts`, both sides of that line, over a real git baseline |
| <a id="fjs-437"></a>FJS-437 | litestone — **`@@unique` over a NULLABLE column was accepted at parse and could not be enforced, so duplicates inserted in silence** | 2026-08-23 | Refused at parse now, beside the `@encrypted` check that exists for the neighbouring cause — a constraint declared, built, and unable to fire. The message names every nullable member and both answers: make it required with a `@default`, or declare `@@unique([…], nullsDistinct: true)`, which is SQL's own word for what SQLite does (Postgres 15 spells the choice `NULLS DISTINCT \| NULLS NOT DISTINCT`) and states the shape rather than inventing an escape hatch. It emits no different SQL, so the index a schema had before the declaration is the index it has after. **Composite only, and that asymmetry is the ruling** (`FJS-D130`): on one optional column `@unique` has a single reading — unique when present — and two of this repo's own columns are correctly that shape. Both in-tree composites were legitimate and neither had said so, which is the whole cost: basecamp's `User @@unique([accountId, username])` and litestone's `DailyStat @@unique([date, accountId])` now declare it. `test/unique-nullable.test.ts`, 7 cases |

Write the Note rows it counts instead`. **A test asserted the silence**, `@from: @from fields not writable — create ignores them`, which is how it survived being the one exception among six; it now asserts the refusal. No app-facing change: `@from` is already `readOnly` in the JSON Schema, so a generated form never offers it and `autoValidate` strips it before the Data boundary — this reaches code writing through the client directly. `test/from-correlation.test.ts` |

| <a id="fjs-424"></a>FJS-424 | mesa — **a derived `const` on the right of an assignment to a reactive `let` was emitted `$runtime.get($runtime.get(x))`, and the runtime CALLS a function it is handed** | 2026-08-23 | `rewriteExpr` is applied at more than a dozen sites and in several of them runs over text `rewriteAssignments` has already put it through — that function rewrites an assignment's right-hand side itself, and its callers then rewrite the whole statement. For a reactive `let` the second pass is harmless: the accessor is `$runtime.get($$sig_x)` and `$$sig_x` is not a name in the map. For a DERIVED the accessor is `$runtime.get(x)`, which still contains `x`, so it wrapped again. `runtime.get` calls a plain function it is given, so a derived holding a FUNCTION was invoked with no arguments and the result called — `$runtime.get(...) is not a function` at mount, half-built, naming neither file nor line — and a derived holding a VALUE silently produced the wrong one, which is the half nobody had noticed. **Fixed 2026-08-23** by making `rewriteExpr` idempotent: it returns without descending into a `$runtime.get(<Identifier>)` call, which is its own output. Idempotence rather than making every caller apply it exactly once, because six of `rewriteAssignments`' callers do not apply it afterwards and *apply this exactly once* is a rule nothing can check. **The shape on this row was wrong and the correction is the useful part**: it is not a nested callback, not `$:`, and not `find` versus `reduce` — a bare `pick = derived(1)` inside a plain function does it. What IS required is that the target be a reactive `let` (a plain local is clean) and the source be a derived (a reactive `let` read is clean). The four reductions recorded here as having failed almost certainly did reproduce it; a probe that reads the wrong key off `compileSource` reports every fixture clean, which is the trap this row cost a day to. `example`'s product page has all four workarounds removed and reads as it was written — `verify` 38/38, `verify:stock` 41/41, `verify:catalogue` 20/20 (including the swatch click, which is the code that was worked around), `verify:cart` 27/27. 8 cases in `test/derived-double-wrap.test.js`, 5 of which fail against the previous compiler. | <a id="fjs-466"></a>FJS-466 | litestone — **a CHECK constraint was never compared, so an enum gaining a member reached the DDL, the snapshot and every generated form, and was refused at runtime by the live database** | 2026-08-23 | `migrate.js`'s own header has listed *change CHECK* as a full-rebuild trigger since the file was written, and nothing implemented it: `diffColumns` compares type, notnull, pk, default and generated, `needsRebuild` adds dropped columns, foreign keys and STRICT, and a CHECK is on none of those. SQLite has no ENUM type, so litestone enforces one with `CHECK (col IN (…))` — frozen at CREATE TABLE and never migrated. **Measured** while adding `refunded` to `example`'s `PaymentStatus`: the DDL snapshot regenerated with the new member, `fli check` was clean, the app booted, and the first webhook to write it died on `CHECK constraint failed: status`, rolling back the whole transactional method — so the ledger row and the payment update vanished together and the drive's symptom was *the order did not move*. Reproduced in nine lines and both directions probed rather than assumed: **narrowing is NOT the fail-open twin it looks like**, because litestone's own validator refuses a value the enum no longer declares before any SQL is built, so only the widening direction is stuck. `@gte`/`@lte`/`@length` emit no CHECK at all (validator-only), which is why this is an enum defect and not a constraint-wide one. Sibling rather than conjunct is the whole of it — a per-clause AND is complemented by the caller's own `NOT`, and `NOT ((pred) AND (salary > X))` is the same oracle with a minus sign, which the suite asserts. A row-dependent predicate therefore still narrows to the caller's own rows rather than refusing. **The cost is stated rather than discovered**: an `OR` branch naming the column narrows the whole read, including branches that do not name it — safe (fewer rows, never more), blunt, and two reads is the way out. Precision there is what the rejected alternative, masking the column with a `CASE` at its reference, would have bought; it was rejected because a masked expression carries params and a single clause repeats the column an unbounded number of times. | <a id="fjs-388"></a>FJS-388 | cli · litestone — **the development workflow wrote tables and the deploy workflow applied files, and the two never met** | 2026-08-23 | Every generator told a developer to run `fli db:push`, which diffs the schema against the live database and creates tables directly, writing no migration file. A model added through push was in the developer's database and in no file, so the next image was missing it: `migrate apply` printed `no migration files found` with a TICK, exited **0**, `/health` answered 200, the deploy was declared good, and `POST /auth/register` answered 500 `no such table: user`. A table-granular guard on 2026-08-22 was reported as most of the fix and **was not**: re-measured 2026-08-23, it compares declared table NAMES to `sqlite_master`, so a new *model* is caught and a new **column** is not — history at `User{id,email}`, a pushed `name` column, `✓ 1 migration applied`, exit 0, over a table with no `name`. A column add is the common change after week one, so the guard passed exactly the traffic it was written for. Worse, **the advice it printed was a closed loop**: *write one: `litestone migrate create`*, and that command answered `already in sync — no migration needed`, because `create()` diffed the schema against the LIVE database, which a pushed database already matches. No way out from inside the tool. **Ruled and fixed as [FJS-D123](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d123)**: one comparison was doing two jobs, so there is now a SHADOW — the migration files replayed into memory — and two questions: *schema ↔ shadow* (what migration is missing) and *shadow ↔ live* (has somebody changed the database). `migrate create` diffs against the shadow, so the delta a push hid is written; the deploy guard is schema-granular over `diffSchemas`, so a column is caught; `migrate baseline` records files as applied without running them, refusing when the database does not actually hold what they build; `migrate dev` is create + apply + drift-check, which is what makes `db push` prototyping-only rather than a workflow a deployed app lives in; and `migrate check` asks the deploy's question of the REPO alone — no database, no container, no network — so `fli deploy:doctor` asks it before the image is built and `migrate apply` asks the same function again at container start. `fli check` carries the structural half. Prisma's shadow database and `migrate resolve --applied` are the prior art, and ZenStack v3 is the sharper precedent: it rewrote Prisma's ORM on Kysely and did **not** rewrite its migration engine. 19 cases in `test/migration-history.test.ts`, 4 in the cli's `checks.test.js`. ONE locale by default (`en-US` + `narrowSymbol` gives the bare `$`/`£`/`€`), since the currency's own home locale answers `US$28.00` for dollars read from London — correct, and not what a price tag says. `example` gained a currency preference on top of it, defaulting to USD, and the conversion is against a FIXED table stated in `web/src/money.js` with the rate printed under the control: a toggle that changed only the symbol would show one number as two different amounts, and a real shop takes a rate from a provider and records which one an order was charged at. 22 spec cases including the NO-BREAK SPACE Intl puts after an unrecognised code, which is what makes an assertion fail against two strings that look identical in the diff |

Anything rendering the result of its own save showed a stale *last updated*, anything caching the returned row cached a value already wrong, and junction hands that row to the HTTP response AND the `svc updated` broadcast, so every open tab took the stale one too. **Fixed by stamping in the STATEMENT rather than reading back off the trigger**, which is the half a re-read could not beat: the re-read was the other candidate (the shape `hydrateFromFields` uses for `@from`) and it costs one extra `SELECT` on every update of every model that has an `updatedAt` — which is nearly every model in every app — where naming the column in the `SET` clause costs nothing and makes `RETURNING` simply correct. The trigger stays and is now the floor: it covers raw SQL and a migration, neither of which comes through the client, and it stands down for a statement that already named the column because `NEW` is no longer `OLD`. Both halves write `NOW_SQL`, exported from `ddl.js` beside `updatedAtFields`, so the two cannot spell the timestamp differently and a row stamped either way sorts with the other. Wired at every path that issues an `UPDATE` and hands back a row — `update` (both the `RETURNING` and the `select: false` branch), `updateMany`, `upsert`'s fast path, `upsertMany`'s `DO UPDATE`, the soft-delete `remove`/`removeMany`, and `restore` — and NOT at the cascade statements that soft-delete a CHILD table, which return nothing and stay the trigger's. **`@@external` answers no stamp columns at all**, in `updatedAtFields` itself so the client and the DDL cannot disagree: litestone emits no DDL for that table, so there is no trigger, and a client stamp with nothing behind it is a silent write into somebody else's table. Two consequences worth naming: an `@@log(audit)` `after` snapshot now carries the timestamp the row actually has, and a post-update policy REVERT now restores the original stamp instead of leaving it moved by a write that was denied. 17 cases in `test/updated-at.test.ts`, one per write verb asserting the returned row equals a re-read — which is the anti-rot mechanism, since a new write path that forgets goes red · [client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) `stampSets` · [ddl.js](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) `updatedAtFields` · [test/updated-at.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/updated-at.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-394"></a>FJS-394 | litestone — **`@updatedAt` bound to the field NAME and ignored the attribute, so the timestamp on any field not called `updatedAt` never moved** | 2026-08-23 | `createUpdatedAtTrigger` matched `f.name === 'updatedAt' && f.type.name === 'DateTime'` and never read the attribute list, so `touched DateTime @updatedAt` compiled, read as though it worked and stood still — while a field named `updatedAt` carrying no attribute got the trigger anyway. Decorative wherever the two agreed, a silent no-op wherever they did not, and invisible by construction: `@updatedAt` implies a DEFAULT, so every row carried a plausible timestamp that had simply stopped advancing, and a screen ordering by *recently changed* was wrong with nothing to see. Fixed by driving the trigger off the attribute and keeping the name match as a FALLBACK — a schema relying on the name would otherwise lose its trigger on upgrade, which is the same data-freshness regression in the other direction. **The fix uncovered a second one, which is why it is one trigger per TABLE rather than one per column**: SQLite blocks a trigger from firing itself but not from firing a SIBLING, so with a trigger each, the first one's nested `UPDATE` re-entered the second, which saw its own column unchanged *by that inner statement* and overwrote the value the caller had just named — measured, a write setting one stamp column by hand had it replaced by `now()`. Inside one trigger the re-entry cannot happen, so each column carries its own guard in the `WHERE`. The one-column body is emitted byte-for-byte as before, so no existing app migrates a trigger that does the same thing. 9 cases in `test/updated-at.test.ts`, including the DDL shape and the trigger-name stability. [FJS-396](#fjs-396) — the value a write RETURNS being the pre-trigger one — is a separate defect and stays open; the last case here pins it so the day it changes is a red test · [ddl.js](packages/litestone/src/core/ddl.js) `createUpdatedAtTrigger` · [test/updated-at.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/updated-at.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-428"></a>FJS-428 | junction — **an app could not send a caller-varied header over the socket at all, and the one value that needed it was hardcoded on both sides** | 2026-08-23 | Over HTTP a value the caller varies per call is a header. The workspace had needed this since it was written, so it was built as one name — `meta.workspaceId` on the frame, lifted to `ctx.client.headers['x-workspace-id']` on the server — with a comment saying "ONE key, deliberately" because merging a client-supplied header map wholesale would let a frame carry its own `Authorization`. Client side: `createJunctionClient({ callHeaders })` and `setCallHeader(name, value)`, lowercased, riding real headers over HTTP and `meta.headers` over the socket; `setWorkspace` is now one of these rather than its own spelling, and the old `meta.workspaceId` is still accepted server-side so an older client keeps working. Found needing it for `example`'s guest basket, where the token is a bearer capability that comes into existence after the socket is already up: undeclared, the shop works until the WebSocket connects and then every basket call answers 404. 7 tests in `tests/call-headers.test.ts`, one of them a real app and a real socket; proven end to end in a browser by `example`'s `verify:cart`, which asserts the connection is live before it adds anything |
| <a id="fjs-427"></a>FJS-427 | junction — **an app that configured CORS through config silently lost all three of junction's own protocol headers** | 2026-08-23 | `cors()` carried them in the DEFAULT for its `headers` option — `X-Service-Method`, `X-Workspace-Id`, `Idempotency-Key`, the three the browser client sends unasked — which is the same thing as carrying them always, right up until an app states a list of its own. `defaultConfig` populates `http.cors.headers` with three unrelated names, and `applyConfiguredCors` passes `c.headers` straight through, so **every app configuring CORS through config replaced the default and kept none of them**. Cross-origin the effect is total and silent: the preflight refuses the header, the request never arrives, and an app whose socket is up never notices because the HTTP path is only the fallback. Fixed by making them additions rather than a default — `PROTOCOL_HEADERS` is unioned with whatever the app declared, case-insensitively deduped — and `callHeaders` ([FJS-428](#fjs-428)) is unioned the same way, so one declaration covers both readers. What comes back is a plausible WRONG SHAPE — `example`'s basket service assembles a cart with its lines, its count and its total, and the caller silently received the raw `Cart` row, so the basket screen rendered empty with a 200 and no error anywhere. | <a id="fjs-434"></a>FJS-434 | litestone — **an `open` value set with a `@@scope` reported SQLite's `UNIQUE constraint failed` for a value the list had deliberately stopped offering** | 2026-08-23 | A value missing from a NARROWED set is two different things and only one of them may be added by typing it: one nobody has ever used, and one the list has retired. `open` treated both as missing and created a row, so the second hit the source model's own `@unique` and the caller was handed `Could not add "Ochre" to ProductColour: creating a Colour for it failed — UNIQUE constraint failed: colour.name` — SQLite's sentence, about a table the caller did not name, saying the opposite of what happened. **Fixed 2026-08-23.** On the `open` path, a set that declares a `scope` or a `where` asks a second read WITHOUT the narrowing before creating anything; a value that comes back is refused as `<value> is in <Source> but is not offered by <Set>`, a `ValidationError` in the same shape every other rule throws, so it renders under the control.
