| <a id="fjs-361"></a>FJS-361 | repo — **Test harnesses leaked their temp directories and their browsers, and neither had a moment it could clean up in** | 2026-08-22 | Re-measured before touching anything and it had grown from 5.8GB to **26GB**, so the row was right about the class and wrong about the size of it. Three separate leaks under one cause — *nothing here has a moment at which it can delete what it made.* **The directories.** `process.on('exit')` does not fire under `bun test` and an `afterAll` races the audit logger's flush ([FJS-362](#fjs-362) measured both), so a run reaps the PREVIOUS runs' on the way in — the one moment the owner is provably gone, past an hour's floor so a concurrent run is untouched. `packages/litestone/src/tmp-dirs.js` is the one owner and `@frontierjs/litestone/testing` exports it; auth's copy is gone, notifications' `removeAtExit` was the dead pattern verbatim, and junction's three migration dirs were hardcoded `/tmp/` rather than `tmpdir()`. It cannot live in `@frontierjs/toolbelt` — a filesystem sweep, and that package does no I/O by ruling ([FJS-D26](#fjs-d26)) — and mesa may not import it either, being the leaf, so its drive says so where it keeps its own. **The biggest one was in SHIPPED code and nobody had counted it**: `makeTestClient` made a directory per test and removed none, **28,546 of them, 3.8GB**, in every app that uses `createTestEnv` and not just here. The scattered ones are worse to maintain than to fix, so litestone's suite and sierra's nine test files now write under ONE root each — one prefix to sweep, so a test added later cannot leak a name nobody listed. Proof is a full 2,839-test rerun with every one of thirteen prefixes flat: `ls-onlog` 153→153, `ls-redact` 72→72, `in-policy`/`ord-policy`/`tern-policy`/`tx-event`/`mig-*` unchanged. **The browsers, which this row never named.** **19 headless Chromes were found alive, reparented to init, the oldest 6.7 days old, holding 5.0GB of RAM and profile directories that were still growing.** A drive that reaches `close()` tidies up; one that throws or takes a Ctrl-C left Chrome running, and Chrome does not notice its launcher has gone. Signal-safe teardown in mesa's `drive.mjs` (which `@frontierjs/ui` reads by relative path, so both kits get it) and in litestone's two studio drives, whose `cleanup()` killed the studio server and had never touched the browser at all. **And the profile removal was failing the other way round, which is why it was invisible**: the `rmSync` SUCCEEDS and Chrome, still winding down, writes the directory back — measured at 0ms it was on disk again 1.5s later with 16MB in it, at 200ms it stayed gone. So `maxRetries` could never have covered it; the removal is not what fails. Every drive run had left a full profile behind for as long as the file has existed — 174 of them, 1.8GB. Two mesa drives and the ui drive now leave **0**. **`scripts/scaffold-build.mjs`** was the last one and is not a harness: both phases scaffold a ~300MB app and remove it in a `finally`, which a Ctrl-C does not run, and `bun run ci` is a three-minute command people interrupt — 1.6GB of it. Trapped, plus the same reap for the SIGKILL no handler sees. **/tmp is 12GB from 26GB**, and 8.4GB of what is left is Claude's own scratchpads — [tmp-dirs.js](packages/litestone/src/tmp-dirs.js) · [drive.mjs](packages/mesa/test/browser/drive.mjs) · [sierra/tests/tmp.js](packages/sierra/tests/tmp.js) · [scaffold-build.mjs](scripts/scaffold-build.mjs) |

| <a id="fjs-423"></a>FJS-423 | litestone — **`@default(cuid())`, `@default(ulid())` and `@default(nanoid())` generated nothing, so a required column declaring one could not be inserted at all** | 2026-08-22 | Only `uuid()` has a SQL DEFAULT `ddl.js` can emit, and the client generated the other three for the **`@id` field alone** — `buildAutoIdMap` skipped any field carrying no `@id`. So a bare `String @default(cuid())` parsed, emitted a plausible table, and failed `NOT NULL constraint failed: <table>.<column>` at the first `create()`. **Fixed 2026-08-22.** A second map beside the id one, `buildGeneratedDefaultMap`, covers every non-id column declaring `cuid`/`ulid`/`nanoid`, applied on all four write paths that massage a payload themselves — `create`, `createMany`, the `upsert` fast path and `upsertMany` (the slow `upsert` delegates to `create`). `uuid()` is deliberately **not** in that table: SQLite fills it from the column DEFAULT, and one owner per value is the point. **Key PRESENCE decides, not `== null`** — a stated null on a nullable column stays null, which is exactly what a SQL DEFAULT does, so `@default(cuid())` and `@default(uuid())` now answer alike in both directions; the id path keeps `== null`, because an id is never legitimately null. Measured the moment `example`'s caravan `jobsDir` moved into its config file ([FJS-415](#fjs-415)): `junction jobs` wrote **"A queue is installed and no handlers are registered. Every dispatch this app makes will find nothing to run it"** for an app with three handlers and a nightly cron — the exact failure that artefact exists to make visible, produced by the artefact itself. The phase body is `app.applyConfigFile()` now, and `tools/app-module.ts` calls it explicitly before `_startForTest()`, in the position production runs it — the same shape as the `autoloadServices` workaround already sitting beside it for the same reason. | <a id="fjs-421"></a>FJS-421 | junction — **the devtools page was cacheable, so it loaded from cache while its own server was down and read as a broken console** | 2026-08-22 | The page is served BY the socket's server, which makes it the one place a cached copy is actively misleading: `Bun.file` was returned with a Content-Type and nothing else, and a 200 with no `Cache-Control`, no `ETag` and no `Last-Modified` is heuristically cacheable — so with the API stopped the console still loaded, the socket could not connect, and the only symptom was a badge reading `disconnected`. That reads as *the console is broken* when what it means is *the app is not running*. `Cache-Control: no-store` on the page, and the badge names the retry (`disconnected — retrying`) so a dead server does not look like a dead page either. The drive asserts both the header and that the socket actually reaches `wsbadge live` — 25 assertions; it had been asserting the panels rendered without ever asserting the connection behind them. Verified against a live basecamp, and the console survives a `bun --watch` restart with no `EADDRINUSE` \| 2026-08-22 · [devtools/index.ts](packages/junction/src/plugins/devtools/index.ts) · [admin.html](packages/junction/src/plugins/devtools/admin.html) |
| <a id="fjs-420"></a>FJS-420 | junction — **the devtools console compiled every unhandled socket frame as JavaScript** | 2026-08-22 | `(h[m.type] \|\| Function)(m.data)` in `admin.html`. `Function` was written as a no-op fallback; as a VALUE it is the constructor, so an unknown frame type compiled its own payload as source. `String({})` is `"[object Object]"`, which parses as an array literal, so the browser threw **`missing ] after element list`** — with the `[` reported at line 3 of a function body nobody wrote. **The server sends eight frame types and the map handles six**: `call_start`, `hook` and `query` are forwarded for Sierra's toolbar waterfall rather than for this page, so on any app doing real work the console filled with parse errors, one per service call and one per query. Reported from a real basecamp session. Fixed to a named `NOOP` — ignoring an unknown type was the intent; compiling it never was. Not remotely exploitable as it stands (the frames are the server's own and their payloads are objects), but `new Function(<socket input>)` is the wrong construct regardless. **The drive could not have caught it**, which is the second half: it drove the console's REST API and never called the app, so the only frame the page ever received was the `state` snapshot on connect. It makes two real service calls now and asserts both the feed rows and that nothing was compiled as source — 23 assertions. **A third defect fell out of that run**: a leftover console from an earlier run held 8503, `ready()` errors are caught and logged by the start phase, so the app came up reporting `devtools=disabled` — a lie, it was asked for and failed — while the drive silently graded a PREVIOUS run's queue and reported half its checks red with nothing naming the cause. The plugin now catches the bind and reports `refused — port 8503 already in use`, which the banner prints, and the drive refuses a port that already answers rather than testing whatever is on it. | <a id="fjs-407"></a>FJS-407 | litestone — **a `@generated` column was invisible to the migration diff, so adding one to a live app emitted nothing at all** | 2026-08-22 | `introspect()` read `PRAGMA table_info`, and **`table_info` omits generated columns** — measured: on a table with two of them it returns `a` and nothing else, where `table_xinfo` returns `a(hidden=0), v(hidden=2), s(hidden=3)`. Adding, dropping, changing the storage and changing the expression were all equally invisible. `introspect` now reads `table_xinfo` and carries a `generated: { mode, expr }` per column; **the pragma is the authority on WHETHER a column is generated and the CREATE statement is the only place the expression lives**, so the expression is parsed off `sqlite_master` (`parseGeneratedColumns`, a depth-and-quote walk rather than a regex — an expression may hold a comma, a paren and a quoted string) and a name the parse missed answers `null`, which the diff treats as *cannot judge* rather than as *changed*. | <a id="fjs-397"></a>FJS-397 | ui — **`MultiSelect` reached parity with the component it replaced** | 2026-08-22 | Four gaps, all closed. **(1) ranked matching** — both this and `CommandPalette` now rank through `@frontierjs/toolbelt/search`; the palette had no ordering at all, so typing a command's own name could leave it below others that merely mention the word. **(2) highlighting** — the matched run is marked through `segments()` and the component's own elements, never an HTML string, so a label out of a database row cannot inject; ranges are per FIELD, because a flat list marks the label at offsets belonging to the sub line. **(3) the >N collapse** — `collapseAfter={n}`, off by default because collapsing changes rendering for every caller that already has one on a screen; the chip is a `<button>`, being the only route back to the tokens. **(4) granular events** — `onadd` / `onremove` / `oncreate` beside `onchange`, which alone could say the selection is now X and never what MOVED. **The ordering is the substance and the test found it wrong**: the first implementation fired `onchange` from `commitValue` before the granular events, so a listener persisting an `allowNew` value was handed a changed selection naming a row the server had never heard of — `commitValue(next, announce)` now announces what moved, then what the selection is. `clearAll` fires `onremove` per value, so *this value left* stays true exactly when the most values leave at once. **Out of scope by decision, not omission**: VirtualList (Mesa has native support and it belongs there), and the `<option>` slot as a source (it rode a hidden native `<select>`, and `<Form>` is `novalidate` with `nativeValidationGuard`, so native participation is decided against here). | <a id="fjs-399"></a>FJS-399 | ui — **`MultiSelect` in single mode accumulated instead of replacing, and committed the FIRST pick forever** | 2026-08-22 | Found fixing [FJS-398](#fjs-398), in the function being rewritten for it. `add()` did `selectedValues.add(opt.value)` in both modes and `commitValue` read `[...selectedValues][0]`, so a single-mode control that already held a value kept it at index 0 — choosing a second option grew the set, changed nothing on screen, and wrote the original value back. | <a id="fjs-398"></a>FJS-398 | ui — **`MultiSelect`'s clear-all ignored `min`, so one click reached the state `min` forbids** | 2026-08-22 | The guard moved to `commitValue`, the one place the selection changes: every action now proposes a `Set` and that function decides, so a fourth mutator cannot arrive unguarded the way `clearAll` did. **The bound is asked about DIRECTION, which is the half a straight move gets wrong** — `min` is a floor on the finished selection, not on every intermediate state, so refusing every state below it refuses the first add and a `min={2}` control can never be filled from empty. A change is refused only when it moves the wrong way past a bound; a selection already outside one may always move back. Two mutants killed: dropping the floor check fails *clear-all is refused at min*, and the direction-blind version — literally what the issue proposed — fails *an add below the floor is allowed*. The second mutant is why the fix is not the one-liner it looked like. 8 cases in a new browser spec over three controls, asserting the committed VALUE rather than token counts, since a value the view does not show is exactly the shape of [FJS-399](#fjs-399) beside it. · [MultiSelect.mesa](packages/ui/components/forms/MultiSelect.mesa) `commitValue` · [multiselect-bounds.spec.mjs](packages/ui/test/browser/specs/multiselect-bounds.spec.mjs) |

The reads are declared beside the model too: `detailQuery` is what `get(id)` asks for when the caller states none, the sibling of the `optionsQuery` that already existed and was passed by nothing in this tree. Named `detailQuery` because `query` means filters at every other boundary here. | <a id="fjs-374"></a>FJS-374 | junction · basecamp — **`createApp({ principal })`, `membershipClaim()`, and basecamp onto declared tenancy** | 2026-08-22 | The work [FJS-D113](#fjs-d113) ruled. **junction**: one resolver per service call, composed INSIDE `withLitestoneDb`/`withTenantDb` — the ordering the whole thing turns on and the one an app cannot arrange for itself, because `getTable()` re-derives its own scoped copy from `ctx.auth.user` and drops anything that lived only on `ctx.locals.db`. `applyClaims` refuses `userId`/`id` by name (a claim says what a caller HOLDS, not who they are) and does not run for an anonymous caller (minting a principal out of claims turns *nobody* into *someone*). `membershipClaim()` is the battery, and its whole safety is one line: no row is no claim. 15 tests against a real Litestone client, because the failure that matters — a caller scoped into a tenant they do not belong to — looks exactly like success to a stub. **basecamp**: sixteen hand-written `@@allow` lines and the `applyStanding`/`withWorkspaceStanding` machinery behind them, deleted. Eight `@@tenant(none)` by name, fourteen `@@tenant(via:)` — **17 models with row policies became 31**, because the twenty-two models carrying no `workspaceId` had nothing to hand-write and were relying on a parent. `findScoped`/`getScoped` no longer restate the workspace clause and `stampWorkspace` is `deriveSlug`, the declaration having desugared the `@default(auth().workspaceId)` that seven services had each written into a stamper of their own. 132 tests, `verify` **301/301**, typecheck at baseline, 19 snapshots current. **Both things the row said to prove, proved**: a caller naming a workspace they are not in gets a sentence and a 403, and every model without the column names its parent or its exemption. **It found five defects, four in the tenancy feature itself and none of them findable from inside the package that owned them** — [FJS-378](#fjs-378), [FJS-381](#fjs-381), [FJS-382](#fjs-382), [FJS-383](#fjs-383), with [FJS-380](#fjs-380) still open. **Not closed by it**: forty-two `workspaceId: ws()` clauses survive inside service methods, each redundant against the declaration but each needing its accessor checked against the eight exempt models first, and `example` has not been given the paved road |
| <a id="fjs-383"></a>FJS-383 | junction — **`tenantClaimGuard` answered 401, so naming a workspace you are not in signed you out of the one you are** | 2026-08-22 | Found by basecamp's browser drive, where the two checks that outlived the tenancy migration were both a `403` that had become a `401`. The guard refuses a signed-in caller holding no tenant claim on a row-scoped service — correct, and the status was not: **401 means *you have not proved who you are*, and the caller has**. Every client is built to treat it as a dead token, junction's own included, so a member of A following a link into B is not refused, they are logged out of A. Two refusals wear the same empty principal and they are not the same sentence — *nothing in this app emits this claim* is a developer's, *you do not belong to the tenant this request names* is the caller's — so `applyClaims` marks that a resolver ran (`ctx.locals['principal.resolved']`) and the guard branches on it. **403 in both branches**; the developer sentence keeps every word it had. | <a id="fjs-382"></a>FJS-382 | litestone — **A delegated child whose parent is OPTIONAL was invisible to every scoped read** | 2026-08-22 | `@@tenant(via: parent)` compiles to a `check(parent)` policy, and the two implementations of that one rule disagreed about a null foreign key: `evalCheck` answers **true** (there is no parent to violate the rule), `compileSql` emitted a bare `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM parent WHERE …)`, which is **false**. So a row that legitimately has no parent yet — a draft, an unassigned record, anything whose relation is `Parent?` — was filtered out of every read while litestone's own `verifyRowPolicies` graded the policy correct, because that checker skips `check()` by name. Surfaced as four dashboard widgets in basecamp going empty with a 200 and no error anywhere. Fixed in the SQL half (`FK IS NULL OR EXISTS (…)`), because the JS evaluator is the one that matches what the declaration says. | <a id="fjs-381"></a>FJS-381 | litestone — **`verifyFieldProtection` reported every field on a tenant-scoped model as unchecked** | 2026-08-22 | The checker seeds a row and then asks whether a protected column comes back — and its seeder satisfies the model's `@@allow` rules alone. Declared row tenancy generates a `@@deny`, which no amount of satisfying allows can get past, so the seed matched nothing and every `@encrypted`/`@guarded`/`@secret` column on a scoped model came back **unchecked**: a green run over an assertion that never executed, which is the failure mode a checker exists to prevent. It only appeared when basecamp moved sixteen models onto the declaration at once. | <a id="fjs-378"></a>FJS-378 | litestone — **`tenancy { strategy row }` let a caller move their own row OUT of their tenant** | 2026-08-21 | Found migrating basecamp onto the declaration it had been hand-writing for fifteen models, when its own tests started failing on the framework version of a rule they had passed for months. The generated rules were `read/update/delete` plus `create`, which asks *may you touch this row* and never *may the row end up there* — so `update({ where: { id: mine }, data: { workspaceId: theirs } })` succeeded, with the WHERE matching legitimately at the moment it ran, and the row landed in somebody else's tenant. Reproduced in twenty lines against a bare client: create INTO another tenant was refused, the move was `ALLOWED`, and the row read back in workspace 2. **The hand-written `@@allow('all', col == auth().claim)` never had the hole**, which is why nothing caught it: `all` expands to every operation including `post-update`, so an allow was graded against the RESULTING row for free, and the generated deny simply did not name that operation. The delegated form had the same gap one hop out — a child re-pointed at a parent in another tenant. **The machinery was already there**: `post-update` is in the parser's own operation list and `checkPostUpdatePolicy` runs after the write inside the transaction, so a violation rolls back. Both generated rules now carry it. Nine tests — the refusal, the row still in its own tenant afterwards, the delegated child, an ordinary edit that must still pass, and `asSystem()` still moving a row deliberately, which is how a support tool does it. litestone 2378 pass, 0 fail \| 2026-08-21 · [core/parser.js § desugar](packages/litestone/src/core/parser.js) · [test/tenancy.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/tenancy.test.ts) · [FJS-D05](#fjs-d05) · [FJS-282](#fjs-282) |
| <a id="fjs-d113"></a>FJS-D113 | litestone · junction — **where a tenant claim comes from when the caller has many, and where a row-shaped standing is resolved** | 2026-08-21 | **Ruled 2026-08-21.** The reframe is the ruling: a tenant claim and a per-request standing are one thing — claims on the principal, from one membership query, read by the tenancy predicate and by the gate. So there is no tenancy feature missing and no gate feature missing, only a seam that lets a claim be resolved **per request** rather than fixed at sign-in by `sessionFields` — which is why it costs **no new schema syntax**. Resolution is API-realm (`strategy database` already asks `tenantFor({host, headers, principal})`, and a header is transport convention, not a fact about data over its whole life), so a `tenancy { … from header(…) }` keyword is refused. One option, `createApp({ principal })`, Hook tier; junction owns the three constraints basecamp learned one at a time — compose inside `withLitestoneDb`, write the PRINCIPAL not just the client, a FRESH object because a WS session is frozen. **The claim IS the proof**: a resolver may emit the tenant claim only once membership is established, because under declared tenancy the version that does not check scopes a stranger into the tenant with a 200. The membership read therefore ships as a battery — `membershipClaim({ tenantFrom, model, subject, tenant, standing })` — and NOT as seed syntax: `@@tenant(via:)` already means *scoped through this parent* ([FJS-282](#fjs-282)), and a declaration would hard-code one model, one subject column and one standing column, which is false for membership through a team or a role that is a join. Does not run for background work, by name. Build is [FJS-374](#fjs-374) \| 2026-08-21 · [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md) § Access control · [IDEAS/membership-tenancy.md](IDEAS/membership-tenancy.md) |
| <a id="fjs-367"></a>FJS-367 | junction — **the shipped `paginate()` hook was inert in every app, and the fix is that it stops publishing a second copy of the page** | 2026-08-21 | Measured before and after. Before: `GET /things?$limit=5&$offset=10` through a service carrying `before: { find: [paginate(20, 100)] }` answered 200 with `ctx.locals.paginate = {"limit":20,"offset":0}` — the caller's page size silently ignored, on every request, for the life of the hook. `$` is transport syntax and the bridge is its one owner (Invariant 10): `splitParams` moves every `$` key onto `ctx.directives`, so `ctx.query.$limit` is never there past it. **Junction had already fixed this exact class once** — the comment above the split names `$limit / $offset / $orderBy / $select` as having been *"silently inert"* — and its own hook was not in that sweep; a grep for `$` reads off `ctx.query` now finds only `parseQuery`'s documented pre-directives fallback. **The fix is not "read directives instead", because that leaves the cause.** The hook NARROWS `ctx.directives` rather than minting `ctx.locals.paginate` beside it: two homes for one fact is what drifted, and narrowing is also what makes a ceiling reach anything, since a custom `find` handing `ctx.directives` to Litestone now gets it without threading a second value. `ctx.locals.paginate` is still written for the callers that read it, and is now typed as the same `Page` rather than a third spelling. **`clampPage` in `core/directives.ts` is the one answer to what a limit means** — the module imports nothing, and `parseQuery` calls it too: the hook used `parseInt` + `Math.min`, so `$limit=abc` gave it `Math.min(NaN, 100)` where the same request through a model service gave the default. A limit of 0 survives, being how a caller asks for the count alone. Worth knowing for anyone reading the old row: a MODEL service was never affected — `paginate: { default, max }` is a service option threaded into `parseQuery` correctly, which is why the breakage survived. Six tests in `tests/index.test.ts`; 1243 junction tests pass \| 2026-08-21 · [hooks-builtin.ts](packages/junction/src/core/hooks-builtin.ts) · [core/directives.ts § clampPage](packages/junction/src/core/directives.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-370"></a>FJS-370 | junction — **`ctx.locals.db` was declared and still unusable: every model accessor typed `unknown`** | 2026-08-21 | **Filed with the wrong cause and corrected by probing it.** The row said junction installs `ctx.locals.db` and declares nothing; it does declare it, in the litestone adapter's own module augmentation, and has all along. What a compile actually said: `ctx.locals.db?.asSystem()` typechecks, `ctx.auth.user?.userId` typechecks, and **`ctx.locals.db?.post.findMany({})` is `unknown`** — which is every actual data call. The client interface carried `[model: string]: unknown` beside a fully-written `LitestoneTable` the index signature never used, so an app got the `$`-methods typed, wrote `dbOf(ctx): any` to do any work at all, and lost the whole client's safety with it (basecamp's is imported by 22 modules). **The shape of the fix is the reason it stayed broken**: an interface's declared members must satisfy its own index signature, and `asSystem(): LitestoneClient` is not a table — so one interface could only ever say `unknown`. Split into `LitestoneClientApi` and intersected with `{ [model: string]: LitestoneTable }`, which the rule does not apply to. `LitestoneTable \| unknown` is not the fix and looks like it: a union with `unknown` collapses to `unknown`, the same trap [FJS-034](#fjs-034) hit on `ServiceDefinition`. **The cost is stated where it is paid**: any accessor name now resolves, so `db.pots.findMany()` compiles — traded knowing a Litestone client THROWS on an unknown property, so a typo is loud on the first call, and an app wanting it at compile time generates types and augments. `tests/context-db-types.ts` is compiled rather than run, so a regression is a `bun run typecheck` failure \| 2026-08-21 · [core/litestone.ts](packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts) · [tests/context-db-types.ts](packages/junction/tests/context-db-types.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-366"></a>FJS-366 | junction — **Every WebSocket connection was evicted after 30s, in every app, because the keepalive had a server half and no client half** | 2026-08-20 | The channels plugin evicted any connection that had not sent `{type:'ping'}` in 30s, sweeping every 10s. `JunctionClient.startHeartbeat()` sends that ping and **nothing called it** — not junction, not sierra, not `fli new`'s scaffold — so a fresh app opened a socket, received `{type:'connected'}`, and was closed with `connection evicted` ~35s later, forever. Reported from a scaffolded app; measured there as a 39.4s close and a reconnect ~1s after each. Invisible unless watched, because the socket carried no event and the reconnect succeeded: a live list simply never updated. Two things were wrong under it. **Only a ping refreshed liveness**, so a client in the middle of service calls over that same socket was evicted while plainly alive. And **the client's default interval was 30s against a 30s timeout**, so calling it would have raced the reaper anyway. Fixed by moving the drive to the SERVER — it pings an idle socket every 15s and evicts one silent for 40s, and any frame counts as liveness, malformed ones included. The client answers from its message handler rather than a timer, which is what keeps a backgrounded tab connected: browsers throttle timers to ~1/min in a hidden tab, slower than any eviction window, and a message handler is not throttled. `channels(setup, { heartbeatInterval, heartbeatTimeout })` are the knobs; `startHeartbeat()` stays for a client whose server predates this, now defaulting to 15s. `tests/heartbeat.test.ts` runs the loop against a real socket at 100ms/400ms — idle is pinged, silent is evicted, pongs hold it, ordinary traffic holds it, and the shipped client holds it with no call by the app |
| <a id="fjs-364"></a>FJS-364 | cli — **`fli admin:generate` wrote one `resources/admin.mesa` holding every model, and declared a second Resource over services the app already had** | 2026-08-19 | Two defects under one file. The rule half: `admin.mesa` is N Resources under a name no model has, refused by `resource-file-name` and `resource-one-per-file` — measured on a two-model fixture, both errors, both Invariant 19. The half that actually breaks an app: it called `createResource('users')` beside the app's own `resources/User.mesa`, which is **two stores over one service** with a live socket on each — a write through the admin leaves the app's list stale and the app's write leaves the admin's, and nothing anywhere says why. Both close together. The model index moved into the layout's `<script module>` (`admin/_module.mesa` exports `models`, the dashboard imports it from there) — a layout is already this surface's own module, and module exports are importable by any other module (Mesa VISION §11, rule 30). The admin now imports the app's own Resource per model and writes one only where none exists, from `fli make:resource`'s template, **never overwriting, `--force` included** — force is about the generated pages, which are disposable. **Both halves of an existing Resource are READ rather than guessed**, and the second half was found by running it: the export name (`Person.mesa` exports `people`, which no plural rule applied to `Person` finds) *and* the service string, without which the irregular generated `/admin/persons/` over a service called `people` — the data worked and the URL, the route folder and the `make:service` hint were wrong together. A resource file exporting no `createResource` is named and its model skipped, rather than generating a page whose import resolves to `undefined`. Verified end to end on a three-model fixture: hand-written `User.mesa` byte-identical after `--force`, `fli check` clean where it previously reported two errors |
| <a id="fjs-363"></a>FJS-363 | cli — **Five scaffolds wrote or read a resource file the repo's own `fli check` refuses** | 2026-08-19 | Invariant 19 names a resource file for its MODEL — PascalCase, singular — and the export for its SERVICE. `make:resource` and `web:resource` built the path as `service + '.mesa'`, `make:model --resource` as `${plural}.mesa`, so `fli make:resource Order` wrote `orders.mesa` and the user's next `fli check` called it an error. The consumer half was wrong with it: `make:route`/`web:route --resource` generated a page importing `resources/${service}.mesa`, a file the fixed commands now never write — and its *does this resource exist yet* probe looked for `${service}.js`, the wrong name AND the wrong extension, so the warning fired on every run including the correct one. Only `make:scaffold` had it right (`${model}.mesa`), which is exactly why CI was green: the `scaffold` phase runs `fli scaffold` and reaches none of the five. Fixed by naming the file for the model in all five, with the split stated in a comment at each site, since the import line legitimately spells the two differently on either side of `from`. `fli validate`'s worked-example comment was stale the same way and is corrected. | <a id="fjs-362"></a>FJS-362 | auth — **The test harness leaked every database it made** | 2026-08-19 | `removeAtExit` registered a `process.on('exit')` handler, and that handler **does not run under `bun test`** — probed with a one-test file that printed nothing from its hook and left its directory behind. | <a id="fjs-351"></a>FJS-351 | litestone — **A generated validation case did not isolate the rule it named, in both directions** | 2026-08-19 | `generateValidationCases` built every case from ONE attribute with no idea what else sat on the field. **A boundary claimed a value the field refused** — `@length(3, 200)` on an `@email` column produced `'xxx'`, so the write was refused and the runner reported *@length allows this value and the write was refused*. Measured on a six-field schema: 8 of 12 boundary cases false, 4 of 6 fields reported broken when nothing was, and the fix a reader reaches for is deleting a rule from the schema. **An invalid case was refused by somebody else's rule and counted as proof of its own** — `''` on that column is rejected by `@email`, so the two cases naming `@length` proved nothing about it. Measured by disabling `@length` in `validate.js`: on a single-rule field the runner says *the schema declares @length(3,20) and the write was ACCEPTED*, on the `@email` field it said **nothing**, and the only entries there were the two false alarms — which appear whether the rule is enforced or not, so the checker could not tell an enforced `@length` from a missing one, and the false alarm is what hid it. The same measurement now yields four findings, all naming `@length`, and zero on a schema with nothing wrong with it. `attempt()` also carried the message every case declared and never compared it; it does now, as a backstop for an implementation refusing for a reason the schema did not predict. Both closed against one judge: `validateField` is now exported from `core/validate.js` — the function that decides this on a real write — because a table of formats in the generator would be a second definition of every rule. **It is asked about the field's OTHER rules only**: whether a value satisfies or breaks the rule it NAMES is established by construction, and asking the implementation makes the runner its own oracle — the first version did, and disabling `@length` then reported *not checked* instead of *the write was ACCEPTED*, losing the finding the runner exists to produce. Caught by measuring rather than by reasoning. **The repair is format-blind**: it grows or trims the factory's own valid sample and asks the validators, so an email grows in front of its own domain and `@startsWith("ORD-")` keeps its prefix with no code here containing either word; shrinking works on alphanumeric RUNS and leaves punctuation alone, which turns `email1@example.com` into `e@e.c` at five. Which dimension is free depends on the rule — a boundary IS its length, an invalid `@length` case need only sit outside the bound, every other rule's length is incidental. What cannot be isolated is `uncheckable`: named, with the blocking rules' own messages, because a rule that stops being asked about is this runner's failure mode one layer up — and **`litestone mutate` excludes it from the kill count**, since a row generated from the original schema appears identically under every mutant, which is the shape the 22 `error` rows had when they scored a 14-mutant schema 93%. Five tests, and basecamp's `Invitation.email` carries `@email @length(6, 200)` again — 6 rather than 5 because `a@b.c` is the shortest string `@email` accepts, so a bound at the floor is unviolatable and the runner says so |
| <a id="fjs-032"></a>FJS-032 | basecamp — **No invitation flow: the setup wizard was the only door in for a human** | 2026-08-19 | `workspaces.addMember` takes a `userId`, so it could only reach somebody who already had an account, and the one route that makes one — `/auth/register` — leaves them with no account row and no workspace, after which every scoped request 400s and they cannot create a workspace either. Closed with `model Invitation`: an offer of membership to an EMAIL ADDRESS, carrying the workspace and the role across the gap where there is no user to hang them on. **The row IS the pending state** — no `acceptedAt`, no `revokedAt`; accepting writes a `WorkspaceMember` carrying `invitedBy`/`invitedAt` forward and deletes it, revoking deletes it, and `@@log(audit)` is what survives either way. Those three columns had been declared since the schema was written and nothing had ever written one. The token is `@guarded(all)`, so no scoped read can answer it and the link is shown ONCE, the shape an issued API key already had. **`invitations.preview` and `.accept` run with no session at all** and are exempt from `sessionScope` for the reason the population they serve is not a member yet and may not exist yet; everything a token cannot decide (unknown, expired, workspace gone, workspace suspended) is decided in one place because none of the hooks that normally decide it are running. An address that already has an account must be signed in as that account — not a password on this method, which would be a second login door with none of the first one's rate limiting and an oracle when it refuses — so `/login/` grew a same-origin `?next=`. **Mail goes through conduit** (`api/src/core/mailer.ts`): the provider is a declared target with a credential ref, and `app.mail` is ABSENT where none is configured, which is a supported state — the invitation still issues a working link and the screen says plainly that nothing was sent. | <a id="fjs-349"></a>FJS-349 | basecamp · conduit · junction — **The three endpoints an Outpost calls took no credential at all, and one of them redirected the fleet** | 2026-08-19 | Measured before and after, against a running API. Before: a POST carrying nothing but `X-Service-Method: heartbeat` answered **200**, moved a server `pending → online`, and registered the Conduit target `outpost:<serverId>` at `http://attacker.invalid:9999` — read back out of `conduit_targets` — which points every later `/exec`, `/deploy` and `/system/prune` for that machine at a host the caller owns, HMAC-signed with this app's own secret. Registered app-level rather than per service, so a new machine-facing method cannot inherit the sessionScope exemption without the signature. Fail-closed in every direction: no secret configured is a refusal, an in-process call with no `$raw` is a refusal, and the reason is logged and never returned. Proven five ways after: unsigned 401, signed 200, replay 401, signature moved to another path 401, body swapped under a good signature 401 — plus four tests in `api/test/services.test.ts`, eleven specs in toolbelt, and a drive check that an unsigned `cleanup.report` is refused. **One fleet secret, and the limit is stated in the code**: a compromised machine can forge another's check-in; per-server secrets need the mint and hand-over that ring 1 has not built |

Found writing the FJS-349 tests, where computing a signature is itself an await, so four correct tests failed and the natural next move is to doubt the code they were testing. Fixed by making it lazy — the promise is created by `then()`/`catch()` and memoised — and covered by a test that awaits between the two calls |

Bun ignores an inline source map and a linked `.map` too; all four measured, so neither runtime was ever going to answer this. **fli answers it instead, and the map is ONE INTEGER**: `transformMarkdown` turns prose into `//` comments rather than dropping it, and `stripScriptBlocks` now leaves the block's own height behind in blank lines instead of cutting them — which collapses the two offsets a mid-file `<script>` used to need into one. `compileCliWithMap` returns `genLine - mdLine`; `core/stack.js` is the one owner of the rewrite (a string pass over `err.stack` and its `cause` chain, not `Error.prepareStackTrace` — that hook is global and V8-shaped, and taking it means re-implementing default formatting for every frame that is not ours, on two runtimes that format differently). The pragma is gone and the body is no longer indented into `run()`, so columns are the author's too. | <a id="fjs-031"></a>FJS-031 | basecamp — **Every service published after every method, reads included** | 2026-08-19 | A `GET` broadcast the row it had just read to every browser in the workspace, from `after: { all: [publishToChannels(workspaceChannel(app))] }` on all seventeen services. `all` means all, and no per-service hook list can exclude reads for itself — junction decides what to announce in `callService`, once, and that place excludes `find`/`get` **by name**, because a custom method that only reads is indistinguishable from one that writes at that layer. So the hook is gone and each service DECLARES `channel: workspaceChannel(app)`, the same function value in the position junction reads it from. The swap cannot half-land: `refuseDoubleBroadcast` throws at construction if a service declares a channel and still runs a marked `publish()` hook, so a file edited halfway fails to boot rather than broadcasting twice. `surface.snapshot.md` carries the difference as *broadcasts on* per service and one fewer `after` chain. | <a id="fjs-154"></a>FJS-154 | basecamp — **The audit trail recorded that something happened, never what changed** | 2026-08-19 | `AuditEvent.diff` was `Json?` and nothing wrote it, so the trail could say a server was drained and not what state it was in. Which columns must not be written down comes from **`db.$protectedFields(accessor)`**, new in litestone — the third sibling of `$checkWhere`/`$checkOrderBy`, same contract, on every flavour of client — rather than a list of names in this app that goes stale the first time somebody adds a `@secret`. A protected field appears as `[redacted]` on both sides, because THAT it changed is the fact an operator needs. `updatedAt` is skipped (it moves on every write and says nothing), long text is cut, and Json columns are compared by value because identity reports every one of them as changed on every write. `/activity/` renders the fields with before → after behind a disclosure; two tests in `api/test/services.test.ts`, one check in the drive |

Horizontal padding stays per-component. **Every button in the repo is 4px taller**; the kit's 65 components, both apps and the guide were re-run. | <a id="fjs-337"></a>FJS-337 | junction · basecamp — **A service had no way to reserve a query key, so a documented `?workspace_id=` fallback was refused before anything read it** | 2026-08-18 | `$`-names are directives (Invariant 10) and everything else is graded against the model's columns, so there was no third answer: `autoFilter` answered *Unknown filter key 'workspace_id' — did you mean 'workspaceId'?* before the hook that reads it ran, and the app could not fix it from its own side either. **`reservedQuery: ['workspace_id']` on the service** is that third answer, and it is the query-side mirror of `@transient`: declared where it is owned, then LIFTED — the keys move to `ctx.reserved` and `ctx.query` is columns alone. Lifted in **`callService`, before the pipeline** rather than in a hook, because the app's own leading hook needs the clean query as much as the derived `autoFilter` behind it, and a custom method runs neither. | <a id="fjs-295"></a>FJS-295 | caravan — **A job handler that never returned held its slot forever and stalled the queue in silence** | 2026-08-18 | Three changes, separate on purpose. **`timeout`** on a handler, a job file or a queue bounds one attempt; absent means no bound, honestly, because a default would kill every legitimately long job in every app that upgraded. A queue-level default is resolved at REGISTRATION so `registrations()` reports the bound that will be enforced rather than the one the handler wrote. A timeout is an **ordinary failure** — it counts as an attempt and retries on the same ladder as a throw, rather than becoming a fifth status. **What it cannot do is stop the handler**, which the row named as the design question: nothing in JavaScript cancels a promise, so the abandoned invocation may still be writing while the retry runs. Delivery here was already at-least-once (`FJS-D35`) and this widens the window rather than opening it — both consequences are handled rather than left to chance, since its later rejection would otherwise be unhandled and take the process down, and its later SUCCESS is the most useful thing a person debugging this can be told (*the work you gave up on finished after 45 minutes*), so it is announced rather than swallowed. **`oldestRunningMs`** is the half for jobs nobody thought to bound, which is most of them: `running: 1` for the life of the process is exactly what a busy queue reports, and a count that never moves beside a climbing age is not — asked of the DATABASE, so another instance's stuck job is in the answer, and `null` rather than 0 where nothing runs. **`drainTimeout`** because a hardcoded 30s is the whole shutdown budget of a deployment whose SIGTERM grace is shorter. **Two whitelist bugs fell out**, the same shape twice: `defineJob` built its definition from a fixed key list and `autoload` re-listed a definition's keys back into `handle(name, fn, opts)`, so a `timeout` in a job file was accepted and dropped twice; autoload passes the definition WHOLE now (`JobRegistrar.handle(job)`), removing the third whitelist rather than adding a key to it. Found by [FJS-343](#fjs-343) printing `**none**` for a job that had just declared 30s — the artefact catching a defect the day after it was written, which is the case for `PHILOSOPHY.md` § *A projection must be legible* stated better than the argument for it did. A synchronous handler that never yields stays unreachable: the timer needs the event loop, same ground as `FJS-294`. 11 tests, four mutants killed · [worker.ts](packages/caravan/src/worker.ts) `_bounded` · [db.ts](packages/caravan/src/db.ts) `oldestRunning` · [autoload.ts](packages/caravan/src/autoload.ts) · [tests/timeout.test.ts](packages/caravan/tests/timeout.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-339"></a>FJS-339 | mesa — **A snippet parameter that is a destructuring pattern compiled and could not work** | 2026-08-18 | The argument arrives as a getter, so `{#snippet row([name, q])}` emitted `(__anchor, [name, q])` and destructured the accessor FUNCTION — `TypeError: function is not iterable`, thrown from the compiled file naming no snippet, the whole enclosing block rendering nothing. **`{#each}`'s answer does not transfer**: a block re-runs per item and a snippet's DOM is built once, so unwrapping the argument at the top of the body would compile, run, and freeze every name at its first value — the frozen-argument bug the getters exist to prevent (VISION §9.5). So the destructuring moves into the READ: one getter, and each bound name compiled to its own path through it (`$$arg0()[0]`, `$$arg0().id`), which keeps a subscription per name. A default, a rest element or a nested pattern is refused by name, because none can be read lazily. **Fixing it uncovered the same trap one layer down** — `_isReactive`'s bare-call heuristic excludes `$`-prefixed identifiers, so the new reads were classed STATIC and written once: the first render passed and every reassignment was ignored, which is the frozen-argument bug arriving through its own fix. Its own comment already recorded the identical miss for `{@const}`. Caught by a browser spec asserting the reassignment rather than the first paint; basecamp's Hub carries the natural spelling again |

The finding points at the ABSENT FILE rather than at the directory, since an allowance is keyed by path: `packages/css` is excused its `AGENTS.md` and is still asked for its `CHANGES.md`. Reports the eight it was written to find — `PROJECT_STATE.md` in conduit, jetty, sierra, testing, toolbelt and frontierjs-vscode, `CHANGES.md` in css and frontierjs-vscode |

| <a id="fjs-346"></a>FJS-346 | junction · caravan — **What an app runs on a clock had no reader, so a schedule that stopped being registered was invisible** | 2026-08-18 | The first artefact written against `PHILOSOPHY.md` § *A projection must be legible*. Every other snapshot here records a surface someone reaches; this one records work that starts with no request, no caller and no screen — the part of an app whose failure is silent by construction, since a route that stops working is a 404 somebody sees and a schedule that stops firing is *nothing happening*, which is indistinguishable from nothing needing to happen ([FJS-327](#fjs-327), [FJS-328](#fjs-328)). `junction jobs --app <module>` reads a BUILT app for the same reason `junction surface` does: a job file registers itself by being autoloaded and a plugin registers timers of its own. **Two registries, kept apart on the page** (`FJS-D36`) — `app.jobs` is durable, `app.scheduler` is a bare in-process timer running in every replica rather than once across them — and **no queue installed renders differently from a queue with no handlers**, because one app does no background work and the other will dispatch into a queue with nothing to run it. Both registries needed a reader before this: caravan's handler map was private and `nextRuns()` sees only the scheduled ones, and junction's scheduler discarded the source expression entirely (`every()` parses to ms, `cron()` compiles to a matcher), so `list()` answered `job_1` and the only record of WHEN was a closure. Live state is deliberately absent — a committed artefact that cannot hold still between two boots is worth nothing. **The CI phase needed no edit**: 17 → 19 snapshots, discovered and rerun from each file's own header. What it found immediately: `basecamp` declares **4 handlers and 0 schedules in code** — every schedule there is a database row registered at runtime, which is exactly the shape that made `FJS-327` possible and is now visible rather than assumed. 10 tests, four mutants killed · [jobs-snapshot.ts](packages/junction/tools/jobs-snapshot.ts) · [app-module.ts](packages/junction/tools/app-module.ts) · [caravan/src/index.ts](packages/caravan/src/index.ts) `registrations` · [scheduler](packages/junction/src/scheduler/index.ts) `describe` **Renumbered from FJS-343**, which had been issued twice. |

It does not reach `console.log`: Bun's console holds its own binding to the stream, so a `console.log` during the build wrote straight past the patch. Measured — `[Caravan] Loaded 3 job handlers: …` was line 1 of `junction jobs --stdout > file`, and the same hole was in `junction surface --stdout` from the day it was written, unnoticed because the repo's own snapshots are written with the default path rather than a redirect. Fixed by patching `log`/`info`/`debug`/`warn`/`dir`/`table` onto `console.error` for the duration and restoring them in `finally`. Found by building [FJS-343](#fjs-343) — a corrupted artefact is the exact failure the legibility corollary is about, so it turned up in the first hour of applying it · [app-module.ts](packages/junction/tools/app-module.ts) `quietly` · [tests/jobs-snapshot.test.ts](packages/junction/tests/jobs-snapshot.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-343"></a>FJS-343 | cli — **`fli new --auth` could not finish: `auth:install` handed its `litestone db push` an EMPTY `ENCRYPTION_KEY`** | 2026-08-18 | Two things stacked, and either alone is enough. The scaffold writes `.env` with a bare `ENCRYPTION_KEY=`, so bun puts an **empty string** on the `auth:install` process's environment at startup, and a child that already has the name set does not take `.env`'s value for it — the push reported no key while the file beside it held a good one. Step 6 existed to fix exactly that by assigning `process.env.ENCRYPTION_KEY` before the push, and **under Bun that assignment does not reach a child at all**: `child_process` hands over the environment the process STARTED with, where node would pass the mutation. Measured directly — `bun -e "process.env.FOO='x'; execSync('printenv FOO')"` prints nothing. Bun is the only runtime `fli` runs on, so the fix had been a no-op since it was written. The adopted values are passed as `env:` on the exec now. **Invisible for two further reasons**, both fixed in the same session: `fli new` caught the failure, warned, and printed `✓ created` anyway, and `runFli` shelled out to a GLOBAL `fli` on PATH, so the tree's copy of this command had never run in a scaffold at all. | <a id="fjs-340"></a>FJS-340 | ui — **`label=""` could not suppress a control's label on any of the thirteen controls that documented it as the way to** | 2026-08-18 | Every form control resolves a missing label from the schema and then from the column name, and each one's comment says the same thing: *`\| \| undefined` because `''` is a real answer here — it suppresses the label*. The code did the opposite. `export let label = ''` made "not stated" and "deliberately blank" the same value, and `label={label \| \| undefined}` then collapsed the blank one back to "not stated", so `Field` fell through to `nameToLabel(name)` and drew a label the caller had just turned off. `Field` itself was right all along — `<Field label="">` renders no label row — so only the pass-through was broken. **A control given a `name` therefore always had a visible label**, which is the shorthand `<Form>` wants and is wrong everywhere else: basecamp's server filter bar has three controls, only the Input needs a `name` (its submit handler reads `e.target.search.value`), and the bar rendered one labelled control 38px tall beside two unlabelled ones — with a *second* label, since the caller had already supplied a `visually-hidden` one.
