| <a id="fjs-342"></a>FJS-342 | toolbelt · junction · caravan — **Four mechanisms answered *has this already happened?* and each built its key at its call site; two of them interpolated caller-supplied text into it unescaped** | 2026-08-18 | The occurrence key is not internal: a stated dispatch id is the jobs table's PRIMARY KEY, which is what makes a replayed dispatch a no-op rather than a second row. So a key that can collide is two units of work silently becoming one. `@frontierjs/toolbelt/history` is the one definition — `occurrenceKey(kind, ...parts)` — and the property it exists for is **injectivity**: `%` is escaped before `:`, because escaping only the separator makes a part reading `%3A` and a part holding a real colon encode to the same bytes; `null`/`undefined` are refused rather than stringified, since `cron:daily:undefined` is one key every fire of that job shares. Two latent collisions closed with it. **Junction's idempotency key** joined four parts and two are outside its control — the header a caller writes and a principal id the auth provider issues — so a principal of `user-1:a` with key `b` and a principal of `user-1` with key `a:b` were one cache entry, which is one caller replayed another's answer. **Caravan's cron fire** interpolated a job name, so `report:daily` at minute 5 and `report` at `daily:5` were one key and the second never ran. Both are byte-identical for input without a `:`. **The outbox id MOVED**, deliberately: it dispatched under the bare row id into a table shared with every caller-stated id, so outbox row 7 and a caller's `7` were one primary key — it is `outbox:<id>` now, and that is a format change undelivered rows cannot survive, which costs nothing while no queue has rows in it and cannot be done free after a deploy. What this does NOT add is a revision-comparison half: it would have no caller, and a second export nothing asks for is the concept budget spent on a guess (`PHILOSOPHY.md` §III). The axiom this came from is ruled at [FJS-D39](DECISIONS.md) — the consolidation was right and the seed declaration was refused. 7 toolbelt tests, 1 caravan; mutation-checked by unnamespacing the outbox id (4 red) · [history.js](packages/toolbelt/src/history/history.js) · [idempotency.ts](packages/junction/src/core/idempotency.ts) · [outbox.ts](packages/junction/src/core/outbox.ts) · [caravan/src/index.ts](packages/caravan/src/index.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-341"></a>FJS-341 | sierra · junction · litestone — **A live store defeated `@version` on any screen holding a draft: the other writer's change was erased with the guard in place and no error anywhere** | 2026-08-18 | `createResource` remembered a version off the STORE, which was the right answer to the wrong question — a WS push reaches the store as an upsert and never passes through a call result, so without it a row a second tab patched left a pre-patch number behind. The cost was the other half: a push moves the number and moves nothing the person is looking at, so a save from a screen holding a DRAFT carried a revision nobody there had read and won the race the column exists to lose. Measured in basecamp. **The version is now recorded from READS this resource performed** — every call result, and a load that was not superseded — and never from the store. `load()` carries its own stamp for that, because a store notification has no provenance: a `set()` from a winning load and an `upsert()` from a push arrive as the same event, and junction's own stamp (`FJS-082`) governs the store rather than this. The cost is a 409 where a silent success used to be, and that 409 is the correct answer; a caller who has genuinely read the newer revision sends it, which `<Form record={row}>` already does by editing the row whole. **The 409 also became actionable**, which is the half that made the old behaviour tempting: `VersionConflictError` declares `data = { model, field, expected, actual }`, junction's `adopt()` carries a declared plain-object payload to the client (class instances and scalars refused — an internal handle is not a payload), and `toConflict(err)` / `resource.conflict(err)` answer the two revisions, where `fieldErrors()` gives the sentence. `junction errors` grew a **Payload** column for the same reason it has `retryable`: nothing above `toFrameworkError` reads anything but the result, so a payload that stops crossing is invisible. Part of the `FJS-D39` argument. 8 tests in sierra, 4 in junction (one end to end against a real Litestone client and a real service), four mutants killed — restore the subscription (3 red), drop the load stamp (1), drop the adoption (3), drop litestone's payload (2) · [resource.js](packages/sierra/src/junction/resource.js) · [field-rules.js](packages/sierra/src/junction/field-rules.js) `toConflict` · [errors.ts](packages/junction/src/core/errors.ts) `adopt` · [client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) `VersionConflictError` |
| <a id="fjs-252"></a>FJS-252 | repo — **The scaffold `fli new` writes is authored against HEAD and installs the published packages, and nothing compares the two** | 2026-08-18 | Two checks, and then a measurement in both directions. The `registry` phase asks npm for every package the scaffold writes and fails on a name the registry has never heard of, or on a published manifest naming a sibling version that does not exist ([FJS-314](#)). The half this row was filed for — a template written against behaviour only the tree has, which installs cleanly and fails at runtime — is now the `deploy` phase's **smoke**: once the container answers health it registers a user and logs them in, at the prefix read from the app's OWN `web/config/sierra.config.js`, so what is asserted is that the client and the server agree rather than that a literal on the CI side agrees with itself. It runs for both package sources, which is what makes the `npm` branch grade the PUBLISHED framework at runtime rather than only at install and health. Verified 2026-08-18: both branches green — the three failures measured on 2026-08-14 are gone on the published side too, auth mounting under `apiPrefix` and `make:deploy` deriving the health path from it — and a deliberately wrong prefix fails with the 404, the cause named and the container's own logs attached, so the check is not vacuously green. `/manifest` is out of reach by construction: `manifestPlugin` is `devOnly` and the container runs `NODE_ENV=production`, so the duplicate derived hooks of [FJS-231](#) stay a working-tree question under their own id |
| <a id="fjs-281"></a>FJS-281 | basecamp — **A db test `process.chdir()`s the whole process, so the suite can fail on a file it is not running** | 2026-08-18 | The chdir was vestigial and both calls are gone. `createClient({ databases: { audit: { path } } })` already wins over the declaration and `resolveDbPath` runs the override through `resolve()`, so the logger path was absolute before the process ever moved — CWD had no bearing on it, and the test that moved everyone else was buying nothing. **The sleep went with it**, which is the other half: the writer buffers ~1s and a fixed 1500ms wait against a buffer is a coin toss under load, which is how this failed once in a full run and passed alone immediately after. One `auditEntries(dir, model, ops)` helper polls for the rows the assertions are about and gives up at 10s so a genuine failure is still a failure; both audit tests call it and both state a 20s timeout, since bun's default 5s does not fit the poll. Verified: `db/test/schema.test.ts` 92 pass alone, basecamp 122 pass across 6 files, and no other test in the repo calls `process.chdir` · [db/test/schema.test.ts `auditEntries`](packages/basecamp/db/test/schema.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-294"></a>FJS-294 | caravan — **Two Caravan instances on one `jobs.db` each fire every cron and each reset the other's in-flight jobs** | 2026-08-18 | The second of the two candidate answers: coordinate for real, rather than declare single ownership and refuse a second opener — a refusal would also refuse the ordinary shapes, a web process that only dispatches beside a worker that only runs, and it would state an assumption the deployment gets to make. **A running row is OWNED.** Each instance takes an id, stamps it on every row `claimNext` takes, and heartbeats into a `job_owners` table on a timer; recovery reclaims only rows whose owner has gone quiet past `lease` (30s, `heartbeat` 5s, both settable from `junction.config.js`). `markDone`/`markFailed` are guarded on the owner as well as on `running`, so a reclaimed job's late completion cannot land on the attempt that replaced it, and a clean `stop()` drops the owner row — but only with nothing in flight, since past `pool.stop()`'s deadline a handler may still be running and dropping it then invites exactly the double execution the owner prevents. **The cron half is the dedup key, not leader election**: the fire is dispatched under `cron:<job>:<epoch-minute>`, so every instance fires its own schedule and all but the first are the no-op a stated id already is (`FJS-D35`). A lease-held leader misses fires whenever the lease is between owners; a key cannot. What it assumes instead is two clocks agreeing to within a minute, which is what cron assumes to fire at the right time at all. Two limits stated rather than hidden: a pre-owners database has `owner_id` NULL on its running rows and they are reclaimed on sight, which is the answer this package gave before owners existed; and the lease is renewed by a TIMER, so a handler that blocks the event loop past it stalls its own heartbeat and has its work taken — same ground as [FJS-295](#fjs-295). 8 tests in `tests/ownership.test.ts` against a real FILE database, because in `:memory:` two instances are two databases and the defect cannot be seen at all; mutation-checked by restoring the blanket recovery and the uuid cron fire, which fails 4 of them. Verified: caravan 149 pass, typecheck clean, `example` `verify:jobs` 10/10 · [db.ts](packages/caravan/src/db.ts) `job_owners` · [index.ts `sweepOwners`](packages/caravan/src/index.ts) · [tests/ownership.test.ts](packages/caravan/tests/ownership.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-334"></a>FJS-334 | junction — **There is no app-level publisher, and the one spelling that would be it is refused at startup** | 2026-08-18 | `app.channels.publishDefault(fn)`, and the whole of the design is that it is a **default, not a second broadcaster**: consulted only where a service declares nothing, so it composes with `channel:` rather than racing it and cannot send one record twice. The three states a service already had are what make that work — `channel: 'posts'` says where and is not asked, `channel: false` refuses it, absent asks the app — so `refuseDoubleBroadcast` is untouched and an app-level `publish()` HOOK is still correctly refused. **Function only, no string form**: one channel name for every service is exactly the shape that hands a subscriber rows no `@@allow` would have let them read, which is why broadcasting is opt-in at all, so the fallback is handed the record and decides per record. **The report is the other half, because this inverts a failure mode** — forgetting `channel:` used to mean a screen that never updates, visible and yours, and now means records on the wire under a rule written for other services, which nothing on the server can see. The plugin names at `boot()` which services fall through, and ONLY when a default is registered: with none, that list is the ruled state and would fire on nearly every service in every app, which is how a warning gets trained out. It extinguishes itself — `channel: false` drops a service from it. `describe()` gained `channel` with it (the name, `true` for a function, `false` for the opt-out, `null` for declares-nothing; `null` is not `false`). 11 tests against the REAL channel manager — the question is which channel a frame resolves onto, and a stub that ignores the resolver answers it by construction — 3 mutants killed. Verified: junction 1209 + 11, typecheck clean, `example` `verify:live` 14/14 · [channels.ts `publishDefault`](packages/junction/src/transport/channels.ts) · [service.ts `publishToChannels`](packages/junction/src/core/service.ts) · [tests/publish-default.test.ts](packages/junction/tests/publish-default.test.ts) · `DECISIONS.md` § API design |
| <a id="fjs-335"></a>FJS-335 | junction — **`@version` could not work through a service: `autoValidate` stripped the version off every patch** | 2026-08-17 | Litestone's PROPERTY SET is mode-dependent and not only its `required[]` — `@version` is emitted for `update` and omitted for `create`. Junction derived ONE `create`-mode document (`_deriveJsonSchema`, no options) and compiled both validators from it, so the version a caller sent was dropped as an unknown key and the Data boundary then refused the write for not carrying one: `VersionRequiredError`, 400, on every patch of every `@version` model. The feature was unusable end to end and nothing was red, because junction's own `@version` tests call `svc.patch(ctx)` directly, where no hook runs — including the bulk guard that refuses a caller-supplied version, which `autoValidate` had already made unreachable. Fixed by deriving per mode and compiling the patch validator from the create property set **plus the version column** — not from the update document whole, which also carries `@id`: litestone writes what it is given, so `update({ where: { id: 1 }, data: { id: 99 } })` moves the row and answers 99, and taking the whole document would have let any caller rewrite a primary key through a PATCH. Four tests in `tests/real-litestone-client.test.ts` go through `autoValidate` first, three of them failing against the line each one guards. Found declaring `@version` on ten basecamp models |
| <a id="fjs-336"></a>FJS-336 | junction — **the browser client's own headers were not in `cors()`'s allow-list, so the HTTP transport was unreachable cross-origin** | 2026-08-17 | The default was `Content-Type, Authorization, X-API-Key`. The client sends `X-Service-Method` to address a custom method over HTTP, `X-Workspace-Id` on every call once `setWorkspace()` is used, and reads `Idempotency-Key` in `callService` — none of them allowed, so the preflight refused and the request never arrived. **Invisible in both apps in this repo**, because a service call takes the socket when one is connected and CORS does not apply to a WebSocket; the HTTP path is the fallback, and the fallback was dead. Measured from basecamp's own page: `fetch` with `X-Workspace-Id` from `:8020` to `:8120` answers `TypeError: Failed to fetch`. The three are in the default now — they are junction's own protocol headers, so an app should not have to know them — and the preflight assertion is in `tests/index.test.ts` |
| <a id="fjs-d27"></a>FJS-D27 | litestone — **Atomic update operators, ruled and shipped: the COLUMN decides one is an operator at all** | 2026-08-17 | `increment` `decrement` `multiply` `divide` on a numeric column, `push` on an array one, on `update` and `updateMany` only. The concurrency argument was always the point — read-modify-write loses one of two increments, and `@version` turns that into a thrown conflict the caller has to retry rather than a write that cannot be lost. **The argument against does not survive contact**: it was that the payload is otherwise VALUES, so `{ views: { increment: 1 } }` and `{ addr: { city: 'x' } }` are one shape to a parser — and they are not one shape to a parser that knows the column, which `writeData` does, and which the where-side already uses to tell a typed-Json path from an operator block. Half the work is the refusals, each by name and with the reason: wrong column type · `create`/`createMany` (nothing to change) and `upsert`/`upsertMany` (its fast path SETs from `excluded` and its slow path calls `update()`, so one could apply the operator and the other could not) · `divide: 0`, which SQLite answers NULL with no error · two operators on one column · an enum array pushed a non-member · and **a column carrying `@lt`/`@lte`/`@gt`/`@gte`/`@maxItems`/`@uniqueItems`**, since the new value is computed inside SQLite where no validator sees it. `push` appends through `json_insert(coalesce(col,'[]'), '$[#]', ?)` — the coalesce is load-bearing, since `json_insert(NULL, …)` answers NULL and raises nothing. Ruling in `DECISIONS.md` § Query & write semantics; 16 cases in `test/write-operators.test.ts`; `docs/querying.md` § Atomic update operators |
| <a id="fjs-183"></a>FJS-183 | litestone — **A rebuild that would destroy an app-created schema object is now BLOCKED rather than warned about** | 2026-08-17 | The open half was the choice the row itself framed: warn, refuse, or re-emit. **Refuse.** Naming the objects in a comment above the SQL is the wrong answer for the one reader who matters — somebody applying a generated migration without reading it, which is who a generated file is for. The rebuild is emitted commented out with three ways forward (recreate them below and uncomment, move it into the schema, or drop it by hand), which is the same shape an un-defaultable new column already used: both are *litestone cannot decide this for you*, and one mechanism for that beats two. `autoMigrate` reports `state: 'blocked'` with the same list and writes no hash, so it resurfaces on every startup. Re-emitting a captured trigger verbatim stays rejected — its body may name a column the rebuild drops, so it would restate SQL that fails at CREATE or at the next write. Three cases in `litestone.test.ts` (the auto path refuses and rolls nothing, the file comments the rebuild out, and a table with no app-created object still rebuilds); ruling in `DECISIONS.md` § Migrations; `docs/migrations.md` rewritten |
| <a id="fjs-d30"></a>FJS-D30 | junction — **Should login work over the WebSocket, the way Feathers does?** | 2026-08-17 | Ruled **no**. The row rested on requirement (3) — channel membership must be torn down and rebuilt when the identity changes — and (3) turned out to be already done, by discarding the connection rather than mutating it: `setToken` cycles the socket (`_disconnect()` then `_openWs()`), so the upgrade carries the new token, `handleConnect` runs again, `channels.on('connection')` fires with the NEW session, and membership is rebuilt from nothing while the old connection's channels drop on close. That is Feathers' leave-anonymous / join-authenticated transition spelled as a reconnect, and **every other line of a Feathers `channels.js` already has an exact equal here** — `app.channels.on('connection', (session, conn))`, `app.channel(name).join/leave`, per-service `publish()` — which both apps in this repo write. So a WS login buys **one HTTP round-trip** and costs a mutable `ws.data.user`, which `FJS-007` and basecamp's `applyStanding()` are both built on being frozen; a half-logged-in socket (new token, old channel set) is silent, since a channel that never fires looks like nothing happening. The rule that follows: **identity change is a NEW connection** — and switching tenant mid-session is deliberately not one, which basecamp handles by joining a channel per membership at connect. The half with no equal here is the app-level publisher, filed as [FJS-334](#fjs-334) \| `DECISIONS.md` § API design · [client/index.ts `setToken`](packages/junction/src/client/index.ts) · [channels.ts](packages/junction/src/transport/channels.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-331"></a>FJS-331 | ui — **a portaled panel was painted at the PREVIOUS open's coordinates, so a click meant to close it landed inside it** | 2026-08-17 | Not a slow animation and not leakage between specs. `x`/`y` survive a close, and the panel is placed in a frame callback — but the frame that PAINTS the newly inserted panel is the one before it, by construction: input is dispatched, the flush inserts the panel, style and paint run, and the `requestAnimationFrame` queued by the click handler does not run until the next frame. So every open shows one frame at the last open's placement, and under load that frame is however long the machine takes. Measured deterministically — open at scroll 0, close, scroll 60, open: the panel is inserted at `top: 134px` where it belongs at `74px`. When the stale coordinates land ON the trigger the click that should close the menu falls inside the panel, where click-away deliberately lets it through, so the menu stays open and `aria-expanded` still reads `true` — the two assertions that were failing, one fact. Fixed in the three components that position a portal this way (`DropdownMenu`, `Popover`, `ConfirmationPopover`): the panel renders `visibility: hidden` and the frame that places it shows it, in the same flush, so it is neither seen nor hit-tested anywhere but where it belongs. 280 toggle rounds under a 16-way CPU load, clean; the same loop found it in 13 before. The spec now asserts the open before inverting it, and a `MutationObserver` holds the contract at insertion, which a poll cannot reach. Two more came out of it: `waitSettled` never settled on a page with a `fill: forwards` animation (it stays in `getAnimations()`, in effect, so the probe timed out and answered `false` 4s later, twice per run), and `DropdownMenu` had never moved focus into the panel at all — `bind:this` is on the surface, which carries no tabindex, and every arrow assertion passes identically with focus left outside the list |
| <a id="fjs-333"></a>FJS-333 | litestone — **`check()` correlated against the MODEL name, so it threw on any model whose table is snake_cased** | 2026-08-17 | Found building [FJS-282](#fjs-282). The `EXISTS` a `check(parent)` compiles to has to name the outer table, and it named the model: `model LogLine` is table `log_line`, so the clause read `"LogLine"."deployId"` and SQLite answered `no such column`. **Every single-word model hid it** — identifiers are matched case-insensitively, so `"Deploy"` finds `deploy` — which is why a feature shipped with tests could still be broken for half the schemas that use it. One line: the correlation goes through `modelToTableName`, the same function the target side already used. A two-word model is a case in `test/tenancy-delegation.test.ts` now, and the generated tenancy rule for a grandchild is the same shape, so the grid covers it twice |
| <a id="fjs-282"></a>FJS-282 | litestone — **A model reached only through its parent is now scoped by `tenancy { strategy row }`** | 2026-08-17 | Both halves landed. **`check()` is a real lookup outside a WHERE**: it answered `true` conservatively in the JS evaluator, so a hand-written `@@deny('all', !check(parent))` held for read, update and delete and permitted a cross-tenant CREATE in silence — half-enforcement in the one feature whose whole job is enforcement. The foreign key is in the data being written and `buildFilterSql` already builds the target's own filter, so the same SQL runs uncorrelated: `SELECT 1 FROM "<target>" WHERE "<referencedKey>" = ? AND (<target policy>) LIMIT 1`, through `ctx.readDb`, which serves the write connection while a transaction is open — a parent and child created together would otherwise deny the child. An ABSENT foreign key allows, which is the answer the tenant column already gives on create. `evalJs` takes the containing operation now, so `check()` with no explicit one asks the right question. **Tenancy generates the rule**: one `@@deny(read, update, delete, create, !check(rel))` per SCOPED PARENT, which is why there is nothing to choose — denies are AND'd, so two scoped parents mean both must be satisfied, the narrowing answer and the direction tenancy always takes. Transitive by fixpoint (a grandchild is scoped once its parent is), self-relations skipped, `check(rel, 'read')` stated rather than inherited because the question is *is that parent mine* and never *may I create that parent*. `@@tenant(via: rel)` narrows to one relation and is refused if it names a non-relation or a parent that is not itself scoped. The *N models are NOT scoped* report now names only the models with neither a column nor a scoped parent, and a second line names what was delegated. 17 cases in `test/tenancy-delegation.test.ts`; `docs/multi-tenancy.md` § Scoped through a parent |
| <a id="fjs-348"></a>FJS-348 | junction — **`autoload-services` was `needsHost`, so every app that autoloads had ZERO services under `@frontierjs/testing`** | 2026-08-17 | `_startForTest` skips every `needsHost` phase — the ones that bind a port, read a config file or install signal handlers — and `autoload-services` was in that list. It binds nothing: it reads a directory and builds objects. The cost was invisible until an app was mounted by the Testing realm for the first time, where every call answered `NotFound: Service 'projects' not found` — **a 404 that reads like a wrong service name rather than an app with no services in it**, which sends you looking at the wrong thing. The flag is dropped; a missing directory is already a silent no-op, so nothing else changes. What a test still cannot reach is `_junction.services.dir`, because `load-config` IS `needsHost` and `junction.config.js` is not read — an app that wants its services mounted in a test states `autoload:` on `createApp` directly, which is also the only form that survives `Bun.main` being the test runner rather than the entry point. Junction's 1193 tests green after the change \| 2026-08-17 · [app.ts](packages/junction/src/core/app.ts) · [basecamp/api/test/services.test.ts](packages/basecamp/api/test/services.test.ts) **Renumbered from FJS-333**, which had been issued twice. |
| <a id="fjs-332"></a>FJS-332 | litestone — **`@unique` over a randomly-encrypted column was declared, built, and could never fire** | 2026-08-17 | Found probing [FJS-207](#fjs-207)'s conflict path with a `@unique @encrypted` fixture that refused to conflict. A UNIQUE constraint is over the STORED bytes and plain `@encrypted` uses a random IV, so the same plaintext stores different ciphertext on every write: measured, two creates of one value both succeeded and the model held two rows, with no error anywhere. Nothing said so, which makes it a uniqueness guarantee an app believes it has — and the shapes that want it (`@secret` on an API key, an encrypted email) are exactly the ones where a duplicate matters. `@encrypted(deterministic: true)` derives the IV from the plaintext and works; `@hashed` is the other answer. Both `@unique` and `@@unique([...])` naming such a column are now refused at parse, beside the `@hashed` conflict rules, naming the model, the field and both ways through. No schema in this tree carried the combination. `docs/encryption.md` states it under the recoverable/filterable table, since *filterable* is what `@unique` needs |
| <a id="fjs-207"></a>FJS-207 | litestone — **A failing batch write reported SQLite's message and never which row** | 2026-08-17 | `UNIQUE constraint failed: post.slug` from a 500-row import named the column and nothing else, so finding the row meant bisecting the batch by hand — and the loop already knew the index. The error now says `data[i] of n`, carries `batchIndex`/`batchSize` for a caller that would rather not parse prose, and for a unique conflict names the values that collided, redacted the way the audit trail redacts them because a `@unique` column may be `@encrypted`. It also says **nothing in the batch was written**, which is true (one transaction, verified) and is the difference between re-running the import and hunting for partial rows. The error is ANNOTATED, never wrapped: its class carries the status and `retryable` past the API boundary, and a batch wrapper would flatten `SoftDeletedUniqueError`'s 409 into an unclassified 500. Three places had the same silence and all three are covered — the insert loop, the row-building map above it (a validation failure named the field and no row), and `upsertMany`, whose loop carried no `try` at all, so a second `@unique` that is not the conflict target arrived raw. `test/batch-errors.test.ts`, 6 cases including that a single-row write is not annotated |
| <a id="fjs-206"></a>FJS-206 | litestone — **A structural filter on an untyped `Json` column was reported as an unknown operator** | 2026-08-17 | Two messages, one cause: neither named the column the caller wrote. `{ meta: { tier: 'gold' } }` on a bare `Json?` threw `Unknown where operator "tier"`, which sends the reader hunting a misspelling when the answer is that the column has no declared type to traverse — it now says so and gives both routes, `@type(...)` to make it traversable and `$raw` with `json_extract` to filter it as it stands. The mirror on a TYPED column threw `Unknown field 'has' on type Address`, which names the type, calls an operator a field, and never names the column: it now says `"has" is an operator and Address has no field by that name, so it was read as a sub-key of "addr"` — the column is `@type(Address)` and a where on a typed column is a path. `WHERE_OPS` is the one set of what an operator IS, built from the three that already existed, because the typed walk cannot tell a sub-key from an operator without it. The unknown-sub-key message also lists the type's fields now. `matrix.test.ts`'s `typedJson × hasOp` cell is promoted from `206:ref` to `ref` |
| <a id="fjs-330"></a>FJS-330 | mesa — **An HTML comment inside a tag closed the element and turned every attribute after it into text** | 2026-08-17 | `parseAttributes` had no case for `<!--` between two attributes, so it fell through to `readAttribute()` and the `>` that ends `-->` read as the end of the tag: `<button id="a" <!-- note --> data-n={n} aria-label="hi">` emitted the comment verbatim into the template and then a TEXT node reading `` ` data-n=1 aria-label="hi" >x` ``. No error, no warning, and the emitted JS parsed, so [Invariant 15](CLAUDE.md#invariants) did not catch it either — the attributes simply did not apply. **Dropped rather than refused**: HTML has no comment inside a tag, so there is no markup here to preserve and nothing a strip can get wrong, and a comment explaining the attribute under it is a reasonable thing to want to write. What matters is that it is CONSUMED. An UNTERMINATED one is refused by name instead, because eating to the end of the file is the same silent failure again. Six cases in `test/compiler.test.js`, the load-bearing one being that `<!--` inside an attribute VALUE is left alone — it is read as a string before the loop can see it, and it is content. Found writing `aria-pressed` onto the kit's new password toggle, where the drive reported *the toggle was never drawn* and the toggle was drawn |
| <a id="fjs-320"></a>FJS-320 | litestone — **A bulk write reported what its TRIGGERS and FOREIGN KEYS wrote in the `count` it answered** | 2026-08-17 | Filed against `@@fts` and true of far more than that. bun:sqlite's `.changes` is a **total-changes delta**, not `sqlite3_changes()`, so it counts every row a trigger or an FK action wrote inside the same statement: measured against a real client, one updated row answered **17** on an `@@fts` model, **2** on any model carrying `updatedAt` — which is most of them, since `@updatedAt` is a SQL trigger — and a `deleteMany` naming one parent answered **4** for its three cascaded children. Only a model with no trigger and no cascade was ever right. Junction hands this number to the browser and a live store's `changed` event carries it, so the count a caller compared against what they asked for described a write that touched rows it never named. **The fix is to ask SQLite rather than the driver**: `rowsChanged(db)` — `SELECT changes()` off the same connection, which counts only the rows the statement itself addressed — replaces every `.changes` that means *rows*, at `updateMany`, both halves of `removeMany`, `deleteMany`, the two single-row `select: false` paths whose telemetry carried the same inflation, and `retention.js`'s log line. The `RETURNING` paths already counted rows and are untouched, and `createMany`/`upsertMany` count iterations. `test/bulk-count.test.ts` holds it: nine cases over five models — fts, updatedAt, no trigger at all, soft delete, cascade, a write that matched nothing, inside a transaction, and `announce: 'rows'` agreeing with the default. Keying by the value is unique only when the values are, and two ordinary shapes are not — a list of repeated primitives, and an array-like whose every item is `undefined` — where a collision did not degrade but corrupted the block list from the first reorder onward. An index cannot collide, so an unkeyed list now always renders the array it was handed; **the trade is node identity across a reorder**, a moved item being rebound into the node already at that position, which an author overrides by stating a key. `$$virtualEach` already keyed by index with no key function, so the two forms agree. `each-unkeyed.spec.mjs` was pinned as still-broken and now asserts the fix and the trade — the rows after a reorder are the nodes stamped before the click — and VISION §9.2 says which spelling to reach for |

It finds the first `<body` in the file now and reports only if THAT one is inside a comment — a file whose only body tag is commented lands there too, which is the same failure — and it points at the mention rather than at the comment enclosing it, because the mention is the token to delete. **The second half is that neither finding was reachable from CI**: the `structure` phase runs `runChecks` over the four APPS, so nothing checks this repo's own tree, and both errors had been sitting under a bare `fli check` at the root. | <a id="fjs-d35"></a>FJS-D35 | junction — **a durable effect had nowhere to be recorded; `ctx.enqueue` and the outbox relay are the answer** | 2026-08-17 | Ruled and built. `ctx.afterCommit(fn)` ([FJS-089](#fjs-089)) runs an effect only if the call succeeded and committed, and buys nothing against a crash — the process dies in between and the effect is never done, with nothing recording it was owed. **The question this row held was WHICH DATABASE the intent row lives in, and one candidate died to a measurement**: litestone opens one connection per declared `database` block and has exactly one transaction manager, over main's — a probe writing to both and throwing rolled main back and left the second database's row standing, so a second block cannot host it. `$attach` does not rescue it either (atomic multi-file commit needs rollback-journal mode; Caravan's queue and litestone's tenants are WAL). So: **main, as an imported `.lite` model** — `packages/junction/db/outbox.lite`, `@@gate("8")`, the same split auth makes, because a litestone system table is invisible to migrations, `ddl.snapshot.sql` and `access.snapshot.md` and a stuck outbox nobody can query is worse. **The queue stays its own file**: the split is intent versus execution, so main takes one small insert and Caravan keeps its polling on its own WAL database. **A second VERB, not a flag** — a closure cannot be written to a table, so the durable half takes a name and a payload. Refuses by name outside a transaction (asked of `db.$inTransaction`, new on every client flavour, because a hook can run against a method `transactional:` does not name), with no model, and with no relay installed. **Delivery is at-least-once and no version of it is not** — two files cannot be one transaction — so the relay dispatches under the OUTBOX ROW's id and caravan's new `dispatch({ id })` makes the replay a no-op; that is the idempotency `unique` deliberately is not, since `unique` frees itself the moment a job is terminal. `fli outbox:install` writes the import line. 25 tests against a real client, a real app and a real queue (11 junction, 14 caravan); six mutants, all killed — two of them only after the tests they exposed as missing were written (the claim's compare-and-set, and the cross-process primary-key collision). `example`'s `pay` is the worked case and lost its `unique` key with nothing replacing it: the state machine already refuses paying a paid order, and what `unique` could never cover was a job that was never queued — `verify:jobs` 10/10, `verify:notify` 9/9 — [core/outbox.ts](packages/junction/src/core/outbox.ts) · [plugins/outbox](packages/junction/src/plugins/outbox/index.ts) · `DECISIONS.md` § API design |

| <a id="fjs-030"></a>FJS-030 | jetty — **an island was built as a page module, so Chrome could not parse the content script at all** | 2026-08-17 | Vite injects its preload helper into every client build that is not a lib or a worker, and that helper is written with `import.meta.resolve` and `import.meta.url`. | <a id="fjs-303"></a>FJS-303 | mesa — **an `{#if}` guard's subtree recomputed before the guard tore it down** | 2026-08-17 | The derivation layer settled to QUIESCENCE ahead of everything that builds DOM, so a memo three blocks deep recomputed in the same breath as the top-level derivation the guard above it was still waiting on. `{#if a[i]}` around `{@const d = a[i][j]}` then read `undefined[j]` and threw from inside a handler, which surfaces as a component that quietly stops responding. **Reproducing it took all three parts and that is the diagnosis**: the guarded value must be DERIVED (so the change arrives through the derivation layer rather than as an assignment), the outer loop FIXED (so the block for a row the new value lacks is still standing), and the read must be a MEMO — `{@const}` — not a text interpolation. A plain array read in an interpolation never failed, which is why the shape looked arbitrary. The layer now settles **outside-in**: one DOM-depth at a time, shallowest first, and within a level nothing whose owning block is already queued — decidable there because the block was woken by the shallower derivation that just ran, where one turn earlier it was not. Propagation is unaffected (a surviving memo still recomputes and still notifies, one pass later) and a memo left dirty recomputes on the next READ, so a branch that lives gets a fresh value and one that dies is disposed before anyone asks. `createEffect`'s own comment had stated this invariant for renders since it was written; the derived tier was added ahead of both passes and did not honour it. The first attempt handed control to the DOM tier whenever any block was pending, which cost an extra observation in a glitch-freedom test — the tell that the condition had to be about OWNERSHIP, not about pendingness. | <a id="fjs-327"></a>FJS-327 | basecamp — **every scheduled job stopped firing at the first restart, silently** | 2026-08-17 | A cron registration is in-process — in junction's `app.scheduler` and in Caravan alike — and the ONLY place a Job's schedule was ever registered was the jobs service's `create()`. Nothing logs, nothing 500s, and the shape of the failure is *the job just never ran*. **`restoreSchedules()` in the job engine is the missing half**: every live scheduled job re-registered at `register()`, which is where the `job:run` handler already goes, so the ROW is the source of truth rather than the request that happened to create it. One unparseable expression is logged and skipped rather than costing every other job its schedule. Not awaited — a fleet that cannot schedule is worse served by an API that will not boot. | <a id="fjs-047"></a>FJS-047 | junction — **the scheduler/Caravan overlap is real; ruled rather than merged** | 2026-08-17 | Three overlaps were filed as one row and only one of them was open. **mail vs notifications** is settled in practice — `notifications/drivers/email.ts` delegates to `app.mail.send()`, so `src/mail` is the transport and notifications fan out over it. **outbound vs Conduit** is a naming overlap with no shared code. **scheduler vs Caravan** was live: junction constructs a scheduler directly and Caravan registers a cron for any `handle()` carrying one, and the two share nothing. **Ruled `FJS-D36`: both stay, and the boundary is stated** — `app.scheduler` is a timer (in-process, no persistence, no retry, no principal), `app.jobs` is durable work, and *a timer that dispatches into a queue is the queue's schedule*. | <a id="fjs-034"></a>FJS-034 | junction — **the typecheck baseline is gone: 138 → 0, and driving it there found eleven type defects on the SHIPPED surface** | 2026-08-17 | The row said the remaining 138 were test ergonomics and reached no user. Half of them were, and the other half were the tests being the only code in the repo that USES junction the way an app does. `scripts/typecheck-baselines.json` no longer names junction at all — absent means 0 — with the suite unchanged at 1176 pass. **A custom method's `ctx` was an implicit `any`.** `ServiceDefinition`'s `[method: string]: unknown` contextually types nothing, so the documented `createService({ name: 'servers', async reboot(ctx) { … } })` is a hard error in any app with `noImplicitAny` and a silently untyped `ctx` in every app without it. The fix is the index signature spelled as a union carrying exactly ONE function type (`ServiceDefinitionValue`) — `\| unknown` collapses back to `unknown`, which is why the obvious widening does not work. **`ctx.result` was declared narrower than every assignment the framework itself makes**, including two of its own (`structuredClone(hit) as ServiceContext['result']`, `idem.result as …`): a short-circuiting `before` hook may set a plain value and `toResponse` handles a bare `Response`. Now `unknown`, and both casts are deleted; `resultData()` / `unwrapResult()` are how the payload is read, which is what the email-hook docs already did by hand. **`createBaseService`'s return type was built from `Service`**, whose `hooks` is the registration METHOD rather than the map — so `createService({ ...createBaseService({ model }) })`, the spread this file documents, did not compile. Now `BaseServiceDefinition`. **`app.events.on('x', () => seen.push(n))` did not compile**: `EventHandler` was `=> Promise<void> \| void`, and TypeScript's return-a-value-where-void-is-expected rule applies to a bare `void`, not to a union containing it — while `emit` already tests the return for `.then` and ignores anything else. Now `=> unknown`, which is what the emitter does. **`config: { http: { helmet: false } }` was both a type error and a silent data loss.** `AppOptions.config` was a one-level `Partial<AppConfig>` while `deepMerge` merges all the way down (now `DeepPartial`), `AppConfig['http']` never declared `helmet` although `app.ts` read it through a cast, and **`createTestApp` spread its config shallowly**, so naming one key of `http` dropped `cors`, `ddos`, `powered` and `maxBodySize` — it deep-merges now, like `createApp`. Four more of the same shape: `bridge.toContext`'s `model` was required and read nowhere (defaults to `service`, as `internal()` always did); `OriginList` refused a single origin string that `cors()`'s own `isAllowed` has always had a branch for; `OAPathItem` names its five verbs, so `paths['/posts'].post` is an operation rather than `OAOperation \| OAParameter[]`; `partitionBulk<T>` demanded the parse return `T` while both callers return a coerced record. **Three in litestone**, reached from junction's examples: `$schema` was `unknown` while `generateJsonSchema(db.$schema)` is the documented line (`AnyLitestoneClient` keeps `unknown` — name a shape there and a generated client is unassignable again, [FJS-018](#fjs-018)); `TableClient`'s last four parameters default off the row, so a hand-written accessor costs one type argument instead of five; `FileStorageOptions` gained `localPath`/`localUrl`/`localPort`, which `providers/local.js` reads and nothing declared, so the local branch of every dev config was an error. **Two things that never ran at all**: `test:example` and `test:all` passed `example/test.ts` as a filter, and bun matches a filter by `.test`/`.spec` in the filename — the path form runs 26 tests that had been silently skipped; and `makeWsApp` in `index.test.ts` called an unimported `channels`, so it could only ever have thrown, and nothing called it. Test-side: one `tests/helpers.ts` for the two casts this suite kept rewriting (`stubbable` for a fetch stub, since Bun's `typeof fetch` carries `preconnect`; `asRecord` for a key the type does not declare, which is Invariant 5 working), 18 duplicate imports removed from `index.test.ts`, and a `let` assigned inside a callback replaced by a collector where TypeScript's flow analysis had narrowed it to `null`. Verified by running: junction 1176 pass · litestone 2295 · auth 137 · caravan 115 · `@frontierjs/testing` 23 · workspace typecheck clean with `auth` and `basecamp` still exactly at their baselines · every junction example booted and served · `example` `verify` 37/37, `verify:jobs` 8/8, `verify:live` 14/14, `verify:notify` 9/9 — [core/service.ts](packages/junction/src/core/service.ts) · [core/context.ts](packages/junction/src/core/context.ts) · [events/index.ts](packages/junction/src/events/index.ts) · [config/index.ts](packages/junction/src/config/index.ts) · [transport/middleware.ts](packages/junction/src/transport/middleware.ts) · [litestone/src/index.d.ts](packages/litestone/src/index.d.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-089"></a>FJS-089 | junction — **a side effect could not be tied to the call succeeding; `ctx.afterCommit(fn)` is the phase that was missing** | 2026-08-16 | `after` means after the METHOD. Hooks run in sequence, so an `after` hook that sends an email runs, a later one throws, and the client is told the call failed with the email already gone — and once `transactional:` landed, the row was rolled back while the email stayed sent, which is worse. Rails states the choice as `after_save` vs `after_commit`; only the first existed here. **`ctx.afterCommit(fn)` is the second.** Queued from any phase, drained once in `callService` on `!ctx.error && !pipelineError` — after the announcement, before `idem.settle`, so a replay cannot be told the call is finished while its effects are still running. **Under `transactional:` it is after the COMMIT for free, which is why this is small**: the transaction is an `around` hook, so `runPipeline` has already returned by the time the drain runs. No transaction state is read, nothing is kept in step, and one drain point serves both cases. The queue lives on the CONTEXT and not on `ctx.locals.db`, which that hook reassigns mid-pipeline. **Observer tier** (`FJS-D06`): a throw is logged and emitted as `junction.aftercommit.error`, never reported as the call failing — the write is committed and the broadcast is out, so a 500 would tell the client a write failed that did not — and the remaining callbacks still run. `withAfterCommit()` is the one owner: the two builders in `bridge.ts`, the internal one in `app.ts`, and `callService` for a context built by hand. **What it is NOT is durability**, and that half is now [FJS-D35](#fjs-d35) with its own question. | <a id="fjs-271"></a>FJS-271 | junction — **`createThread(path, data)` dropped its second parameter, and the reason was a wrong measurement** | 2026-08-16 | The row said `workerData` is not delivered on Bun 1.3.11 — `globalThis.workerData`, `self.workerData` and `Bun.workerData` all `undefined` inside the worker — and filed the fix as a protocol decision: a reserved `{ __init: data }` envelope, a separate `init` handler, or deleting the parameter. **Re-measured, and it IS delivered**: `node:worker_threads`' own `workerData` export carries it, which is the one place the original probe did not look. All three globals really are undefined, so the observation was right and the conclusion was not. So no protocol changed and nothing was reserved. `workerData()` is the read half, exported from `@frontierjs/junction/workers`, answering **`undefined`** where Node answers `null` — on the main thread and in a worker given none alike, because a caller cannot act on the difference between *nobody passed anything* and *somebody passed nothing*. **Setup and work stay on separate channels**, which is what made the envelope unnecessary: setup is read once at module scope, work arrives per message. Three things came with it. `createPool(path, count, data)` — a pool could not configure its workers at all. **One `spawn()`**, the only place a `Worker` is constructed, because a pool RESPAWNS after an error and a respawned worker built without the setup data serves a different configuration than its siblings from the first failure on. And **a pool now rejects on a handler that threw** — `workerHandler` posts `{ __error: true, message }` back and `exec()` resolved with it, so the caller's `await` succeeded, the failure was a property nobody reads, and `stats.completed` counted it as work done; found while writing the tests. `tests/workers.test.ts` runs real threads, since the whole question is what a second thread receives and a stand-in answers whatever it was written to answer — which is how an exported, documented parameter shipped going nowhere. Mapping the members satisfies it and still refuses an undeclared column. | <a id="fjs-288"></a>FJS-288 | litestone — **`hooks.before.all` declared sixteen operations and reached four of them** | 2026-08-16 | Eleven of the sixteen names in `SETTER_OPS ∪ GETTER_OPS` had no call site, and `exists` was in neither set, so registering on `all` or on `setters` was silent in both directions — the hook never fired and nothing said it would not. Fixed by giving the runner ONE call site: `installHooks(table, ctx, modelName)` wraps a built table once, and the two sets are what it reads. **A hook now fires exactly once per call the caller made, named for the method they named** — which is what the two `this` bindings decide: a hooked operation runs against the RAW table, so its own internal calls (`upsert` → `create`/`update`, `findMany({recursive})` → `findMany`) do not announce a second time; everything else runs against the wrapper, so a delegating helper (`transition` → `update`, `findFirstOrThrow` → `findFirst`) still reaches the hook of the operation it delegates to. Consequences worth knowing: **hooks are now the outermost layer** — a before hook sees the arguments as the caller wrote them, ahead of the plugin door and of any stamping (`@sequence`, nested-write extraction) `client.js` does — an after hook on `update` can now replace the result, which it silently could not, and an `upsert` that inserts fires `upsert` rather than `create`. `exists` joined `GETTER_OPS`. Seventeen operations × before/after, each asserted to fire once with nothing else, in `test/litestone.test.ts` § hook coverage; a new operation belongs in that grid. | <a id="fjs-010"></a>FJS-010 | junction — **an `asSystem()` write announced nothing, so every open tab stayed stale** | 2026-08-16 | Litestone's `onEvent` had zero Junction subscribers and no way to gain one after construction, so a write outside a service call — a Caravan job, a hook reaching for `db.asSystem()` — reached the database and neither the bus, nor a channel, nor a browser heard it. **Fixed in both halves.** Litestone grew `$tapEvents(fn)` — `$tapQuery`'s shape one event kind over, which is what closed [FJS-D04](#fjs-d04) — and Junction's `announceDataWrites` taps it at `createApp({ db })`, routing what it sees into the same bus + channel fan-out `callService` uses. **The listener Set is shared BY REFERENCE across every scoped ctx copy**, which is the whole mechanism: `asSystem()` and `$setAuth()` spread that object, so a per-copy Set would miss the one write nothing else announces. **Every service write passes the tap too, so suppression is keyed on the SERVICE NAME**, not a boolean — a blanket *am I in a call* swallowed the audit row an orders hook writes, which is a different service and genuinely unannounced; measured, then narrowed. The ALS scope survives the emitter's `setImmediate` because it propagates through the scheduling. **The fast path is preserved**: the guard is `!emitter && !ctx._eventListeners.size` and a tap never makes `emitter` truthy, where the naive version cost every app a measured ~6x write. Two limits, both reported rather than guessed: a **function** `channel:` resolver takes `(rows, ctx)` and an orphan write has no ServiceContext, so it gets the bus and one named warning. A function PARAMETER of the same name was already handled — `rewriteExpr`'s walker extended its scope with `collectParams` and with nothing else — which is why the hole only opened when the shadow was declared rather than received, and why every component in the repo happened to avoid it. Scope tracking now also collects what a scope DECLARES: `var` across the whole function body (it hoists), `let`/`const`/`function` per block, and a `for` head's own binding. | <a id="fjs-316"></a>FJS-316 | ui — **four defects in `&lt;Form&gt;`, two of them in what a save SENDS** | 2026-08-16 | The first browser pass over the component that is meant to make an app stop writing forms by hand. **`method="auto"` never resolved.** `_send` tested for `create` and `patch` and fell through to `service.upsert` for everything else — including its default, `auto` — so every automatic save went to upsert. A generated control is refused because its message is on screen beside it; a hand-written form still validates where it always did, in the resource on the way to the service. **`$context.form.disabled` had no consumers.** The prop was documented, put into the context and read by nothing: a disabled form rendered fully editable controls, and a form mid-save stayed typable. | <a id="fjs-314"></a>FJS-314 | cli — **`fli ws:pub` published five packages pinned to a sibling version it had just bumped away** | 2026-08-16 | Found by reading the registry straight after the 17-package release, not by any check. `@frontierjs/junction@0.1.4`, `@frontierjs/litestone@1.1.5`, `@frontierjs/mesa@0.1.3` and `@frontierjs/sierra@0.1.3` each pinned `@frontierjs/toolbelt@0.1.0`, and `create-frontier@0.1.1` pinned `@frontierjs/cli@0.1.1` — npm had neither, because the same run bumped toolbelt to 0.1.1 and cli to 0.1.2. Five packages the registry served happily and nobody could install. **The cause is which file bun reads.** `bun publish` rewrites a `workspace:*` spec, which is why step 02 uses it at all — `npm publish` ships the spec verbatim and no registry resolves one — but it takes the sibling's version from `bun.lock`, not from the sibling's `package.json`, and step 01 wrote every bumped version into the manifests without refreshing the lock. The window is one release wide and closes on its own: the next install makes the lock agree, so the command works again with nobody the wiser. **Three fixes, and the third is the one that matters.** Step 01 refreshes the lock before step 02 packs and stages it into the release commit (a manifest disagreeing with the lock beside it fails `--frozen-lockfile` in every image build). The two missing versions were backfilled to npm under a temporary dist-tag, so `latest` did not walk backwards onto them — all 17 published packages resolve. And the `registry` phase now asks the question that was missing: every published manifest's sibling ranges are handed to npm to resolve, exact pin and range alike, so semver is not reimplemented here; **an unsatisfiable one fails by name.** Checked against a negative control — an injected `@frontierjs/toolbelt@9.9.9` fails 12 packages and names both sides. An OPTIONAL peer is excluded (it is allowed to go unmet) and unreachable counts as resolvable, since the network story is told once already. **What this closes is a blind spot, not just a bug**: `registry` asked whether a NAME resolved, `scaffold` installs from packed tarballs rather than the registry, and `deploy`'s npm branch — the one thing that would have caught it — was allowed to fail by name for [FJS-267](#fjs-267), so the two defects hid each other for the length of a release — [01-version-all.md](packages/cli/commands/workspace/publish/_steps/01-version-all.md) · [02-publish-all.md](packages/cli/commands/workspace/publish/_steps/02-publish-all.md) · [ci.mjs](scripts/ci.mjs) |

Then the form tail, eight defects, which is what closing [FJS-077](#fjs-077) needed anyway. Then the Dropdown family: two kit defects and **a Mesa runtime defect that broke every compound component behind a conditional** ([FJS-311](#fjs-311), [FJS-312](#fjs-312)). Then the Tabs family, whose arrow keys selected the disabled tab and whose Home/End answered neither end ([FJS-313](#fjs-313)). The last three specs were the components called "mostly static markup", and they still cost one ([FJS-315](#fjs-315)) — a keyed list given the same key twice, leaving orphaned nodes on screen. **The two that cost nothing are worth naming too**: `Button`/`Btn` and `Label`/`Card` came out clean, which is the evidence [FJS-056](#fjs-056) never had. | <a id="fjs-315"></a>FJS-315 | ui — **Pagination gave two ellipses the same key, and left an orphan on screen per page visited** | 2026-08-16 | Found by the first browser pass over `Pagination` ([FJS-028](#fjs-028)). | <a id="fjs-308"></a>FJS-308 | repo — **two theme switchers, neither of which drove the design system, and both apps wrote a third by hand** | 2026-08-16 | The `themeStore` clause of [FJS-056](#fjs-056), and it was worse than filed. `@frontierjs/ui/stores/themeStore.js` added a `.dark` class to `<html>`; **`@frontierjs/css` has no `.dark` rule anywhere**, so `setMode('dark')` changed a class and not one pixel — and it documented a `<ThemeProvider />` component that does not exist and never has. `@frontierjs/sierra/theme` set `data-theme` on `<html>`, which the design system reads nowhere either. **Neither had a single caller in the repo**, and `example` had gone and written its own applier in `web/src/prefs.js` — which is the symptom that says a mechanism was never real. **Ruled by what each package can do.** `@frontierjs/css` owns the vocabulary — eleven `theme-*` classes, each a block of inheriting custom properties and nothing else — but it ships zero JavaScript by design, so it cannot own a switch. Beating first paint needs a script in `<head>`, and only the build can write one, so **Sierra owns the switch**; the kit could only ever have been a second answer, so `themeStore.js` is deleted rather than fixed. Sierra's module now applies a CLASS from a list the app declares (`theme: { themes, default, system, persist, key, apply }`), `setTheme` refuses a name the app did not declare and prints the list with it, `toggleTheme` cycles because "the other one" is not a question eleven themes can answer, and a persisted theme the app has since dropped is ignored rather than restored as a class with no stylesheet behind it. **The element is `<html>` and is deliberately not a knob**: `<body>` does not exist when a `<head>` script runs, so a `target: 'body'` would be a setting whose only effect is to bring the flash back. `apply: 'attribute'` keeps the old spelling for an app that wants it. `example` drops its applier and gains what it could not write for itself — the theme applied before first paint — and its `verify:ui` asserts the two halves separately (`settings.themeApplies`), because a class on the wrong element and a class no stylesheet defines look identical from outside. | <a id="fjs-d34"></a>FJS-D34 | litestone — **Should a bulk write be able to announce its rows, and who asks for it?** | 2026-08-16 | **Ruled 2026-08-16 — per CALL, with a client-level floor.** `announce`: `collection` (default) · `rows` · `none`, precedence option → `createClient({ announce })` → `collection`. Per model was the tempting answer — it is where `@@log` and `@@softDelete` live and declared-in-the-seed is the house thesis — but batch size is a property of the CALL, and one `Order` model carries both a three-row cancel and a two-million-row purge. Adaptive-on-size is not decidable at all: the count is unknowable before the statement without a second query, so a threshold has to spend the memory before it can apply. `none` earns its place because *no subscribers* is not something a caller can express, and a nightly purge nobody is watching should not send every tab back to the server. **The cost is memory, not a query** — `updateMany`/`removeMany`/`deleteMany` already append `RETURNING *` on a logged model, so the change is one condition wide and free wherever a trail is already written, and it is ANDed with the audience so an app that opts in with nobody listening still takes no `RETURNING`. `upsertMany` gets the better answer at this tier: the create/update split is already computed, so each half announces truthfully rather than the batch calling itself an update. **An announced bulk row goes through `read()`** — off `RETURNING` it still carries `@guarded` and `@encrypted` columns that every other event path strips, and `announceBulk` owns the three-way branch so that cannot be right at four sites and wrong at the fifth. Refusals are loud both ways: an unknown value is `InvalidAnnounceError` (400) thrown BEFORE the statement, and `announce: 'rows' `on a jsonl model is refused by name — append-only, no `RETURNING`, no row that could ever be announced. `DECISIONS.md` § Query & write semantics — [litestone/core/client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [litestone/drivers/jsonl.js](packages/litestone/src/drivers/jsonl.js) · [test/write-events.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/write-events.test.ts) · relates to [FJS-307](#fjs-307) |
| <a id="fjs-079"></a>FJS-079 | ui — **a `DateTime` column got no control, and the hole was invisible** | 2026-08-16 | `Input` refuses to map `format: date-time` to `datetime-local` and is right to: Litestone stores an INSTANT and that element reads and writes a wall clock with no zone, so the offset is truncated going in and a zoneless string is parsed as UTC coming out — **two shifts, in opposite directions and of different sizes**, on a control that looks perfectly correct. So the column fell through to a text box and nothing said so. **The fix is a control, because the conversion has to happen at both edges and a type attribute cannot hold one.** `DateTimeInput.mesa` renders the instant into local components going in and reads local components as local coming out, which round-trips exactly as long as the zone shown is the reader's own — and the `.field-addon` beside it NAMES that zone, because "14:30" without one is the ambiguity the whole file exists to close. Three details are the substance: an incomplete entry answers `''` rather than an invented instant (`datetime-local` reports empty for every state short of a whole date and time); a stored value carrying seconds gets `step="1"` so someone changing the day does not silently truncate `09:30:45` to `09:30:00`, asked of the VALUE because the schema says `DateTime` and not how precise this column's rows are; and there is deliberately **no `oninput`** — a handler taking the native event would read `e.target.value` and get the wall clock, the one value this component exists to stop anyone sending, so `onvalue(iso)` is the only callback that carries a value. Wired the way `FJS-D17` rules: Sierra's table answers `datetime` for the format, `FormField.mesa` binds that name, and an app writes neither. **The filed blocker — "`DatePicker` exists and is unverified" — had expired**: `DatePicker` is verified, and it is still not the answer here, because it is a range picker an app CHOOSES rather than the control a `Date` column gets. Asserted in a real browser, because the entire defect lives in the browser's zone; the spec prints the offset it measured and says out loud when a run at UTC could not ask the question — [DateTimeInput.mesa](packages/ui/components/forms/DateTimeInput.mesa) · [field-rules.js](packages/sierra/src/junction/field-rules.js) · [datetime.spec.mjs](packages/ui/test/browser/specs/datetime.spec.mjs) |
| <a id="fjs-077"></a>FJS-077 | ui — **controls that resolved nothing from the form context, and four more that shadowed the schema they did read** | 2026-08-16 | Filed as four deaf controls plus a label bug; running it in a browser found **eight defects, three of them structural**. `Switch` carried no `name` at all, so a form's dirty tracking could not see the field. `RadioGroup` wrapped itself in a `<Field>` only when a `label` PROP was passed — and inside a `<Form>` nobody passes one, because the label is a schema fact — so it rendered bare: no label, no `required`, and structurally nowhere to put a server error. `Combobox` put no `name` on its input, which is the one rule `controls.js` states in bold. `Select` read the rule for its enum members and never for `required`, with a comment beside it calling the required a schema fact. `Textarea` had a `required` prop and ignored `maxLength`. `NumberInput` resolved neither bound, and those feed the ± buttons as well as the input — **a schema minimum the component could not see was a stepper that walked straight past it**. And five controls passed `label={label \|\| name}` to `<Field>`, which is always truthy when a name is given, so the raw column name arrived as an EXPLICIT label and `@label("Customer")` was unreachable through the kit. All eight fixed; `RadioGroup`'s selected card also stopped hand-mixing a `color-mix()` and reads the tint ramp, per the rule the two token-divergence issues left behind. `aria-required` rather than a native one where the element is not the value — a radiogroup is not labelable, and a MultiSelect's inner box is a search field whose resting state is empty, so `required` there refuses every submit. **What makes this closed rather than plausible is one fixture**: twelve controls in one `<Form>`, every field declaring a `title` that is NOT its title-cased column name — so a control that shadows the schema is a different string on screen rather than a coincidence — then one rejected submit naming every field at once, because a control's own error line takes a request, a throw and a form that mapped it, and no render test can produce one. 36 assertions — [form-tail.spec.mjs](packages/ui/test/browser/specs/form-tail.spec.mjs) · [ui/components/forms/](packages/ui/components/forms/) |
| <a id="fjs-307"></a>FJS-307 | litestone — **Every bulk write, every hard `delete` and `restore` were invisible to `onEvent`, and to `$tapEvents` with it** (filed as FJS-305, which was already the `CommandPalette` id — renumbered rather than reused) | 2026-08-16 | Measured before and after against a real client, one call per method: **seven of eleven write methods announced NOTHING** — `createMany`, `updateMany`, `upsertMany`, `removeMany`, `deleteMany`, `delete` and `restore`. The filing said `restore` fired and did not name `upsertMany`; running it said otherwise, which is why the fix starts with a grid. So [FJS-010](#fjs-010)'s announcement covered single-row writes and stopped at the bulk boundary: a job that `createMany`d a hundred rows left every open tab stale, and a hard `delete` left a row on screen that no longer exists. **Ruled and built: a rowless announcement is a first-class kind, not a gap.** Litestone events now carry `scope` — `row` (one row, `result` is it) or `collection` (`count` rows matching `where`, and this statement never built them) — and the discriminator is STATED rather than read off `result`, because `result: null` is not one fact: a **`select: false` write is row-scoped and has no row**, and that third case was already being emitted and already being dropped by Junction, which is the argument for the shape. `delete` and `restore` had their rows all along and announce per row (`remove` / `update`, matching what the audit trail already calls them); the four bulk methods announce a collection. A write matching no rows announces nothing, or a filter that hit nothing would send every tab back to the server. Junction routes a rowless event as **`changed`** — one name for all three operations, since the only honest answer on the other side is the same for each — and `where` reaches the in-process bus but **never the wire**, because a filter is made of the caller's own values. The browser store answers `changed` with the reload it already gives an undecidable record, burst-coalesced. What is NOT covered: no drive exercises this end to end, because `example` has no bulk write reachable from a request — the frame is asserted in Junction's suite and the store's answer in the client suite, and the link between them is the generic frame dispatch every other event uses. Per-row announcement for a bulk write was the open half and is ruled — [FJS-D34](#fjs-d34), `announce: 'rows'` — [litestone/core/client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [junction/core/litestone.ts](packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts) · [junction/client/index.ts](packages/junction/src/client/index.ts) · [litestone/test/write-events.test.ts](packages/litestone/test/write-events.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-119"></a>FJS-119 | ui — **the kit's toasts were fire-and-forget: an id came back and nothing took one** | 2026-08-16 | `add/success/error/warning/info` each returned an id and `remove(id)` was the only thing that ever accepted one, so a long action could announce that it had STARTED or announce how it went, never both on one toast. Every caller therefore said nothing until the end — a transition that crosses the network looked like a click that did nothing — or left a *Sending…* on screen for good. **`toasts.loading(message)` now returns a handle**: `{ id, update(type, message, duration?), dismiss() }`, with `toasts.update(id, patch)` underneath it for anything holding a bare id. A loading toast carries a **spinner instead of a verdict icon** and no drain bar, because nothing knows how long the work will take and a bar that empties while the request is still open is a lie about the state of the system; settling it gives it the lifetime it did not have. **Three details are the substance.** Settling a toast the reader already dismissed answers `false` and does NOT put it back. `update()` reschedules the auto-dismiss only when the caller states a duration, so editing a message does not silently restart a running timer. And the pending timers are held by id and cleared on remove — settling twice, which is a retry that fails and then succeeds, used to leave the first timer running to remove the second message early. **Proven where it matters rather than only in a unit**: the kit drive asserts the whole lifecycle including NODE IDENTITY — an update that removes the toast and adds another looks identical on screen and loses the reader's place in the stack — and `example`'s `verify:ui` now drives a real order transition through it, marking the loading toast and finding the same node settled. `basecamp`'s `channels/index.mesa`, the screen this was filed from, drops its workaround: `busy` stays because it is what disables the row's buttons, which is a different job from saying what is happening |
| <a id="fjs-d04"></a>FJS-D04 | litestone — **How does a subscriber attach to `onEvent` after construction?** It is fixed at `createClient` and there is no post-construction subscribe, unlike `$tapQuery(fn)`. Mirroring `$tapQuery`'s shape is the obvious move. Unblocks [FJS-010](#fjs-010) | 2026-08-16 | **Ruled 2026-08-16: mirror `$tapQuery`, and it was two lines.** `db.$tapEvents(fn)` adds to `ctx._eventListeners` and returns an unsubscribe, exactly as `$tapQuery` does for queries; the Set sits beside `_queryListeners` and is shared by reference for the same reason. **One thing the mirror had to add**: a query tap is read at the point of firing, but an event tap has to be visible to the UPSERT FAST PATH's guard, or subscribing silently costs every app a measured ~6x write. So the payload carries `event` (a transition has no `operation`) and the guard is `!emitter && !ctx._eventListeners.size`, which keeps the fast path for an app that taps nothing.
