| <a id="fjs-d05"></a>FJS-D05 | litestone — **Multi-tenancy config API.** db-per-tenant only today; the config shape is undesigned | 2026-08-16 | **Ruled 2026-08-16, and built: tenancy is DECLARED in the seed — one `tenancy { }` block beside `database { }`, two strategies.** The filed question was a config shape; what it needed was an OWNER. db-per-tenant worked and its configuration lived in three places that could disagree — a `createTenantRegistry()` call, a `tenants:` slice in `litestone.config.js` the CLI read three keys of, and nothing in the schema — so `litestone tenant create` and the running app could each be right about a different directory. Precedence is now stated once: **option → declaration → default**. The other half was larger: **row-level tenancy was not a framework concept at all**, and basecamp hand-writes the rule on fifteen models. `strategy row` desugars into **`@@deny`, never `@@allow`** — allows are OR'd within an operation, so a generated allow would WIDEN every read to the whole tenant — plus a `@default(auth().<claim>)` stamp, and it is two rules because `checkCreatePolicy` runs before the stamp: an absent column is legitimate on create and belongs to nobody on read. `@@tenant(none)` / `@@tenant(column:)` answer what the block cannot judge, and models declaring neither are reported once by name. Resolution is asked rather than copied — `registry.tenantFor({host, headers, principal})` — and `createApp({ tenants })` installs `withTenantDb` in place of `withLitestoneDb`. Deferred with a reason: `check(parent)` delegation, [FJS-282](#fjs-282). `DECISIONS.md` § Access control — [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md) § Access control · [docs/multi-tenancy.md](packages/litestone/docs/multi-tenancy.md) · [tenancy.js](packages/litestone/src/core/tenancy.js) |
| <a id="fjs-d06"></a>FJS-D06 | repo — **The coherence-review vocabulary proposals** | 2026-08-16 | **Ruled 2026-08-16.** Filed as one row, and it was never one question. **§1 `Hook` splits into three, not five** — Hook (may mutate or halt), Guard (allow/deny only), Observer (receives, cannot act) — on the axis the proposal named, which is the axis WordPress (`apply_filters`/`do_action`), Directus (filter/action hooks) and Payload (one access fn whose return type decides) all split on. None of them shipped more than two tiers and Rails/Prisma/Ember went the other way and removed mechanisms, so **Delegate is refused**: `onPasswordResetRequested` is the only one in the tree and auth's docs saying *required* costs nothing. The tier is the value; the renames (`conduit.hooks` → `observers`) are incidental. **§4 `Provider` is adopted with the plainer meaning — a third-party entity the app includes** (identity provider, payment provider), not the filed *swappable implementation of a contract*. The two nearly coincide here because almost every swappable implementation IS an integration point, and conduit already had the word for exactly this (`'provider'` target kind = external REST API). `Adapter` refused — it names a shape, not a party. **It must never mean Laravel's Service Provider**, which is this repo's **Plugin**. Two in-tree collisions cleared rather than tolerated: `app.provide` → **`app.claim`** (it never provided anything) and `_metricsProviders` → **`_metricsSources`** behind `app.registerMetricsSource`, which also retires §5's private-field reach-in. **§6**: Manifest ceded to MV3 — the Deployment half was already done (`Release`) and sierra's route table followed ([FJS-284](#fjs-284)); Gate and Policy both stay; **Channel is a broadcast set and a delivery medium is a Transport** ([FJS-285](#fjs-285)); `Edge` refused; `Trust Hierarchy` retired for *the gate ladder* ([FJS-286](#fjs-286)). **§7 `Slice` deferred** — Django Apps, Rails Engines and Phoenix Contexts each earned the name because a THIRD PARTY shipped one, and here both slices are in-repo. The map column is fixed regardless. §2/§3/§5/§8 keep their own ids. Unblocks [FJS-D10](#fjs-d10) — [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md) § Naming & vocabulary · [IDEAS/coherence-review.md](IDEAS/coherence-review.md) |
| <a id="fjs-d09"></a>FJS-D09 | litestone — **Migrations, second tier** — rollback stance (`--backup`?), row-count assertion after rebuilds, second-granular timestamp collisions | 2026-08-16 | **Ruled 2026-08-16, and built.** **No `down`, ever**: a rebuild is a `DROP TABLE`, so the inverse of *drop a column* is *invent the values it held*, and a generated down that runs, succeeds and leaves a database only looking restored is worse than none. The way back is a copy taken first — `migrate apply --backup[=dir]` copies EVERY declared SQLite database before the FIRST is migrated (one taken as each database's turn comes round is a copy of a half-migrated fleet) and refuses to migrate anything if a copy fails; off by default, and without it apply NAMES what it cannot take back — a pending file that drops a table, and every `.js` migration, whose contents nothing can read. Whether the previous RELEASE can still serve is the other question and stays `release --from`'s. **Row count**: a copy reading fewer rows than the original holds is an error to nobody — SQLite inserted what it was asked, the runner saw a statement return — and `DROP TABLE` is the next line; the rebuild now compares the counts in between, as a CHECK on a one-row temp table whose constraint NAME is the message SQLite prints, since `RAISE()` is trigger-body only. **Timestamps**: filename order IS apply order, so a new migration is named after the last file in the directory rather than after the clock — same second, same label used to overwrite, same second, different labels applied alphabetically (`evolve` before `initial`). `DECISIONS.md` § Migrations — [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md) § Migrations · [backup.js](packages/litestone/src/core/backup.js) · [migrate.js](packages/litestone/src/core/migrate.js) `rowCountGuard` · [migrations.js](packages/litestone/src/core/migrations.js) `nextMigrationName` |
| <a id="fjs-d10"></a>FJS-D10 | junction — **The deferred API cluster** | 2026-08-16 | **Ruled 2026-08-16 — two adopted, two refused, and the fourth was a defect wearing a naming question.** **`IAuth` partial acceptance — ADOPTED**: the interface declares six required methods and Junction invokes two (`verifySession` on both inbound paths, `sessionFor` behind `runAs`); the other four belong to `@frontierjs/auth`'s `/auth/*` routes and are called nowhere in the package, so an app was stubbing four methods that never run. `SessionVerifier = Pick<IAuth,'verifySession'> & Partial<IAuth>` is what `createApp({ auth })` and `setAuth()` take — DERIVED, because a hand-written member list drifts and because `Partial` is what keeps a fuller provider assignable past the excess-property check. **`publish()` string shorthand — REFUSED**: `channel: 'posts'` is already the declarative spelling and `svc.pipelines()` refuses a service declaring both, so a third spelling would arrive with a rule already policing the second. **Typed `createSchema` inference — REFUSED as filed, adopted cheap**: types come from the seed (a model service is validated by `autoValidate` off the JSON Schema and writes no schema at all), so `createSchema<T>()` takes the type the author already has and constrains the field map to its keys, rather than an inference engine competing with zod. **`createClient` options grouping — REFUSED** (it renames the container and leaves the precedence ladder exactly as subtle); **`setters`/`getters` → `read`/`write` adopted in name, and unblocked 2026-08-16** — grading the name found the mechanism reached 4 of its 16 declared operations, which is [FJS-288](#fjs-288); it reaches all seventeen now, so the rename is free to land — [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md) § API design |
| <a id="fjs-d17"></a>FJS-D17 | sierra — **The UI plugin system is limited** — what else should a UI plugin be able to contribute? | 2026-08-16 | **Ruled 2026-08-16: a plugin contributes a CONTROL, and a control is two registrations in two packages.** `registerControl(name, resolve)` in sierra's `field-rules.js` says which control a column gets — a name, a descriptor, or null to decline; `registerFormControl(name, Component)` in `@frontierjs/ui/controls` says what that name renders as. The split is a dependency rule rather than a taste: field-rules is a leaf that has to run in plain Node (`formFields()` is called from a test, a prerender and a snapshot) and the kit peers only on mesa and css, so a NAME is the only thing that crosses. **The row's premise had also expired twice** — the field LIST shipped 2026-08-15, so it was not `make()` only; and the real limit was never the table's size but that it was a `switch` in a published package with an `{#if}` ladder behind it in a second one, so contributing a control meant forking both. The kit's five are now entries in the same table a contribution enters (`FormField.mesa`), and a registered name REPLACES a built-in of that name. Refused: a control over a `readOnly` column (the Data boundary refuses the write, so the form could not submit), and a resolver answering a component rather than a name. **The other two surfaces are the same mechanism and neither ships until its generator does** — a cell/detail renderer and a filter control, tracked at `IDEAS/overview.md` 1.1, because a registry with no consumer is a name nobody can call — [DECISIONS.md](DECISIONS.md) § UI substrate · [field-rules.js](packages/sierra/src/junction/field-rules.js) · [controls.js](packages/ui/controls.js) |
| <a id="fjs-129"></a>FJS-129 | ui — **`CommandPalette` declared its own `--cp-*` token namespace** | 2026-08-16 | 276 lines, 21 custom properties, five defined without reading a token — `--cp-radius: 12px`, `--cp-font: 'SF Mono', …`, `--cp-shadow: 0 24px 80px rgba(0,0,0,0.72)` — plus five literal colours and a paddings-and-font-sizes-in-px layout no theme or `.dense` could reach. Now **254 lines and two knobs** (`--cp-accent`, `--cp-font`), each reading a token: `--card-radius`, `--font-mono`, the `--text-*` ladder, the `--space-*` ladder, `--shadow-lg`, `--rule`, `--surface-raised`, `--motion-fast`. **Three of its own things turned out to be shipped ones.** The keycaps were a local `.cp-kbd` while `code.css` styles `<kbd>` on the ELEMENT, so deleting the class was the whole fix; the badge is `.badge` with a tone; and the active row's three hand-mixed `color-mix()` calls are the package's TINT ramp — set `--bg-mix` and `--tint-surface`/`--tint-rule`/`--tint-ink` follow, already contrast-measured across every theme. **The scrim became a token in `@frontierjs/css`** (`--scrim`, tokens.css): the palette dimmed at 0.72 while every `<dialog>` in the package dimmed at 0.45, and neither could be retuned by a theme. It is a literal rather than a blend of `--ink` because a scrim never inverts — mixed from ink it goes white on a dark theme. Classes are `fjs-cp-*` now, and **two drives named the old ones** — `example`'s and `basecamp`'s — which is the cost of renaming a kit class and is worth knowing before the next rename: a component's class names are part of its published surface the moment anything drives it. `basecamp`'s went unnoticed until its own drive ran, because grepping the apps' `src/` finds nothing; the selectors live in `web/test/`. 27 assertions in the kit drive, seven of them the failures above asked as measurements |
| <a id="fjs-305"></a>FJS-305 | ui — **`CommandPalette` handled every keypress twice** | 2026-08-16 | `on:keydown={handleKeydown}` was bound on the backdrop AND on the search input, and keydown bubbles — so one press ran the handler twice. Every arrow key **skipped a row**, and **Enter ran the chosen command twice**, which for a command palette is the difference between one order and two. Invariant 11 says the nearest delegation root owns an event and a handler must fire once; this is that rule inside a single component. Found by the first spec to press ArrowDown twice and check WHICH row was active — `example`'s `verify:ui` has driven this palette since it was written and asserts that it opens, filters and runs a command, none of which can see a doubled press. The handler now lives on the backdrop only, where it still sees the input's keys by bubbling, and the drive counts invocations rather than trusting an idempotent action |
| <a id="fjs-128"></a>FJS-128 | ui — **`DatePicker` was a second design system inside the first** | 2026-08-16 | 468 lines of local `<style>` declaring **107 custom properties, 47 of them defined without reading a single design token** — its own six-rung radius scale, its own font-size scale, `font-family: sans-serif`, eight literal colours, and four **base64 PNG** nav arrows, which are black pixels and therefore invisible on a dark theme and unable to follow a tone. Now **320 lines and six declared properties**, four of which are the documented knobs (`--dp-cell`, `--dp-accent`, `--dp-band`, `--dp-layer`) and each reads a token. Zero literal colours, zero base64. Radii come from `--pill-radius`/`--card-radius`/`--field-radius`, type from `--font-primary` and the `--text-*` ladder, spacing from `--space-*` **so `.dense` reaches the whole panel**, elevation from `--shadow-lg`, and the focus ring from `--ring-*` — drawn here rather than restated as a recipe, because `focus.css` opts elements in BY CLASS and cannot name a class this kit invented. **The selected day is `.chip`**, so `--fill`/`--on-fill` derive its text colour against any tone a theme defines instead of assuming white. Every internal class is now `fjs-dp-*`, so `.disabled`, `.start` and `.end` are only ever compounds of one — the file no longer writes a selector that reads like package vocabulary. **The blocker was the safety net and it was built first**: `FJS-028`'s drive covers this component with 30 assertions, six of them the failures above asked as measurements — a theme switch moves the painted colours, `.dense` moves the padding, overriding `--dp-cell` moves the disc, the grid track AND the band's bleed, and the arrows are SVG rather than a background image. Rewriting it also surfaced one defect of its own, caught by the screenshot rather than the suite and now pinned: the band rule and the range-end rule are equally specific and the band's comes first, so setting only `--bg-mix` on the end left it painted in the band with a fill's text colour. `FJS-129` (`CommandPalette`) is the same shape and still open |
| <a id="fjs-306"></a>FJS-306 | repo — **`@@hasTemplates` exists only in the Data realm — there is no `$withTemplates`, no `$onlyTemplates`, and nothing above Litestone knows the feature at all.** It is the exact parallel of `@@softDelete`: the attribute adds `isTemplate Boolean @default(false)`, filters it out of every read by default, and is opted back in with `{ withTemplates: true }` / `{ onlyTemplates: true }`. Measured against a real client — default `['live-a']`, withTemplates `['live-a','tmpl-a']`, onlyTemplates `['tmpl-a']`. **Above that boundary it vanishes**: zero references in `@frontierjs/junction`, zero in `@frontierjs/sierra`, and `DIRECTIVE_PARAMS` names eight `$` keys of which none is a template. So an app declaring `@@hasTemplates` has a template list screen it cannot build over HTTP or from the browser — the same shape as the restore screen [FJS-290](#fjs-290) just closed for soft delete, one feature over, and worse, because soft delete at least HAD the wire name and the server-side parse before that fix. **The cost is the shape of the gap, not its size**: `parseDirectives`, `QueryDirectives`, `buildQueryString`/`buildWsQuery`, `RESERVED_PARAMS` and the two `$check*` validators are each a table naming a fixed set, so a Data-realm feature that grows a per-call option is invisible to all six until someone edits all six. Deciding whether the answer is two more entries or a rule that derives them from the schema is the actual question | 2026-08-16 | **Fixed 2026-08-16.** One row per directive rather than two more entries in six lists. `@frontierjs/toolbelt/directives` is now a table of `{ param, name, read }` — `DIRECTIVE_PARAMS` and `RESERVED_PARAMS` derive from it and `parseDirectives` is a loop over it, so a new directive is one row and cannot arrive half wired. `$withTemplates` / `$onlyTemplates` are that row: `QueryDirectives` declares them, `parseQuery` reads them from `ctx.directives` and the `$`-in-query fallback alike, the service forwards them to `findMany`/`findUnique`/`findFirst`, and the browser client emits them on both wires. Sierra needed no plumbing — its router and resource are pass-throughs — but `matchesQuery` held its own two lists and now reads `DIRECTIVE_PARAMS`, keeping only the question it is the one that can answer: `$onlyTemplates` is undecidable from a record, so it reloads. The parity test asserts in both directions and was what caught the client half. — [toolbelt/directives.js](packages/toolbelt/src/directives/directives.js) · [junction/core/directives.ts](packages/junction/src/core/directives.ts) · [litestone/core/client.js:2219](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js#L2219) · relates to [FJS-290](#fjs-290) |
| <a id="fjs-292"></a>FJS-292 | litestone — **A caller asking for `$search` on a model with no `@@fts` gets a 500.** Measured: `GET /api/products?$search=widget` → `500 GeneralError: search() is not available on "product" — add @@fts([field1, field2]) to the model`. The MESSAGE is right and names the fix; the STATUS says the server broke. It did not — the caller asked for something this model does not offer, which is a 4xx, and the difference is not cosmetic: a 500 pages someone, is counted as an availability incident, and is retried by clients that would not retry a 400. Now reachable from the browser client for the first time, since `$search` was one of the three directives it could not express until [FJS-290](#fjs-290). The error class needs a `status` — `toFrameworkError` reads a numeric `status` off any thrown value, so this is one property on the class Litestone already owns, not a mapper in Junction | 2026-08-16 | **Fixed 2026-08-16.** `CapabilityNotDeclaredError` in Litestone — 400, `retryable: false`, carrying `model`/`asked`/`requires` and naming the attribute that would make the request legal. One property on a class Litestone owns, which is all `toFrameworkError` needs; `errors.snapshot.md` records it as `BadRequest`, and picked up the lock and `SoftDeletedUniqueError` rows that had gone stale in it. `restore()`, `transition()` and `optimizeFts()` threw the same bare `Error` and are the same class now. **The half nobody had looked at**: on a model that DOES declare `@@fts`, the find branch destructured `{ rows, total }` off the array `search()` returns, so a search that matched answered `{"limit":20,"offset":0}` — no data, no total, 200. Same shape as the `restoreMany` of [FJS-245](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md), a method typed on `LitestoneTable` that nothing executed. Total is now a second id-only pass, skipped when the first page is short enough to be the total. — [litestone/core/client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [junction/errors.snapshot.md](packages/junction/errors.snapshot.md) · relates to [FJS-290](#fjs-290) |
| <a id="fjs-293"></a>FJS-293 | litestone — **`onlyDeleted` on a model that declares no `@@softDelete` is a silent no-op.** Measured on `example`, whose `Order` has no soft delete: `GET /api/orders` and `GET /api/orders?$onlyDeleted=true` both answer `total: 3` — the same rows, with nothing anywhere saying the directive did not apply. `onlyDeleted` means *show me the deleted ones and nothing else*, so answering the live rows is the opposite of what was asked, and it is the class of failure the two `$check*` validators exist for: a filter typo is a 400 by name and a sort typo is a 400 by name, while a directive the model cannot honour is nothing at all. Same for `withDeleted`, `withTemplates` and `onlyTemplates` — `onlyX` is the loud case because its answer is wrong rather than merely unfiltered. Whether a directive the schema cannot honour should refuse or be reported is the question; **silence is the one answer that is clearly wrong**, since it is indistinguishable from an empty result the caller can act on | 2026-08-16 | **Fixed 2026-08-16.** Refuse — a 400 by name, the same class [FJS-292](#fjs-292) gave the methods, naming the model and the attribute it does not declare. `onlyDeleted`/`onlyTemplates` on every read and write. **`withDeleted`/`withTemplates` deliberately do not refuse** — they ask to WIDEN, and on a model that hides nothing the full row set already IS everything, so the answer is right rather than accidentally right; generic code that does not know the model asks exactly this (Studio passes `withDeleted: true` on every row it opens). Only the flag that cannot be satisfied at all refuses. **The silence had a mechanical cause**: `sdMode()` answered `'live'` for a model with no soft delete and every caller guarded the call with `softDelete ? injectSoftDeleteFilter(…, sdMode(args)) : where` — so on exactly the models that could not honour a flag, the function that could have refused it was never called. `applySdFilter(where, args)` is `applyHtFilter`'s sibling and is asked on every read; two inline ternaries in `findManyCursor` and `search()` that computed the mode by hand are the same call now. An INCLUDE builds its own SQL and reaches neither function, so it carries the refusal itself, naming the target model. — [litestone/core/client.js](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · relates to [FJS-306](#fjs-306) |
| <a id="fjs-290"></a>FJS-290 | junction — **Three of the eight directives cannot be expressed from the browser client.** `FindParams` declares `query`, `offset`, `limit`, `orderBy`, `select`, `populate` and nothing else, and `buildQueryString` emits exactly those — so `$search`, `$withDeleted` and `$onlyDeleted` have a server that reads them (`parseDirectives`, `@frontierjs/toolbelt/directives`), a URL grammar that carries them, a `QueryDirectives` type in `core/context.ts` that names them, and no way for `client.service(x).find(…)` to ask. Sierra inherits the hole, since its resource is a pass-through of this client: a `.lite` declaring `@@softDelete` gives an app a restore flow it cannot build a list screen for, and FTS5 search is reachable only by hand-writing a URL. **Two things at once, which is why it is filed rather than fixed in passing**: `FindParams` also CONTAINS `query`, so it cannot honestly be renamed `directives` (Invariant 10 — the container would be the conflation the invariant exists to prevent) until the filters come out of it. Adopting `core/context.ts`'s `QueryDirectives` as the one owner does both — it is already the server-side name for this exact set — and turns the client's second argument into the same noun the bridge, the router and Sierra's resource all now use. | <a id="fjs-289"></a>FJS-289 | junction — **`autoFilter` and `autoSort` were asking the client a question it could not answer, and reading the shrug as a yes.** Both derived hooks pass the accessor to `db.$checkWhere` / `db.$checkOrderBy` untouched — `accessorOpt ?? ctx.service` — and a service that declares no `model:` is named for its URL, which is PLURAL. | <a id="fjs-297"></a>FJS-297 | mesa — **`close`, `cancel`, `toggle`, `beforetoggle` and `invalid` were DELEGATED, so `<dialog on:close>` never fired** | 2026-08-16 | Found the hour `@frontierjs/ui` got a browser drive of its own ([FJS-028](#fjs-028)), by the first spec that closed a `Drawer`. Mesa routes every handler through one listener on the delegation root and keeps a list of the events that do not bubble; five names were missing from it, and a miss there fails in the worst available way — the handler is bound to a root the event never reaches, so the element renders, the BROWSER does its half, and the component's half silently never runs. `Modal` and `Drawer` are both `<dialog on:close on:cancel>`: Escape closed the dialog natively, `handleClose` never ran, `bind:open` was never written back, and the caller's state said *open* for the rest of the page's life — **so the overlay could not be reopened**. Nothing threw, and `example`'s `verify:ui` had covered `Modal` since it was written, because it asserted focus going IN and never the way out. Bubbling is now measured in a real browser rather than assumed (`ui/test/browser/specs/events.spec.mjs` reads `event.bubbles` off the dispatched event) and pinned against the compiler by five cases in `compiler.test.js` |
| <a id="fjs-298"></a>FJS-298 | mesa — **a `children` snippet reached `{...$attributes}` and threw on a getter-only DOM property** | 2026-08-16 | `restProps` is *everything the caller passed that the component did not declare*, and a component built around `<slot />` declares no `children` — so `{#snippet children(x)}` arrived as a spread attribute and `spreadAttributes` did `el.children = fn`, which is a getter on `Element`. The TypeError came out of an effect and took the whole component's render with it, from markup that reads exactly like the documented snippet-prop form. Two fixes, because there were two faults: `children` is slot content and never an attribute, so it joins `class`/`$class` in the skip set; and a function whose property is a getter with no setter is now skipped rather than assigned. **The test is a getter WITHOUT a setter and not the absence of a setter** — an event-handler property is a plain own property in some DOM implementations, and refusing to assign where no descriptor exists stopped `onclick` being forwarded at all, which the suite caught immediately |
| <a id="fjs-299"></a>FJS-299 | ui — **`Tooltip` documented `let:tipId`, which is not Mesa, and `<slot tipId={…}>` delivers nothing** | 2026-08-16 | The component's whole accessibility contract is that the trigger carries `aria-describedby` pointing at the tooltip, and the id is generated inside the component — so the caller has to be handed it. The docblock said `<Tooltip let:tipId>`; Mesa has no `let:` directive at all, so it parsed as an unknown attribute and the body read an undefined variable. Measured while writing the first tooltip spec: an attribute written on a `<slot>` is silently ignored too ([FJS-304](#fjs-304)), so there was no working spelling of the documented feature. `Tooltip` now declares `children` and renders `{@render children(tipId)}` with `<slot />` as the fallback for content that does not need the id, and its docblock states both real forms — the `id` prop, and the snippet parameter |
| <a id="fjs-300"></a>FJS-300 | ui — **`DatePicker` threw on month navigation whenever the new month had fewer weeks** | 2026-08-16 | Both panes iterated `{#each { length: 6 }}` and read `calendar[week][day]` from inside an `{#if calendar[week]}` guard. Moving from a 5-row month to a 4-row one recomputes the inner reads before the outer guard tears the row down, so the read landed on `undefined` — `Cannot read properties of undefined (reading '6')`, thrown inside a click handler, where it surfaces as a calendar that simply stops responding. Fixed by iterating the weeks the month actually has (`{#each startDateCalendar as week}`), which also deletes the fixed-six assumption; the general ordering hazard is [FJS-303](#fjs-303). Found by the first spec that clicked *next month*, on a component that had never been opened in a browser |
| <a id="fjs-301"></a>FJS-301 | ui — **`DatePicker`'s `disabledDates` disabled the day BEFORE the one it named, west of Greenwich** | 2026-08-16 | `new Date('2026-01-22')` is UTC midnight by specification, and every other date in the component is a local midnight from `createTimestamp` — so in MST the entry disabled January 21st. Measured, not reasoned: the spec asserted the 22nd was marked and the 21st was. `enabledDates` had it too, and the range form (`'a:b'`) at both ends. A bare `YYYY-MM-DD` is now parsed as a local date and anything carrying a time is left to the platform parser, which is the case where an offset was actually stated |

| <a id="fjs-284"></a>FJS-284 | sierra — **the route table was still called a manifest in code** | 2026-08-16 | Renamed in the direction [FJS-D06](#fjs-d06) ruled. `generateManifest`/`renderManifest` → `generateRouteTable`/`renderRouteTable`, `scanner/generate-manifest.js` → `generate-route-table.js`, the config key `manifest.output` → **`routeTable.output`**, and the object the postbuild pipeline is handed → `routeTable` — which is also the key a user post-build plugin reads off `closeBundle({ outDir, root, config, routeTable })`. | <a id="fjs-296"></a>FJS-296 | auth — **`POST /auth/login` read an object body field as a Litestone where-operator, so the address was a FILTER** | 2026-08-16 | Filed and closed while typing [FJS-063](#fjs-063)'s handlers, which is what made it visible at all. `{ "email": { "contains": "@" } }` reached `sys.user.findFirst({ where: { email } })` intact: with a correct password it signed the caller in as the first matching row, `startsWith` walked the user table with no address known, and `{ "$ne": null }` came back a **500 carrying Litestone's own query vocabulary** to an unauthenticated caller. Not a straight bypass — the password was still verified against whichever row matched — but it turns a spray into a targeted-account selector. `register` was the one that WROTE it: an array email created a user whose address was the array coerced by SQLite, 201 and no complaint. **The cause is structural rather than local**: a raw route has no `autoValidate`, because that is derived from the model and these routes are upstream of every service. So `plugin.ts` grew one reader — a declared field is a string or the request is a 400 naming it, and an undeclared key does not travel. Four tests in `routes.test.ts`; probed before and after |
| <a id="fjs-063"></a>FJS-063 | auth — **login is a user-enumeration timing oracle** · **`plugin.ts` types `app` and `ctx` as `any`** | 2026-08-16 | Both halves. The oracle: an absent address and a user with no password credential both returned before the bcrypt, measured at 9ms against 119ms for a wrong password — so an identical 401 still told a caller which addresses have accounts, in a package where `requestPasswordReset` goes to real trouble not to. Both branches now `await payPasswordCost(password)` against a cost-12 hash of a value nobody holds; what is left between the paths is one database read. `flows.test.ts` bands the floors of three runs at a factor of two, and asserts `DUMMY_HASH` still carries `BCRYPT_COST` — the literal cannot follow the constant, and drift would reopen the gap narrower and silent. The types: `register(app: App)`, `boot(app: App)`, every handler `(ctx: TransportContext)`, the return `Plugin`, `createAuthServices(): Service[]`, and three `as any` casts at call sites deleted as needless. **It paid for itself immediately** — [FJS-296](#fjs-296) above is what the first clean compile pointed at, and a second finding went the other way: `rateLimitHook` is documented for both contexts and its comments say so, but it was TYPED `(ctx: ServiceContext) => void`, so auth's `any` was the only reason its own routes compiled. Junction now returns a `BridgeHook`, whose parameter is wider and therefore still assignable to `Hook` |
| <a id="fjs-285"></a>FJS-285 | notifications — **`channel` means two things fourteen lines apart in one file** | 2026-08-16 | Renamed, in the direction [FJS-D06](#fjs-d06) ruled: a delivery medium is a **Transport**, `channel` stays junction's broadcast set. `Channel`/`BuiltInChannel`/`ChannelError` → `Transport`/`BuiltInTransport`/`TransportError`, `NotificationDriver.channel` → `.transport`, the plugin option `channels:` → `transports:`, `NotificationChannelNotImplementedError` → `NotificationTransportNotImplementedError`. `app.channel(...)` in the in-app driver is untouched and now reads unambiguously — it is the only `channel` left in the package. **The old option name throws naming the new one** rather than being ignored: an unknown key configures nothing and would surface as a missing driver at the first send, which is the failure mode the rename exists to remove |
| <a id="fjs-096"></a>FJS-096 | notifications — **`notify()` addresses a `User`, and a customer is not one** | 2026-08-16 | The unit of address is now **`Recipient`**, whose `id` is optional — the row took both ways out and they turned out to be one. A customer had to be passed as a `User` with an invented id (`customer:<n>`), which works for email and writes an in-app row keyed by an id nothing will ever query. Now each transport states what it needs and `notify()` checks it BEFORE any delivery: `inApp` refuses an id-less recipient with `NotificationRecipientError`, `email` refuses one with no address anywhere, a registered driver is exempt because only it knows what it addresses by. Eager, so a two-transport notification cannot half-land. The near-miss is named in the message too — a recipient carrying `userId` is reported as a probable `SessionContext` with the one-line fix, because `ctx.user` is `{ userId }` and reads as id-less. `example/api/src/notifications/OrderConfirmation.ts` now leaves the id off instead of inventing one |
| <a id="fjs-050"></a>FJS-050 | notifications — **Unconfirmed: `ctx.app.notify` from a service hook** | 2026-08-16 | Confirmed, and pinned: `packages/notifications/tests/hook.test.ts` registers a real service with a real after hook, and the row it writes is the evidence the hook ran. The real consumer is `example/web/src/resources/Notification.mesa` |
| <a id="fjs-049"></a>FJS-049 | notifications — **`plugin.ts` implements only `register()`** | 2026-08-16 | `boot()` and `shutdown()` both added. `requires: ['mailer']` (already there) proves the mailer PLUGIN is configured; `boot()` proves `app.mail` is actually set, which a mailer plugin that registers and installs nothing would otherwise leave to the first send of the deployment. `shutdown()` awaits each driver's own optional `shutdown()`, isolated — one that throws is logged and the rest still run. Also cleared while in there: `app._db`/`app._drivers` were two enumerable properties under a comment claiming they were symbol-keyed; they are one `Symbol.for` state object now, so nothing leaks onto the app surface and `notify()` on an unconfigured app names the missing plugin |
| <a id="fjs-072"></a>FJS-072 | notifications — **The package's own runnable example cannot run** | 2026-08-16 | `examples/Notification.mesa` is now the resource file `example/` actually runs — `createResource` from `@frontierjs/sierra/junction` in a `<script module>` with no markup (Invariant 18), `isUnread` and `markRead` beside it. The three imports it named (`resource`, `derived`, `get` off sierra's root) never existed. `examples/wiring.ts` carried the same class of error in prose and was corrected with it: `ctx.params` (which exists nowhere in Junction — path captures are `ctx.route`), `db.asSystem().users` (the accessor is `db.user`), and `ctx.user` handed straight to `notify()` as though a `SessionContext` were a recipient |
| <a id="fjs-125"></a>FJS-125 | css — **`theme-notebook`'s `--ink-mute` is 2.67:1 and fails AA wherever it is text** | 2026-08-16 | The whole ramp moves, which is the first of the two ways out the row named: fitting the failing rung alone puts it within 0.06 of the old `--ink-soft` and the three tiers collapse into two, so `--ink-soft` drops to make room. Both are the same colour scaled uniformly in linear RGB — chromaticity exact, the operation `tokens.css` and `press.css` already use for this — so it is the identical sage, dark enough to read: `--ink-soft` #59615b → #474e49 (4.94 → 6.62 on `--surface-sunken`, the tightest ground) and `--ink-mute` #858c83 → #60655f (2.67 → 4.61), a 1.44× step between them. **notebook is now an ordinary member of `contrast.spec.js`'s theme list** rather than an exclusion with a reason attached, which is what makes it impossible to forget to remove — and it had been excluded from BOTH the neutral-pairs test and the code palette's AA pass for exactly this |
| <a id="fjs-310"></a>FJS-310 | css — **a toned block's text is `--tint-ink`, and one pair of it is under AA** | 2026-08-16 | Filed by me while closing [FJS-027](#closed), and closed the same way: `--tint-ink` now goes through the tone-as-text legibility window after the 55% blend, which is the 2026-08-08 ruling (*clamped, not blended*) reaching the last place in the package that had not applied it. The row listed three ways out and this is the third; the other two were measured and rejected — retuning `sunset`'s `--color-warning` moves a brand hue to fix one pairing, and raising the blend past 55% flattens every well-tuned theme to fix one. Measured across 10 themes × 7 tones: a **no-op on 53 of the 70 pairs**, 17 move and all 17 move UP, none regresses, sunset/warning goes 3.86 → 6.77. The row's caveat about the window being wrong for a tint (it sits on `--tint-surface`, not `--surface`) was measured rather than reasoned about: the tint is 10% tone into the surface, close enough that one window serves both. `contrast.spec.js` now asks all three questions a tone faces — as a FILL, as TEXT, and as a toned BLOCK — and the third had never been checked |
| <a id="fjs-164"></a>FJS-164 | css — **the focus ring's STYLE is fixed** | 2026-08-16 | `--ring-style`, and the row's counter-argument is what shaped it: a token a theme can write is a token a theme can weaken. It is REGISTERED rather than merely named — `@property` with a `syntax` of `solid`/`dashed`/`double` — so a value outside the list is invalid at computed-value time and falls back to the initial value. **A theme writing `--ring-style: none` gets a solid ring, not no ring**, and the same for `hidden` or a typo; there is no spelling of "off". `dotted` is excluded for the reason `none` is: at 2px it is materially harder to see, which is the property being protected. So the answer to *may a theme restyle the accessibility guarantee* is yes, from a list the platform enforces. press.css takes `double` |
| <a id="fjs-163"></a>FJS-163 | css — **only three components expose their typography to a theme** | 2026-08-16 | One triple — `--label-font-weight` / `--label-text-transform` / `--label-letter-spacing` — read by all three of the places the row named: a table head, a tile's label and a nav group's heading. **The weights are unified rather than each kept behind its own escape token**: they were 600/0.04em, 500/0.04em and 700/0.06em, none documented and none reachable, and three answers for one role is the defect rather than a constraint to preserve. Headings gain `--heading-font-weight`, written as the fallback arm exactly like `--heading-letter-spacing`, so unset keeps h1/h2 at 700 and the rest at 600 and one declaration moves all six. That closes the row's own framing — the SIZE ladder was themable and the treatment was not |
| <a id="fjs-162"></a>FJS-162 | css — **motion is not themable — no duration and no easing token** | 2026-08-16 | Four rungs named for the job rather than the length — `--motion-fast` (a colour, a border, a background changing), `--motion-base` (a control changing shape), `--motion-enter` (something arriving or leaving), `--motion-slow` (a measurement moving) — plus `--motion-spin`, `--motion-shimmer` and three easings. **A spinner's easing is deliberately not a token**: `linear` at the use site, because a spinner that eases reads as broken hardware rather than a slower spinner. The row is right that `prefers-reduced-motion` is a different question, and the test says so — a literal duration INSIDE that query is correct (it is the reader's setting, and `tokens.css` hands the spinner back a slower 1.6s there), so the sweep for literals excludes it by walking the parent rules, and excludes the harness's own `transition: none !important` as the one stylesheet on the page with no `href`. That sweep found one the grep did not: `.skeleton`'s 1.4s shimmer. **The overlay tier caught the alias trap in the act** — the first version aliased `--overlay-time: var(--motion-enter)` at `:root`, which resolves once and inherits past every `.theme-*`, so a theme retuning the ladder would have moved everything except the overlays; it is read at each use site instead, which puts a comma inside a transition segment and turned `overlays.spec.js` red against CSS that was exactly right |
| <a id="fjs-094"></a>FJS-094 | caravan — **a job file could not declare its schedule** | 2026-08-16 | `HandlerOptions` grows `cron` and `timeZone`, so a registration says WHEN it runs beside WHAT it does, and `defineJob` passes both through — a `*.job.ts` file now declares everything about itself and needs no line in `app.ts`. The schedule is registered in **`handle()`** rather than in `schedule()`, which is the part that makes it reachable at all: `handle` is the only call autoload makes. `schedule(name, expr, fn)` is now sugar over it, so a cron declared in a file and one declared in a call are the same registration rather than two paths that can disagree. A name is a schedule and not a list of them — re-registering replaces, where two `schedule()` calls with one name used to fire the job twice a minute — and an unparseable expression is refused naming the JOB, because the expression alone no longer says which file to open. `example/api/jobs/sweep-abandoned.ts` was named `.ts` to keep it out of the autoload glob; it is `sweep-abandoned.job.ts` now, the `queue.schedule(…)` line in `example/api/src/app.ts` is gone, and `verify:jobs` passes 8/8 with the cron read off the file — [caravan/src/index.ts](packages/caravan/src/index.ts) · [tests/declaration.test.ts](packages/caravan/tests/declaration.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-093"></a>FJS-093 | caravan — **a job handler could not reach the app, and ran as nobody** | 2026-08-16 | `defineJob('x', async (job) => …)` was called with one argument — no `job.app`, no second parameter, and `autoload.ts` imported the file without passing anything — so an autoloaded `*.job.ts` could not call `app.service(…)`. In this framework that is what a job mostly wants: the service layer is the one place a write is announced ([FJS-D21](ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md#fjs-d21)), and a job reaching for `db.asSystem()` instead writes silently ([FJS-010](#)). Caravan is handed `app` in its own `register(app)` and kept it; `example/api/app-ref.ts` was the workaround — a module-level back-reference `app.ts` set and job files read, saying so in its own header. **A handler now takes a `JobContext`**: the job's facts plus `app` and `auth`. `ctx.app` closes the filed half; `ctx.auth` closes the one filed beside it in `caravan/CLAUDE.md` — a job had no principal, and no principal is STRANGER(0), so every job in every app carried `{ auth: { user: SYSTEM } }` by hand, which *also* ran a customer's work with the authority of the shop. `dispatch()` records `app.principal()?.userId` in an `actor_id` column and the worker runs the handler inside `app.runAs(actorId, …)`, so a service call in a handler names no `auth` and inherits one. **An id, not a session** — a caller demoted between asking and running is graded at what they hold now, where a snapshot is a privilege outliving its own revocation. Nobody asked (cron, boot) resolves to `createApp({ system })`; an unresolvable actor fails the job by name rather than downgrading. Proved in `example`'s audit trail: the `book-courier` write names the staff member who pressed Ship, where every background write used to say `system`. `example/api/app-ref.ts` is deleted — [caravan/CHANGES.md](packages/caravan/CHANGES.md) 2026-08-16 · [tests/job-context.test.ts](packages/caravan/tests/job-context.test.ts) · [junction/CHANGES.md](packages/junction/CHANGES.md) |
| <a id="fjs-090"></a>FJS-090 | caravan — **a job's name was stated up to three times and nothing reconciled them** | 2026-08-16 | Two halves. **The file is the reconciliation**: autoload compares the `defineJob` name against the file it is in and THROWS on a mismatch, naming both and saying which to rename — `send-emial.job.ts` used to register a handler no dispatch ever reached, and the only symptom was a job that silently never ran. **The definition is the dispatch handle**: `defineJob` returns a `JobDefinition<T>` and `dispatch(bookCourier, { orderId })` takes the name off it, so a call site states no string and `data` is typed by the handler that will receive it rather than being `unknown` on both sides. `handle(definition)` registers one whole, options and cron included. Dispatch-by-name still works — a separate worker process holding the handler is a real shape and refusing it would be worse. `example`'s two dispatch sites now import the definitions, which also deletes the hand-written `ctx.app as { jobs?: … }` cast at each: importing a job file puts Caravan's augmentation of `app.jobs` in the program — [caravan/src/autoload.ts](packages/caravan/src/autoload.ts) · [example/api/services/orders.service.ts](example/api/src/services/orders.service.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-048"></a>FJS-048 | caravan — **the `junction.config.js` caravan section was partial** | 2026-08-16 | `register()` honours every key of it now: `db`, `jobsDir`, `cleanupAfter`, `pollInterval`, `queues`, `admin`. What blocked four of them was that `createCaravan()` opened the database and built the workers at construction, before any app existed to hand a config over. **The database opens on first use** and **the workers are built in `start()`**, after autoload — so nothing a config file sets has been spent by the time `register()` reads it, and a queue named by a config file, a job file or a runtime dispatch all arrive the same way. Two adjacent defects came out with it: the merge tested truthiness, so `cleanupAfter: 0` — the way to turn the sweep off — read as unset from either side (now `=== undefined`, and `admin: false` with it); and `stop()` closed the database while the pool kept the workers holding it, so a `start()` after a `stop()` polled through a closed handle. A dispatch before `configure()` opens the queue early and is warned about by path rather than silently running against the wrong file. 8 tests — [caravan/src/index.ts](packages/caravan/src/index.ts) · [tests/junction-integration.test.ts](packages/caravan/tests/junction-integration.test.ts) |
| <a id="fjs-256"></a>FJS-256 | cli — **`fli ws:atlas` links `@frontierjs/css` from unpkg, so the page does not render without the network** | 2026-08-16 | Inlined, built from committed source on every run — `src/index.css` is 48 `@import … layer(name)` lines plus the `@layer a, b, c;` declaration, so each import becomes an `@layer name { … }` block and the declaration is carried over first and verbatim (without it, layer order falls back to first appearance, which agrees with the declaration only by accident of import order — `packages/css/build.js` refuses to ship a bundle missing it for the same reason). **Not `dist/`, which the row proposed and which is that file already**: it is gitignored and built on demand, so inlining-if-present and linking-if-absent makes the OUTPUT depend on whether somebody ran `bun run build` — and these pages are committed snapshots the `snapshots` phase regenerates and diffs, so two machines would disagree about a file neither changed. Measured while fixing it: the tree's `dist/` held a two-day-old `frontier.css` and **no `frontier.min.css` at all**, which is the same defect wearing a stale copy. **Every import must resolve or the bundle is refused**, stated as *all of them* rather than as a floor — a count is a guess a small tree fails and a truncated big one passes — because a partial bundle renders, looks nearly right, and is missing whichever layer went absent. Comments are stripped by a quote-aware pass (60% of that package's source; `content: "/*"` is legal CSS and is asserted). **The row's third edge closed first and painfully**: the derived range was not merely interim, it was wrong — below 1.0 a caret pins the MINOR, so `@^0.16` means `>=0.16.0 <0.17.0` and excluded every published copy the day css bumped its minor in the tree. Measured that day: `@^0.16` 404, no range 302. The link survives only as the fallback for a tree that cannot read the package at all, and carries no range. Verified by loading the generated page in headless Chrome with no stylesheet request in it: `body` at the field theme's own ground, `--surface` resolving, `.btn` taking its padding off the space ladder. 4 new tests; the two that sliced the local CSS at the first `<style>` now name it by id, one of which had started passing vacuously — [repo-map.js](packages/cli/core/repo-map.js) · [repo-atlas.js](packages/cli/core/repo-atlas.js) |
| <a id="fjs-067"></a>FJS-067 | mesa — **the deferred set: `tick()`, `{#virtual each}` in SSR, variable-height virtual lists, full hydration SSR, TypeScript** | 2026-08-16 | Four of the five were never open, and the fifth was not what the row said. **`$tick()` ships** and is tested (`runtime.js`, `runtime.test.js` §4) — it chains onto a resolved promise, which lands the callback after the flush microtask a signal write already queued. **Full hydration SSR and TypeScript are deferred by the language spec** (VISION RULE 20), and **variable-height rows by RULE 34** — those are roadmap, which this register says belongs in `IDEAS/`, not defects. **`{#virtual each}` produces no SSR output is simply false**: `renderToHTML` sets `isBrowser` true so the block runs, and a 1000-row list measured 25 rows plus a 39000px bottom spacer — a prerendered page gets its first screen, which is what the row wanted a `{:static}` fallback to provide. Five documents and two source comments said *renders nothing*; all seven now say what it does. `$: fn()` post-execution hooks was an orphan phrase with no referent anywhere in the repo. **What the audit did find is a real defect and is fixed**: the documented options (`height=N viewport="500px"`, VISION §9.7, required by RULE 34) were swallowed into the `as` binding, so every component written from the docs died at compile time as `Unexpected identifier 'height'` — naming neither the block, nor the option, nor the file's real problem. They parse now, at bracket depth 0 so a destructuring default is not mistaken for one; an unknown option and a non-positive height are refused by name; `height` skips measurement (a row sized by a stylesheet measures 0 on first paint) and `viewport` sizes the block's own element, which IS the scroller. 8 tests, both halves mutation-checked |
| <a id="fjs-291"></a>FJS-291 | mesa — **the Vite plugin's virtual CSS module could not fire, and `css: false` dropped the stylesheet on the floor** | 2026-08-16 | The route is deleted rather than repaired. The compiler's `css` is a **destination, not a switch** — truthy inlines the scoped rules as `$runtime.addStyles(id, …)`, falsy extracts them onto `ctx.css.result` — and the plugin was passing its own option straight through while reading the answer as if the word meant the same thing on both sides, so `if (css && ctx.css?.result)` was a condition no compiler could satisfy. Gone with it: `cssCache`, the `?mesa-css` id, its `resolveId`/`load` pair and `handleHotUpdate`'s CSS invalidation. **Inlining is the one route, and it is the one Sierra's plugin takes for the same file** — the ids are content-addressed, which is what lets a prerendered page and the client agree about which styles are already on the page (Invariant 12); a second delivery route here would have made one `.mesa` file's CSS arrive differently depending on which plugin compiled it. `css: false` now means what it says — the block is compiled and DROPPED — and it is stated in the option doc, the README and a test, instead of being a silent consequence of a branch nobody could reach. Its one live victim was `mesa-bench`, whose config set `css: false` to avoid virtual CSS modules and thereby rendered every bench component unstyled; the option is off its config now. Pinned in three places: the inline path over a real dev server (`test/vite-server.test.js`), the absent second route and the honest `false` (`test/vite-plugin.test.js`), and an extracted stylesheet having no reader (`test/vite-compiler-resolution.test.js`). Wiring Vite-owned stylesheets up remains possible and is filed rather than dropped: `IDEAS/css-delivery.md` (overview 5.20) holds what it would buy, why it cannot be done in one plugin alone, and the measurement it wants first |
| <a id="fjs-493"></a>FJS-493 | jetty · toolbelt · sierra — **A record pushed on a channel was upserted whatever it was, so a row that had LEFT the list stayed in it** | 2026-08-26 | Found building `example/extension/`: the dock's despatch queue, loaded as `{ status: 'paid' }`, got the order it had just SHIPPED back as `orders ship` and put it straight into the list it had left. **A record is an announcement about a ROW and a live list is the answer to a QUERY**, and nothing on the wire says a row has left a filter — there is no such event — so a store that applies every announcement it gets is wrong here and only here. Sierra answered this in `FJS-011` with `matchesQuery`; the two live stores had one implementation between them and it was on the wrong side of the dependency direction. **Fixed by moving it, not by repairing it a second time** — `@frontierjs/toolbelt/match`, the fourth pure half to come down for `FJS-059`'s reason after `/hooks`, `/jsonschema` and `/inflect`. Sierra re-exports, so every caller there is unchanged, and its test keeps the SEAM (does `createResource` hand the matcher down, built over the model it resolved) plus one line asserting the re-export IS that function rather than a copy made to fix an import. **Three answers, three actions**: in the filter upsert, out of it REMOVE, undecidable RELOAD — coalesced onto a microtask, since a burst of undecidable pushes arrives together and every answer but the last is thrown away by the one after it. **The store now remembers the query its rows answer**, because nothing else can grade an arriving record; `set()` clears it, since rows put there by hand are not the answer to the last `populate()`'s question, and `null` is not `{}` — an empty filter admits every row, *nobody has asked yet* can grade none. The field table is `fieldShapes` off the schema the resource was given (new in `/jsonschema`, and what `buildFieldRules` now reads its own type and nullability through, so there is one owner of *what type is this field*), `{}` where it was given none — exactly sierra's fallback on a registry miss, degrading one way: a string operand against a numeric column reads as no match. **The dock's render-time filter is gone**, which is what gives `verify:extension`'s *the shipped order leaves the despatch queue* teeth — it passed either way while the screen was also deciding. 6 cases in `test/phase3.test.js`, negative-controlled (forcing the verdict to `true` turns 4 red); 31 behavioural cases moved to `toolbelt/test/specs/match.spec.js` with 6 more for the shapes. Green: jetty 445, toolbelt 251, sierra 1114, typecheck clean on all three · [match.js](packages/toolbelt/src/match/match.js) · [jetty resource.js](packages/jetty/src/resources/resource.js) · [jetty store.js](packages/jetty/src/resources/store.js) · [FJS-059](#fjs-059) · [FJS-011](#fjs-011) |
| <a id="fjs-535"></a>FJS-535 | junction · litestone — **A window minted its edge off a page that was walked in a different order, so a tie across the edge lost rows — silently, once per tie** | 2026-08-26 | The first window is an ordinary `findManyAndCount` and the cursor is minted off its last row, which is what makes growing a window cost no extra query (`FJS-D145`). The page was ordered by the CALLER's `orderBy` and the cursor by the TOTAL order — the caller's plus the tiebreaker litestone appends — so where two rows tie on every sort key the page stopped wherever SQLite happened to stop and the edge named where the total order says it stopped. The rows between the two were never served, and the list reported itself complete. **Invisible in every fixture built one row at a time**: an ordering only goes partial on a tie, a tie on `createdAt` is a burst of writes inside one millisecond, and `window.test.ts`'s existing non-unique walk passed because it ordered ASCENDING over sequential ids, where SQLite's own order and the total order agree by accident. **Found by building it**: basecamp's audit trail, five rows sharing one timestamp across the 50-row edge, two of them gone. `table.orderTotal(orderBy)` is the fix and it is one owner — the caller's ordering plus whatever makes it total, `null` where no tie can be broken — used for the page's ORDER BY and for the cursor alike. A list that cannot be totally ordered now carries **no edge rather than a wrong one**: the read is still answered, since a read is not a request for a window, and `endCursor` is null so nothing asks for a position that cannot be named. Regression is the same walk with the tiebreaker running AGAINST the scan order (`orderBy: '-rank'` over tied rows), which loses 12 of 25 without the fix. junction 16 pass in `tests/window.test.ts`, litestone green, `basecamp: verify:screens` 35/35. [litestone.ts `findWindow`](packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts) · [client.js `orderTotal`](packages/litestone/src/core/client.js) · [window.test.ts](packages/junction/tests/window.test.ts) · [FJS-D145](DECISIONS.md#fjs-d145) |
| <a id="fjs-321"></a>FJS-321 | mesa — **`e.currentTarget` in a delegated handler was the delegation ROOT, so `\| self` admitted nothing** | 2026-08-16 | Under `addEventListener`, `currentTarget` is the element the handler is written on, and `e.target === e.currentTarget` is the standard *was this element itself clicked* test — it is exactly what the `\| self` modifier compiles to. Delegation puts one listener on the root and hands every handler the raw event, so `currentTarget` was the root: the comparison was false for precisely the events `\| self` exists to admit, and true for none. Silent, and in the direction where nothing happens at all. `_makeDelegatedHandler` now defines `currentTarget` per node before calling the handler and drops the override afterwards, so a native listener further up the same dispatch still reads its own. Found underneath [FJS-322](#fjs-322) — a command palette whose backdrop could not be clicked away — and it reaches every hand-written `currentTarget` comparison, not just the modifier. Two tests, one of them that the native listener is unaffected — [runtime.js](packages/mesa/src/runtime.js) · [runtime.test.js](packages/mesa/test/runtime.test.js) |

## See also

- `ISSUES_ARCHIVE.md` — closed rows aged out of § Closed. Same table, verbatim. Read when tracing a specific id, never cold.
- `CLAUDE.md` § Live hazards — traps that are **correct behaviour**; knowing them saves a day. Not issues.
- `DECISIONS.md` — settled rulings. A `FJS-D##` closing here lands there.
- `IDEAS/` — roadmap and design records for work not started. `IDEAS/overview.md`'s wave 0 is the subset of this file that happens to be argued in an ideas file.
- `HANDOFF.md` — per-session narrative, newest first. History, not a ledger.
- `VERIFYING.md` — how to promote a `stale?` row to `open` or `closed`.
